# Architecture

**Scope:** TeamRun's packages, how its engine runs inside Noldova Desktop Runtime, its window part, the contract its methods, events and failures keep with the window, and the MCP server through which teammates reach TeamRun and shared MCP servers.

## The packages

| Package | Folder | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| `@noldova/teamrun.services` | `src/noldova.teamrun.services` | The response envelope every service answers with. |
| `@noldova/teamrun.protocol` | `src/noldova.teamrun.protocol` | TeamRun's methods, events, models and error codes, and its MCP server. |
| `@noldova/teamrun.core` | `src/noldova.teamrun.core` | The chat engine: projects, conversations, messages, approvals, teammates, and the replies it runs through providers. |
| `@noldova/teamrun.providers` | `src/noldova.teamrun.providers` | The Claude, Codex, Grok, OpenCode, Cursor, fx and Antigravity providers and the ACP client they share, behind the engine's provider contract. |
| `@noldova/teamrun.runtime` | `src/noldova.teamrun.runtime` | TeamRun's runtime part: the engine and its providers, hosted by Noldova Desktop Runtime. |
| `@noldova/teamrun.cli` | `src/noldova.teamrun.cli` | TeamRun's command-line part: the `teamrun` commands, which reach the runtime part through its methods. |
| `@noldova/teamrun.window` | `src/noldova.teamrun.window` | TeamRun's window part: the views and documents the window shows, an Angular package. |
| `@noldova/teamrun` | `src/noldova.teamrun` | TeamRun's module: its declaration, `module.json`, its runtime part at `@noldova/teamrun/runtime`, its window part at `@noldova/teamrun/window` and its command-line part at `@noldova/teamrun/cli`. |

Core and the runtime part reach the database through context's data packages, `@noldova/context.data`, `@noldova/context.data.sql` and `@noldova/context.data.sql.sqlite`, whose connections, transactions and migrations are asynchronous.
A connection runs one piece of work at a time, so a change that reads and then writes, such as a revision or a version check, runs in one transaction, and every call inside it takes that transaction rather than the connection, which would wait for the transaction to end.
In the app, the runtime part's `ModuleDatabaseConnection` reaches the module's database, which Noldova Desktop opens and migrates, and Pages uses the same connection, so their statements never interleave.

Core also has a testing entry, `@noldova/teamrun.core/testing`, which `src/api/testing.ts` and its declarations `src/api/testing.d.ts` give.
It holds the test doubles in Core's `src/testing/`, `ManualTimer` and `ManualDelay`, which Core's and Providers' specs share; the main entry never exports them, and a spec checks that.
The build copies every declarations file in a package's `src/api/` beside its compiled entries, so the testing entry needs only its export and its place in `files` in Core's `package.json`.

Product names and trademarks, such as the providers' names above, belong to their owners.

## The module

- An app lists TeamRun in its `desktop.modules` as `{ "id": "teamrun", "package": "@noldova/teamrun" }`, as Noldova Desktop's `MODULES.md` says for modules from packages.
- `module.json` declares the module `teamrun` with its runtime and window parts and contributes every method and event the runtime part registers; a test fails when the two differ.
- Its command-line part's commands are declared in `module.json`'s `cliCommands` and defined in `cli.json` beside it; a test fails when the two differ or the part registers another list.
  A command reads its values, makes one or more requests of the runtime part's methods, and returns their JSON with a line of text per item.
- The module's version is the package's version, which the build stamps from the root `package.json`; a test fails when `module.json` names another.
- `module.json` depends on the Terminal module, whose window package the window part uses, at the exact version that package is installed at, since Noldova Desktop takes one exact version per dependency and starts the Terminal module first; a test fails when the two differ.
- `module.json` also depends on the checkpoints module, at the exact version the runtime package takes `@noldova/checkpoints` at, so Noldova Desktop starts it first and the runtime part gets the service it publishes as `checkpoints.service`; a test fails when the two differ.
- `module.json` names the computer-use module as an optional dependency, at the exact version TeamRun's specs install `@noldova/computer-use.runtime` at, so the runtime part finds the apps service it publishes as `computer-use.apps` when the app includes that module and it is active, and works without it otherwise; a test fails when the two differ.
- `module.json` also names the Browser module as an optional dependency, at the exact version TeamRun's specs install `@noldova/browser.window` at, since Noldova Desktop runs a window's command only for a module its `module.json` names: without it, the window's `browser.openPage` for a chat's page is refused, and a teammate's Browser call finds the page closed; a test fails when the two differ.
- `module.json`'s `noldovaDesktop` names the Noldova Desktop versions the module accepts, as Noldova Desktop's `MODULES.md` says under "Noldova Desktop versions", in its one form `>=<lowest> <first refused>`, now `>=0.0.9 <0.1.0`, whose end is the next minor below 1.0, or the next major from 1.0.
  The version the module builds and tests against, the root `package.json`'s pin, lies inside that range, now 0.0.9, which is also where the range starts; `MODULES.md` has a module raise its lowest version only when it starts to use something newer.
  A Noldova Desktop release that doesn't break modules then needs no TeamRun release.
- Every TeamRun package takes Noldova Desktop's, context's and the kit's packages as peer dependencies, so the app installs one copy of each; the Dependency pins check fails on one among a package's dependencies.
  Its peers on Noldova Desktop's packages take `noldovaDesktop`, and those on context's and the kit's packages a range of the same form, from the version the module builds against, or one below it, to the next minor, now `>=0.0.8 <0.1.0` and `>=0.0.18 <0.1.0`; the Dependency pins check fails when a peer on context's or the kit's packages names an exact version or starts more than one version below the pin, or when the ranges differ from `noldovaDesktop`, end elsewhere, or leave out the pin of the project that installs the package, the root's, or `src/`'s for the window part.
- **Node's API:** the runtime part runs in the app's main process on the Node.js that Electron embeds, 24.22.0 in Noldova Desktop 0.0.9's Electron 44.9.1, so it calls only what that Node has.
  The build type-checks it against `@types/node` 26 and the tests run on Node 26.13.1, so a call only Node 26 has passes both, and a review keeps it out.

## The window part

- The window part is `@noldova/teamrun.window`, the module package's name with `-window` added, which `@noldova/teamrun` lists among its dependencies at its exact version and re-exports at `@noldova/teamrun/window`, as Noldova Desktop's `MODULES.md` says.
- It is an Angular package, compiled in partial compilation mode, since the window's build compiles no TypeScript from installed packages.
- It takes Angular, Noldova Desktop's window package and the kit as optional peer dependencies, at the versions Noldova Desktop's build gives, so the app's install adds no copy of its own: the one copy is the window workspace's, which npm hoists to the app's root `node_modules`, and only it loads.
- It also takes Terminal's window package, `@noldova/terminal.window`, as an optional peer at the version the app's `@noldova/terminal` brings, which the app's window app, `app/src/noldova.teamrun.window.app`, pins as Noldova Desktop's build requires, so it shares that one copy with the terminal's own part.
- It takes Git's window package, `@noldova/git.window`, as an optional peer the same way, at the version the app's `@noldova/git` brings, and while it is active gives the Git view the shown chat's folder through `GitFolder.provide`, the folder the chat's agents work in; the provider is released when the part deactivates.
- It takes the GitHub module's window and protocol packages, `@noldova/git.github.window`, `@noldova/git.github.protocol` and `@noldova/git.hosting.protocol`, as optional peers the same way, at the version the app's `@noldova/git.github` brings, and the module manifest names `git-github` as an optional dependency, so the part may call its methods and follow its events.
  While active, the part keeps one `GitHubReferenceSource` for the window, which the history message gives every message's Markdown as its `references`, a reply's and the person's own alike, so a GitHub link looks the same whoever wrote it; the message's text stays as typed, so the composer, an edit and Copy keep the plain URL.
  - A link counts when it is `https://github.com/<owner>/<repository>/issues/<n>` or `.../pull/<n>`, with an optional trailing `/`, query and fragment; any other link, another host, a port or user info stays a plain link (`GitHubReferenceLinks`).
  - Text outside code and links that reads `<owner>/<repository>#<n>` counts too, as the issue address `https://github.com/<owner>/<repository>/issues/<n>`, which GitHub forwards to a pull request.
  - A bare `#<n>` counts as an item of the chat's repository: the part reads the chat folder's status through `git.status` when the chat first shows a message and again each time the chat opens, from Git's protocol package, `@noldova/git.protocol`, which the part takes as an optional peer at the version the app's `@noldova/git` brings, with `git` an optional dependency in the module manifest.
    The repository is the remote's path when its host is `github.com` and the path is `<owner>/<repository>`; the history message gives the Markdown a reader for that repository, so the text is read again once it is known.
    With no Git module, no remote, another host or a status that fails, `#<n>` stays text, and the part reads every folder again when Git comes back.
    A read still under way when Git goes off, or overtaken by a newer read of its folder, is dropped, so a folder's repository stays unknown while Git is off.
    The part doesn't follow `git.changed`: it fires when a watched folder's status moves, which a remote added or changed alone never does, and a watch polls the folder's status every 5 seconds.
  - Each item is read once per window through `git-github.readReference`, whatever link or message names it; the chip shows its reference with GitHub's mark and a muted icon while the read is under way, and `GitHubReferenceLooks` turns the answer and `GitHubLooks`' look for its kind and state into the chip and its card.
    At most 4 reads run at a time, the rest wait their turn, and the source remembers the 500 items named last.
    Each part of the card opens its own GitHub page through the app's open-link path, as the chip does: the state badge, the `#<n>` and the title the item's page, as GitHub's answer gives it; the repository's name `https://github.com/<owner>/<repository>`; the author their profile page as GitHub's answer gives it, which for an app is its app's page, or, when the answer gives none, `https://github.com/<login>`, or `https://github.com/apps/<name>` for a `<name>[bot]`; a pull request's lines and files `.../pull/<n>/files`; and an issue's comment count the issue's page, where its comments are.
  - Signed out, offline, a failed or unreadable answer, or the GitHub module not running: the reference stays a plain link exactly as the reply wrote it, nothing asks the person to sign in, and a failure is written to the window's log.
    A sign-in, or the module starting, reads every remembered item again; a sign-out, or the module stopping, turns every chip back into a plain link and drops the reads under way.
- It is built and tested in the Angular project in `src/`, the root's npm workspace, which installs those versions from the root's `package-lock.json`.
- The app's window entry, `app/src/noldova.teamrun.window.app/src/main.ts`, gives the window the kit's code highlighting worker through Noldova Desktop's `providers`, started from `app/src/noldova.teamrun.window.app/src/code-highlighting.worker.ts`, which Angular's build bundles only from the app's own code.
  So a reply's code is tokenized and its grammars load in that worker, off the frames the window draws; a worker that fails is reported once, and the kit then highlights on the page.
- The window activates it with its context, and the part keeps that context until the window deactivates it.
- While active, the part gives Terminal the folder new terminals start in, through `TerminalStartFolder`: the shown chat's project folder, or the folder of its own of a chat without a project, or the home folder with no chat tab open.
- While active, the part also provides the window's terminal owners, through `TerminalOwners`: a chat's own terminals belong to the owner `teamrun.chat/<id>`, which the protocol's `Resources.chatTerminalOwnerPrefix` starts.
  New chat terminal, `teamrun.newChatTerminal` on Ctrl+Alt+\` (Cmd+Alt+\` on macOS) and in Terminal's shell menu, opens one for the shown chat, in the shell menu's tab group, and is disabled with no chat shown.
  The `teamrun.newTerminals` setting, General by default and shared by every device, decides whether Terminal's +, Toggle Terminal's first open and, on Terminal 0.0.17, the shells in its shell menu give a general terminal or one of the shown chat, a general one with no chat shown; Terminal's New terminal, its key and the View menu always open a general one, and from Terminal 0.0.18 the shell menu's shells do too.
  A reply's shell code blocks, those its fence tags bash, sh, shell, zsh, powershell, pwsh or cmd, offer Run once the reply has stopped streaming, through the kit's `shellRuns` on its message; the person's own messages offer none.
  Run sends the block's code to the reply's chat through `ChatTerminals`, which `ChatTerminals.of(context)` finds while the part is active, and which runs it with `TerminalOwners.runAsync` as the chat's owner, in the chat's folder, with the shells the block's language names: Bash for bash, Bash or Zsh for sh and shell, Zsh for zsh, PowerShell for powershell and pwsh, Command Prompt for cmd.
  The `teamrun.runOpensIn` setting, Chat by default and shared by every device, is read at each Run: Terminals panel runs it there as above; Chat hands it to `InlineTerminals`, which the window gives `ChatTerminals.provide`.
  `InlineTerminals` starts it with Terminal's `startRunAsync` and `shown`, so no tab opens or takes the focus: the first Run goes to Terminal's choice, and each later one into the chat's inline terminal, refused while its shell is busy.
  It keeps the session the Run gave as the chat's inline terminal, from `shown`, before anything is typed, so its pane attaches and the shell gets the pane's size first, and the Run's place: the message, the site (its text, its plan, or a step of Worked for) and the block.
  A Run Terminal refuses as busy leaves the terminal where it was and asks under its own block: Stop it and run runs it again with `Interrupt`, which sends Ctrl+C and types once the shell is back at its prompt, and Run in a new terminal runs it in Terminal's choice, which is another terminal, so the inline terminal becomes that one; an interrupt that finds the shell still busy asks again.
  A Run into a terminal that ended, closed or restarted runs again in Terminal's choice; any other refusal or failure is posted as a failed chat action.
  When the next Run moves the terminal to another block, the earlier block keeps its Run's output: `InlineTerminals` reads it with `readRunOutputAsync`, cut by the shell integration's marks, and the earlier block's slot shows its text in a kit code block, until a Run there again; a run Terminal no longer has keeps none.
  The runtime keeps that output with its block, so it shows again after a reload or a restart: `ChatTerminals` saves it with `RunOutputSave`, a `RunOutput` of the chat, the message, the site, the block and Terminal's `TerminalRunOutput.toJson`, into the `runOutputs` table, keyed by the message, the site and the block, which replaces what that block kept; a rewind that removes the message removes its outputs, and deleting the chat removes the chat's.
  A block counts as kept once the next Run has moved the terminal on and that save has landed, so a reload or a quit in between keeps nothing for it, as for the latest Run; the earlier block shows its output only once the save has landed, or its failure is posted, so an output the window shows is one a reload brings back.
  A chat keeps at most 8,000,000 characters of output, `Resources.runOutputsKeptCharacters`, so that `RunOutputList` answers in one frame, well inside Noldova Desktop's 16M-character limit: each save drops the chat's oldest saved outputs beyond it, and an output larger than that isn't saved, and takes what its block kept with it.
  When a chat first shows in a window, `InlineTerminals` reads its kept outputs with `RunOutputList`, once until the chat closes, and adds each one at its block, except the live terminal's block and a block the window already holds an output for; a read or a save that fails is posted as a failed chat action.
  The kept output is the person's own: it is never in a message's text, so no teammate reads it.
  The message, the plan card and the step whose site that is name the block, and each block with a question or an earlier output, in the kit's `shellRunOutputs`, so the kit draws its `nolShellRunOutput` slot under that block, across its width, and the slot's `nol-teamrun-inline-terminal` takes the chat's terminal: it opens it there with Terminal's `EmbeddedTerminal`, with the slot's injector, at the first slot that asks, and each later Run's slot moves the same element, so the session never reopens; a Run that gives another session disposes of the old one.
  Each Run's block shows its state in the kit's `shellRunStates`: Running, the Run button busy but pressable and named "Run bash command (running)", from the Run's start until Terminal's `TerminalRun.ended` resolves, then Ended, named "Run bash command again"; a Run refused as busy, or given no place, names no state, and a shell without integration ends at once, so its Run never looks running.
  Ctrl+` inside the inline terminal goes back to the chat's message box: the terminal's element takes the key before the pane does, so Terminal's Toggle Terminal, which keeps Ctrl+` everywhere else, doesn't run and the dock stays as it was.
  A Run focuses the terminal once its slot holds it; a slot that goes, as a history row the list recycles, takes the element out, hidden, and the next slot of that place puts it back.
  The terminal grows to 15 rows, at most the rows that fill half the history's viewport, counted from Terminal's row height, but at least 8 rows, so a prompt and its output show, even Windows' Git Bash prompt of 4 rows, on a short history, though never more than the history holds with a row to spare for the terminal's inset; it fits again as the history or the row height changes, not as its output grows; the next session's terminal opens at the same rows.
  It draws only the terminal of the chat on screen, and closes a deleted chat's and, once `ChatTerminals` is removed, every one; a Run that starts after its chat closed keeps nothing, so the chat doesn't come back.
  Terminal types it into the chat's newest terminal that is free, and opens one only when none is, so chats never share a terminal and a busy shell gets a new one; nothing runs until the person presses Run.
  A Command Prompt terminal is never free, since Terminal tells a free shell by its shell integration, which Command Prompt lacks, so each Run of a cmd block opens a new terminal until the terminal module tells a free Command Prompt apart.
  Any installed shell of the kinds takes the block, such as Git Bash on Windows or Zsh on Linux; with none installed, Terminal opens nothing and refuses the run for `shellKinds` with a message naming the kinds, which the failure below shows.
  An empty block, a language without shells, a block `TerminalOwners.canRun` refuses, such as one with a control or invisible character, and a run Terminal refuses or fails, post `teamrun.chatActionFailed` keyed `RunCode/<chat>` with Open chat.
- While active, the part also gives computer use the colour of the border it shows around every display while an agent controls the screen: the window's accent, the root's computed `--nol-accent`, through computer use's `computer-use.borderColorSet`.
  `ComputerUseBorder` sends it when the window activates the part, again in `reconnectAsync` and when the window reports computer use active again, since computer use's runtime part keeps the colour only while it runs, and whenever the kit paints the root's style with another accent, as a change of theme or mode does.
  It sends only an accent written `#RRGGBB`, the form computer use takes, so with another the border keeps the last colour it got.
  A refused or failed request changes nothing, since computer use may be absent; the border then keeps the last colour it got, or the kit's accent.
  The person's switch for the border, `computer-use.showBorder`, is computer use's own setting, which the app shows among the module's settings.
- While active, the part also follows Noldova Desktop's `desktop.timeFormat` and `desktop.dateTimeFormat`, through `TimeFormatService`, which writes a time in them with Noldova Desktop's `DatePattern`: the time format for a reply's time in Activity and Changes and a plan's reset today, and the date-and-time format for a reset on a later day, a computer use grant and a chat's tooltip in the Projects view.
  A change redraws the times shown, a blank or missing format reads as Noldova Desktop's 12-hour default, `h:mm a` or `MMM d, yyyy, h:mm a`, and once the part is deactivated the defaults hold again.
  The Limits figures write a reset on a later day in one short form that follows the date-and-time format's order without its year or weekday: its day and month, with its time within a day, such as `Oct 23, 8:00 AM` or `23 Oct, 08:00`, and alone beyond, such as `Oct 27` or `27 Oct`; a format with no day or month left writes `d MMM`.
- The part's views share one `ChatStoreService` for its context, which holds what the window part took from the runtime part and keeps it in step through TeamRun's events.
  When the runtime starts again, the events sent meanwhile are lost, so `reconnectAsync` reads the store's state again, as a resync request does: the catalog, the working replies, the pending approvals and the selected conversation's messages, approvals and members.
  Nothing in the engine publishes `StateResyncRequested`, so each other reader of engine state listens through `EngineResync.on`, which takes both that event and `EngineResync.request`, and `reconnectAsync` calls `request`: the Tasks panel, the projects' and the organization's layouts, the composer's default, the organization and its messages, the teammates' last replies, what a card's teammate worked on, and the model lists of the composer's picker, the teammates view and the members list read again, the model lists from the runtime rather than the model catalog cache, which a runtime resync request also expires, and `reconnectAsync` asks Pages through its `PagesViewComponent.resync` entry, which reads the open pages and each loaded chat's pages again and has each open page document read its page again, with the bar of an edit made meanwhile, since Pages takes nothing from TeamRun; once the chat store has read its state again, `reconnectAsync` has the title-model setting load its models again for the providers it now lists; the reply history, the Tasks view and the sub-agents view follow the chat store's resyncs.
  It answers whether that worked, so a failed read builds the part again; a store that only watches reads the open replies again, and before the window activates it, it answers false.
  When Open folder runs again before the earlier one's reply comes back, the folder opened last ends selected, whatever order Core answers in, and an earlier folder is selected only while no later one has been; an open still waiting when the person selects a project, or opens or starts a chat, adds its project without selecting it.
- What is tied to a chat follows the store's `shownConversationId`, and not its `selectedConversationId`: today Activity, Changes, Plan, Terminal's start folder and the Projects view's marked chat.
  It is the selected chat while its tab is open, or else the last chat shown whose tab is still open; a chat tab that another document, such as Settings, covers is still open.
  It is `null` only when no chat tab is open.
  Opening a chat from the Projects view or a search marks that chat at once, through `conversationOpening`, until it is selected, its tab closes, another chat comes into view or a project is shown.
  A chat whose tab is already in view, as in another group, is selected at once instead.
  Its preview tab replaces the one shown, so the chat shown leaves view first, and meanwhile neither another open chat's row is marked nor that chat selected; a chat selected whose tab stays open isn't marked again while the new one loads.
  Closing the last chat tab keeps the selection, for the commands that act on the last chat, such as focusing its composer, which opens it again, but empties those views.
  A chat's own terminals follow it too: the window shows the shown chat's and hides every other chat's, so with no chat shown their tabs close but their shells keep running, hidden, and the tabs come back when the chat is shown again; quitting the app stops them.
  A show Terminal refuses posts `teamrun.chatActionFailed` keyed `ShowTerminals/<window>`, since a window shows one chat's terminals at a time and a later failure replaces the earlier one, with Open chat for the chat it showed, and the next chat shown is shown as usual.
- Organization, Teammates, Providers, MCP servers and Computer use are documents, `teamrun.organization`, `teamrun.teammates`, `teamrun.providers`, `teamrun.mcpServers` and `teamrun.computerUseGrants`, with one instance each, so they open as one tab in the middle, as Noldova Desktop's Settings does, and a second open shows that tab.
  Organization's instance is an organization's id, so each organization opens as its own tab; the View menu waits for the newest listing and opens its first organization under its name, or My organization's tab, whose view shows the load error with Retry, while none can be read; once a listing has answered and the list holds no organization, Show organization is unavailable and opens nothing, while before the first listing answers it stays available, since the catalog loads only when something asks for it; a tab kept under the earlier instance `organization` shows My organization too.
  The View menu opens Organization through `teamrun.showOrganization`, and TeamRun's Settings page rows and the View menu open the others through `teamrun.showTeammates`, `teamrun.showProviders`, `teamrun.showMcpServers` and `teamrun.showComputerUseGrants`; the composer's links show Teammates and Providers in a dialog, so the chat stays in front.
  They aren't views, so no dock holds them after a layout reset, and a saved layout's earlier views of them don't show.
- Every list of providers or teammates sorts by its shown name with the protocol's `NameOrder`: in the runtime's language, with case ignored and accents kept, so a tie keeps the earlier order.
  Core sorts once, in `ProvidersService.listAsync`, by kind, then label, and `TeammatesService.listAsync`, by name, so the runtime's provider and teammate lists, the command line's `list-providers` and `list-teammates` in their text and JSON, the MCP tools `teamrun_list_providers` and `teamrun_list_teammates`, which run them, all share one order; `list-chats` names each chat's teammates by name too.
  The window's store keeps its `providers` sorted by label and its `teammates` by name as events change them, so the composer's provider and responder menus, the mention completions in each section, a chat's members and Add's menu, the Teammates page and the Providers tab's two lists follow; the kinds keep their own order.
  The MCP servers panel's rows and switches and the teammate dialog's provider choice show a kind beside each name, so they sort by the kind's name, then the provider's, and the dialog starts on its first enabled provider.
  The Providers tab, `ProvidersViewComponent`, has three parts, in this order: Your providers, every provider grouped by its kind; Found on this computer, the folders the search finds that no provider uses yet, grouped the same way; and Add provider, which opens its panel in the form where the button was.
  Each kind's group has a small heading with the kind's name and its count muted beside it, then, in Your providers, its install state, flush with the section title's and the card's left edge, and its rows in their own card under it, a space-4 below the card before it with no rule between; the card takes the side panels' darker fill, `--nol-window`, at the maintainer's ask, and draws its border and the lines between its rows in `--nol-menu-border`, the kit's nearest border that stands out from the panel at least as much as the design's line, in light and dark; a row no longer repeats its kind, and a kind TeamRun no longer lists is headed by its id.
  The kinds keep a fixed order, most used first, `Resources.providerKindOrder`: Codex, Claude Code, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, Grok, OpenCode and Antigravity, then the other kinds TeamRun lists, ACP agents among them, in its order, then the kinds it doesn't list by id; a kind's providers keep the order they were added, and its found folders their names' order.
  In each part all the groups are subgrids of one grid, so the plan chips, limits and ⋯ menus line up down the part.
  Your providers and Found on this computer are two `ConfigurationTableComponent` bodies, which a grid can't reach through, so the two parts don't share columns.
  A machine's heading can later wrap the kinds' groups the same way.
  The panel, `ProviderAddComponent`, has a card per kind with its install state, "Needs <program> first" for a kind that can't install for a program the computer lacks (its `missingTool`) and "Can't install here" for one that can't otherwise, and a last card for an ACP agent, a name filled in with the kind's first free name (Personal, Work, Lab, Spare, then Spare 2, …) and shown with the kind, and one link for an existing folder; Cancel or Escape closes it.
  Add installs a kind TeamRun installs in the panel, with its phase, progress and Cancel, then adds the provider; for a kind TeamRun doesn't install itself, the panel shows the install command, and Add runs it in a terminal below the panel, `ProviderTerminalComponent`, and adds the provider once it finds the tool, or says the command ended without installing it, while Try again only looks again.
  While Add installs or waits on the command, the cards, the name and the folder link are off, so the provider added is the one Add was chosen for.
  The provider dialog stays for the composer's Add a provider and for a provider's Edit.
  Each list is `ProviderListComponent`, one bordered card whose rows line up their name, plan, limits and ⋯ menu columns, with rows `ProviderRows` builds and the menu `ProviderMenuComponent`.
  A row's menu cell starts with `ProviderStepComponent`, the one action its state needs: Sign in while it needs a sign-in, or Sign in again after a failure, Copy link and Open sign-in page after Didn't open? while a browser sign-in has its page, and Cancel while a sign-in runs in the browser or a terminal.
  `ProviderListComponent` takes the step as its own `rowStep` template, beside the menu's `rowActions`: in a pane under 40rem, where the meters go, the step moves to its own line under the row, at its end, so the name keeps its width.
  A row signing in says where it finishes, in the browser or in the terminal.
  A terminal sign-in whose terminal closes while its provider is still signed out leaves the row saying the sign-in didn't finish, as "the terminal closed before <command> ended" with its login command, in the failed colour, with Sign in again, until the next sign-in starts (`ChatStoreService.unfinishedSignIns`); one the person stops leaves no such note, and a check's own error comes first.
  A failed sign-in's row says its reason in the account's place, in the error colour and whole, wrapping under the name rather than cut to its tooltip: a check's error, or else "Connection error", with the account in the tooltip, and its step offers Sign in again (`Resources.signInAgainStatuses`); an expired sign-in's row says "Sign-in expired" under the name in the warning colour, with a warning dot, keeps its account below it, and offers Sign in (`ProviderRow.warning`); a used-up provider's row says which limit it used up the same way (`ProviderRow.warning`); only a check's error, a failed check and an unfinished sign-in tint the row with the error colour (`ProviderRow.isError`), an expired or used-up row is tinted with the warning colour, and a signed-out or not-installed row is untinted; the step is a primary button, as the design draws them; no provider reports when its sign-in expired, so the row gives no date.
  A used-up provider's row names in its warning line, under the name, which limit it used up and when it resets, from `UsageFormatterService.usedUpNote`, keeps its account below it, and keeps its plan and limits.
  A sign-in's terminal shows under Your providers in `ProviderTerminalComponent`, titled Terminal · Sign in <provider> · <kind>, with Terminal's `EmbeddedTerminal` for its session from `ChatStoreService.terminalSessions`, on a block in the kit's dark terminal look (`nol-terminal-dark`) in light and dark mode, as the design draws it; its × only hides it, Show terminal in the row shows and focuses it again, and it stays while the terminal is open, after a failed check too, and goes once the terminal ends (`ChatStoreService.openSignInTerminals`).
  A sign-in or install the Providers form starts launches with `opensTab` false, so its terminal shows only in the form, 16 rows high, and in no Terminals tab; the install's × hides it until Show terminal in the Add panel; once Add closes, or shows another type, a running install's terminal stays under the rows (`ProvidersViewComponent.installTerminals`), and Add reopens on that type; the composer's provider dialog closes, so its install and sign-in still open a tab, whose launch asks Terminal for `Resources.providerTerminalRows`, 16 rows, as its `minimumRows`, while a launch with no tab asks for none, which Terminal refuses.
  On Windows, every provider terminal, a sign-in, a sign-out or an install, asks Terminal for PowerShell or Command Prompt (`Resources.windowsProviderShellKinds`, `TerminalLaunch.shellKinds`), so the provider's Windows CLI is found even when Terminal's Default shell is a WSL distribution, whose bash has no `claude`; elsewhere it takes any shell.
  While Terminal's part is off in the window, the terminal's place says so.
  Your providers' heading ends with Checked <time> ago, the oldest check among the enabled providers, or, while that check runs, Checking… with the search's spinner in the same place and size, and a refresh that checks each enabled provider whose kind TeamRun lists again, three at a time, each row showing its result as soon as its check ends, so a slow or hung check holds only its own row and the heading reads Checking… until the last one ends; Found on this computer's ends with Searching… or Searched <time> ago and a refresh that searches again; the times count in minutes, hours and days from `ClockService`, read again each minute.
  The tab searches once the kinds load, and again from its refresh.
  While the kinds fail to load, Found on this computer says it hasn't searched yet, with no spinner and its refresh off, until Retry loads them.
  It checks the tools and runs one `ProviderProfileDiscover` for each kind TeamRun lists, all together, and says which kinds it looks for meanwhile; each kind's found folders show as its search ends, while Your providers stays usable, and a later search replaces a kind's folders when that kind's search ends.
  A found folder shows its name, its path, its account and its state with its plan, and its Add, named with the folder's account, or its path when it has none, adds it at once by its found label and checks its sign-in; the folder then moves to Your providers with the state the check finds, and the focus to the next found folder's Add, or after the last to the new provider's ⋯ button, or stays on Add when the add fails.
  With no provider, Your providers asks the person to add one found below or to choose Add provider, or says only None yet beside found folders; with none found, Found on this computer says that Add provider installs one and signs it in.
- The composer's picker names its provider, beside its button and on its provider row, by the provider's own name and its kind, such as "Personal · Codex", so teammates on two providers of one kind read apart.
- A chat's member rows and default teammate, and the Teammates page's rows and avatars, name the provider the same way.
- A teammate's avatar, in the chat, the members row, the composer, the Teammates page, sub-agent cards and the Organization view, takes its colour from `ChatStoreService.teammateColor`: `TeammateColor.byCreation` numbers the team by when each teammate was made, a tie by id, through the kit's author colours but the first, which is the Default's, seven, and round again, so the first seven teammates never share one and none looks like the Default; a teammate the store doesn't list, or an avatar with no store, takes the colour its id gives, as the kit's `colorKey` would.
- A chat with no choice of its own opens the composer on a provider type the person has.
  It starts from the default teammate's settings, or else the only teammate's type and provider when the person has just one, or else the last sent choice or the settings it showed for the previous chat.
  The chat's teammate's type wins over that, from the first teammate by name whose provider is enabled, on that provider.
  Without such a teammate, the starting choice stays when its type has an enabled provider or no type has one, and otherwise the composer opens on the first type that has one.
  A choice kept on its type that names no provider, or a disabled one, takes the type's enabled provider, the teammate's, else the one in the type's usual folder, else its first, and keeps its model and effort.
  The store's `startingChoiceOf` holds this order; an empty chat brought forward for a teammate it doesn't hold yet keeps that teammate's starting choice as its responder, so its composer opens on it as on a new chat.
- The composer's Effort menu and the teammate dialog's Effort field offer only the effort levels the provider lists for the chosen model, by the provider's own names for them, else by their ids with a capital first letter; TeamRun keeps no list of levels of its own.
  Codex lists no names, so TeamRun names its levels as Codex's model picker does, `xhigh` as "Extra high"; Claude Code's picker shows its ids as they are.
  With no level chosen, they show the level the provider's configuration sets, as `ProviderModel.configuredEffort` carries it, else the provider's default level for the model, or "Model default" when the provider names neither, and TeamRun sends no level; choosing another model starts it at no level.
  A configured level the model doesn't list shows as that level, marked not in the current catalog, since the provider runs it as it is.
  Choosing another provider keeps the chosen level only when the new provider lists it for the model; otherwise, as when the new provider names no levels for it, it starts at no level, so a provider is never sent a level it didn't offer.
  The provider's default level, `ProviderModel.defaultEffort`, carries the kit's "Recommended" tag in both, even while a configured level is the one shown.
  A model that lists no levels has Effort off, and the teammate dialog notes below it that effort levels are unavailable and the model's default is used.
  Claude Code lists no default level, so its default is the level its session reported for the model, through the same provider, in a turn sent with no level, kept while the runtime runs.
  The session reports it in its init message on some hosts only, so when that names none the turn reads it from the session's applied settings.
  A Codex turn with no model or level chosen sends neither, so Codex runs what the person's Codex configuration names, and TeamRun never overrides it.
  So Codex's list of models shows, through `config/read`, the configured model as the default, by its id when Codex doesn't list it, and the configured level as each model's configured effort, beside the default level Codex lists for the model; when `config/read` fails, the list keeps the default Codex marks.
  A configured level is the configured effort of every listed model, as it is in Codex, and is never added to a model's levels, which stay the ones Codex lists; the list is read with the chat's folder, so a project's own configuration counts, and a list for no folder serves the teammate dialog.
  A turn checks a chosen model and level against this same list, and lets through the configured level, which Codex runs as it is, so a choice saved while TeamRun listed it still runs.
  A chat with a thread and nothing chosen shows what its last reply reported, since Codex resumes a thread sent no model and no level on the ones it stored; the configuration's show only for a new thread, or once a model or a level is chosen, when Codex takes the rest from its configuration.
  The composer's Responder rows follow the same rule, each by its own responder's thread in the chat, so a row names what that responder would run there.
  So do the chat's Members avatar tooltips, the default teammate's and each member's.
  The composer reloads this list quietly on window focus and at a reply's end, past the cache, as the configuration can change at any time, and keeps the list it has when such a reload fails.
  A list read while `config/read` fails is marked partial, and doesn't clear a teammate's choices.
- A model's speeds come from its provider, and TeamRun keeps no list of them; every provider's are one Speed row in the composer's menu and one Speed field in the teammate dialog, each offering that provider's own choices.
  Claude's are Standard and Fast, for a model that supports fast mode, with Standard its default.
  Codex's are its service tiers, by Codex's names for them, with a Standard tier of TeamRun's, which a thread asks for as no tier.
  With none chosen, a Codex thread runs at the model's default tier, or Standard when Codex names none, as the menu shows.
  fx's are Standard and Fast, for every model of a session that lists fx's `fast` option with its `false` and `true` choices, as a capture of fx 0.0.16 shows; the option's current choice is the default, and a chosen speed is set with `session/set_config_option` before the turn, and every turn, one that chose no speed included, is served the option's choice in the answer.
  A session without that option offers no speed, and a turn there drops a chosen one.
  fx's Ultra mode isn't offered: it's only the launch flag `--ultrafast`, which asks for Ultra "when the model supports it", and fx's listing doesn't say which models do, so the menu couldn't show whether a reply would get it.
  A provider's adapter sends a chosen speed only when its list of models names the model with that speed, and drops it otherwise, at the turn: for a model the list leaves out, and for Claude when the list can't be read; Stop while Claude lists them ends the turn as stopped.
  When Core reads a provider's models, each teammate of that provider, and the default teammate on it, loses a speed the list doesn't name for its model.
  A served speed comes only from what the provider's answer names; when Codex's thread answer leaves the tier out, TeamRun reports none.
  When the provider says the chosen speed can't serve, the composer notes it below the control with the provider's reason and the speed the reply ran at.
- A Claude teammate's chat is summarised where Claude Code chooses unless a context window is chosen in the teammate dialog's Context window field, or by a teammate with `context_window`: 200K, 400K or 1M, or any size from 100K up to the model's window.
  The chat is summarised as it nears a chosen size, while the model's own window stays the same; the field's hint names both, and Default names the model's window.
  Until a result of the model has reported its window, since TeamRun last started, Default and the hints name no window, and the sizes go up to 1M.
  Claude's list of models offers that range for each model, up to the window the model's last result reported as `modelUsage[model].contextWindow`, or up to 1M before one did, with the presets that fit.
  The adapter sends a chosen size as Claude Code's `autoCompactWindow` setting, and a change starts the chat's query again on its session, so it applies from the next message.
  Core refuses a size the model doesn't offer, naming its bounds, and when it reads a provider's models, each teammate of that provider loses a size its model no longer offers.
  An update's size is checked against the provider, model and size the teammate had when the check began, and Core refuses the update with `Conflict` when another save changed any of them meanwhile, so a size checked for one model never lands on another.
  The context meter shows the chosen window when it's smaller than Claude Code's.
- A Codex teammate's context window is the model's own unless one is chosen the same way: 128K, 256K or 500K, or any size from 64K up to the model's window, since Codex's `model/list` names no sizes.
  The adapter sends a chosen size as `model_context_window` in the config of each `thread/start` and `thread/resume`, and Codex uses 95% of it, which it reports as the thread's `modelContextWindow`, so the context meter shows that window.
  Codex lowers a size above the model's maximum to that maximum when its model catalog names one and keeps the size as it is otherwise, so a size above the model's window can delay compaction past what the model takes and fail the turn.
  So until a thread started without a chosen size has reported the model's window, since TeamRun last started, the only size is 128K and Default names no window.
  Once one has, the adapter reads the model's own window as the reported one over 0.95, rounded to 1,000 tokens (475,000 reads as 500K), and offers it as the top size and the maximum, after the presets below it.
  Only a thread this turn started counts, never a resumed one, which may keep a size chosen earlier; a `model_context_window` in the person's own Codex config is read as the model's own window.
- A teammate's permission mode is chosen per teammate from the modes its provider lists, which come from the provider and are listed with its models as `ProviderModelList.modes`; with none chosen, a teammate runs in the mode TeamRun has always run that provider in, the list's `defaultMode`.
  Each mode says whether it lifts the provider's restrictions, as `unrestricted`, whether it only reads and plans, changing nothing, as `readOnly`, and gives the provider's own account of what it allows, as `description`; an adapter runs a chosen unrestricted mode as it runs any other, and runs a mode it doesn't list as its default mode.
  Each adapter also names its modes and default as `IProviderAdapter.modes` and `defaultMode`, which Core reads without listing models.
  The teammate dialog offers them as its Permission mode field, after Speed, by the provider's names, with the default tagged Default and the chosen mode's description under the select; the composer offers them from its permission button, in the place of the old "Auto-approve requests" switch, which shows the current responder's mode in this chat by name and opens the modes under "Permissions in this chat · <provider>", with their descriptions, the responder's default tagged ("Alice's default" for a named teammate), and a last row, "Change Alice's default…", that opens the teammate, noting that TeamRun's own tools follow this mode.
  For a named teammate the composer sets its `ChatMember.mode` for this chat only, and the teammate's own Permission mode stays its default, for new chats and for every chat that chose none for it; for the chat's own settings it keeps the mode with them, and their turns send it, and a new chat's settings start in the default teammate's own mode, never in a mode another chat chose; the chat keeps those settings as its responder, and the engine sets the responder (`ConversationEngine.setResponderAsync`) and keeps the last mode chosen there for each provider kind of the chat, which TeamRun's tools follow in a turn of those settings and its wakes, as they read a teammate's `ChatMember.mode`; a later choice of a teammate or of another kind leaves it, and the tools take the stricter of it and the mode the turn started in; deleting the chat, alone or with its project, drops it (`ConversationEngine.forgetAsync`).
  A teammate's reply takes the chat's mode for it when the reply starts, so a queued or woken reply follows a change made while it waited, and a move of the teammate to another provider clears the modes its chats chose for the earlier one.
  During a turn, TeamRun's own tools follow the stricter of the mode the turn started in and the chat's mode now: a change to a mode that asks, or to a read-only one, applies from the next call.
  Choosing the default saves no mode, and a mode the list no longer names shows as not in the catalog.
  Choosing an unrestricted mode first asks, in one confirmation both places share, `UnrestrictedModeConfirmation`, and Close keeps the mode chosen before; while the responder runs in one, the permission button shows a warning glyph in the error color in the place of its shield, kept when the bar folds the button to its glyph alone, and shown as a badge on the end corner of the picker's expand glyph once the button folds into the picker's menu.
  The picker's accessible name ends with the mode's name while the mode is unrestricted.
  When the responder's catalog names a `planMode` (`ProviderModelList.planMode`, which Core sets from the adapter's when the list has that mode), the permissions menu adds a Plan first checkbox under the modes, "Plans for your approval before it changes anything", that sets the chat's `Chat.planFirst`; it is hidden while the responder's mode in the chat is read-only already.
  It shows on while the responder still plans first (`Chat.plansFirst`), so it shows off once that responder's own plan is approved, and checking it then makes every member plan first again.
  While it is on, the permission button shows Plan first, with the plan glyph in the place of its shield, and the Window learns of an approved plan from `StateInvalidated`.

  | Provider | Modes, as TeamRun lists them | How a turn sets one | Source |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Claude | Auto (`auto`), Manual (`default`), Accept edits (`acceptEdits`, the default), Plan (`plan`, read-only), Bypass permissions (`bypassPermissions`, unrestricted); not Don't ask (`dontAsk`), which denies what isn't pre-approved | the query's `permissionMode` | the Agent SDK's `PermissionMode`, 0.3.263 |
  | Codex | Read Only (`read-only`, read-only: the read-only sandbox, asking on request), Default (`auto`, the default: workspace-write, asking on request), Full Access (`full-access`, unrestricted: danger-full-access, never asking), Plan (`plan`, read-only: the read-only sandbox, asking on request, in Codex's `plan` collaboration mode) | the thread's `sandbox` and `approvalPolicy`, and for Plan the turn's `collaborationMode` | Codex's built-in approval presets |
  | Copilot | Agent (the default), Plan (read-only) and Autopilot (unrestricted, "(experimental)" as Copilot says), from the session's `mode` option, and Allow All (`allow_all`, unrestricted), its `permissions` option `allow_all` turned on | `session/set_config_option`: the `mode` option, and `allow_all` turned on for Allow All, left as Autopilot sets it, and off otherwise | Copilot 1.0.103's `session/new` answer, checked live offline: Autopilot turns `allow_all` on, and Agent or Plan turns it off |
  | Grok | its ACP modes, which its session takes through `session/set_mode` but doesn't list (the agent's `takesUnlistedModes`): Default (`default`, the default), Plan (`plan`, read-only) and Ask (`ask`, read-only), and Always-approve (`bypassPermissions`, unrestricted), its permission mode rather than an ACP mode | `session/set_mode`, and Grok's `--permission-mode` at launch: `bypassPermissions` for Always-approve and `default` otherwise, so its own settings' default doesn't apply | from published shapes: its public source, xai-org/grok-build at 2bdd1d6, where `session/new` and `session/load` answer with no `modes` and only the `model` and `thought_level` options, and `session/set_mode` takes `default`, `plan` and `ask` and runs any other id as `default`; Always-approve checked live on Grok 1.0.70 over `grok agent stdio` with TeamRun's launch arguments: with `--permission-mode default` a command that wrote a file and reached the web asked first, and with `bypassPermissions` the same command ran without asking |
  | OpenCode | Build (`build`, the default) and Plan (`plan`, read-only), from the session's `mode` option, and Allow All (`allowAll`, unrestricted), Build with every permission allowed: TeamRun's tools, files outside the folder and repeated calls too, over the person's, a project's and a built-in agent's rules for them; an organization's managed settings, which OpenCode reads after TeamRun's, still set an agent's rules | `session/set_config_option`, and at launch `OPENCODE_PERMISSION` and the built-in agents' rules, as [The runtime part](#the-runtime-part) says: every tool TeamRun knows asks in Build, `edit` is denied for Plan, since OpenCode applies the person's own rules after an agent's, and every tool is allowed for Allow All; the person's own MCP servers' and custom tools follow the provider's own-servers switch | its public source, anomalyco/opencode at v1.19.4, and checked live on 1.19.4 (capture-135): with its free models and no sign-in, `session/new` offers `model` and `mode` (`build`, `plan`) and no ACP modes; Plan asked nothing and wrote nothing, Allow All wrote without asking, and Build asked before an edit, a command and an unapproved TeamRun tool |
  | Cursor | Agent (`agent`, the default), Ask (`ask`, read-only) and Plan (`plan`, read-only), from the session's `mode` option or its ACP modes; Plan ends in a `cursor/create_plan` request, which TeamRun shows as the reply's plan with an approval; and Allow all (`allow-all`, unrestricted), TeamRun's own, since Cursor has no mode that asks for nothing: Agent with each of Cursor's permission requests allowed once by TeamRun without asking | `session/set_config_option`, or `session/set_mode`; Allow all runs in Agent, as the agent's `allowAllMode` | Cursor's ACP documents, and checked live on CLI 2026.10.22-27dcf5f on macOS (capture-136): `session/new` offers the ACP modes `agent`, `plan` and `ask` and the `mode` and `model` options, and both calls keep the session; on a free plan no prompt ran a tool, so what each mode allows is not yet checked |
  | fx | Auto (`auto`, the default: an automatic safety review checks each action instead of asking), Ask (`ask`) and Full access (`full-access`, unrestricted: every action without asking, outside fx's sandbox), from the session's `mode` option | `session/set_config_option` | fx 0.0.16's source, `src/builtins/modes.zig` and `src/acp/sessions.zig`; not checked live, since fx's turns need a card on file with its Vercel AI Gateway, which the maintainer chose not to add (Nov 3, 2026) |
  | Pi | TeamRun's own, since Pi has no permission system: Ask first (`ask`, the default: asks before each tool but `read`, `grep`, `find` and `ls`), Read only (`read-only`, read-only: refuses those other tools) and Allow all (`allow-all`, unrestricted: asks nothing, as Pi does on its own) | `TEAMRUN_PI_MODE` in the environment of `pi --mode rpc`, which TeamRun's extension, passed with `--extension`, reads before each tool call | Pi's README: it "does not include a built-in permission system", and it runs with the permissions of the user who launched it |
  | Antigravity | Default (`default`, the default), Auto Edit (`auto_edit`) and YOLO (`yolo`, unrestricted), from the session's `mode` option | `session/set_config_option` | its ACP server 1.3.0's `session/new` answer, checked live (`antigravity-session-modes.recording.ts`): YOLO auto-approves all tools, and a session starts in Default |

  An ACP turn sets the mode after the model and effort, through the session's `mode` config option when it has one and `session/set_mode` otherwise, and only when it differs from the session's.
  A chosen mode other than the default that the session doesn't offer fails the turn before its prompt; a default it doesn't offer leaves the session in its own.
  A session that lists no modes, with neither a `mode` option nor `modes`, offers the agent's own when its `takesUnlistedModes` says so, as Grok's does, and a new session is taken to start in the agent's default mode, so only another mode is set, while a loaded or resumed one is always set, the default too, since Grok restores the plan mode a session was left in when it loads it.
  Copilot's Allow All runs in its default mode with `allow_all` turned on, and fails before its prompt when the session has no such switch to turn on; Grok's Always-approve runs in its default ACP mode, as the agent's `launchMode`, OpenCode's Allow All in Build, and Cursor's Allow all in Agent, as the agent's `allowAllMode`, where the turn allows each permission request Cursor sends, once, after TeamRun's own rules for its tools, and asks the person none of them once the turn is out of Plan: a plan-first turn asks them as Agent does while it plans, and allows them from when it leaves Plan after the person approves the plan; TeamRun's own tools still ask by the rule below.
  A session can start unrestricted, from the agent's own settings or on a resume, so after the mode the turn turns off an agent's permission switch, such as Copilot's `allow_all`, unless the chosen mode is that switch or another unrestricted mode, and then fails before its prompt while the mode option, `modes.currentModeId` or that switch still names an unrestricted mode when the chosen one isn't unrestricted, also when neither that mode nor the default is listed.

  When Core reads a provider's models, each teammate of that provider, and the default teammate on it, loses a mode the list doesn't name; a list that names no modes clears none.
  A teammate, or the default teammate, moved to another provider in the teammate dialog keeps no mode, since its modes are the provider's own; the composer keeps the chat's mode on another provider only when that provider lists it.
- A teammate's mode in one chat is its membership's `ChatMember.mode`, set with `ConversationSetMemberMode`; `null` follows the teammate's own mode, and so does one its provider no longer lists, so a reply runs in the chat's mode while its provider lists it and in the teammate's otherwise, a hand-off's reply included.
- A teammate's model, effort and speed in one chat are its membership's `ChatMember.settings`, set with `ConversationSetMemberSettings`; `null` follows the teammate's own, and a set one runs every reply of that teammate in the chat, a hand-off's reply included, on its model, where `null` is the provider's default model, and its effort and speed, where `null` is that model's default.
  A reply on the chat's own model keeps the teammate's context window only when that model is the teammate's own; on another it runs with the model's default.
  Moving the teammate to another provider forgets them in every chat, with its session and its mode, since its models are the provider's own.
  As with the teammate's own model, a chat's model that the provider's model list no longer names isn't cleared: its next reply still asks the provider for that model; a refresh of the list clears only a teammate's unlisted speed, mode and context window (`TeammatesService.clearUnlistedAsync`).
- TeamRun's own tools ask by the turn's permission mode, the requested mode its adapter lists or else its default.
  In an unrestricted mode, which asks for nothing, a permission request below High risk is allowed without asking; a High-risk one still waits for the person, and so does one whose allowing the base remembers for the chat, such as computer use's grants of the screen and of one app ([Permission requests](#permission-requests)).
  In a read-only mode, such as Plan, a tool call the turn's session doesn't list as `readOnlyHint` is refused, not asked, as `Denied (teamrun.readOnlyMode)`; in every other mode they ask as before.
  Claude Code and the ACP providers run a TeamRun tool without their own permission card only when it is one of the turn's approved tools, which ask the person themselves where they must, or one the turn's session lists as `readOnlyHint`, which trusts the hint of whichever module serves that `teamrun_` tool, since the session takes it from that module's own descriptor; any other TeamRun tool shows the provider's card first, and runs once the person allows it.
- An ACP provider's models come from its session's `model` option, and its levels from the session's `thought_level` option when it has one.
  When the option groups its choices, a group whose choices are all named by a level word (minimal, low, medium, high, xhigh or max), alone or as "<group> (<level>)", such as Low and High under Flash, is one model, named by the group, whose levels are its choices by those words; any other group's choices, such as Gemini Flash (High) and Gemini Pro (High) under Google, and a choice outside a group, stay models of their own.
  When it is flat, Antigravity only: choices whose ids end `-high`, `-medium` or `-low` and whose names end `(High)`, `(Medium)` or `(Low)` over the same base and base name become one model with those levels, and a plain choice with that base and base name is its "Standard" level.
  Either way only that Standard level, when there is one, is the model's default, marked Recommended, since it's the one the agent itself marks; the level shown selected is what a turn with nothing chosen runs: the session's current choice in its model, else the Standard level, else the model's first level.
  That's a new session's level, since the listing reads a new session; a resumed session left on another level is set to the level the listing showed.
  Choices join only when two or more share the model's id and name and no other choice has its id; otherwise each stays a model of its own.
  A teammate saved with a level's choice shows as that model at that level.
  A turn sets the option to the chosen level's choice, and its reply names the model and the level the same way.
  A model whose choice takes the `thought_level` option away, as Muse Code's `muse-wonder-1.0` does, runs without a level, even when the teammate has one.
  Each session's `model` and `thought_level` options, with their choices' values, names and groups only, are written to TeamRun's log.
- On TeamRun's Settings page, the Data folder row's Show in folder shows the module's database file, selected, in the system's file manager, at the path the runtime part gives.
  Noldova Desktop's Settings pages hold only setting rows, and its Gallery only the kit's parts, so the teammates and providers stay TeamRun's own documents, the row can't show the path itself, and TeamRun's own parts have no Gallery yet.
- From activation until a view loads it, the store only watches: it reads the open replies once and follows only their events and the runtime's resync events, so the chat tabs show the working state of a reply that `teamrun send` or another window started.
  The first view loads it in full, and the watch's subscriptions end then.
- On activation it registers the Projects view, `teamrun.projects`, in the left dock.
  Each view declares its side, group and order through Noldova Desktop's view contribution, so a fresh install and Reset the layout open the same layout: Explorer on the left; on the right an upper group of Background tasks, Pages and Git above a lower group of Plans and Sub-agents; Activity and Changes at the bottom; each group showing its first tab.
  Pages comes before Git, which Noldova Desktop's Git module orders at 20, so at the default window size of 1280×800, where the right dock's tab row holds only two of the three, Pages shows and Git waits behind Show all tabs.
  Git declares its own place, after Pages, in the git module.
  The view lists the open projects and their conversations, pinned ones first, from the window part's store, so it follows changes made in any window.
  Each section header is a tree row's height, as New chat, Open folder and Open browser are; the first stands a section header's space under them, and each later one the action rows' gap under the section above, whether that section is open or folded, so header to header and row to header match action to action top to top.
  It registers with `ContentPadding.None`, so its list scrolls over the whole pane with the scrollbar along the pane's end edge from top to bottom, and carries the view padding inside it: `--nol-content-padding-inline` on both sides, `--nol-view-padding-block` above, and that and `--nol-space-2` below.
  After the projects, a Chats group lists the chats without a project that aren't pinned and aren't a teammate's Organization chat, newest first until the person orders them, ten until Show more; New chat with no project open or from a chat without a project starts one, asking for no folder, and its tab's breadcrumb is Chats.
  Right after the pinned conversations and before the projects, an Organizations group (`OrganizationExplorer`) lists every organization from `OrganizationCatalog` in its order, even one with no teammate and no department, and shows only its header, with "+", while there is none; choosing an organization's name folds or unfolds it and opens its own Organization view, with the organization's id as the document's instance and its name as the title, as Enter on the row and Open organization in its row menu do without folding it; under each, the teammates it places in no department by name, then every department of it as one flat list in the Organization view's order, a nested one by its path, such as `DevOps › Infra`, each expanding into its leader and members by name; a teammate it doesn't place shows under no organization.
  The organization whose view is in view is marked as a chat row is, in the selected fill with `aria-selected`, and while it is no chat and no project is marked: `ChatStoreService.organizationInView` holds it, which `showOrganization` sets while the view is the active document or is pressed or focused and clears when that view goes out of view or closes, an organization going out of view clearing only its own mark, and `leaveOrganization` clears when a chat is opened from the Explorer.
  A teammate's row opens its Organization chat in that organization (`Chat.organizationChat`, by organization and teammate), asking the engine for one (`OrganizationChatOpen`) when the teammate has none there yet and logging a refusal, and shows that chat's mark as a chat row does, but no spinner or waiting glyph, since its status dot already says it; each organization's and each department's folding is kept in `collapsedProjects` by their keys, `organization:<id>` for an organization.
  An Organization chat shows on its teammate's row only, never under Pinned or a project, even one pinned while it was under Chats or moved into a project, since the rows read Organization chats from every project.
  "+" on the group's header asks for a name in `RenameDialogComponent` under the heading New organization and creates the organization through `OrganizationActions`, which sends `OrganizationCreate`; the new organization arrives with the engine's `OrganizationChanged` event.
  An organization's row menu renames it (`RenameDialogComponent` under the organization's own heading), sets, changes or clears its default folder, shown as the row's tooltip, and deletes it after a confirm, the last one too, each through `OrganizationActions`, which sends `OrganizationUpdate` with only the field it changes, or `OrganizationDelete`, and logs a refusal; the name dialog refuses a name over the engine's limit, saying the limit, or one holding a tab, a line break or another control character, saying so; the catalog follows the engine's `OrganizationChanged` and `OrganizationRemoved` events, and closes a removed organization's Organization view.
  A teammate's row shows its `TeammateStatusComponent` as a dot alone, named for a screen reader, hung in the twistie's column so the names line up with the departments'.
  The group's rows stand the rows' gap apart, as the Projects and Chats rows do, inside a department and after it.
  A teammate's row menu moves its Organization chat into a project or a folder, or attaches, replaces or detaches the chat's folder, through `ChatPlacement`, the chat rows' own placement code, opening the chat with `OrganizationChatOpen` first when the teammate has none there yet; its tooltip names where the chat works: its attached folder, its project's folder, the organization's default folder, or the teammate's name.
  A chat in the group drags, or moves with Move row up and down, to a new place in the group and not out of it.
  Each group's heading is a kit section header with its own icon, a pin for Pinned, folders for Projects and the chat icon for Chats.
  A heading's rows start their text at the heading label's edge, and their fill one row padding before it, running to the row's end; a project's chats start one step further in, and a project's twistie sits in the gutter, centred on the headings' icons.
  Open browser, after Open folder and drawn as it is with the Browser's globe, runs `browser.openPage` with no profile and no page, so each choice opens one more Browser tab on an empty page in the person's own browsing, whose address field the Browser focuses; the command `teamrun.openBrowser`, "Open browser", does the same from the window's one search, with no key.
  Both are there only while the Browser module is on: the window part reads whether it is on as it starts, with `isDependencyActive`, then they go when Noldova Desktop says it went off or failed, and come back when it says it is on again.
  Chat titles are muted, and the marked or hovered one is in the full text colour; New chat, Open folder and Open browser keep their look.
  The headings use the kit section header's uppercase, smaller text, as `[uppercase]="true"` sets it.
  A drag released where it started saves no order, in the group, in a project, among the pins or among the projects, and a drop moves the dragged row by its id, so a list change during the drag doesn't move another one.
  The order is kept in the view's layout under its own key, so it survives a restart and shows in every window, with Show more's chats behind the shown ones and a new chat behind the ordered ones, as in a project.
  A chat's menu has Move to project… for a chat without a project and Move to another project… for one in a project, which open the other open projects, in the Projects order that `ProjectsLayoutService.ordered` gives, then Move to a folder….
  For a chat without a project, it counts the chat's files once the person picks a project or a folder and, when it holds some, asks first, naming how many it copies and that its own Git history isn't copied; it opens a chosen folder as a project only then, so a cancel opens nothing.
  A chat in a project moves without a count, as dragging it does.
  Deleting a chat without a project whose folder holds files names how many it removes, says the chat can be moved to a folder to keep them, and offers Move to a folder… beside Delete, which opens the folder chooser, since a dialog button opens no submenu; a count that fails is reported and deletes nothing.
  Its replies' file links, written images, steps and edited files, and its terminals, use the chat's folder as a project's chat uses the project's.
  It opens the folder the person chooses with the window's folder chooser as a project, forgets projects, creates, renames, moves and deletes conversations, and searches them; deleting and forgetting ask the person first, and forgetting leaves the folder's files.
  Opening a project selects it, so New chat starts there; a chat of another project, or without one, that stays shown is set aside, and focus or a click in it doesn't take the selection back until its tab is shown again or its row is chosen.
  The list marks the shown chat unless it is set aside, and with no chat marked, the selected project's row, so it marks where New chat starts.
  Commands about the chat on screen, such as Stop reply, Escape on the page, Focus composer and the rewind dialog's fork note, act on the set-aside chat while it is shown.
  The store keeps the set-aside chat's members, with their modes, beside the selected chat's, and loads them again after a member change or a resync.
  The composer of the chat on screen, selected or set aside, drops a responder who leaves that chat.
  Core publishes `ConversationDeleted` for each conversation a delete or a forget removes, whichever window or the CLI asked, and every window drops it from its list, forgets its tab and its image tabs, whether attachments, images a reply wrote or generated, or images pasted or picked in its composer, which closes them and takes them out of Back and Forward, and forgets the images' bytes, reads and sizes it kept.
  The window's image viewer records the image tabs it opens for each chat and forgets each of them, open or closed, when the chat is deleted; an open image tab it didn't record, such as one Noldova Desktop restored from an earlier session, forgets itself when it hears the deletion or finds its chat gone as it opens, and no tab is forgotten twice.
  An image tab's instance, which Noldova Desktop keeps with the open tab, names its chat as `chatId`; a tab an earlier version opened keeps `conversationId` until it closes, since Noldova Desktop can't change a tab's instance, and the window reads that too.
  The window keeps those within bounds, dropping the oldest first: the images opened in a tab from their own bytes, generated, pasted or drawn from a data URL in a reply's Markdown, at most 32 and 48 MiB of decoded bytes, always keeping the one just opened, since its tab reads it back from the window; the images read from their files, attachments and images a reply wrote, at most 16 and 48 MiB of their own, counted once each read ends, since each kind has its own budget rather than sharing one; and at most 256 images' sizes.
  Each window closes a deleted chat's terminals, in every window, through `TerminalOwners.closeAsync` when it hears the chat's `ConversationDeleted` through its context, also in a window whose store only watches and never loaded the chats, and a close that fails posts `teamrun.chatActionFailed` keyed `CloseTerminals/<chat>`, with no Open chat, since the chat is gone.
  A chat deleted from the CLI while no window is open keeps its hidden shells running until the app quits, since only a window closes them: the runtime part can't take Terminal's runtime API until Terminal offers it without its window package.
  Terminal 0.0.17 saves the terminals an app opened with their owner and restores them at each run, so until then that chat's shells come back hidden at each run, with no chat to show them.
  A chat's unsent message is saved as the composer's mention value, its text with each chosen teammate as a token, so a reopened chat, or one after a restart, shows its mentions again.
  A Send with a file the draft hasn't saved yet first waits for the draft save under way, or writes the pending one, then sends each file by the path that save stored, so a file's bytes go to the runtime once; if that save fails, Send sends the file from its data.
  The window drops a chat's unsaved draft before its tab closes, so the draft isn't saved to a chat that is gone.
  A draft save that Core refuses because the chat is gone drops the chat, with no error and no second save.
  Each draft save names the revision it replaces, the one the window read or saved last, or 0 when it read no draft; Core refuses a save whose revision is no longer the draft's with `Conflict`, before it writes a file, so another window's newer draft and its files stay, and the window drops what it had pending and reads the draft again, with no error.
  A chat whose draft the window couldn't read says so in its composer, and each save of what is typed there reads the draft again first, saving nothing while that read fails; when the read finds no draft it saves what is typed, and when it finds one it saves nothing over it and shows it in the composer, as for a refused save, so a draft the window never showed is never replaced; a read that fails because the chat was deleted meanwhile keeps nothing.
  The window keeps one unsaved draft per chat, so a chat's draft that couldn't be saved stays pending while another chat's is typed and saved.
  A message sent from a window discards its draft only when no other window's draft reached that window while it sent, by a refused save or a read, so a send never removes a newer draft it didn't show.
  A window that missed the event drops the chat the same way: a resync keeps each chat it no longer lists, in a project it still lists, until Core answers about it, and drops the ones Core no longer has, and a tab whose chat the window doesn't list, such as one restored when the window opens, asks Core, which answers `NotFound` for a deleted chat.
  When Core can't answer about a chat, the chat stays listed, and the resync finishes before it shows why.
- The window's one search, Noldova Desktop's `desktop.search`, gets two sources from `TeamRunSearchSources`: Chats, `teamrun.chats`, and Messages, `teamrun.messages`.
  Chats answers from Core's `ChatFind`: before the person types, its recent items are the 20 latest updated chats, and while they type, the chats whose title contains the text, the latest first; a row's detail is its project and its teammates in the order they joined, and its total counts every chat that matches.
  Messages answers from Core's `MessageSearch` and has no recent items: a row is a snippet around the text, its detail who wrote it, its chat and how long ago, and opening it opens the chat at that message.
  Each hit carries its message's author, sender name, teammate name, provider and provider kind, and the row names the author as the chat does, through `FormatterService.authorName`: the sender's name, such as TeamRun or a teammate's for a woken-in message, or You for the person's own; the teammate's name or the provider's line for a reply; and TeamRun for TeamRun's own notes.
