  Both leave out the chats the window knows were deleted, their total lowered by as many, and a row whose chat was deleted since opens nothing.
  Both ask Core only once the person stops typing for 150 ms: Noldova Desktop asks every source at each keystroke and gives up the earlier ask, and a source given up within those 150 ms asks Core nothing.
  TeamRun has no search button of its own: Search chats, `teamrun.searchConversations`, has no key and runs `desktop.search` with `{ "source": "teamrun.chats" }`, which opens the one search under the Chats filter and is the one Noldova Desktop command a window part may run.
  A click previews a conversation as the `teamrun.conversation` document and a double click keeps it open, with the project's name as its breadcrumb.
  The open tab follows its chat's place: a move to another project, from Chats into a project, or an attached folder rewrites the breadcrumb, and a tab opened before the store holds its chat or its project gets its breadcrumb once it does.
- A finished reply's Markdown image shows in its place, column-wide up to 24rem high and opening in the viewer, when its address is a file of the chat's project, read as the images a reply wrote are, or a base64 `data:` URL of a PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP or SVG image, in any chat.
  A data URL shows only up to an attachment image's 10 MiB of decoded bytes and, for a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP, up to 40 million pixels as its header says, read before anything decodes it, so a small file with huge dimensions never reaches the decoder; beyond either it shows its alt text.
  A web address shows as a link that opens in the browser, which the window never fetches, and any other address shows its alt text.
- A screen reader hears replies through the kit's `Announcer`, politely, from `ReplyAnnouncements`, which the chat on screen updates once the store and the chat's history have loaded, from its working replies, its pending approvals and the chat's history: "<teammate> replied" when a reply ends Completed, through the store's reply-end watchers, and "<teammate> asks: <card heading>" once an approval or question card appears in any of the chat's replies, working or ended.
  It never says a reply's text or anything while a reply streams, and nothing for a reply stopped, failed, interrupted or removed before it started.
  It says each at most once, by the reply's or approval's key, and nothing for a chat whose tab is hidden or for what was already there when the chat opened.
- A finished reply ends with the kit's message footer: Copy, the time, and a stats button after the time, which shows the reply's tokens in and out, else its context percentage, else only its icon, and opens a panel, the kit's popover, below it and start-aligned.
  The panel lists each figure the provider reported, from `ReplyUsage`, and leaves out the rest: tokens in, with from cache, written to cache and new; tokens out, with reasoning; cost; context; time worked; and model and effort; a reply with neither tokens in nor out says so in one line.
  New input is tokens in less the cache counts, only for Claude and Codex, whose input count holds them; whether Grok's does is unproven, so its panel shows the cache counts without new.
  An ACP agent's `PromptResponse.usage` isn't read: its schema is still unstable, and whether it counts the turn or the session is unproven until a recording shows it.
- A working reply's Stop sits alone at its footer's end, on the row's line, as the kit's compact button, at the theme's `button-compact-height`.
  The reply keeps its height when Stop goes only while that height is no more than the icon button's target, as in the kit's default theme, where it is that target; a theme with a taller compact height makes the footer row that tall while Stop shows.
- A reply draws `$ … $` and `$$ … $$` as math; the person's own messages turn the kit's `math` off on their message, so `$` and `$$`, as in a shell's `kill $$`, show as typed.
- A generated image that failed shows in its place as "Image generation failed", in the error colour, with the reason under it when its stored `failure` gives one, so replies saved before the window read it show it too.
  Codex's reason is the image generation limit when its `failure` names it, and any provider's is its `failure`'s non-blank `message`, as Grok's raw output carries it.
  A finished generated image the window can't show, such as one of an unknown format or over the size its reader takes, with no saved copy, says it couldn't be shown; one still being generated shows nothing until it ends.
  An ACP agent's failed tool call is a failed image when the agent's image reader knows it, as Grok's does by its raw input's `variant`, `ImageGen` or `ImageEdit`, and its raw output's `error` and `message` strings, each cut to 500 characters with an ellipsis, become the stored `failure`.
  Another failed tool call stays a failed step under the reply's steps.
- Cursor names a generated image by its file in the `filePath` of a `cursor/generate_image` notification, which TeamRun reads by reference as Grok's are read.
  The path must be absolute with no `.` or `..` segment, and inside the chat's folder or a temp folder (the system's, and `/tmp` on Linux and macOS) as written, before anything touches it, so a network path is never opened; its real path, links resolved, must be inside one of them too, and a file in a temp folder must belong to the person on Linux and macOS, since another account can create it there first.
  The file must be a regular file, never a pipe or device, opened, on Linux and macOS, without following a link or waiting; it must be the same file (device and inode) as the one checked, still inside, and an image of at most the image limit; Grok's images take the same file checks.
  Cursor's documents call `filePath` a suggested path and don't say when the file is written, so TeamRun reads Cursor's images only at the turn's end, after Cursor has answered; one unreadable then shows as a failed image whose `failure` says which files TeamRun shows.
  The image is stored and shown like any generated image; the notification never carries its bytes.
- A message's attached file shows as the kit's attachment button, with the file's full path where TeamRun saved it as its tooltip and its description, while its name stays the button's name.
  A click, Enter or Space opens it with Noldova Desktop's `openFileAsync`, which opens only documents and text and data files; any other file, such as a program, is shown in its folder with `showInFolderAsync` instead, and never run.
  When its folder can't show it either, the window asks Core's `AttachmentExists`, which answers only for the chat's own message attachments: a file Core no longer has posts a notice that it is no longer in TeamRun's data folder, and any other answer reports that it could not be shown in its folder.
  Core answers as it does for any attachment's file, through the walk of its links that the bullet on where Core keeps attached files describes, so a file reached through a link to a network or device path answers that it is gone without the link being followed, and nothing is posted once the chat was deleted while it asked.
- A reply's link to a file, by a path the kit's Markdown view gives with `fileLinks` on, opens an image of the chat's folder in the viewer among the reply's images, and any other file of the folder with `openFileAsync`, showing it in its folder with `showInFolderAsync` when the system won't open it.
  A link by an absolute path outside the folder, or in a chat with no folder, opens the same way, never in the viewer, since Core reads images only inside the folder; an image there goes straight to `showInFolderAsync` and is never opened, whatever the system would open.
  A slash before a drive, as in `/C:/a.png`, is read as the drive.
  A link to a folder shows the kit's folder mark and goes straight to `showInFolderAsync`, so Core's file check never refuses it as a file.
  When the message's Markdown gives the paths of its file links (`filePathsChange`), the window asks the runtime's `LinkedFolderList` which are folders, in one request for the paths that reply hasn't asked about yet, and passes those back as `folderLinks`; an answer for paths the reply has since changed marks nothing.
  Core reads each path through the folder probe, off its thread, and leaves out one that doesn't answer within the probe's deadline.
  A click uses the same answers; when the runtime can't say, the link opens as a file, as before, and the next click asks again.
  A link it won't follow, a relative path with no folder, a `../` path out of the folder or a network path, posts why it wasn't opened.
  When its folder can't show it either, as for a file that was moved or deleted, the window reports that it could not be opened or shown and may have been moved or deleted; a relative path that leaves the folder, a network path and a `file:` URL open nothing, the last since the kit doesn't make it a link.
  The chat and the reply history's Plan view share this handling, and the chat's handling of web and chat links, through `ReplyLinksService`, so a plan's links there open, show in their folder or are refused with the same notices as in the chat.
  A click on a link there only follows it, without choosing that plan's row, as Enter on it does.
- The composer's picker is one button, "<who> · <model> · <effort> ▾", where who is the @mention of the teammate who answers, or the provider's kind without one, with the provider's name and what its plan has left muted to its left.
  It opens one menu with Model and Effort side by side, and Speed under Effort only when the model has speeds; below them the provider row, which without a teammate also opens Provider type, and for a named teammate "Edit @<name>" and "Answer without a teammate".
  Without a teammate, under the provider row, one row per enabled provider of another kind that doesn't need a new sign-in, so none that is signed out or whose sign-in expired, while one whose check hasn't run or couldn't tell (Unknown, Error) is offered as the Provider type submenu offers it, labelled as the provider row is ("Work · Claude Code"), switches the chat to that kind and provider in one choice; a teammate's picker has none, since a teammate's kind is its own.
  The teammate who answers is the one the person mentions; for a named teammate the picker's model, effort and speed apply in this chat only, as the chat's `ChatMember.settings`, and "Make it @<name>'s default" saves them to the teammate and lets the chat follow it again.
  A named teammate's Provider row shows its provider and opens the enabled providers of its kind, as the default teammate's does; choosing another saves the teammate as its dialog does, with no mode, and Core forgets the teammate's session and chosen mode in each of its chats, or refuses the change, with the composer showing why, while one of them has an open reply.
  The composer first loads the new provider's models, then saves the teammate once, whatever the person does meanwhile; until then the row and its menu show the picked provider in every chat's composer, a newer pick replaces it, picking the teammate's own provider again cancels it, and a pick made while a move saves waits for that save.
  A model the provider doesn't list is saved as none, and with it the effort and speed, so the teammate starts at that provider's default, while a load that fails keeps them.
  `ChatStoreService` keeps the pending move and one save chain per teammate, so composers open side by side share them: a named teammate's model, effort, speed and provider save one change at a time, each waiting for the save before it, failed or not, and then re-reading the teammate from Core and sending only the fields its own setting changes, so no save carries another's stale values; when Core's list can't be read, the save is skipped and the teammate is named in a notice, or in the teammate dialog itself when the dialog saves.
  The teammate dialog's save joins the same chain with only the fields the person changed from the teammate it opened with, and drops a pending move, so the dialog's provider stays.
  `TeammateUpdate` is a patch: Core changes only the fields its params name and keeps the others, reading and saving the teammate in one transaction.
  So two writers that change different fields of one teammate, such as a window and a teammate's `teamrun_update_teammate`, keep both changes, and of two that change the same field, the later save wins.
  An older window's save names every field, so it still replaces them all.
  An update must change at least one field and may name no field it doesn't have, so a misspelt field is refused rather than saved as nothing, and a sender skips a change that would change nothing.
  One that sets the model, effort, speed, mode or context window also names a new provider or the provider its sender read, as `expectedProviderId`, and Core refuses it with `Conflict` once the teammate is on another provider, so a model chosen under one provider never lands on another.
  A composer's model, effort or speed pick is read on the teammate's provider as the window shows it, or, when it was made during the window's own move and that move's save went through, on the provider it moved to, so such a pick lands after the move.
  When the save chain's fresh read finds any other provider, including the move's target reached by another writer's save, the pick names the shown one and Core refuses it.
  An unavailable teammate never answers in the composer, so its row never shows a disabled or missing provider.
- The composer's bar folds as its chat narrows, down to a document group's 13.75rem minimum: first the provider line beside the picker hides, then the composer's side insets shrink to a half step, except in a panel, where `inPanel` keeps its card a space-3 in from the sides and bottom at every width, then the picker shows who answers alone, with the same accessible name and menu, as the permission button shrinks to its minimum, then the context gauge shows as its track alone, with the figure in its tooltip and accessible name, then the permission button shows its glyph alone, with the same accessible name and menu, and last, where even that doesn't fit, the permission button hides and a Permissions in this chat row heads the picker's menu, opening the same modes.
  While the permissions are in the picker's menu, the picker's tooltip, accessible name and menu name them too, and the focus moves with them: from the permission button to the picker as it hides, and from the row to the reopened menu as the button comes back.
  It folds by measurement, not by fixed widths, so it holds in every theme and text size: a ResizeObserver compares the composer's width with what the bar needs unfolded, Add files and Send, the provider line, the permission button's whole, minimum and glyph-alone widths, kept from before they fold, the picker's whole and short widths, and the gauge's whole and track-alone widths, which the gauge measures from its label and track, kept laid out but hidden while it shows its track alone.
  Since none of those widths depends on the fold, the fold changes once each time the width crosses a step, either way.
- It registers the Background tasks view, `teamrun.backgroundTasks`, first in the right dock's upper group.
  It registers with `ContentPadding.None`: the view scrolls itself over the whole pane, with the scrollbar along the pane's end edge from top to bottom, and carries the view padding inside it.
  The view lists the background tasks of the chat shown in the middle area, and shows its empty state while no chat is shown there, even while one stays selected; the tasks are the commands a provider runs on in the background, in two groups, oldest first as the chat reads: Finished, first ended first, which folds from its header and shows its last ten under Show more, then Running, first started first.
  The view opens at its end and follows new tasks there, unless the person scrolled up from it.
  Finished counts every finished task of the chat, listed or not: the window lists the first page, and Show more, once it has shown every listed task, asks for the next page of 100 and takes the focus to the newest task it shows, just above those already shown.
  A command the list cut ends with an ellipsis, and its row reads the whole command with its output when it first shows open.
  A task that runs a sub-agent is left out there and from its reply's running-tasks count, as the Sub-agents view owns it.
  Each task is the kit's tool call, with its command, its status and how long it ran, its output so far behind its toggle, read through `BackgroundTaskRead` when it first shows open, with the kit's Loading and Retry, a failed one's exit code, and the kit's Cancel while it runs; Clear finished tasks in the Finished header clears the chat's finished tasks through `BackgroundTaskClearFinished`, and shows why under the header when that fails.
  Stop asks the runtime, which asks the provider; when the provider refuses, the provider's error shows under the task, and Stop stays.
  A reply's header counts its tasks that still run, such as `2 tasks running`, beside its state.
  A Claude task can outlive its reply: TeamRun keeps the chat's Claude Code session open while a task runs, so a command a reply started in the background runs on after the reply is done, until it ends or is stopped.
  A Codex task can outlive its reply: Codex keeps the command running on the thread, and TeamRun keeps following the thread until the command ends.
  The window reads a chat's first page of tasks once it shows the chat, and follows them through `BackgroundTaskUpdated`, `BackgroundTaskOutput` and `BackgroundTasksCleared`; a resync reads them again.
  A clear's answer or event drops the chat's tasks that ended at or before the clear's time, so a task that ended after it stays, though its end may reach the window before the clear's answer.
  Core takes each start, end and clear time at least a millisecond after the one before it and after the times saved for any chat it has read, so a task that ends in the clear's millisecond, or after the clock steps back, still ends after the clear.
  After a clock that was once far ahead, Core goes on a millisecond past the latest time it gave or read until the clock catches up, so the times stay in order rather than going back.
  While no document is open, the document group shows the part's placeholder, `teamrun.noChat`, which asks the person to choose or create a chat where the history's text starts, with New chat, which starts a chat in the selected project as the Start chat command does, and Open a folder under it once the window's store has loaded, and Add a provider before them while no provider exists; at a document group's 13.75rem minimum the buttons wrap, each row on the text's start edge; Noldova Desktop moves the focus to the first of them when the last chat closes.
- Pins, the order of projects and conversations, and collapsed projects and groups are the Projects view's layout, its view state, which TeamRun keeps in its database as Noldova Desktop's state rule says.
  `ProjectsLayoutSave` keeps the layout as the window sends it, as the `projectsLayout` row of the `preferences` table, records it in the change feed and sends `ProjectsLayoutUpdated` to every window, which shows it unless a save of its own is under way, and then shows the last one it got, with any change it holds on top, once its last save settles; `ProjectsLayoutRead` reads it when the view opens and on a resync, and a resync's answer is skipped when a change or another window's layout came while it waited.
  A change made before the first read settles shows at once and is saved once it does, on the layout found then: another window's layout that came meanwhile, else the kept one, else the stored or empty layout.
  A first read that fails is logged; with no other window's layout come meanwhile, it saves nothing, so a layout that wasn't read never replaces the kept one: the window keeps its changes on top of what it shows and reads again at its next change or a resync, or at once when one came while the failed read was out; a save that fails is logged, and its change stays in that window, and a read at a later resync that fails is logged and keeps the layout shown.
  When moving the stored layout fails and another window's layout came meanwhile, the window opens on that layout.
  A layout an earlier version kept in the window's local storage, under `teamrun.projectsLayout`, shows at once and is saved with `onlyIfNone` once a read finds no layout kept, then removed; when another window kept one first, the save keeps that one and the window shows it; one that can't be read as a JSON object is dropped, and a failed save leaves it for the next start.
  The layout's fields are `collapsedProjects`, `pinnedChats`, `projectOrder`, `chatOrder` and `collapsedGroups`, which names the collapsed ones of the Pinned, Projects, Chats and Organizations groups, each title a kit section header that collapses and expands its group; a name the window has no group for is skipped.
  The `20261025230000_ChatLayoutNames` migration renames a kept layout's `pinnedConversations` and `conversationOrder` to them, in the `projectsLayout` row and its change feed payloads, before any window reads it, so no window saves a move that could replace another window's change.
  A layout that already has a field under its new name keeps that value, and the old name goes either way.
  A layout an earlier version left in local storage is read under either name, the new one first, and moves to the runtime as above.
- Collapsed departments and what the cards show are the Organization view's layout, one per organization, kept the same way as the `organizationLayout:<organization id>` row of the `preferences` table.
  `OrganizationLayoutSave` takes the organization's id with the layout, keeps the layout as the window sends it, records it in the change feed and sends `OrganizationLayoutUpdated` with the same id and layout to every window, not `OrganizationChanged`, and a window's view follows only its own organization's; `OrganizationLayoutRead` reads one organization's layout when the view opens and on a resync, with the same rules for a change made before the first read, a save under way and a resync's late answer as the Projects view's layout, except that a first read that fails keeps the changes made since unsaved until a read gives the layout kept, the next change's, a resync's, or another window's save.
  Its fields are `collapsedDepartments`, which holds the collapsed departments' ids, `cardsShow`, the fields every card shows (Status and Now unless saved otherwise), and `cards`, each card's own choice by teammate id, which wins over `cardsShow` until it is cleared; each set of fields is an object of `Status`, `Now`, `Usage`, `Last` and `Model` to true or false, and a name the window doesn't know is skipped.
  A layout an earlier version kept with `view` and `focus` fields still loads, those fields skipped, and the next save drops them.
- `TeammateWorkRead` answers what a teammate worked on, for its Organization card, as `TeammateWork`, from the replies it completed, the ones whose message has its `teammateId`, the `Provider` author, the `Completed` status and an end, which `IX_messages_teammateId_endedAt` holds newest first; a reply that failed or was interrupted doesn't count.
  `folders` holds each chat's working folder once, the chat answered in last first: the attached folder, else the project's root, else the chat's own folder; `folder` is the first of them, or null when the teammate never answered.
  `documents` holds the Markdown and text files, `.md`, `.markdown` and `.txt`, its replies created or changed, by the provider's report or the checkpoint's comparison, at most 20, each once at the end of the reply that last changed it, the newest first; a path relative to the working folder is made absolute, a file whose last change deleted it or moved it away is left out, and a moved file is listed at the path it moved to.
- A chat's reading position, where its history was left, is the window part's view state, which Noldova Desktop gives to the module's database: the engine keeps it in the `readingPositions` table, one row per chat, with the message the view was at, that message's sequence and its distance from the view's top.
  A chat left at its end has no row, so it opens at its end, as it does when its row's message is gone.
  A chat opened at its row reads only the page around that message, so Jump to latest there first reads the chat's newest page, and the history shows it as a new history at its end and follows it, never drawing the old window's end; the kit's list keeps the view where it is until it comes.
  A history that already holds the newest page scrolls to its end without a read, and when the read fails the store posts it and the list scrolls to the end of the rows it has.
  A rewind drops the chat's row in its own transaction when the row names a removed message, and each window only forgets its copy; a window that finds its row's message gone opens the chat at its end and drops the row only while the row still names that message, so a newer position another window stored stays.
  The history reports a position on every scroll, and the window keeps the newest in memory; the history asks the store to write the chats whose position changed in one `ReadingPositionSave` request two seconds after scrolling stops, and the store writes them too in the save step it registers, which runs when the window closes or the app quits and first waits for a write already under way, so a scroll costs no write and a crash loses only the positions changed since the last write.
  The window reads every chat's position once, with `ReadingPositionList` when its store loads, and from then on keeps its own; when two windows show the same chat, the last one to write it wins.
  Deleting a chat deletes its row, and the rows aren't in the change feed, since no other window or the CLI follows them.

### Where errors show

- A failure shows where the person acted when that place is still on screen: a dialog's own message, the Teammates, Providers and MCP servers pages' own banner with its Dismiss for a change to a teammate or a provider, for a chat started from the Teammates page and for a switch of a provider's own MCP servers, the composer's attachment message, an image tab, a reply's history, and Background tasks.
- Every other failure is a Noldova Desktop notification at error severity, of one of the four kinds `module.json` lists in `contributes.notifications`; a notice of the same kind and key replaces the earlier one, and Noldova Desktop dismisses them all when the part is withdrawn.
  A window that closes doesn't withdraw its part, so its notices stay in the list; a window part that is disposed dismisses its loads and its failed terminal show itself.
  Noldova Desktop keeps one list of notices for every window, so each notice has one owner: the window part that acted posts its own, the runtime part posts `teamrun.replyFailed`, and a window dismisses its own on events every window gets.
  - `teamrun.messageNotSent`, keyed by the chat: a send the runtime refused, with the reason as its text, Open chat, and, when the refusal names a provider, Enable provider while it is disabled and Manage providers.
    It goes when a message is sent in the chat from any window, when the provider it names is enabled or signed in, when the teammate it names moves to another provider, or when the chat is deleted.
  - `teamrun.replyFailed`, keyed by the chat and posted by the runtime part: a reply that failed while no window showed its chat, by the shown chats every window reports, titled by the teammate, with the reply's last error as its text and Open chat; it goes once any window shows the chat or the chat is deleted.
  - `teamrun.loadFailed`, keyed `<window>/<load>` by the window part's own id and what it failed to load: the supported providers, the catalog of projects, chats and teammates, a chat (`chat/<id>`), the running replies, the first-launch check or the providers' tools, with Open chat for a chat and Try again, `teamrun.reload` with the window's `windowId` and the notice's `noticeKey`.
    Try again loads each failed part of that window again: from another window it goes through the runtime's `teamrun.windowReload`, which publishes `teamrun.windowReloadRequested` with the id, and only the window it names acts; Load TeamRun's data again without an id reloads the window it runs in.
    When the runtime answers that the window is closed, the window that ran Try again posts "Couldn't try again", "The window this failed in was closed or reloaded.", in that notice's place, without Try again.
    A chat whose load failed is selected again only while it is still the chat asked for; otherwise only its messages load again.
    It goes once that load works, from Try again or from any later load; a chat's goes too once Try again fails while no tab or selection holds the chat any more, or after the chat was deleted.
  - `teamrun.chatActionFailed`, keyed `<action>/<target>`, such as `RenameChat/c1`: any other action that failed, titled by the action, such as "Couldn't rename the chat", and an action on a provider by its name too: the provider's label as the Providers list shows it when the failure comes, so two providers of one kind differ, such as "Couldn't sign in to Work", the label given it for an added provider, the kind's name as the window lists it for an install, which acts on a kind, such as "Couldn't install Claude Code's tool", or "the provider" for one the window doesn't list, with the reason as its text and Open chat when it acted on a chat; it stays until the person dismisses it, the same action on the same target fails again, or the chat it acted on is deleted.
    Its `OpenAttachment` notice, "Couldn't open the attached file", for an attached file Core no longer has, is at info severity, since the file is gone rather than the action failing.
- Open chat is `teamrun.openChat` with the chat's `chatId`, or its `conversationId` from a notice an earlier version posted, and Enable provider is `teamrun.enableProvider` with its `providerId`; a deleted chat opens nothing.
- A notice Noldova Desktop refuses to post is written to TeamRun's log by its kind and the failure's name, without its text.
- A notice's handle that arrives after the notice was dismissed is dismissed only while no newer notice of that kind and key was posted, since Noldova Desktop gives the newer one the same id.

## The runtime part

- Noldova Desktop Runtime loads `RuntimePart` and gives it the module's context; the part composes the engine over it and owns nothing else but the failed-reply notices.
- Each TeamRun method is a Noldova Desktop method named `teamrun.<method>`, with the method's first letter lowered, such as `teamrun.projectOpen`.
- Each TeamRun event is published on a Noldova Desktop event channel named the same way, such as `teamrun.messageCreated`.
  The fields that name a chat are `chatId`, `shownChatId` and a rewind result's `chat`, while the chat methods and events keep their earlier names, such as `teamrun.conversationCreate`, until their `MethodName` and `EventName` members take the word chat.
- The engine's tables live in the module's database, and its migrations are the part's Noldova Desktop migrations.
  `DataFolderRead` answers the module's folder, `modules/teamrun` in the data directory, and its database there, `teamrun.sqlite`, as Noldova Desktop keeps them; the command-line client works through the same runtime, so it uses the same folder.
  The part's connection turns foreign keys on, whatever the host does.
- TeamRun doesn't open a provider's credentials file or ask for its token, and stores no provider's sign-in: the provider's tool keeps its sign-in, and a sign-in check asks that tool.
  The one exception is the values a person types into a local ACP command's variables, which are kept in the system's secret store and passed only to that command.
  The provider's row in `teamrun.sqlite` keeps the answer, as `authStatus` and `identity`:

  | Kind | How the check asks | `identity` holds |
  |---|---|---|
  | Claude | `claude auth status --json` | Email, plan, organization, sign-in method |
  | Codex | The app-server's `account/read`, with `refreshToken: false` | Email, plan, sign-in method |
  | GitHub Copilot | Opens an ACP session, then reads the headless server's status | User name, sign-in method |
  | Grok | ACP `initialize`, `authenticate` with `{headless: true}`, then its account and plan methods | Email, plan, organization, sign-in method |
  | OpenCode, and an ACP agent the person adds | Opens an ACP session | Nothing |
  | Cursor | Opens an ACP session, then `cursor-agent about` | Email, plan |
  | fx | `fx status --json`; `auth` of `missing` means signed out, and anything else goes on to open an ACP session | Nothing |
  | Antigravity | ACP `initialize`, and whether `antigravity-acp/acp_token.json` exists in the profile, unopened | Nothing |
  | Muse Code | Opens an ACP session on `muse acp --no-session-log` | Nothing |
  | Pi | `pi auth check --provider <provider> --json`, the provider read from `defaultProvider` in the profile's `settings.json`, or, when that names none, from `get_state` after starting Pi | Nothing |

  Only the provider's row keeps the identity: a turn reads none, and a reply's `ObservedSettings` hold the kind, model, effort, speed and tool version that served it, with no email, plan, organization or sign-in method; `ReplyIdentityMigration` removes the `identity` that Claude's and Codex's replies kept before from the stored messages and their change feed entries.
  It removes the fields, not the bytes: earlier copies can stay in the database file's free space, since a streaming reply's rewrites freed them before the migration and only a `VACUUM` outside the migration's transaction would clear them, and in the last three backups Noldova Desktop takes before it applies pending migrations, until they are overwritten.
  Finding profiles to offer checks whether named entries exist in a folder, without opening them; three of these are sign-in files: OpenCode's `opencode/auth.json`, Pi's `auth.json` and Copilot's `config.json`, where its command-line tool can save its token in plain text when there's no keychain.
  Listing the person's own MCP servers reads `.claude.json` whole, Copilot's `mcp-config.json` and `settings.json`, the `.mcp.json` and `.github/mcp.json` files from the chat's folder up to its git root, and plugin `.mcp.json` files, and asks Codex's `config/read`; these can hold secrets, such as a server's token, and TeamRun keeps only the names.
  `.claude.json` also holds the person's account details under `oauthAccount`; on a signed-in computer it had no field named for a key or token, but Claude's program has a `primaryApiKey` setting, so the file isn't promised to hold no key.
  With Claude's `ownMcpServers` on, a turn also passes the user's and its repository's entries from `.claude.json` to `setMcpServers`, expanded, and TeamRun keeps none of them.
  TeamRun chooses by name which environment variables a provider's program gets, passes those on and keeps none, so a key in a variable stays with the person's environment and that program.
- Each provider the person adds has a profile folder its tooling owns, named to the tooling by one variable: `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` for Claude Code, `CODEX_HOME` for Codex, `COPILOT_HOME` for GitHub Copilot, `GROK_HOME` for Grok, `XDG_DATA_HOME` for OpenCode, `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` for Cursor, `GEMINI_HOME` for Antigravity and `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` for Pi, which each provider's kind names.
  Claude Code's provider in its usual folder, `~/.claude` or what `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` names in the environment TeamRun passes on (the login shell's, or the registry's on Windows), gets no `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` from TeamRun, in its sign-in check, sign-out, model listing, plan usage and turns; only a folder of its own gets the variable.
  The usual folder is Claude Code's one person folder, so its terminal sign-in runs without the variable too, and signing it out warns that it signs the person's own Claude Code out; the kind list rebuilds Claude Code's profile from the environment passed on each time the window reads it (`refreshDescriptorAsync`), and the window reads it again before it opens a sign-in terminal or asks to confirm a sign-out for a kind with person folders, comparing folders as `ProfileFolderPath.isSame` does, so the terminal and the warning follow the same folder as the turns.
  On macOS, Claude Code 2.1.323 with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` set, even to `~/.claude`, didn't find the sign-in it made without it: `claude auth status --json` answered `loggedIn` true without the variable and false with it, on a signed-in test Mac (Nov 21, #338).
  fx is the exception: fx 0.0.16 keeps everything in `~/.fx` and reads no variable that moves it, so an fx provider's folder is the person's own `.fx`, which its kind names as a person's folder, and no variable is set for it.
  A kind can name other variables too, as `signInVariables`, each with its subfolder in the profile folder; its sign-in terminal sets them beside the kind's variable.
  Cursor's are `CURSOR_DATA_DIR` at `data` everywhere, and where that build keeps its sign-in file, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` at `home/.config` on Linux and `APPDATA` at `home/AppData/Roaming` on Windows, so `cursor-agent login` in the sign-in terminal and every Cursor process read the same one; Cursor's shell tool runs each command with Cursor's own environment, so a command in a Cursor turn sees that `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` or `APPDATA` too, and a run on Linux showed git missing the person's `~/.config/git/config` and gh the person's settings in it; on macOS neither is set, so commands keep the person's own; the MCP servers Cursor starts get a clean environment, without these; Cursor's processes keep the person's `HOME`, since on macOS Cursor keeps its sign-in in the login keychain, which every Cursor profile on the Mac shares: a capture signed in through one profile showed the sign-in in a second, and `cursor-agent logout` in one signed out both.
  OpenCode runs in the person's own setup: the `opencode` they installed, found on the `PATH` or in `~/.opencode/bin`, in their home folder, with their own configuration, plugins, project files, keys and sign-in, as its own command line has them; TeamRun installs no copy of it, makes no profile for it and writes nothing into its folders.
  A provider's folder is OpenCode's `XDG_DATA_HOME`, where it keeps `opencode/auth.json` and its sessions: `~/.local/share` on every system, or what the person's environment names, is the person's own (`OpencodeDataFolder`), another folder holds another sign-in and other sessions beside the same configuration, and its launches leave out keys and tokens so that it uses that sign-in.
  A provider saved with the folder TeamRun once made, `profiles/opencode/default` in its data folder, runs in the person's own too, and that folder stays as it was: the sign-in made in it isn't used, and a chat whose session is there starts a new session with a note, since `session/load` finds a session only in its own data folder.
  Another folder TeamRun once made keeps its `opencode/auth.json` and sessions, and its `config` and `home` are no longer read.
  Approvals rest on two things TeamRun sets on each launch (`OpencodeEnvironment`), as OpenCode 1.19.5's source merges them and a live run on 1.19.5 and 1.18.31 showed.
  In `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT`, which comes after a project's files, `build` and `plan` get the rules a chat runs by: every tool TeamRun asks about asks, with Plan's `edit` denied, the approved TeamRun tools allowed, OpenCode's read-only tools, `read`, `glob`, `grep`, `list`, `skill` and `lsp`, and its `todowrite` allowed, with a `.env` file's read asking as OpenCode's own rules ask, and Allow All allowing all.
  The same rules go under `build` and `plan` in OpenCode's deprecated `mode` key too, which OpenCode merges into its agents after every configuration, an organization's included, so a `mode` entry of the person's or a project's can't change them.
  They also stay on, primary and shown there, so a configuration can't turn them off and leave a chat in its own `default_agent`, which TeamRun selects `build` over on each session; and starting a sub-agent asks, on a card that names it, but for `general` and `explore`.
  `OPENCODE_PERMISSION`, which goes over every configuration it read, the person's, a project's and an organization's, and the same content's `general` and `explore`, refuse those tools and a `.env` file's read outside Allow All: OpenCode's ACP answers a permission request only for a session it tracks, so a sub-agent's `ask` shows no card and holds the turn until the person stops it, as the live run showed, where a refused sub-agent reports back and the chat's agent can run the tool itself and ask.
  TeamRun's rules name no tool TeamRun doesn't know, such as one of the person's own MCP servers' or custom tools or a plugin's: the provider's own-servers switch decides those.
  `OPENCODE_PERMISSION` starts with a rule for every tool, which refuses them while the switch is off and asks for them while it's on in a chat turn, and `general`'s and `explore`'s rules start with one that refuses them either way, before allowing the read-only tools again; Allow All allows them only while it's on, in a chat turn.
  OpenCode has no setting that turns off every server its settings name, so in a chat turn the person's own servers still start with OpenCode while the switch is off, as Pi's do, and only their tools are refused.
  A launch outside a chat turn, such as a model list, a list of effort levels or a sign-in check, turns each of them off by name (`OpencodeMcpServers`), as Codex's and Copilot's do: `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` sets `mcp.<name>.enabled` to `false` for each server named in the files OpenCode 1.19.5's source loads, and in the person's own content, keeping their entry's other fields.
  Those files are `config.json`, `opencode.json` and `opencode.jsonc` in the `opencode` folder of `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, or of `~/.config`; the file `OPENCODE_CONFIG` names; unless `OPENCODE_DISABLE_PROJECT_CONFIG` is `1` or `true`, `opencode.json` and `opencode.jsonc`, and the same in a `.opencode` folder, in the person's home, where those launches run, and each folder above it up to the repository root, or to the file system's root outside a repository; the same two in `~/.opencode` and in the folder `OPENCODE_CONFIG_DIR` names; and last the same two in the managed folder, `/Library/Application Support/opencode` on macOS, `opencode` under `ProgramData` on Windows and `/etc/opencode` elsewhere, or the folder `OPENCODE_TEST_MANAGED_CONFIG_DIR` names.
  A relative `OPENCODE_CONFIG`, `OPENCODE_CONFIG_DIR` or `OPENCODE_TEST_MANAGED_CONFIG_DIR` is resolved against the launch's folder, as OpenCode resolves it.
  They are read as JSON with comments and trailing commas, as OpenCode reads them; a file there that exists but can't be read or parsed, or is larger than 32 MB, refuses the launch, naming the file.
  A file OpenCode loads after the content turns none of those servers back on, since OpenCode merges each file over the last key by key, so its entry without an `enabled` of its own keeps the content's `false`; an entry that sets `enabled: true` itself does turn it on.
  A server named only in a macOS managed preferences profile, `ai.opencode.managed`, which OpenCode reads through `plutil`, or in a remote configuration the person's sign-in names, still starts: TeamRun reads neither before the launch.
  A launch outside a chat turn, such as a model list, the effort levels or a sign-in check, also sets `OPENCODE_PURE` to `1`, OpenCode's `--pure`, which skips every external plugin, the person's config folder's and `.opencode/plugin(s)`'s included, so none of their code runs there; a chat turn keeps them, and their tools are refused as [The runtime part](#the-runtime-part) says.
  Since a plugin's `config`, `auth` and `provider` hooks can add providers, their models and their sign-in methods, the model list and the sign-in check leave out what a plugin adds, while a chat turn has it: on Linux x64, OpenCode 1.19.5 loaded a plugin in the person's config folder for `opencode models` and `opencode acp` without it, and with it loaded none and listed the same models, the plugin adding none, and its `initialize` and `session/new` answered as before; `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` setting `{"plugin":[]}` and `OPENCODE_DISABLE_DEFAULT_PLUGINS=1`, which turns off only its built-in ones, don't keep them out.
  The rule for every tool is in `OPENCODE_PERMISSION` and not in `build`'s and `plan`'s, since OpenCode takes the last rule that matches, in the order the rule's name first appeared, and an agent's rules from the person's own settings come before TeamRun's: there it would beat a tool those settings name, refusing a command that should ask.
  The cost is that outside Allow All the built-in sub-agents can't edit, run commands, reach the web, read a `.env` file or leave the folder, so they only read and search, and that TeamRun's rules for the read-only tools replace the person's own for them in the built-in agents.
  Outside Allow All, a `read` rule for some files in the person's own `OPENCODE_PERMISSION` or `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` goes into each of those agents' rules after TeamRun's rule for every file and before its `.env` rules, so it still applies there; a `read` rule in their settings files is overridden there, since TeamRun doesn't read those files, and applies only in an agent of their own that names it.
  There, a rule that the person's or a project's `opencode.json` gives every agent for a read-only tool comes before TeamRun's rule for every tool, which then refuses that tool.
  The person's own value of `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` is kept when it is plain JSON, with its agents' other settings, and dropped when it isn't, so a value with comments is lost; so is their own `OPENCODE_PERMISSION`, which goes under TeamRun's, after its rule for every tool, so a rule of theirs for a tool TeamRun doesn't name, or for a file under `read`, still applies.
  What the person's or a project's `opencode.json` and `.opencode` folder can then change: providers, models, MCP servers, plugins, commands, instructions, autoupdate and sharing, and agents' other settings, and an agent's rule for a tool TeamRun doesn't name, which goes past the switch: their own server's tool that `build`'s rules allow by name runs with no card.
  What can still go past TeamRun's approvals is what can go past their own OpenCode's, and a repository's own files can do it as well as the person's configuration, since a project's `opencode.json` and `.opencode` folder stay on: opening a repository trusts them, as running `opencode` in it does.
  An agent the configuration or the repository defines isn't one TeamRun can name, so it keeps its own rules once the person approves its start: with `bash: allow` it runs commands with no further card, and with `bash: ask` it holds the turn until the person stops it; with no rule of its own it is refused, and with the own-servers switch on, a tool of the person's own asks there, so it holds the turn too.
  A `mode` entry of the person's or a project's for `general` or `explore` makes that agent primary, with its rules over TeamRun's, and a chat's agent can still start it with no card, since the `task` tool starts any agent it is named; it no longer lists that agent, though, so it takes a model that names an agent the tool doesn't offer.
  TeamRun doesn't pin those two under `mode`, since a `mode` entry makes an agent primary, and they would no longer be sub-agents.
  A plugin in either runs code when OpenCode starts for a chat turn and can act for itself, with no card at all.
  OpenCode takes the last rule that matches, in the order the rule's name first appeared, so a `*` that a built-in agent's `permission` names after `bash` beats TeamRun's `bash` for that agent.
  A project's rules on a built-in agent can't otherwise skip the card, since the launch's content comes after them, and a `*` in the plain `permission` can't, since the agent's own rules come after it.
  OpenCode reads the person's and the project's instruction files itself, as [Instruction files and memory](#instruction-files-and-memory) says, so TeamRun sends none.
  OpenCode 2.x is refused with a reason until a capture shows it keeps the approval rules TeamRun sets, and a version before 1.18.31 too; Fork stays off: OpenCode 1.19.4's `session/fork` answers with a copy of the whole session, and ACP's fork names no turn to fork at, so a rewind can't keep only the turns before it; it has no `unstable_forkSession`.
  OpenCode sends no plan updates, plan-limit meters or sub-agent rows, so those stay empty for it; its `usage_update` carries the context's tokens used and size.
  After an approved edit OpenCode also asks the client to write the file, `fs/write_text_file`, though TeamRun's `initialize` offers no file writes; TeamRun answers it as unsupported, as for any request it doesn't serve, and OpenCode, which doesn't wait for the answer, writes the file itself and logs the refusal on its standard error.
  Cursor runs as `cursor-agent [--model <model>] --disable-project-configs acp`, the model chosen at launch, with a `cli-config.json` in its profile that TeamRun writes before each launch: the `static` channel, which turns off `cursor-agent update` and the background update that, as read from the 2026.08.11-e8db854 and 2026.10.22-27dcf5f bundles, an interactive `cursor-agent` schedules 2 s after it starts (`acp` schedules none in either, and live runs of the earlier build saw no update in `acp` with or without the channel; an update installs into `~/.local/share/cursor-agent/versions` and points `~/.local/bin/cursor-agent` at it, never changing the copy that ran), the `allowlist` approval mode, and an allow list with each approved TeamRun tool as `Mcp(teamrun:<tool>)`.
  A chat turn's process starts in the turn's folder, and any other launch in the profile folder, since Cursor's ACP server reads the project's rules and `AGENTS.md` from the folder it starts in, not from the session's `cwd`.
  A chat turn first reads the allow and deny rules of the person's own `cli-config.json`, in their `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`, else `.cursor` in their home, and writes them before TeamRun's tools, so Cursor in TeamRun runs and refuses what the person's own Cursor does; a file there that can't be read or is larger than 32 MB refuses the turn, naming the file.
  An allow rule of the person's whose MCP server is `teamrun` or holds a `*`, such as `Mcp(*:*)`, `Mcp(*:<tool>)` or `Mcp(teamrun:*)`, is left out, so TeamRun's own approvals alone decide its tools and such a tool still asks in TeamRun; a deny rule stays as written, so one naming `teamrun` or `*` refuses TeamRun's tools too.
  A `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` that is the profile's own folder, compared without case on Windows, gives no rules, so TeamRun's earlier approvals never come back as the person's.
  A launch outside a chat turn writes the person's deny rules but none of their allow rules, so a title session runs nothing the person's allow rules would let through unasked; it shares the profile's one file, since whether Cursor keeps its sign-in with a config folder of its own is unchecked until a signed-in live check, and keeping the deny rules means a chat turn running beside it, should Cursor read the file again, loses none of them.
  A launch outside a chat turn, such as a title session or a model list, points `HOME`, and `USERPROFILE` on Windows, at the profile's empty `title-home` folder, so Cursor reads none of the person's `~/.cursor` hooks or MCP servers there; on macOS it keeps the person's home, since Cursor finds its keychain sign-in through it, so a chat's title there comes from its first message, with no Cursor title session.
  Its `initialize` names no version, so no version is refused, and its `cursor_login` method only reuses a sign-in made with `cursor-agent login`, so TeamRun never calls `authenticate`: `session/new` works without it, and one that fails with `-32000` reads it signed out.
  A signed-in check then runs `cursor-agent about`, with `NO_COLOR`, and takes its `CLI Version` as the version, its `Subscription Tier` as the plan and its `User Email` as the account; when `about` fails, the check stands without them.
  Cursor's model ids carry their settings in brackets, such as `default[]` for Auto, and `--model` takes them as listed.
  Cursor answers `session/resume`, `session/fork` and `session/status` with `-32601`, so a chat resumes through `session/load`, which finds a session only in the folder it was made in; TeamRun resumes in the chat's folder, and a load that fails starts a new session with a note.
  Cursor keeps a session only once a turn in it saved its conversation, so a session whose first turn Cursor refused can't be loaded: as read from the 2026.10.22-27dcf5f bundle, `session/load` then answers `-32602`, `Invalid params`, with Cursor's `Session "<id>" not found` in the error's `data.message`, which the note names after ACP's message, as it does for any ACP error whose data carries a reason its message doesn't.
  When its run fails for something the person has to do, Cursor sends no error, as read from that bundle.
  It sends a message chunk of two line breaks and `Please sign in to continue`, `Upgrade your plan to continue`, `Add a payment method to continue` or `Check your settings to continue`, and ends the prompt with `end_turn`.
  A Cursor turn leaves such a chunk, exactly as sent, out of its answer and fails, so a chat isn't titled from it either.
  It fails as signed out for the first refusal, and with Cursor's words for the others, such as "Cursor refused the turn: Upgrade your plan to continue.".
  The plan refusal reports no plan window: a chat answered on Auto right after it, so it refuses the model for the plan rather than saying the plan is used up.
  The refusals are read from the bundle, not yet seen live.
  TeamRun signs Cursor in only with that browser sign-in, never with an API key, since it never reads, stores or passes a Cursor credential; `CURSOR_API_KEY` and every other `CURSOR_` variable are left out of its processes.
  Cursor's ACP server offers no fork, compaction, plan usage or team MCP servers, so those stay off for it, each with its reason; it tells of its sub-agents only through `cursor/task` notifications, which TeamRun doesn't show as rows yet.
  fx runs as `fx acp`, found on the `PATH`, in `FX_INSTALL_DIR` or in `~/.local/bin`, as `fx.exe` on Windows, where fx's row shows with its page and no Install button, since its releases have no Windows build yet and its install command is a `bash` script, in the chat's folder, or the person's home folder without one, with `NO_COLOR`; `AI_GATEWAY_API_KEY`, `VERCEL_OIDC_TOKEN` and every other credential, `FX_`, `VERCEL_` and `AI_GATEWAY_` variable are left out, so fx signs in only from the person's own `~/.fx`.
  TeamRun passes no `FX_HOME`: fx 0.0.16 doesn't read it, and with it set fx still shows the sign-in, model and permission mode of `~/.fx` and writes nothing to the named folder.
  It doesn't move `HOME`, which would separate fx but take the person's setup from every command fx runs.
  The sign-in check asks `fx status --json` with the same environment: `auth` of `missing` means signed out, with a hint to run `fx login`, and no `fx acp` starts; anything else, or an answer it can't read, goes on through `fx acp` as the shared adapter's check does.
  Its `initialize` names no sign-in method, and signed out it refuses with `-32600` and asks the person to run `fx login`; the agent's `isSignedOutRefusal` makes the shared adapter read that refusal as `-32000`, so a model listing lists none, and a turn fails with the kind's sign-in message.
  fx also sends `-32600` to a signed-in person, for a credential it can't refresh or a model it can't use, so a refusal that doesn't name `fx login` stays an error with fx's words.
  A program named `fx` that answers neither `fx status --json` as fx does nor `fx acp` shows in the check as possibly another tool of that name; a program that answers either is taken as fx.
  fx's session lists its `provider` option before its `model` option, both in the `model` category, beside `fast`, so the agent's `modelOption` names `model` as the one the adapter lists and sets; `effort` is its `thought_level` option, and the provider stays as fx's profile has it.
  A chat resumes through `session/resume`; the prompt answer's `usage` names the input, output, cache-read, cache-write and reasoning tokens, which `FxUsageReader` reports.
  Every fx provider would use that one fx, so fx's kind has `singleAccount` on every platform.
  fx's ACP server offers no fork, `/compact` command, plan usage, sub-agent rows or stop for a background command, so those stay off for it, each with its reason.
  fx's turns aren't checked live: its Vercel AI Gateway refuses a signed-in turn with HTTP 403 `customer_verification_required` until a card is on file, and the maintainer chose not to add one (Nov 3, 2026), so the ability table's fx cells stay, and what waits on a signed-in capture stays as this document says; a live run of fx 0.0.16 on Linux x64 checked its own `fx login` sign-in and that `fx status --json` then reads it signed in.
  The person chooses an existing folder, or TeamRun makes a new, empty one at `profiles/<kind>/<name>` in the module's folder, private to the person, in the same transaction as the provider's row; its name is the label made safe for every platform by the protocol's `ProfileFolderName`, which the window also uses to show the folder before it's made, and a label whose folder already exists is refused.
  The kind's `prepareProfile` readies that new folder in the same transaction, since the window opens the sign-in terminal as soon as the provider is added, before any check or turn: Cursor claims it as TeamRun's and makes its sign-in folders there, as its first launch would, because a sign-in that wrote into the unclaimed folder made that launch refuse it as not TeamRun's; a failure removes the folder and adds no provider.
  An empty folder the person chooses, for a new provider or a move, is readied the same way, since its sign-in can come before any check too, and a failure there keeps the folder and leaves the provider as it was; a folder with anything in it is left to the first launch, which refuses one that isn't TeamRun's.
  A folder another provider already uses is refused too, checked in the same transaction and compared as the search for profile folders compares them, so two providers never share one sign-in and one session store.
  A kind whose sign-in this computer keeps once for every profile names why in its `singleAccount`, as Cursor does on macOS and fx everywhere, and a second provider of that kind is refused with `Conflict` and that reason, in the same transaction.
  The add dialog's type select and the Providers view's add panel show such a kind disabled once it has a provider, with that reason in a note under the select and as the card's note, and start on the first type still free.
  They move off a type that becomes taken while open, and onto the ACP agents row when every type is taken, which adds nothing until an agent is chosen there.
  Such a sign-in is also the person's own CLI's, so for such a kind with a profile the window's profile hint (`Resources.sharedSignInProfileHints`) and Sign out's confirmation (`Resources.formatSignOutShared`) say that signing in replaces the account the person's own CLI uses and signing out signs it out too, and Cursor's sign-in message says so on macOS.
  A kind can also name in its profile's `personFolders` the folders whose providers run in the person's own setup with the person's own sign-in, as Grok names its home and the folder TeamRun once made for Grok, since a provider in either runs in the person's Grok home, and OpenCode its data folder and the one TeamRun once made for it; a sign-in or sign-out terminal for such a provider leaves the kind's variable as the person's environment has it.
  For a provider in one of those folders, Sign out's confirmation (`Resources.formatSignOutPersonal`) says it signs the person's own CLI out too and that signing in again replaces its sign-in, and Grok's profile hint says choosing the Grok home shares that sign-in while another folder keeps one of its own.
  Each kind also names its tooling's usual folder on this computer, the one its own command uses when no provider names one.
  The composer offers only added providers, so a chat or the default teammate saved with no provider moves at start-up to the added provider in that folder; with none, the chat asks for a provider, with Send off, until one is chosen, and a teammate's hand-off to it starts no one.
  Core refuses a person's send that names a kind and no provider as `InvalidParams`, naming the kind, and stores and starts nothing; the command line's `send` needs `--provider` with `--kind`, except in a teammate's own send, and with neither it refuses a default teammate that has no provider, pointing to Settings and `--provider`.
  A new folder is never signed in, so Add provider starts its sign-in as soon as the provider is saved, once its kind is installed; an existing folder may already be signed in, and waits for Sign in in its menu.
  `ProviderProfileDiscover` finds profile folders no provider uses yet, for the Providers tab's search to offer.
  For Claude Code, Codex, Copilot and Grok it looks in the usual folder in the home folder, in the folder the kind's variable names when it is set to another, and in the folders beside each whose names start with its name, such as `.claude-work`; for every kind it also looks in the kind's folders under `profiles/<kind>` in the module's folder, but never in a folder the kind's search skips, such as Grok's `profiles/grok/title-home`, where Grok's title sessions run, and `profiles/grok/default`, an earlier TeamRun's Grok folder that now means the person's own Grok home.
  A folder counts only when it holds one of the names the kind's tooling writes there, such as `settings.json` or `projects` for Claude Code, or `auth.json` or `config.toml` for Grok, whose installers write `config.toml` in `~/.grok` on every system and read no `GROK_HOME`, which only the program reads, and TeamRun lists the names without opening any file in it.
  A sign-in check reads the signed-in account too, without starting a session or sending a prompt: Codex and Claude Code report an email, Grok its email from `x.ai/auth/info`, GitHub Copilot its GitHub user name from its headless server's `auth.getStatus`, and Antigravity and Pi none.
  Pi's check runs `pi auth check --provider <provider> --json` for the provider that `defaultProvider` names in the profile's `settings.json`, or else the provider of the model `get_state` reports, so a key in the environment counts, and shows signed out, saying to choose a model in Pi, when there is neither.
  Muse Code's check opens a session on `muse acp --no-session-log`, so it leaves none behind: signed out, the server refuses the session, which TeamRun reads as signed out when the refusal is ACP's sign-in error, `-32000`, or, with any code, carries the text Muse Code's ACP guide gives, "log in to Muse before opening a session", since the guide gives no code, which stays unconfirmed until the live check, and TeamRun names `muse login`, which runs in a terminal and keeps the sign-in in Muse Code's own place, as `muse logout` does, since its ACP server can't sign out and shows its device code only to an editor that can show links.
  On macOS Muse Code keeps its sign-in in the login keychain (its changelog, 1.4.2 and 1.4.12), which a config folder doesn't separate, so Muse Code's kind has `singleAccount` there.
  A profile in the person's own config folder runs with their environment as it is, and any other is named with `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, which every program Muse Code runs inherits: on Windows x64 `gh` 2.102.0 kept its configuration under that variable's folder rather than `%AppData%`.
  Pi has no sign-in command, so its sign-in terminal runs plain `pi`, where the person types `/login` and then `/quit`, and the check runs when Pi quits or the terminal closes, as the terminal's hint and notice say.
  Claude Code also reports its plan and organization and Codex its plan, while Grok's organization, or else its team, comes from `x.ai/auth/info` and its plan from `x.ai/billing`'s `subscription_tier`; Codex's `unknown` plan counts as none.
  The account gets its own 10 s: Grok tries `_x.ai/auth/info` and then `x.ai/auth/info`, also after the first hangs, and then `_x.ai/billing`, and `x.ai/billing` only when Grok answers `-32601`, each within 10 s, so a hung billing call adds 10 s once, and Copilot's headless server gets 10 s to start and answer `connect` and `auth.getStatus` and is stopped after it, so a hung account read leaves the check signed in with no account reported, rather than holding the Providers tab.
  The Providers tab and `list-teammates` show the email, or else the user name, in the same place, and a signed-in provider that reports neither shows Account not reported there; one signed out shows a stored account, or nothing.
  The Providers tab shows the plan as a small tag in its own column, such as Max, with each plan by the name its provider's own pages use, from one table in the Window's resources (Claude Code's `pro` and `max`; Codex's `plus`, `pro`, `enterprise`, and `team`, `business` and `self_serve_business_usage_based` as Business, ChatGPT's name for Team since its rename), any other lowercase code written as words, and a plan with a capital, such as Grok's display name for its tier, as sent, and no tag where none is reported or it is blank, the same for every provider; a provider whose account names no plan, or a blank one, shows the one its latest plan read names with the windows, so Copilot shows the `planTier` of `session.quota.get`, such as Free or Pro, from the read the Providers tab asks for, with no reply first, a reply's read, which doesn't read the plan, keeps it, and a plan read that names none clears it, while a read that names only the plan and no windows, such as Copilot Free's, leaves the status bar's plan usage item hidden; the organization or team is in the account's tooltip; `list-teammates` and `teamrun_list_teammates` list them as `plan`, the same plan, and `organization`.
  Each provider takes a row: a dot in its status's colour, with the status as its tooltip and accessible name, before its name and kind, with its account on the line under them, ellipsized, with the account, the organization, the windows its limits don't show, such as credits left, and the profile folder in its tooltip; its plan; and its limits, up to two of its provider's own windows, chosen as the status bar chooses them, a used-up one first, each with the status bar's short label, such as 5h, Week, Premium or Quota, a short meter of what's left and what's left in words, such as 58% left or 75 of 300 left, with when it resets only once the window nears its limit, and the window's full figure and reset as its tooltip; a provider whose plan read names no window says <kind> reports no limits there, and a provider not read yet shows nothing; the meter and figure are amber from 75% used and in the error colour from 90%; assistive tech reads the label and figure, and the bar is hidden from it.
  The limits' labels, meters and figures each line up down the list, for rows with two windows, one or none.
  A provider is used up while one of its windows is, its used percentage, or else its count against its allowance, rounding to 100 or more, and that window's reset hasn't passed.
  Core's `PlanUsageService` keeps that one state on each report it holds, as `usedUp` and `usedUpUntil`, the latest reset of the used-up windows, or `null` when one of them names none; Sustain reads it there too, through `list()`.
  It lets the state go at that reset with no new report, checking the clock after each wait of at most a minute, so a sleep or a clock change can't hold it past the reset, or at a newer report with usage left, and publishes the report again either way, so no restart is needed.
  The provider menu and the responder list in the composer, the teammate dialog's provider list, the Teammates page's connection cell and a chat member's tooltip show such a provider as Used up, resets <time>, with the reset its time today, its weekday and time within a week, such as Fri 9:00 AM, and its date beyond, as the provider's row says too, or Used up when no reset is known.
  The composer adds no note of its own for a used-up provider, since those tags and the status bar already say it, and still sends: nothing moves between providers, and the turn runs as before.
  A turn that fails while Core holds its provider's plan as used up puts `usedUp` and `usedUpUntil` on its error detail, and the reply names the provider and its reset on a line above the error, such as Codex · Work: used up, resets <time>, with the reset written as the provider menu writes it, or used up when no reset is known, and was used up until <time> once that reset has passed.
  A plan reading that the turn's provider reports after the failure, and that shows the plan used up, updates the error the same way: Copilot reports its plan used up with no reset as it reports a quota refusal, and its quota read then brings the reset; Claude Code's reading, which it starts as the turn starts, can land after the turn failed.
  A refusal reported with no used-up reading at all shows the provider's own error alone.
  A status that needs words, such as a failed check's error, Not installed or Signing in…, takes the account's place, in the error colour when it failed; a failed check on a provider shown as Connected or Not checked shows the failed dot and Connection error instead.
  While a provider signs in, its dot shows in the accent colour and pulses unless motion is reduced, and a terminal sign-in shows the command its terminal runs after the note; a found folder whose sign-in isn't checked yet shows Account not reported and Checks when added.
  A provider signed into the same account as an earlier one of its kind, by email or user name whatever their case, is tagged Same sign-in as that one.
  A kind's install or update shows on its heading in Your providers, Installing… or Updating… with its percent after the count and Cancel at the heading's end while it can be cancelled, and every provider of that kind keeps its account, plan and limits meanwhile; a kind with no provider there shows it once, on a line at the top of Your providers, such as Installing Claude Code… setting up.
  A kind whose own copy TeamRun installed and that has an update says "version <n> is available" after its count, with Update to <n> at the heading's end; a kind whose installation names a copy found outside TeamRun's own install (its `foundPath`), as one on `PATH` from npm, Homebrew or winget or a path the person chose, says it updates on its own, with no Update.
  A heading's Update or Cancel is centred on its text without making the heading taller, so every kind's card sits as far under its heading's text.
  Install, Update and Cancel are also in Add provider's dialog, on a line under the provider type that says what installed means for that kind, with Get a subscription for a kind with no provider yet.
  The page announces once, through the shared announcer's polite live region, when a kind starts installing or updating and when a provider starts signing in or out, never each percent.
  It remembers each announcement by its kind or provider, not by its words, so two providers with the same name are each announced.
  The ⋯ button at a provider's end, named More actions for it, opens its menu with only the actions that apply, in this order: Sign in while it is unknown, or always for a kind whose sign-in is known only once a prompt runs (`ProviderKind.confirmsSignInAtPrompt`), as an added ACP agent's, whose session may start with no account, since the row's own step offers Sign in while it is signed out, expired or in error, and the sign-in's own actions while one runs; Stop sign-out while a terminal waits; Sign out; Edit…, Check again, Show profile folder, which shows the folder selected in its parent in the system's file manager through the base's `showInFolderAsync` and says on the page why when the base refuses it, such as for a folder no longer there, then after a line Disable or Enable, and Remove… in the error colour.
  The amber, the Same sign-in tag and the meter use TeamRun's own styles from the kit's tokens until the kit has a warning colour, a danger menu item, a warning tag and a short meter.
  In a list narrower than 40rem the meters give way, leaving each window's label and figure, so each provider's name and kind stays whole.
  A folder found gets only the kind's found check, which tells whether it is signed in from the tooling's own files and starts nothing that reaches the provider's service; its account, plan and limits are read by the full check once the person adds it, so a found folder shows no account and no Same sign-in tag.
  Claude Code's found check runs `claude auth status --json` with `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1`, and shows only signed in or out: on Linux x64 each run opened one connection to Anthropic without the variable and none with it; Codex's runs `codex login status`, signed in on exit code 0 and signed out on 1, and starts no `codex app-server`; fx's runs `fx status --json` alone, never `fx acp`; Antigravity's looks for its token file.
  GitHub Copilot, Grok, OpenCode, Cursor, Muse Code and an added ACP agent can't tell without starting their program, so their found folders show Not checked; so do Pi's, though `pi auth check` tells without starting Pi once the profile's settings name a `defaultProvider`, since no capture shows yet that it connects nowhere, which every found check must; the versions in the kinds' headers come from `--version`, which connects nowhere.
  A folder whose found check fails is listed as an error, and the failure is logged with its kind only; the usual folder is named after the kind and the others after the folder, without its leading dots.
  The search holds a work item while it runs, and a second call for the same kinds gets the running one's result; every call leaves out the folders a provider took while the search ran.
  TeamRun never reads, stores or passes a provider's sign-in; the values a person types into a local ACP command's variables are kept in the system's secret store and passed only to that command.
  Codex, Grok and Antigravity sign in through the provider's own sign-in that TeamRun starts.
  An ACP agent's sign-in starts with the person's own home and app-data folders, `HOME`, `USERPROFILE`, `APPDATA`, `LOCALAPPDATA`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` and `XDG_DATA_HOME`, and their `XAUTHORITY` and `BROWSER`, which the agents' exclusions leave out of their other processes, in place of those its other processes get, and keeps the kind's variable, so the browser it opens is the person's default with their usual profile, can open an X display whose cookie isn't in `~/.Xauthority`, and no browser profile is made in the provider's folder; Grok's other processes get the person's own folders too, and its sign-in stays in `GROK_HOME`, the person's `~/.grok` by default.
  Claude Code, Copilot, OpenCode, Cursor and fx sign in through their own login command, `claude auth login`, `copilot login`, `opencode auth login`, `cursor-agent login` or `fx login`, which the kind names: Sign in opens a terminal through the terminal module's `TerminalLauncher`, in the profile folder with the kind's variable set to it, unless the folder is one of the person's own the kind names, as fx's `.fx` is, and its sign-in variables to their subfolders, joined with the folder's own separator, and has the terminal run that command once its shell is ready.
  The terminal runs the copy of the CLI the provider's turns run, whether TeamRun installed it or found it on the PATH: before it opens, the window asks the runtime for the provider's sign-in command (`ProviderSignInCommand`), and the adapter answers the kind's command with the folder of the program its turns start put first on the person's shell `PATH`, under the name that environment already uses, when that folder holds the command's launcher, as Cursor's `cursor-agent.cmd` sits beside the `node.exe` it starts on Windows; when it doesn't, as for Pi, whose program is a shared Node, or when the runtime can't answer, the terminal runs the kind's command as the `PATH` finds it.
  When the login command ends, as Terminal's `commandEnded` tells with an exit code, TeamRun checks the provider's sign-in at once: when the check finds it signed in, TeamRun closes the terminal, and otherwise leaves it open, so the person can read why, and the provider's Signed in as cell shows the failure.
  When the terminal ends, as its shell exits or its tab closes, TeamRun checks the sign-in again too, unless it already closed it; a shell that can't tell its command's end, such as Command Prompt or fish, is checked only then.
  A Restart of a running shell doesn't end it.
  The window opens one sign-in terminal at a time for each provider, in that window: Sign in for a provider whose failed sign-in's terminal is still open closes that terminal first.
  A sign-in's or install's terminal launch that opens a tab, from the composer's provider dialog, asks Terminal for at least 16 rows, `TerminalLaunch.minimumRows` from `Resources.providerTerminalRows`, so a shorter dock grows while it's open: OpenCode's sign-in list draws a frame about 12 rows tall, and in a 10-row dock each redraw landed two rows low and left old frames.
  While the sign-in waits, the row's Cancel closes the terminal and ends TeamRun's wait without a check, and the menu's Check again and Remove… stay on; removing or disabling the provider closes it too, and one the person already closed counts as closed.
  A signed-in, enabled provider's menu offers no Sign in, except for such a kind, and Sign out where its kind signs out, which asks the person first.
  For Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Cursor, fx and Antigravity, whose kinds have `supportsSignOut`, `ProviderSignOut` runs the provider's own sign-out: `claude auth logout` with the provider's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `cursor-agent logout` with the environment a Cursor turn gets, `fx logout vercel`, `fx logout codex` and `fx logout grok` in turn with the environment an fx turn gets, `account/logout` on the profile's Codex app-server, or Grok's `_x.ai/auth/logout` after `initialize`, then `x.ai/auth/logout` while Grok answers `-32601`, or ACP's `logout` for Antigravity, below.
  Grok 1.0.70 answers only the first; when it answers neither, the sign-out fails with a reason naming Grok's version and saying to update it, and an answer without `ok: true`, or with `api_key_still_set`, fails it with Grok's reason.
  fx 0.0.16 signs out of one account per `fx logout`, Vercel's by default, and answers one with no login with exit code 0, so the three leave no fx sign-in, whichever the person used.
