  One that fails doesn't stop the others: every account's `fx logout` runs, and the sign-out then fails with each failed account's reason, as a `TimeoutException` when each of them ran out of time.
  A `claude auth logout`, `cursor-agent logout` or `fx logout` that doesn't finish within the sign-in check's time fails with its own reason, which names the time, Cursor's and fx's as a `TimeoutException`, and one that exits with an error fails with its output.
  Core then checks the sign-in and records it, also after a sign-out that failed or timed out, and a check that reads the provider signed out counts as done.
  A sign-out that fails, unless its check reads the provider signed out, or whose check still reads it signed in, as when its tool also holds an API key, leaves the provider signed in, and the page's error line says why: the sign-out's own failure first.
  A provider disabled during its sign-out is recorded Unknown without a check, and one moved to another folder meanwhile is left as its move recorded it.
  A sign-out is refused while the provider signs in, and a sign-in while it signs out; the menu hides Sign out while the provider signs in, in TeamRun or in a terminal.
  Copilot signs out only with `/logout` in its own session, which its kind names as `signOutCommand`: its Sign out opens its profile's `toolCommand`, `copilot`, in TeamRun's terminal with `COPILOT_HOME` set to the profile folder, after a dialog that names the folder and says a shared keychain may sign out other connections too.
  Pi signs out the same way: 1.1.0 has `/logout` in its own session and no sign-out command outside it, so its kind names `/logout` as `signOutCommand` and its Sign out opens `pi` in TeamRun's terminal with `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` set to the profile folder.
  That terminal is followed like a sign-in's: the row shows Signing out with Stop sign-out in Sign in's place, TeamRun checks the sign-in when the tool ends or the terminal closes, and closes the terminal once the provider reads signed out.
  A second sign-out while that terminal runs, such as a chat's `/logout`, follows it instead of opening another; once the tool ended still signed in, a new sign-out opens a new terminal in its place.
  OpenCode's ACP server has no sign-out, so its kind names `opencode auth logout` as `signOutCommand` with `signOutEndsWithCommand`: its Sign out runs that command itself in TeamRun's terminal, with the provider's folder as its `XDG_DATA_HOME` unless the folder is the person's own, where the person chooses which stored sign-in to remove.
  That sign-out ends with its command rather than with a signed-out check, since OpenCode's free models answer with no account, so its check, which starts a session, still reads it signed in: on 1.19.4, a profile with no credentials stored replied and offered only the free models.
  When the command ends with exit code 0, TeamRun closes the terminal, checks the sign-in and shows what it finds, Signed in with no account on the free models, and a chat's `/logout` says it signed out; another exit code leaves the terminal open with the command's failure, and the sign-in is checked when it closes; when the terminal closes without the command's exit code, as in a shell TeamRun can't follow, the sign-in is checked and a chat's `/logout` says TeamRun couldn't tell whether the sign-out finished.
  Antigravity signs out with ACP's own `logout`: its agent sets `logsOut`, and its Sign out stops that provider's running turns, starts its ACP server and, when `initialize` reports `agentCapabilities.auth.logout`, as 1.3.0 does, sends `logout`, then removes the profile's `antigravity-acp/acp_token.json`, which its sign-in check reads; Core then checks the sign-in as for the others.
  Until the sign-out ends, the adapter starts no server for that provider but the sign-out's own, so no turn, sign-in check, model list or plan read, and a stopped turn doesn't reopen its session; a plan read reads nothing, the others fail saying the provider is signing out, and the stopped reply says it stopped for that.
  Core doesn't check a provider in the folder it is signing out of, and drops a check that was running when the sign-out started, so the row keeps what it showed until the sign-out's own check, and never shows that reason.
  A server that doesn't report it fails with a reason saying that version doesn't support signing out, and an `initialize` or `logout` that doesn't finish within its time fails with the sign-out's own reason, which names the time.
  Every Sign out dialog says a reply running now may fail.
  A chat's message that is exactly `/logout`, ignoring case and the spaces around it, with no attachments, isn't sent: the composer asks to sign out the provider the message would go to, with the same dialog and through the same path as that row's Sign out (`ProviderSignOut` in the window), and shows below the box that it signed out, or why not.
  A message that is a teammate's mention and `/logout`, such as "@Ada /logout", does the same for the provider of the teammate it names, the last one when it names several, so nothing reaches a provider as a raw `/logout`.
  Other text with `/logout`, or attachments, is sent as a message.
  The install command's terminal, from Add provider's dialog or panel, is followed the same way: when its command ends, TeamRun looks for the tool again and closes the terminal once it finds it, and otherwise leaves it open, so the person can read why, with the row showing the result; closing it looks again.
  Pi's own installer, run there on Linux and macOS, ends by asking whether to start Pi now, and a yes keeps Pi running in that terminal, so TeamRun finds Pi and adds the provider once the person quits it; where Node.js is missing, the installer offers to install it, which can run `sudo apt-get` for Node.js or for xz-utils, so TeamRun's own commands use no `sudo` but Pi's installer may.
  Install on a kind whose failed install's terminal is still open closes that terminal first, without a look, so each kind has one install terminal at a time.
- Each command-line kind registers a tool beside its adapter, which `ProviderToolList` checks afresh on every call: whether its command is found, its `--version`, the official install command for this platform and the page where the person subscribes.
  The adapters keep their command in a `CommandSource`, which looks for it again on each use while it is missing, so a tool installed while TeamRun runs works without a restart: on the `PATH` TeamRun started with, then in the folder each official installer uses, which a new install adds only to later shells' `PATH`, such as `~/.local/bin`, Codex's `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\OpenAI\Codex\bin`, Cursor's `%LOCALAPPDATA%\cursor-agent` and winget's `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Microsoft\WinGet\Links`, and last the copies TeamRun installed itself: on Windows the newest Copilot, and the pinned OpenCode.
  Cursor's Windows install ships no `cursor-agent.exe`, only a `cursor-agent.cmd` that runs `cursor-agent.ps1` in PowerShell, which a process started without a shell can't run, so on Windows the locator finds that `.cmd` and starts what the `.ps1` would: the `node.exe` beside it with the `index.js` beside it, as in the unpacked package, or else those in the newest dated folder under its `versions` folder, as Cursor's installer lays them out, with folders of the same date ordered by the `HH-MM-SS` part newer builds add, where the `.ps1` leaves such a tie open; an override that names a `.cmd` is followed the same way.
  That start sets neither of the launchers' two variables: the `.cmd`'s `CURSOR_INVOKED_AS` only decides whether Cursor shows a terminal tip about its `agent` command, and the `.ps1`'s `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE` only speeds up Node's start with a cache in `%LOCALAPPDATA%`, outside the profile; on Windows x64, with TeamRun's Cursor environment, `--version`, `about`, `status` and `logout` gave the same output and exit codes started directly as through the `.cmd`, and `acp` answered `initialize` and refused an unsigned `session/new` with -32000.
  Add a provider in the empty chat's placeholder and the composer, and Settings' Manage providers, open the Providers tab instead.
  The first launch is the `firstLaunch` row of the `preferences` table, which `FirstLaunchFinish` writes once, keeping the first time, and records in the change feed.
  While the placeholder shows with no provider and the first launch isn't finished, it opens the Providers tab with a welcome line above the form, while `ChatStoreService.welcoming` holds, and the search already running, with no dialog.
  Skip for now and Continue sit in a footer along the tab's bottom edge, which stays in view while the form scrolls above it.
  Both finish the first launch, and Continue is on once a provider is signed in; Skip leaves the form open as a tab, and the tab opened later has no welcome line.
  Continue shows "Your first teammate", `FirstTeammateComponent`, in the form's place: Name, filled in after the earliest signed-in provider's kind, such as Codex, and followed by its @mention, and Answered by, that provider; model, effort and permissions keep the provider's defaults, and Choose now opens the teammate dialog, "Let's add your first teammate", with the step's name and provider.
  The @mention takes at most the name field's end half, cut with an ellipsis, and the field keeps that half as its end padding, so a long name scrolls in the field rather than running under its mention.
  Start chatting, or the dialog's Save, makes the teammate and starts its first chat with it without a project; Not now makes nothing, and both go back to the form.
  An empty chat offers Open a folder under its text while no project is open, on the text's start edge, as the placeholder does, so a chat started without a project always has a way to open one; the composer offers Add a provider while no provider exists.
- Every program a provider starts goes through Noldova Desktop, which owns its stop and its record.
  The providers pass their whole computed environment as the request's variables, so Runtime's minimal environment doesn't apply to them.
- A provider's environment comes from the person's current one, which Noldova Desktop reads again for each start, not from the one TeamRun started with.
  On Windows it is the runtime's with the machine's and the person's saved variables over it; on macOS and Linux it is what the person's login shell exports, so a provider started while TeamRun runs from the Dock still sees the profile's `PATH`.
  Each adapter builds its process's environment from that read with its own rules, such as the credential variables Claude and Codex withhold, so a tool installed or a variable set since TeamRun started reaches the next start.
  Claude's leaves out every `CLAUDE_*`, `CLAUDEPID`, `CLAUDECODE` and `ANTHROPIC_*` variable but the routing switches, so a variable in the person's shell can't change which profile signs in; a chat turn still keeps the person's own Claude Code settings, such as `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY` or `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS`, as their own Claude Code would.
  Those are the `CLAUDE_*` variables Claude Code's environment variables reference listed on Nov 4, 2026 (`Resources.claudeSettingVariables`), less the ones Claude Code sets for its own processes, such as `CLAUDECODE`, `CLAUDE_PID` and `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`, those whose name holds a key, token, secret, password, credential, authentication or certificate, `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, and `CLAUDE_CODE_EFFORT_LEVEL`, which would beat the teammate's effort.
  That reference runs ahead of the Claude Code TeamRun pins, 2.1.263 with Agent SDK 0.3.263, so some names on the list do nothing until a version bump, which checks the list again.
  The list also leaves out the switches that choose who serves Claude beyond the routing ones, `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_MANTLE`, `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_ANTHROPIC_AWS` and `CLAUDE_CODE_PROVIDER_MANAGED_BY_HOST`, so a chat turn reaches the same service as the sign-in and the model list, which see only the routing switches.
  It leaves out the settings only the Agent SDK's mode reads, which a person's own Claude Code ignores: `CLAUDE_CODE_EXIT_AFTER_STOP_DELAY`, which would end a chat's open query between turns, `CLAUDE_CODE_RESUME_INTERRUPTED_TURN` and its `_MAX_AGE_MS`, `CLAUDE_CODE_STARTUP_FAILURE_RESULTS` and the `CLAUDE_AGENT_SDK_*` ones, and `CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS`, which would skip the chat's permission mode.
  A client certificate or a certificate store set in the person's shell, `CLAUDE_CODE_CLIENT_CERT`, `CLAUDE_CODE_CLIENT_KEY` or `CLAUDE_CODE_CERT_STORE`, doesn't reach Claude either, nor does `CLAUDE_CODE_API_KEY_HELPER_TTL_MS`, since their names match the credential filter.
  So a TeamRun started inside a Claude Code session passes none of that session's ids, account, email, executable, process or mode switches to a chat turn; a title session, a sign-in, a sign-out and a model listing keep none of them.
  A program already running keeps the environment it started with: Codex's one app-server per provider, or a Claude chat's open query, sees a change only once it starts again.
- While any reply runs, the part holds one Noldova Desktop work item.
  Each rewind, move, install and sign-in request holds its own work item while it runs, so Noldova Desktop asks the person before quitting during one.
  On deactivation the part first refuses a new rewind or move as `Unavailable`, waits for the rewinds and the moves still running, then ends the replies and the providers' programs, and only then closes its connection; an install or a sign-in ends when its provider stops.
- Before each reply's turn the engine takes a checkpoint of the chat's folder through the checkpoints module's service: the project's folder, known by the project's id and its root, a chat's attached folder, known by its path's hash and the path, or else the folder of a chat without a project, known by the chat's id and that folder's path; and keeps the checkpoint's id on the reply's row, in `messages.checkpointId`; the folder's id goes in `messages.checkpointFolderId` as the turn starts, whether or not the checkpoint is taken.
  After the turn it compares the folder with that checkpoint and reads each change's diff with the folder now; these are the reply's working-tree evidence, in the shape the window has always read, and a diff is kept only while it is text of at most 1 MiB, cut to its first lines.
  The evidence leaves out the files the provider reported and the paths Git ignores, as `git status` does, so a build or test run in the reply doesn't bury its edits: one walk, `git ls-files -z --others --ignored --exclude-standard --directory`, lists them, a folder ignored whole as one path, and the changed paths it lists or that are inside a folder it lists are left out; it never lists a tracked file or a path inside a submodule; a removed path outside them, such as a file of an ignored folder the reply deleted whole, is checked with one `git check-ignore -z --stdin` of its highest missing folder, or of itself, those inside a submodule left out, and the removed paths under a missing folder Git doesn't ignore with a second, the paths the index lists left out of both since Git never ignores them, so an ignored file of a removed folder, such as a `.pyc` of a deleted package, stays out; when that check fails, the listed paths are still left out, and a failure that isn't Git's is thrown rather than taken as nothing ignored; a folder in no repository, no Git found, or a Git that can't start or run within 30 seconds (`TEAMRUN_GIT_TIMEOUT_MS` in tests), ignores nothing, and the checkpoint itself still keeps every file.
  It then lists at most the first 1,000 changed files and reads at most 4 MiB of diffs in all; a reply past either bound gets a note that its evidence is incomplete, as a package install would.
  A changed file counts as the reply's only when it was written while one of the reply's own tools ran, within 2 seconds of its ends: from each tool step's start to its end, a command, a file change, or any step that names its tool, such as an ACP delete or move or an MCP tool call (an ended status, a Pi step's output, or a Claude Code tool's result), and each background task's; a step Codex marks read-only opens no window, and a terminal tool's step or a Claude Code command run in the background stays open until the turn ends.
  A file is timed by the later of its modified and status-change times, so a move, `cp -p` or an extract that keeps the old modified time still counts, or when it's gone by its nearest existing folder's, and one that can't be timed counts as the reply's.
  Something else that writes while one of the reply's tools runs, such as a pull during a long build, still counts as the reply's, since the times can't tell them apart.
  A path a tool step of the reply reports, in its files or as a command's write targets, a `mv`'s source and target included, always counts as the reply's, as does anything under it, whatever its times; a path at or above the chat's folder, such as a write to `.`, counts nothing under it.
  A move made by a command that reports no paths, such as one inside a script, rests on the filesystem updating the status-change time; one that doesn't goes to the note.
  The rest changed while none of its tools ran, such as by a pull, a checkout, another program or another reply's background command, and go to a changes note, "changed while this reply ran, but not by its tools", naming the first 5 and how many more, that the reply's card shows, never to its changes or the Changes panel.
  Each read asks for at most what is left of the 4 MiB, so a diff cut there is dropped and spends the rest, and the files after it have no diff.
  The capture takes the reply's signal, so Stop and quitting end it, and a stopped capture ends the reply before its turn starts.
  The compare and the reads take a signal that starts as the provider's turn returns, before its last tool calls are stored, and that only a Stop or quitting after that aborts, so a reply the person stopped during its turn still lists what it changed; a Stop after the turn, up to the reply's hand-off and a wake reply's too, ends the reply Cancelled even when its answer was complete, while one during it leaves a turn the provider still completed Completed, and a stopped compare leaves the reply without evidence and with the note.
  Such a Stop comes from another window or the command line's cancel: the window that stopped the reply waits for its end and refuses a second Stop meanwhile.
  A checkpoint the module doesn't take, or a compare it can't make, leaves the reply without them and is written to the log by the failure's kind or the module's outcome; the reply goes on.
  The compare sees only the chat's folder, so a reply whose command steps ran with a `cwd` outside it gets a working-tree note that changes made there aren't listed.
  Each `cwd` is resolved against the chat's folder, and both are compared by their real paths, so a symlink, macOS's `/tmp`, or a case the file system ignores names no folder; the note names each outside folder once while they are short, and the chat's folder otherwise.
  A reply whose commands ran when the folder wasn't compared gets a note saying so instead: for want of a checkpoint, after a failed or refused compare, after a turn that failed or was stopped, and after a turn the provider started itself on a wake.
  These notes and the note that the evidence is incomplete are marked `changesNote`: the Changes panel lists the reply with them, and Activity leaves them out.
  The Changes panel's page says whether any ended reply of the chat ran a command, so an empty panel then says that no files changed in the chat's folder rather than that none were edited.
  A rewind asked to restore files previews the restore of the first removed reply's checkpoint and restores that plan; a refused preview or restore restores nothing, and the rewind's result says why in plain words, as it does for a reply without a checkpoint.
  The files are restored before the transaction that removes the messages, so when that transaction fails after a restore the rewind is refused as `Internal` with a message saying the files were restored and the messages kept, with the error's own message, and a second rewind removes them.
  The restore is limited to the paths the removed replies changed, as their changes details list them: the provider's own reports, a command's writes and the files their tools changed, each with the paths under it; a file changed outside them, such as one a reply's changes note names, a pull or another program's edit, stays as it is now, and a file outside the folder, or one whose name holds a backslash, is left out.
  When a removed reply's details may not list every file it changed, because its comparison failed, was refused, left files out or was stopped, or because it took no checkpoint, the whole folder is restored instead.
  The checkpoints need no Git, so a folder without Git has them too, and the module never touches the project's own Git; the ignore check above is the one Git TeamRun runs in the folder, and it only reads, never touching the checkpoints.
  `GitIgnores` runs the `git` (`git.exe` on Windows) it finds in the path's absolute folders, read at each check, passing over macOS's `/usr/bin/git` when the active developer folder is missing, as `DEVELOPER_DIR` names it or else the folder `xcode-select` chose, the Command Line Tools' or Xcode's, since that stand-in then asks to install them, started in Git's own folder and pointed at the work folder with `-C`, with `-c core.fsmonitor=false` and `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`, so neither a `git.exe` in the work folder nor a `core.fsmonitor` program its Git config names runs.
  A rewind keeps the default teammate's provider session when the provider can fork it through the last kept reply's turn; a named teammate's session restarts, told what was said before.
  Codex forks the thread through the turn at once (`thread/fork`), and Grok forks the session through the prompt index its echo of the prompt carried (`x.ai/session/fork`).
  Claude Code forks at the next turn: that turn resumes the session at the kept turn's last transcript entry with the Agent SDK's `resumeSessionAt` and `forkSession`, so Claude Code makes the fork in the provider's own profile; the SDK's own `forkSession` runs in TeamRun's process and finds the transcripts only through the process's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`.
  Copilot, OpenCode, Cursor, Antigravity and Muse Code can't fork, and the rewind dialog says so in one sentence for each provider that can't.
  The snapshots earlier versions kept as `refs/teamrun/snapshots/*` in a project's Git are left as they are: nothing reads, migrates or removes them, and no clean-up ships for the refs earlier builds left.
  Showing the restore's plan before it runs is left for a later look.
- Core keeps the files attached to messages and drafts in `<data folder>/attachments`, with a draft's under `draft-files/<chat id>` there.
  A sent message's files sit in that folder itself and go after the commit that removes the message: deleting its chat or forgetting its project, or a rewind, which keeps the files of a rewound User message for the draft it gives back; at start-up, before Core serves a tool call or resumes a queued send, the drafts' sweep also removes the files there that no message names, such as a crash between a save and its send's commit leaves, and a removal that fails is logged and left to that sweep.
  Before it loads one, says whether it is still there, which a draft's attachments also ask, keeps a draft's file, copies a file by its path for a send or a prepared attachment, removes a prepared file, or lists, cleans or writes into a folder, Core reads every link on the way to it with `lstat` and `readlink`, without following any: from the attachments folder down, and through every folder of a link's target from its root, at most 40 links.
  A link whose target is a network or device path, any but a drive path on Windows as Noldova Desktop's file policy takes it, a longer chain or a link it can't read refuses the file or folder before anything stats or resolves it: a file counts as gone, a load, a copy or a kept draft file is refused, saying that it is reached through such a link, and so is a write into such a folder, which clean-up leaves alone.
  So is a path with a `.` or `..` name, since the system would apply it after following a link that the walk, reading the path as written, never saw.
  The data folder and the folders above it are trusted, so a data folder on a redirected or roaming profile share still works.
- A file the person chooses attaches by the grant Noldova Desktop issues for it, never by its bytes or path: the window asks `grantFilesAsync` for an unsaved file, image or not, and sends `teamrun.attachmentPrepare` its grant, so a file of up to 100 MiB never passes through a frame.
  The runtime part redeems the grant in that request through `IRuntimeFiles.redeemAsync`, Core copies the open file in 1 MiB chunks into the drafts folder within the 10 MiB per image and 100 MiB per file limits, and the runtime part closes the file once the copy ends; a close that fails is logged by its kind and keeps the copy's answer.
  A grant that is unknown, already used, expired or another module's, a file that isn't a regular file or can't be read, and a file whose size or modified time changes while it is copied are refused and leave no copy; the window discards the grant of a file it can't prepare.
  An input by grant is refused anywhere but `teamrun.attachmentPrepare`, since only that request holds the file.
- A restored unsent message's image previews by address, with no bytes passing as text: the window asks `teamrun.attachmentShare`, Core finds the draft's or a message's file with the checks a load makes, and the runtime part shares it with the asking page through Noldova Desktop's `IRuntimeFiles.shareAsync`, as its own media type and for Noldova Desktop's default 10 minutes, answering only the grant.
  The window shows the grant's `desktop-file:` address, which the window's `img-src` allows, and discards the share when the file is removed or sent, the chat changes or the composer goes; Noldova Desktop ends it at a page change too.
  A share that is refused, or an address that fails to load, leaves the image a file row, and the rest of the draft restores.
  The image viewer, a message's attached images and an image tab show an attachment by a share the same way: the viewer keeps the shares among its 16 latest reads, discards one that drops out of them or whose chat is deleted, shares again a minute before Noldova Desktop's 10 minutes end, and shares once more when an address fails to load.
  A share is discarded only once every load drawing it has drawn, so a share that drops out before it is answered still shows, and is discarded once its load has drawn, or at once when no load waits.
  The viewer times its shares with a monotonic clock, `performance.now()`.
  Copy and Download read an attachment's bytes through `teamrun.attachmentRead` at that moment, since a canvas that draws a `desktop-file:` image can't be read back.
  An image a reply wrote in its project is shown by a share too, through `teamrun.writtenImageShare`: Noldova Desktop shares only from the module's data or work folder, so Core reads the file with `teamrun.writtenImageRead`'s checks and keeps a copy of those checked bytes in the data folder's `shared-images` folder, named by their SHA-256, so two different images never share a name; a generated image's copy that Core stored in `images` is shared as it is.
  A copy stays in use for 10 minutes, Noldova Desktop's longest share, after it was written or last shared, and is never removed while in use; the copies take at most 256 MiB, and when a new one would pass that, the ones no longer in use go first, else the share is refused.
  A copy is flushed to disk before it takes its name, so a crash can't leave a named copy short of bytes.
  Core removes the copies no longer in use when it starts; when the file system refuses, it logs why and starts anyway, and any other error stops the start.
  A copy the system won't remove yet, such as one a page still has open, is logged and stays until a later clean.
  A copy shared again before a restart may be removed at start while a page still shows it, so its address fails to load and the window reads its bytes instead.
  An SVG, a refused share or an address that fails to load comes as bytes through `teamrun.writtenImageRead`, as before; Copy and Download read a written image's bytes that way at that moment.
- A chat can have no project: its `projectId` is null, and it works in a folder of its own, `<data folder>/chats/<chat id>`, which Core makes with the chat and again before each send, wake and rewind, so a chat kept over a restart finds it.
  The folder starts with an empty Git root of its own, so a provider's or an agent's walk up from it stops there and never reaches a checkout around the data folder, and the busy check of a reply covers that folder exactly; TeamRun writes nothing in that Git.
  `ConversationList` and `ConversationListEmpty` with a null `projectId` list these chats, and `ConversationFolderCount` counts the files in a chat's folder at any depth, leaving out its `.git`, reading the folder asynchronously so a large tree, such as one with a `node_modules`, doesn't hold up Core's other requests.
  Moving such a chat to a project copies its files into the project's folder, merging folders both have; a path of the same name that isn't a folder on both sides refuses the whole move with `Conflict`, naming the paths, and copies nothing.
  Links are copied as they are, so a relative link to the chat's own files, such as `node_modules/.bin`, still works in the project; a link that would point elsewhere once moved, an absolute one into the chat's folder or a relative one out of it, also refuses the whole move with `Conflict`, naming the links.
  A copy that fails partway removes every file and folder it made in the project before the move fails, newest first, so the project is as it was and a retry isn't refused by the chat's own files; it tries every one, and one the system won't remove is named in the log while the move fails with its own error.
  A move, like a delete, is refused while a reply runs in the chat, and a move also while one runs in the target project.
  A move, an attach and a detach are also refused with `Conflict` while the chat's work still runs in its folder after its reply ended, since that work keeps the folder it started in: the refusal names each running background command by its command and each running helper by its description, and a teammate's background work for an ended reply whose run stays open for its provider's own work (#139).
  The copy reads and writes asynchronously, outside any transaction, so Core goes on answering other requests; meanwhile the project and the chat's folder are held as a rewind holds them, so a turn or a rewind in either, another move of the chat or into the project, a delete of the chat, or a forget of a project that has chats is refused with `Conflict`, while a wake, a background command's or a watched chat's, waits with no reply and starts once the hold ends.
  The move is then recorded in a short transaction, and a failure there, such as the project forgotten during the copy, removes what the copy made, newest first, and leaves the chat where it was.
  Noldova Desktop asks before quitting during a move, and a quit waits for it to end; a crash or a power loss mid-copy leaves the files copied so far in the project and the chat without a project, so moving it there again is refused with `Conflict`, naming those files: the person deletes them from the project's folder, or moves the chat to another project.
  Once the chat is moved or deleted, Core deletes its checkpoints through the checkpoints module's `DeleteFolder` and then its folder; a held-up delete or a failure keeps the folder and writes why to the log, and each start sweeps the folders whose chat is gone or has a project, trying again, and logs a `chats` folder it can't list or a folder whose chat it can't read.
  Deactivation stops the sweep at once, with the refusal of a new rewind or move and before any step that waits, which releases no further folder and ends the checkpoints delete under way, and waits for the folder it is releasing before the database closes, even when an earlier step of the close fails.
  Such a chat can instead work in an existing folder the person attaches with `ChatFolderAttach`, kept as the chat's `attachedFolder`: its replies, their checkpoints and its terminals use that folder in place from the next turn, and nothing is copied; `ChatFolderDetach` takes the chat back to its own folder.
  Attach resolves an absolute path and checks it as a reply's folder check does, refusing with `InvalidParameters` a path that isn't absolute or a folder that is missing, can't be read or doesn't answer in time, and with `Conflict` a chat in a project or while a reply runs in the chat or in that folder; moving the chat to a project clears it.
  Attach holds the folder from before its folder check until the chat's row is updated, so no reply starts in that folder meanwhile, and checks again in the update's transaction that no reply has started in the chat.
  An attach, a replace and a detach keep the replies' checkpoints: a rewind restores from the folder the reply ran in when the chat works there again, and otherwise restores no files and says the chat's folder has changed since, before it looks for the checkpoint.
  Of the later replies, those that never started, such as one failed by its folder check, and those that ran in another folder are left out, so neither their files nor a partial comparison or a missing checkpoint of theirs widens the restore to the whole folder.
  Every reply keeps the folder it starts in, a wake the provider runs ahead of the running reply too.
  A started reply from before the folder was kept counts as having run in the chat's current folder.
  An attached folder's checkpoints are known by `folder-` and the first 32 hex digits of the SHA-256 of its path, compared without case on Windows, so chats attached to one folder share them, as a project's chats do; a chat's delete, move or sweep removes only its own folder and that folder's checkpoints, never the attached folder or its checkpoints.
  The chat's menu offers Attach a folder…, then Replace the folder…, which asks first, and Detach the folder; the tab's breadcrumb and the row's tooltip name the attached folder, and deleting such a chat says the folder and its files stay.
  A reply whose attached folder is missing, can't be read or doesn't answer fails before its provider starts, with a note that names the folder and says to restore it, attach another or detach it, or to check its drive or share.
  The migration `20261024190000_ProjectlessConversations` rebuilds `conversations` with a nullable `projectId` in its one transaction under deferred foreign keys, so every message, member, draft and reading position that points at a chat stays.
  The migration `20261025200000_ChatTitles` gives each chat still stored with the earlier default title "New conversation" the default title "New chat", edited or not, since `edited` also marks a chosen responder, a move and a teammate added or removed, in its row and in the change feed's payloads for it, with its update time kept, so it keeps its place and is still named after its first message.
  The migration `20261025210000_ChatNames` renames the `conversations` and `conversationTeammates` tables to `chats` and `chatTeammates` and each `conversationId` column to `chatId`, with its indexes made again under the new names, renames the change feed's `Conversation` and `ConversationMember` entities to `Chat` and `ChatMember`, and moves each stored `conversationId` field to `chatId` at its known paths, in the rows and in the change feed's payloads, so a tool input with its own `conversationId` keeps it.
  These moves, and the other renames of stored JSON fields, keep a value already under the new name and drop the old name either way.
  A unique index is named `UX_<table>_<columns>`, as context's `createUniqueIndex` names it from 0.0.12, and a plain one `IX_`: the migration `20261125090000_UniqueIndexNames` gives a database made earlier, whose unique indexes on `projects`, `messages`, `teammates` and `sharedMcpServers` are `IX_`, the `UX_` ones, each made before its `IX_` one is dropped, so uniqueness holds throughout and no row changes.
  Pages' own migration, `20261111000000-pages`, which TeamRun applies among its own by id, adds the `pages` table, one row per page with its chat and JSON, and `pageVersions`, the last 50 versions of each page with each one's author and HTML, both indexed by their chat and with no key into TeamRun's tables.
  Each version also writes the page's current HTML to `<data folder>/pages/<chat id>/<page id>.html` once its transaction commits, so a failed commit leaves the copy of the version the database holds.
  A copy whose write fails after the commit is logged by its kind, and the save keeps its answer, since the version is saved.
  Deleting a chat removes its pages and that folder through the Pages API, and each start removes the pages of chats that are gone, as a project's forget or a failed removal leaves them.
  The migration `20261108000000_MessageText` indexes the Text details of each settled message, one that isn't Pending, Running or AwaitingApproval, in the FTS5 table `messageText`, keyed by `messages`' rowid and kept in step by triggers on `messages`, so a streaming reply's updates leave it alone until the reply ends.
  A later migration that rebuilds `messages` by copy, drop and rename drops those triggers and renumbers the rowids, so it must create the triggers again and refill `messageText`.
- TeamRun keeps organizations, each with its id, name, optional default folder, its order in the list, and its own tree; `OrganizationService` keeps each in the `organizations` table, one row per organization with its JSON, and records each change in the change feed as `Organization`.
  `OrganizationList` returns every organization with its tree, by order and then by creation; `OrganizationCreate` adds an empty one last, `OrganizationUpdate` renames it or sets or clears its folder, keeping a field it isn't given, and `OrganizationDelete` removes an organization, the last one too, with its departments, placements and layout, recording each in the change feed, and leaves its teammates and chats, each chat no longer an organization's chat, so it shows in its project or under Chats; the migration makes My organization once, so a start after the last one is deleted lists none until one is created.
  Every department, placement and layout method takes the organization's id, and a department id from another organization is not found; the runtime sends `OrganizationChanged` with the whole changed organization, `OrganizationRemoved` with the id of a deleted one, and after a teammate is deleted, `OrganizationChanged` for each organization it was placed in.
  An organization holds only the teammates placed in it, and a new teammate joins none; a teammate's placement is keyed by the organization and the teammate, so one teammate can sit in several organizations.
  An Organization chat belongs to an organization and a teammate, which `OrganizationChatOpen` takes as `OrganizationChat`; its role line comes from its own organization, none once that organization is deleted, and it runs in the folder attached to the chat, or else, with no project, in its organization's folder, or else in its own folder or its project's, as any chat does.
  The migration `20261124090000_Organizations` adds `organizations` with My organization, `my-organization`, rebuilds `departments` and `teammatePlacements` with the organization's id, keyed by organization and teammate, leaves a teammate with no placement in none, and renames the kept layout to My organization's.
  The window keeps every organization in one `OrganizationCatalog` per window part; a view's `OrganizationScope` follows its document's instance, and its `OrganizationStoreService` and `OrganizationLayoutService`, which the components inside the view share, read and change that organization only, an organization not listed yet showing as an empty one.
- The organization is a tree under the person: departments and teammates, each under one parent, the person, a placed teammate or a department, to any depth and with no cycle, the protocol's `Organization` refusing a duplicate, an unknown parent and a cycle; a parent is an `OrganizationParent`, its `OrganizationNodeKind` `Person`, `Teammate` or `Department` and, but for the person, its id.
  A department has a name, a node type of its organization, an optional leader, who sits directly in it, and an order among the departments under its parent; a teammate's `TeammatePlacement` holds its parent and its order among the teammates there, and a teammate with none is not in the organization: a new teammate joins none, and no target, view or role line counts it.
  The leaders are the departments' leaders and the teammates with someone under them; `managerOf` walks up from a teammate to the first teammate parent or other department leader, and `departmentOf`, the department nearest above, is what `members` and the views read until they draw the full tree, so a placed teammate in no department, under the person or under a teammate, still shows as one of your direct reports.
  A message to a department reaches its leader, then every placed teammate whose `departmentOf` is that department; to the leaders, the leaders, then each teammate directly under the person; to everyone, every placed teammate.
  `OrganizationService` keeps it in the `departments` table, one row per department with its JSON, which holds its `parent` node, and its department parent's id for the foreign key, and `teammatePlacements`, one row per placed teammate with its JSON, keyed by the teammate's id with a foreign key to `teammates`, and records each change in the change feed as `Department` or `TeammatePlacement`.
  The migration `20261123090000_TeammatePlacements` adds `teammatePlacements`, copies each row of the earlier `departmentMembers` to a placement under its department, numbered from 0 in the order the rows were added, places each teammate in no department under the person, numbered from 0 in the order the teammates were created, drops `departmentMembers`, and rewrites each department's JSON from `parentId` to `parent`.
  `OrganizationList` reads it; `DepartmentCreate` puts a department last among the departments under its parent, with its leader, if given, placed in it first in the same transaction; `DepartmentUpdate` renames it or sets or clears its leader, placing a leader from elsewhere in it last; both refuse a leader the department would sit under.
  `DepartmentMove` puts a department under another parent or at another place among its siblings, numbering both sibling lists from 0 again and refusing a parent that is the department or under it; `TeammatePlace` does the same for a teammate, or takes it out of the organization with a parent of null, and `DepartmentMemberSet` places a teammate last in a department, leaving one already there where it is, or with no department last among the teammates directly under the person; a teammate that leaves the department it leads stops leading it.
  `DepartmentDelete` puts the department's departments in its place under its parent and moves its teammates to its parent after the teammates already there; taking a teammate out does the same with what sits under it, and deleting a teammate takes it out in the deleting transaction.
  Each organization keeps its own node types (`Organization.nodeTypes`), such as Branch, Team or Department, each a `NodeType` with an id, a name no other type of the organization has, ignoring case, and an optional Material Symbols icon and color; a type only names its departments, which behave alike, and `typeOf` gives a department's type.
  A new organization has no type; `NodeTypeCreate` adds one last, `NodeTypeUpdate` renames it or sets or clears its icon or color, and `NodeTypeDelete` refuses a type a department has, saying how many have it; each saves the organization's row and records it in the change feed as `Organization`.
  `DepartmentCreate` refuses a type its organization lacks, and `DepartmentUpdate` changes a department's type when given one.
  The migration `20261126090000_NodeTypes` gives each organization with departments a Department type, id `department`, and each other organization none, and gives each department that type, in the tables and in the change feed's `Organization` and `Department` payloads.
  After each change, and after deleting a teammate that was in a department, the runtime sends the whole organization to every window as `OrganizationChanged`; these methods don't invalidate the catalog.
  The window keeps it in `OrganizationCatalog`, apart from `ChatStoreService`: it reads `OrganizationList` when the window opens and again on each resync, rather than trusting the events it may have missed, and an organization changed, added or removed while a reading is on its way keeps that change, the reading's other organizations still landing.
  The Organization view draws it as a tree: you at the root, then each top-level department, each with its leader's card under it, its other members' cards in name order off one line, and the departments under it nested the same way; a collapsed department hides its leader too and shows how many people it hides, its nested departments' people included.
  A teammate the organization holds under you reports to you directly: `OrganizationNode.treeOf` puts your direct reports, in name order, beside the top-level departments, and the Tree draws them there, never under a group of their own, so the headcount counts what the Tree shows; their details say Reports to you.
  A teammate under one of them, at any depth outside a department, is one of the root's `members`, as a department lists its members, and `OrganizationNode.managers` names the teammate it reports to, and its details say Reports to <name>, which no direct report shows.
  The teammates reporting to a node, you, a teammate or a department, are laid in a grid of `OrganizationNode.columns` columns, each card a member's width: as many as report up to four, at least four from there, and from seventeen the square root rounded up, so a large team stays near square; a node with departments under it lays its reports instead first in the row of its departments, each card as wide as a unit's.
  The Organization view is the Tree alone, with no other view to switch to; its header names the organization for screen readers only, then holds the status key on the left and, on the right, the headcount, such as 4 teammates · 3 units, Cards show, Types… while editing, and Edit structure; a department's card sums up its leader's and members' statuses, such as 2 working · 1 idle, and nothing when it has no one, beside its headcount on a filled badge.
  Cards, departments and you are drawn on `--nol-panel` in light and `--nol-raised` in dark, until the kit's `--nol-card` token from ui-components#336 is pinned.
  The Tree draws on one kit `nol-canvas` under the header, on the panel's colour with a dot grid, which fits the chart to its top; you, each department's card and each teammate's card are its items, which its map shows, since ui-components 0.0.22's canvas finds items inside child components.
  The header's Cards show menu switches the fields every card shows, and each card's own menu, "Choose what <name>'s card shows and where <name> works", switches its own, which win until "Use what every card shows" or "Clear every card's own choice", then under "<name> works in" its project, its folder's name or its own folder, moves the teammate's Organization chat into a project, in the Projects order, or a folder's project, attaches or replaces a folder, or detaches it; the card opens the chat first when it has none, and `ChatPlacement`, shared with the chat's own menu, keeps the chat's usual rules: a move of a chat whose folder holds files asks first, as does replacing a folder, and a chat in a project offers no folder; a card draws its usage as the provider's first limit with the share used, on a bar across the card filled in the muted text's colour, or the warning colour from 80% used, through the kit meter's variables, and its model with its effort.
  Each teammate's card takes the keyboard focus and is the kit's popover trigger, opening on hover and keyboard focus: `nol-teamrun-organization-details` shows the teammate's status, whom it reports to, `Organization.managerOf`: the teammate it is placed under, the nearest leader of its department or the ones above it, or you, its model, its usage, where its Organization chat works, its project, its folder's name or Its own folder, its last words, cut to three lines, and how long ago, and every reply it runs now with how long; a control inside the card taking the focus closes them, so the card's menu and fields stand alone.
  The details are a hover card, setting the kit's popover variables to 18rem, the hover surface, its border and the text's colour; they hang against the card until ui-components#359 gives the popover an offset, and the Work line waits for #333's work summary.
  Beside the canvas, behind a 1px line in the border colour, the Organization chat panel, `nol-teamrun-organization-chat`, talks to the first department until another is chosen: from the To choice, which the view projects into it as `[nolTeamrunOrganizationTo]` under its head, for a group and a teammate alike, its label above a full-width select listing all leaders and your direct reports, everyone, then your direct reports by name and the teammates under them outside a department by name, then each department, its leader and members in the tree's order (`OrganizationTargetChoice`, keyed by `OrganizationTargetKey.of`); a card's name, a button reading Message <name>; or a unit's name, a plain-text button reading Message <name> that opens the unit's group message outside editing; a press elsewhere on a card pans the canvas; below 48rem wide it goes under the canvas, behind a line along its top, the canvas then 32rem high and the panel 30rem.
  A card's current activity is the chat of its teammate's earliest running reply, with how long it has run in seconds, minutes, or hours and minutes, and how many more run, ticking each second, or Nothing running, or Stopped; its last message is the latest reply it finished, from `TeammateStatusStoreService`, in at most two lines.
  Four more fields show what the teammate works on, and the card reads each only while its field is on, and again when one of the teammate's replies ends, a chat is rewound or deleted, or a project's folder moves: its folder's Git branch, changes and upstream from the Git module; its open pull requests, with CI and review, from the GitHub module, for the newest ten of its folders; the documents it last edited, from `TeammateWorkRead`; and the pages and canvases it made or last edited, from `teamrun.pageEditedList`; each says so plainly when its module isn't installed, GitHub is signed out or a read failed.
  Edit structure switches the view into editing until Done editing; it isn't kept.
  While editing, the panel beside the canvas is the unit editor: it asks for a unit until one is chosen by its card's Edit button, pressed while that unit is chosen, or a click on its card, which is outlined in the accent colour, and `OrganizationUnitSelection`, which the view provides to every card under it, holds the choice, so collapsing or expanding a unit keeps it; Close and Done editing clear it, and a unit that leaves the organization brings the ask back.
  `nol-teamrun-organization-unit-editor` heads the unit with its type's icon and name, then names it in a Name field, chooses its Type, its Leader and whom it Reports to, and ends with Delete and its type's name, such as Delete Team; a rename that is empty, unchanged or refused puts the stored name back, as `OrganizationEdits.renameAsync` does wherever a unit is renamed.
  While editing, a department's card shows its name as text with its Edit button; a teammate's card shows its name as text, as outside editing, since a teammate is renamed in the Teammates page's teammate dialog; each member's card, not the leader's, offers Move out of unit, which places it under you after a confirm, and the department it is in; every teammate's card offers Remove from the organization, which after a confirm naming the organization, and where the teammates and units under it go when it has any, sends `TeammatePlace` with no parent: the teammate leaves every view, the Explorer and the To choices, keeps its chats, and Add teammate places it again; and Add department adds one under you.
  The Tree edits every node where it is drawn: each teammate's card, a leader's too, chooses whom it reports to, and each department chooses in the unit editor where it sits, from `OrganizationPlaceChoice`'s list of every place in the tree in its drawn order, you first, but the node itself.
  A department's leader is chosen among its leader and members in name order, then every other teammate, whom `DepartmentUpdate` places in it, in the unit editor.
  Under you, each teammate and each department, Add teammate, through `OrganizationAddTeammateDirective` (`[nolTeamrunAddTeammate]` with the parent, and `[nolTeamrunAddTeammateTitle]` for the dialog's title), opens `nol-teamrun-organization-add-teammate-dialog`: a Teammate choice of the team by name, leaving out the teammate it adds under, then New teammate…, which opens the teammate dialog and chooses the teammate it creates, or keeps the choice before when that dialog closes; Add places the chosen teammate there last, moving it from wherever it was, holding the choice, Cancel and Add while it does, and closes, or stays open saying why it couldn't; Cancel and Escape place no one.
  `nol-teamrun-organization-teammate-search`, a search over the team that lists no one until a name is typed, then the matching teammates and New teammate…, which places the chosen teammate the same way, is kept for a view that wants a search, though no view offers it now.
  The Tree draws each pair of add buttons right under its owner's card, or under a department's leader, in two equal columns, so the line from the card runs through their middle to the reports and departments below; each report in a grid's first row draws its own part of the bar, so the bar ends at the outer reports' middles and a lone report has none.
  Add department there and under you opens `nol-teamrun-organization-department-dialog`, which asks for a name and an optional leader, any teammate or New teammate…, which opens the teammate dialog and leads with the teammate it creates, and a type, the organization's first or New type…, and creates the department there with that leader and type; it can't save without a type, and with no types it opens New type on top at once.
  New type…, in that dialog and in a department's type choice, opens `nol-teamrun-organization-type-dialog`, which asks for a name, an icon from the kit's `nol-icon-chooser`, or none, and the icon's color from the kit's `nol-color-chooser`, a standard swatch, a custom color or none, and creates the type with `NodeTypeCreate`, which the choice then takes; closed without one, the choice keeps what it had.
  While editing, `nol-teamrun-organization-type-choice` chooses a department's type, sending `DepartmentUpdate` with the type, in the unit editor, at the editor's full width, and the view's Types… button opens `nol-teamrun-organization-types-dialog`, which lists the organization's types to edit in the type dialog, sending only what changed, or delete, showing the runtime's refusal of a type a department still has.
  A custom color is sent in lowercase, as the protocol's pattern takes it; `nol-teamrun-organization-type-icon` draws a type's icon in its color, a swatch through `ColorChoices.toCss`, before the department's name in the Tree, the unit editor and the Explorer, and choices name a department with its type, such as Design (Team).
  What the person reads calls a department a unit, such as Add unit and 3 teammates · 2 units, and a string bound to one department takes its type's name, such as Delete Team or Team name.
  In the Tree a teammate's or department's card can also be dragged onto another node, you included, through `OrganizationDropDirective`, which places it there last; a card let go over itself is neither marked nor moved, so a drag released where it began changes nothing.
  Every move, chosen, added or dragged, places the node last by sending `Resources.organizationLastOrder`, which the runtime takes as the end, and `OrganizationStoreService` sends each edit of an organization only once the one before it is answered, so edits made within one round trip land in the order the person made them.
  A move, chosen or dragged, that would put a node under itself is refused before it is sent, with the message the protocol's `Organization.moveError` gives, the runtime's own refusal.
  Delete and Remove ask first, through `OrganizationStoreService`'s methods, and a change that fails shows why until it is dismissed.
  The Tree shows each message moving between its writer and its receiver along its lines, unless the `teamrun.organizationMessageFlow` setting is off or the system asks for reduced motion.
  `OrganizationFlowService` follows `MessageCreated` and `MessageUpdated`, and draws a teammate's reply as a message to that teammate from whoever wrote what it answers, you or the teammate its `sender` names, then the reply back.
  A message you write is written while its reply is Pending and sent once the reply runs, a teammate's message is sent when its reply is created, and the reply is written while it runs, sent once it is Completed, and fades when it fails, is cancelled or is interrupted.
  It also follows `MessageSending` and `MessageSendAbandoned`: a teammate's send with `teamrun_send_message` is written from the call's start to the teammate of the chat it goes to, as `ChatStoreService` knows that chat's Organization teammate, is sent once the message arrives there, with no second pulse from the reply it starts, and fades when the send is abandoned.
  A send to a chat with no Organization teammate the window knows, or to the sender's own chat, shows nothing.
  `nol-teamrun-flow-overlay` colours a message, from the view's `flowColors`, by the type of the unit whose leader or member it goes to, or else the one it comes from, so a reply from a unit's member back to you takes that unit's colour, and draws any other in the accent colour; it covers the Tree's own box, with its lines and glow behind the cards and its pulse over them, so a pulse crossing a department on its route stays in sight, and draws a slow, faint flow of small pulses while a message is written, then a pulse along the line once it is sent, then a glow on the receiver's card, each step ending on its animation's end.
  While it draws a flow it measures the routes again after every render, and whenever the Tree resizes, so a route follows a card that renders late or moves while the Tree keeps its size.
  `OrganizationFlowService` also ends a sent flow once its pulse and glow would have ended, and a faded one once its fade would have, timed on `ClockService.after`, so a flow no overlay draws, with the view closed or to a node a collapsed department hides, never pulses later; it keeps at most `Resources.organizationFlowRemembered` flows.
  Its durations are local constants in `Resources` until ui-components#379 gives the kit motion tokens.
  The route comes from the nodes the Tree marks with `data-flow-node`, `data-flow-parent` and `data-flow-shape`: `TreeFlowLines` reads each node's line up to its parent, along the bar under you or a department, a department's stem or the bus over a report grid, in the Tree's unzoomed pixels.
  A line never passes behind a card, and an off-line stretch keeps half the report grid's column gap, `--nol-space-2`, from the cards it passes: a report below a grid's first row climbs the column gap on its left, or on its right in the first column, to the bus, and a department under a report grid climbs the column gap or the grid's side nearest to it, then the bus or, beside a one-column grid with no bus, inside the head's card.
  Each leaves its own card, and enters the head's, that gap inside the edge, where the pulse is drawn over the card.
  A report laid first in a row of departments climbs as a department does, over the bar its row shares.
  `FlowRoute` goes up from the writer to the first node they share and down to the receiver, leaving out any stretch it would go back along.
  `FlowFrame` turns a box into the container's unzoomed pixels.
  A card's Status is `nol-teamrun-teammate-status`, a dot three eighths of a rem across in the status's colour and its word, coloured too while waiting or stopped, from `TeammateStatusStoreService`; a Working dot fades to a quarter and back every 1.6 seconds, held still while motion is reduced; the header's status key draws the same for Working, Waiting for you, Idle and Stopped.
  Each teammate's status is derived, not reported: `TeammateStatusStoreService` reads `ChatStoreService`'s open replies in every chat, pending approvals, providers and plan usage, and gives each teammate, in this order, Waiting for you while a reply of its own awaits an approval or has an approval or question pending, open or not, such as a sub-agent's or background task's approval or an async question kept across a restart, each counted for its reply's teammate, which `ChatStoreService.waitingTeammates` learns from the open replies, a chat's history or the reply's summary, Working while one runs, Stopped while it can't run until the person fixes something, with the reason, `TeammateStopReason`, that `ChatStoreService.teammateStopReason` gives: its provider is deleted, turned off or signed out, or TeamRun lacks its kind, the rule that marks a teammate unavailable in the composer; a failed or interrupted reply doesn't stop it, Usage limit while its provider's plan is used up and Sustain is off or none of its entries whose accounts can reply, by the same check as `ChatStoreService.providerStopReason`, has room, so its next message can't run, and Idle otherwise.
  Deleting a chat drops its pending approvals.
  Its activities are its open replies, each with its chat, status and start, oldest first, dropped as each ends; each teammate's status and activities are a signal of their own that changes only when its value does, so a change renders only that teammate's card.
  Each teammate's last reply, what its card says it last said, is its latest Completed reply in any chat with text, which `TeammateLastReplyList` answers in one query: for each teammate it walks the partial index `IX_messages_teammateId_endedAt`, which `20261122090000_TeammateLastReplies` adds over the Completed provider replies with valid JSON by teammate and end time, down from the last ended, then the last saved, to the first whose text in the `messageText` index isn't blank by JavaScript's whitespace, so it reads no other message; the store reads it when it starts, on a resync and whenever a chat holding one is rewound or deleted or that reply is no longer a finished reply with text, and in between takes each reply that finishes, as `MessageUpdated` says, when it ended no earlier than the one it holds.
  A reply that finishes while the list is read is applied again once the reading lands, a chat rewound or deleted meanwhile that holds a reply of the answer makes it read again, a reading a newer one replaced is dropped, and `lastRepliesError` says why the last reading failed while the store keeps what it held; each teammate's last reply is a signal of its own like its status.
- The person can message the organization: one teammate, a department, which reaches its leader first and then its direct members, not those of the departments under it, the leaders and each teammate it holds in no department, or everyone, every teammate the organization holds, in a department or not; a teammate the organization doesn't hold is never a recipient.
  A teammate's Organization chat is a chat titled `Organization · <name>`, without a project until it is moved into one, with the teammate as its member, whose `organizationChat` holds the organization's id and the teammate's; each organization has one per teammate, found by that key, made by `OrganizationChatOpen`, which answers it without sending anything so a view can open it, and kept for the next; `ChatStoreService` finds them, in any project, by the view's organization and the teammate.
  Every message of the person to an Organization chat, from the Organization view or the chat's own composer, is a regular `MessageSend` and reaches the teammate after a line the engine reads from the organization in the send's transaction, that says the person sent it from the Organization view, its place, leading or a member of its department or in none, and who it reports to: its department's leader, or for a leader or in a department without one the nearest leader of a department above, or the person.
  `nol-teamrun-organization-chat` is the panel the Organization view shows, filling its host, for the `target` the view sets and that choosing a recipient changes, reported as `targetChange`; its head reads "Chat with" the teammate, the department, all leaders and your direct reports or everyone, with the group icon before a group.
  For one teammate it opens that teammate's Organization chat, through `OrganizationChatOpen` the first time, and shows the chat's own history and composer, the composer answering with that teammate alone: its responder fixed, with no mentions and no way to answer without it; when the chat can't be opened, why shows in its place.
  For a group it shows the composer alone, as a fan-out, without the model and mode pickers, since each recipient answers with its own settings: a send goes to each recipient's Organization chat in turn, opening it first when needed, with that recipient as its responder, and slash commands work as in a chat.
  Above its message box it says how many teammates the message goes to, "Goes to N teammates", the recipients the target reaches through the placements, and the box takes that line as its description.
  While the message is being sent or a recipient it reached has a running reply, Stop shows beside Send: the recipients not yet sent to get nothing and show Stopped, the message queued for a recipient that was already replying is removed while it still waits, so the cancel doesn't release it, then each reached recipient's running reply is cancelled, and Stop goes away so the next message can go at once.
  The fan-out's unsent text is kept in the window's own storage by `KeptComposerDraftService`, keyed by the view's organization and the recipients' ids, sorted, and cleared once sent.
  Above the composer each recipient has a row with its name and its reply's status from its own chat, Queued, Replying, Waiting for permission, Replied, Failed, Stopped or Interrupted, or No reply yet: its running reply's status, else that of the last reply that ended in it while the panel was open, else that of its latest message, read as a one-message page when the panel opens, when that message is a reply; choosing a row opens that teammate's chat in the panel.
  Why a send failed shows between the rows and the composer, and the composer, set `inPanel`, sits its card a space-3 in from the panel's sides and bottom, as the To choice does, with the count over it in the muted colour at 65% and small text.
  When a send reaches some recipients and fails for others, sending the same text with the same attachments again goes only to the ones it didn't reach; other text or other attachments go to everyone.
  An attachment is the same while it stays in the composer, by its own key there, since each send prepares it again under a new stored path.
- Message search reads the `messageText` index, which `20261108000000_MessageText` makes: an FTS5 table with the trigram tokenizer, one row per message keyed by the message's rowid, holding its Text details joined by spaces, so reasoning, tool calls and the other details are never found.
  Only a settled message has a row: triggers on `messages` add it on an insert or an update of `json` once the message isn't Pending, Running or AwaitingApproval, remove it when the message goes back to one of those or is deleted, so a deleted chat's messages leave it with them, and the migration fills it from the settled messages saved before; a row whose JSON isn't valid gets no text.
  `MessageSearch` matches the text as one quoted phrase, case-insensitively, the last saved first by the index's rowid, so it stops at the result limit, and counts at most 1,001 matches from the same index: past 1,000 the total is 1,000 and `capped`, which Noldova Desktop will show as 1,000+.
  So typing reads the index rather than every message's JSON, as `ConversationSearch`'s `LIKE` scan does.
  A message's rowid follows the order it was saved in, which is its `createdAt` order, since each insert sets `createdAt` to its own time and no migration has rebuilt `messages`; they part only for messages saved within the same moment or after the clock went back, and then the order saved wins.
  A trigram is 3 characters, which the index can't match for a shorter text, so a text of 1 or 2 characters reads the text of the last 2,000 index rows, `Resources.shortSearchRows`, with a GLOB, the last saved first, and counts at most 1,001 of them the same way.
  Each character of the GLOB is a bracket of every character up to U+1FFFF that folds with it, `Resources.foldClassOf`: a character folds to the lower case of its upper case, or to its own lower case when its upper case is more than one character, such as ς with σ and Σ, µ with μ, ß with ẞ, ǅ with Ǆ and ǆ, or ᾈ with ᾀ, so it folds as the index does.
  The table is made once, on the first short search, and over-folding, such as ı with i, only finds more, never less than a longer text.
  Among those rows it finds what a longer text finds and more; when the index has more rows, the result is `capped` with 1,000 as its total, since older ones went unread, so a longer text never counts more.
  Until Noldova Desktop shows `capped`, the window answers a capped search with at least 1,000, so the person never sees a short text count fewer than a longer one.
  The bound keeps a short text's answer in the tens of milliseconds whatever the history's size, since `ConversationsService` answers on the runtime's one event loop: about 6 ms for 2,000 rows of 360 characters and 40 ms for 2,400 characters each on Linux x64, where reading all of 200,000 rows took about 1 second.
  A search that finds fewer messages than its limit counts those it found and runs no count.
  The window sends a message search only once the one before it has answered, and drops one typed over while it waited, so a slow answer never queues others behind it.
  The index is keyed by a rowid `messages` doesn't fix, so a `VACUUM`, which may renumber those rowids, would also have to make the index again; TeamRun runs none.
- `ChatFind` reads `chats` with a `LIKE` over each title, case-insensitively for ASCII, as `ConversationSearch` does, and adds each chat's teammates' names from `chatTeammates`.
- A new chat is named after its first message, whoever sent it: while the chat still has the default title and no one chose one, `ConversationEngine` gives it the message's first characters, or its first file's name, in the send's own transaction.
  `ChatTitles` then asks a model for a better title in a background session and returns at once, so asking never holds or fails the chat's turn.
  It asks the model of the Title model setting, `teamrun.titleModel`, whose value is the provider's id and the model's, when that provider still exists, is enabled and isn't signed out; then the model answering the first message, the first mentioned teammate's when there are several, and none when no one answers.
  Each model has 60 seconds, and with both failed the first characters stay; a failure logs the provider's kind, never its text.
  So every new chat makes one more request, two when the chosen model fails, counted against that provider's plan; the title session's usage is dropped, so it shows nowhere.
  The asks aren't capped: each is one short turn, ended at 60 s, beside the chat's own first turn on a provider, so many chats opened at once at most double the turns starting.
  A title goes through `ConversationsService.giveTitleAsync`, which keeps the chat's update time and place, doesn't mark it edited, and refuses once a person chose a title, even one chosen while the ask ran: `ConversationRename` sets `titleChosen`, and a chat a caller created with a title is never asked about.
  `StateInvalidated` then tells the windows.
  A title that only repeats part of what the turn sent, as Copilot's first `session_info_update` repeats its whole prompt, with the role in it, is no title of the agent's own and is left out.
  Any other title an ACP agent gives its own session with `session_info_update` during the chat's first turn, while no model's title was applied, is the chat's title and ends any ask still running, even after every model failed; one after that turn ends or after a model's title is ignored, and ACP has no way to ask for a title, its unstable `session/set_title` only storing one on the agent.
  Deleting the chat or shutting down ends the ask, and a late answer is dropped.
  The Title model's choices are the window's: "Same as the chat", then the models of each enabled provider whose kind can name a chat (`ProviderKind.namesChats`), with a default row that stands for another listed model named as the default entry ("Default model · Opus 5.5"), loaded when Settings asks and again when a provider changes; a failed load isn't kept, and a kept list is read again once the model cache's 5 minutes pass, or once the engine restarts, when the store's resync expires every list the cache holds.

  A title session is an adapter's own turn with `TurnRequest.isolated`, run through `askIsolated`, the one entry for every isolated turn, a handover's own summary's too, in the empty folder `title-sessions` under the data directory, with nothing to resume, no TeamRun tools, no shared servers, no instructions from TeamRun and the provider's default effort, in the provider's default mode or, for an ACP agent, its isolated mode; where a provider has no title session below, it runs no summary either.
  Core's `TitleTurn` listener keeps the answer's last text and refuses every approval and question.
  What each provider allows:

  | Provider | No tools | Out of its session list |
  |---|---|---|
  | Claude | `tools: []`, `maxTurns: 1`, an empty system prompt, and none of the person's own MCP servers, even when the provider uses them. | Kept out: `persistSession: false`, and the chat closes after the turn. |
  | Codex | The thread's config turns off every tool feature in a read-only sandbox that never asks. In a signed-in run of CLI 0.165 it couldn't read a file, run a command or start a sub-agent; only its MCP resource listings remain, with no server to list, and the model can still name tools from its instructions that it doesn't have. | Kept out: `ephemeral: true`. |
  | Copilot | Its launch flags take its tools away: `--available-tools=none`, with `--no-ask-user` and `--no-custom-instructions`, on top of the `--disable-builtin-mcps` every Copilot launch has, and the `Info:` lines the first adds to its text stay out of the answer. While the person's Copilot has hooks, which flags can't turn off, there is no Copilot title session, so the chat keeps its first message's title or takes the next model's: `*.json` files in the machine's policy folder, `/etc/github-copilot/policy.d` on Linux and macOS and `GitHub\Copilot\policy.d` under `ProgramData` on Windows, which Copilot loads first and `disableAllHooks` doesn't stop; `*.json` files in `hooks` under the provider's `COPILOT_HOME`, or `~/.copilot` for the default profile; a non-empty `hooks` in its `settings.json`; or an installed plugin's `hooks.json` or `hooks/hooks.json`; and any of these that can't be read. On Windows Copilot also loads policy hooks from the subkeys of `HKLM\Software\Policies\GitHub\Copilot`, which TeamRun doesn't read: Node has no registry API, and reading it would start `reg.exe` before each title, so a machine whose policy sets hooks only there still gets a Copilot title session. TeamRun assumes the title folder, `title-sessions` in its data folder, sits in no repository: with the data folder inside one, such as a home kept as a dotfiles repository, Copilot would also load that repository's `.github/hooks` and the hooks in its `.claude/settings*.json`, which TeamRun doesn't look for. | Not allowed: ACP offers `session/list` and `session/close` and the CLI's `sessions` command only imports, so it stays in Copilot's own store, under the `title-sessions` folder and never under a project. |
  | Other ACP agents | ACP can't remove an agent's built-in tools: the session has no MCP servers, the client offers no file or terminal access, and each permission request is refused, in an empty folder. Since what needs no request still runs, the session starts in the agent's most restricted mode: fx's and Grok's Ask, Cursor's read-only Ask and Antigravity's Default; on macOS, where a Cursor title session keeps the person's home, Cursor has no title mode, so it names no chat and isn't offered as a title model, and the chat keeps its first message's title or takes the next model's; a mode the session doesn't offer fails the ask, except Antigravity's Default, its usual mode, so an Antigravity session that doesn't offer it asks in the mode it starts in. An added agent isn't asked: it has no known restricted mode and may start the person's own MCP servers. Nor is OpenCode, which runs in the person's own setup, so a title session would start their own MCP servers and plugins. Neither is Muse Code, whose modes are approval levels, none read-only. | ACP can't keep a session unlisted, so it stays in the agent's own store where the agent keeps one, under the `title-sessions` folder and never under a project. |
  | Pi | `pi --mode rpc` starts with `--no-tools`, which Pi's own CLI help gives as "Disable all tools by default (built-in and extension)", and `--no-extensions`, so none of the person's extensions and not TeamRun's approvals extension load, keeping only Pi's built-in llama.cpp provider with `--extension builtin:llama.cpp`, its one built-in extension that adds a provider in Pi 1.1.0, and with the person's MCP servers, skills, context files, prompt templates and themes off, as its model list and sign-in check start it. A title model whose provider only a person's extension adds can't be set, so there is no Pi title session and the chat takes the next model's title, as with Copilot's hooks. Checked on Pi's source at v1.1.0 and its double, not yet in a signed-in Pi. | Kept out: `--no-session` saves no session file, so the session is in none of Pi's lists, and the Pi closes after the turn. |
