- A disabled provider keeps its row, profile folder, last sign-in check and teammates; `ProviderSetEnabled` sets whether it is enabled, records the change in the change feed and publishes `ProviderUpdated`, and disabling it ends a sign-in it is running, in Core or in a terminal, with no sign-in check after.
  While it is disabled, its sign-in check, sign-in, sign-out and model catalog are refused with `Unavailable`.
  So is a send that names it as the provider, or, from a teammate, names none while it is the provider in its kind's usual folder, and a person's send to one of its teammates; a teammate's send to one of them starts no reply from that teammate.
  A read of its plan use returns nothing without asking its provider, and a wake-up its provider asks for starts no reply.
  A reply already running when its provider is disabled runs to its end, and so does a wake-up reply already waiting behind it, since that provider's turn is already running.
  Any other reply on it still waiting to start in that chat, one naming it or one with no provider while it is its kind's usual one, fails with the provider-disabled text.
  The window offers it in no teammate or composer picker, shows its teammates greyed, with "(provider disabled)" after their names, and names it with "(disabled)" on the default teammate's row and in the composer's default responder.
  The person's send to one of its teammates, or with it as the chosen provider, is refused with `Unavailable` before anything is stored, with a message naming the provider; a teammate's hand-off to one of its teammates starts no one, as for a signed-out provider.
  Enabling it brings everything back as it was.
- A reply in a project's chat whose folder is missing, is no longer a folder, or can't be read, fails before its provider starts, with a note that names the folder and says to restore it or move the chat with its menu's Move to another project…; the next reply runs once the folder is back.
  The check is an asynchronous read raced against a 3 s deadline, once for all the replies of a send, so a disconnected drive or share holds up only those replies: past the deadline, they fail with their own note, that the folder didn't answer in time and to check its drive or share is connected.
  The deadline and the listing's wait come from `FolderCheckTimings.createDefault()`, and the read and the waits from the `stat` and `NodeTimer` that the runtime passes to `FolderProbe`; a check that fails as a project opens is logged.
  While a read of a folder has passed its deadline unanswered, later checks of that folder wait on it rather than start another, so a stalled share holds one thread, not one per check.
  A chat without a project makes its own folder anew, so its reply runs; one with an attached folder checks that folder in the same way, and its notes say to attach another folder or detach it.
  `ProjectList` answers each project as a `ProjectListing`, with whether its folder is missing or unreachable; it checks every folder at once, waits up to 500 ms for the checks, and answers with each folder's last known state.
  Every check, by a turn, a listing or Open folder, publishes `ProjectFolderChanged` when it changes what Core keeps, including a check that ends after its listing answered; a check's result is kept only if no check that began after it has been kept, so an older read never overwrites a newer one.
  The Projects list shows such a project's name muted, a `folder_off` glyph after it labelled "Folder missing or unreachable", and "The folder is missing or didn't answer: <path>" as its tooltip, since the event says only whether the folder can be used; the mark follows those events, so opening a folder that is still missing keeps it.
  The CLI's commands read each listing's project and show the project as before.
- A named teammate's turn on a new session in a chat opens with who it is, "You are @Cursor, a teammate in this TeamRun chat.", then what was said before; a later turn opens with "New messages for @Cursor since your last reply:".
  The default teammate's new session in a chat with earlier messages opens with the transcript preamble instead, "This chat continues from an earlier session that is no longer available.".
  The words are the same for every provider and say who the teammate is, not that it joined: a provider's reasoning that restated the earlier "You join this chat as @Cursor" read as the person joining.
  The reply still shows the provider's reasoning as the provider writes it.
- The engine reads the module's `teamrun.chainHopLimit` setting at each teammate hand-off, so a change applies to the next one.
  It is a whole number from 0 to 100, 4 by default, and counts both the teammates a reply's mentions start and a turn's messages to other chats; a person's own messages are never limited.
  The setting is shared by every device: the runtime that applies it has no device of its own, and Noldova Desktop reads a device setting without a device as its default.
- A message that is only a `/` command, its text without its mentions starting with `/` and a name with no space or `/`, as in `/review src`, reaches the turn as `TurnRequest.command`.
  An ACP provider passes it on as the agent's own command only when the agent listed it in the session's latest `available_commands_update`: the prompt is then the command's text alone, without the role, the chat so far or TeamRun's notes.
  Each turn keeps its own lists, from every notification the turn's agent process sends, from before the turn begins, since agents send it right after `session/new` or `session/load`, and waits up to 3 seconds for the session's list: a turn starts with no list, so a resumed session's list is the one the agent sends on that load, and a process held past its turn for background work keeps sending to its own turn's lists, never to a later turn's.
  A command the agent didn't list, or one sent when it listed none in time, answers with a note naming the command and the ones the agent lists; a command sent with files answers that TeamRun passes a command on only alone; neither sends a prompt, and a new session opened for it isn't kept.
  On a new session the command goes without the chat so far, so the next turn on that session sends the whole chat once, as a new session's first prompt does, and the instruction files TeamRun passes itself, Antigravity's, come with that prompt too.
  The adapter keeps which sessions still owe that in memory only, so after the app restarts, the next turn on such a session sends its message as any resumed session's turn does, without the chat so far or those files.
  `/compact` keeps its own path, the provider's compaction.
  Codex answers a command it's known to have with a note before anything starts, as the "/ commands" table below says, and sends any other `/` word as a prompt, so "/tmp is full" still asks it to clean it.
  Pi answers its built-in commands that way too, and passes on a command sent without files that RPC `get_commands` lists, its extension commands, prompt templates and `skill:` commands: it goes to `prompt` as its text alone, without the role or the chat so far, and Pi runs it, an extension command answering `handled` at once; any other `/` word goes inside the prompt, as any message does, so "/tmp is full" still asks Pi to clean it; the next turn on a new session a command opened sends the whole chat once, kept in memory only.
  The names are each provider's own lists, kept in `Resources`: Codex's terminal app commands and its `prompts:` custom prompts, and Pi's built-in commands.
  Claude Code takes a command its session lists as the prompt's whole text, without the chat so far or TeamRun's notes, so the Agent SDK runs it as its own; its role stays in the system prompt.
  The list is the Agent SDK's initialize response, `supportedCommands()`, read when the query opens, before any message, until the session's `system/init` names its `slash_commands`; from then on it is that list, replaced by each `commands_changed`, so a new chat's first command is checked before anything runs.
  A command's aliases count as listed, and since `slash_commands` names no aliases, a name it keeps keeps the aliases the last full list gave it; a full list that comes after the session already gave one is dropped.
  Its built-in commands and bundled skills, from its commands reference, answer with a note naming the commands the session lists when the session doesn't list them, or when the list didn't come within the request timeout, and a listed command sent with files answers that TeamRun passes a command on only alone; neither sends a prompt, and a new session opened for it isn't kept.
  Any other `/` word it doesn't list goes wrapped, as before, and a new session that began with a command sends the whole chat once with its next message, as an ACP session does.
  Other providers take the message as a prompt, as before.
- Each teammate's turn carries `TEAMRUN_SENDER_TOKEN`, 32 random bytes the engine issues as the turn starts and revokes when the provider's run ends, except that a wake reply of a process that outlives its turn routes that turn's token to itself until it ends, through `WakeTurn.adoptSender`, as a later turn on such a process does through `TurnRequest.adoptSender`, and its instructions, after its role, ask it to keep work of several steps in its own plan tool (Claude's task tools, Codex's plan, an ACP agent's plan), which TeamRun shows as the reply's live plan, and never to type the plan as a checklist, then, on a provider whose own background sub-agents' results don't reach the chat after its turn, the adapter's `backgroundSubAgentsNote` asking it to wait for each one's result before it ends the turn, then end with a few lines on `send`, `list-chats` and `list-messages`, named with the product's command and the module's id, such as `teamrun teamrun`.
  A turn with TeamRun's tools gets the tools in their place, with `teamrun_send_message` for sending and answering, and no line on the command line: Codex's sandbox, in its default mode on Windows, kept the command from reaching the app, as `send` failed with "runtime-directory access was denied" and `list-chats` on the crash handler's pipe, while a Grok teammate in the same folder ran it.
  The command they name runs in the turn's shell whether the app is installed or runs from a checkout: as it activates, the part writes `command` in the module's folder, with a `sh` script named after the product's slug and, on Windows, a `.cmd` beside it, each running the runtime's own program in Node mode with Noldova Desktop's command line entry (`CliEntry.entryPath`) and `--data-dir` set to the runtime's, and each teammate's turn gets a `PATH` that leads with that folder before the `PATH` Noldova Desktop reads as the turn starts, under the spelling that environment already uses, such as Windows' `Path`.
  The `.cmd` holds no path and nothing but ASCII, since `cmd.exe` reads a batch file in the console's code page and would misread a folder with a letter outside it, such as a user folder: on Windows the turn also carries `TEAMRUN_COMMAND_PROGRAM`, `TEAMRUN_COMMAND_ENTRY` and `TEAMRUN_COMMAND_DATA_DIR`, which the `.cmd` reads, and outside a turn, without them, it refuses with a line on why.
  WSL's shells aren't covered: the `sh` script there would start the app's Windows program by its Windows path.
  An installed app's own command doesn't do: on Windows it is a `.cmd` that Bash can't find, on macOS it is on the `PATH` only when the person links it, on Linux there is none, and a development app has none at all.
  When the folder can't be written, the part logs the failure's kind, and teammates' instructions keep the plan-tool line but leave out the lines on other chats rather than name a command that fails.
  Both scripts clear `CHROME_CRASHPAD_PIPE_NAME` before they start the program: Electron's Node mode opens the crash handler pipe that variable names, which the app's own environment passes down, and in a sandboxed shell that fails and writes the failure into a `debug.log` in the turn's folder, which the chat's Changes then lists.
  Without the tools, the instructions have teammates pipe a message into `send <chatId> -`, which reads it from standard input and refuses a terminal there rather than wait, from a quoted here-document or here-string, so `cmd.exe`, which reads the `.cmd`'s `%*` again, and every other shell leave it whole; in PowerShell they first set `$OutputEncoding` to UTF-8, since Windows PowerShell pipes text to a program in ASCII and turns every other character into `?`.
  Claude and the ACP providers add the turn's variables to the process they start for the turn; Codex, whose one server serves every chat, sets them on the thread through a `shell_environment_policy.set.<name>` override when it starts or resumes the thread.
  The override names the path variable `PATH`, whatever its spelling in the environment: on Windows, Codex's sandbox adds its own process's `PATH` when the command's environment has none under that exact name, and of two spellings it keeps `PATH`, so a `Path` override would be dropped and the command not found.
  A Claude turn on the chat's open query sets its token with the SDK's `applyFlagSettings` before its message goes, so the commands it starts carry its own token; a command started earlier and still running keeps the token of its own turn, and its sends are refused once that turn ends, and a query that refuses the new token is stopped and its session resumed on a new one.
  A send that carries a token is stored as the person's kind of message with a sender, the teammate and chat of the turn that holds the token, and providers read it as `From @<name> in chat "<title>" (<id>):`, with the chat's current title, or `From @<name> in chat <id>:` once the chat is gone; a send can't name its sender any other way, so a token the engine didn't issue, or one whose turn has ended, is refused as `Unauthorized`.
  A reply that names a chat by its id shows that id as a link titled with the chat's current title, which opens the chat, when the window holds the chat; an id inside a longer token, such as a path or a longer hex string, a code span or block, a link or a web address stays as text, and so does one in a reply still streaming that is at its end, until more of the reply follows it, or after a code span, link or autolink whose closing mark hasn't arrived.
  While a reply streams, the window keeps each part's ids from its last update and looks for ids again only from the last 37 characters it had, since an id that starts there may yet change with the characters that follow it, so an update costs the text it adds, not the whole reply.
  A `teamrun-chat:` link to a chat the window doesn't hold opens nothing.
  Only what the window draws changes: the stored text, Copy and what goes back to a teammate keep the raw id.
  A send with a token that asks for one of its kind's unrestricted modes is refused as `Unauthorized` too, so a teammate can't start a turn in a mode the person never confirmed; a send it leaves to the target chat's chosen teammate runs in that chat's chosen mode.
  A teammate that drops the variable sends as the person, whose sends are refused in a chat with a running reply and start no chain.
  A chat's default teammate gets the same token and instructions, and its send has a sender with no teammate id, named as the window names its replies, by the kind's display name and its provider's label as `FormatterService.providerLine` does, such as "Codex · Work", or by the kind alone for a provider labelled as its kind or gone; the name is stored when it sends, as a teammate sender's is, and holds at most 100 characters on one line, with no control character, so a label that would break that gives the kind alone; providers read it as `From the default teammate (<provider>) in chat "<title>" (<id>):`, with no `@`, so it never reads as a teammate of that name, and the instructions say to answer it with no `--teammate`.
  A token's send never starts its sender or adds it to the target chat: its own name stays among the message's mentions, a given responder that is its sender starts no one, and when the message names no one else, the given responder answers, or the default teammate with the given settings, or else the responder chosen in the chat's composer, which the chat keeps as `responder`: a teammate, or the default teammate's kind, provider, model and effort.
  When a chosen teammate is the sender, has been deleted or has left the chat, when a chosen default teammate's kind isn't registered or its provider is gone, disabled or of another kind, when the sender is that chat's own default teammate, or when none is chosen, the message is posted and no one starts; nothing falls back to the settings of an earlier reply.
  `ConversationResponderSet` sets or clears the choice, refuses a teammate or provider that doesn't exist and a provider of another kind, and keeps the chat's update time.
  With `start`, it sets the choice only when the chat holds none and otherwise returns the chat unchanged, so a starting choice never replaces a choice that a mention or another window set while the window still held no choice.
  A choice the person didn't make, such as the composer's starting choice or a model it settles on, never replaces a teammate who is in the chat with no teammate: Core checks this in the write's transaction and returns the chat unchanged, whatever the window holds of the chat's members.
  For every choice the person didn't make, the window takes the responder the returned chat holds, and the composer shows it; a chat whose remembered kind is gone shows its teammate with the first kind, and a mode picked then is that teammate's mode in the chat.
  The composer writes it whenever the person picks a teammate, the default teammate, or the default's kind, provider, model or effort, when a send names a teammate, who then answers next, and when a chat with no choice opens, with the settings shown but never the last chat's teammate, so the runtime always holds what the composer shows; a send that names no one writes nothing, so a pick made while it is on its way stays.
  The settings a chat with no choice opens with are the default teammate chosen in Settings, which `DefaultTeammateSet` keeps in the `preferences` table and records in the change feed, refusing a provider that doesn't exist or is of another kind; until one is chosen, they are those of the last message the person sent, and changing the choice changes no chat that already has one.
  The last message's settings, without its teammate, are the composer's default choice, which `ComposerDefaultSave` keeps as the `composerDefault` row of the `preferences` table, records in the change feed and sends to every window as `ComposerDefaultUpdated`, by the same rules as the Projects view's layout: `ComposerDefaultRead` reads it when the window opens and on a resync, a send made before the first read settles shows at once and is saved once it does, and one an earlier version kept as `defaultChoice` in `teamrun.composerChoices` in local storage is saved with `onlyIfNone` once a read finds none kept, then removed from there, or dropped when it can't be read.
  The window shows the new choice at once and sends a chat's choices to Core one after another; when the last one is refused, it shows the last one Core kept.
  When a chat opens, or its kept choice changes, the composer takes the kept choice over the window's own copy in local storage, which then follows it; a change to that copy, such as one from another window, is written to the runtime in turn.
  That copy keeps each chat's choice under `chats` in `teamrun.composerChoices`.
  A copy an earlier version kept under `conversations` moves there once, when the window first reads it, the new key first when both are there, and is read where it is while local storage refuses the move.
  A token's send counts one hand-off past that turn's reply, so a chain across chats stops at the same limit, with the same note on the message; a chat whose reply is running takes it after that reply, as a hand-off waits, while a person's send there is still refused.
  A chat wake counts one more past the highest hop of the work it watched, and a sub-agent's wake one past its turn's reply, so two chats that send and wake each other stop at the same limit, with the same note on the wake's message and no turn.
  A turn a provider starts on its own goes on from the reply whose session woke and keeps that reply's hop, which the engine keeps in memory for each reply from the moment it starts, so a wake that comes while the reply's group still runs keeps it too, until its chat goes; had it begun again at 0, so a send from such a turn escaped the limit.
- A message mentions a teammate with a token, `[@<label>](mention:<teammate id>)`, whose label may be the full name or only its first words, or with `@` and a name in plain text, as older messages and teammates' replies do.
  Plain text matches the longest full name first, then a first word that begins exactly one teammate's name; a word followed by `/`, or by `.` and a letter, such as `@angular/core`, is a package or a path and names no one, and the same word after a name's first word ends the name before it, so `@Angular Bot/core` takes only `Angular`.
  The engine checks a send's mentioned ids against the text and every teammate, and a provider reads each token as `@` and the teammate's current full name.
  No mention counts inside code or after `\`.
  In what a teammate writes, its replies and its sends, a name inside quotation marks is text too, so a reply that quotes a chat's title, such as `"@Opus hi"`, hands nothing off.
  A quote opens at a quotation mark with no letter, digit or `_` right before it and closes at its closing mark on the same line with none of them right after it, so the marks in `'90s` and `didn't` close nothing.
  A teammate's reply hands off only to the teammates it addresses: a mention that starts the reply or a line, after at most three spaces, a list marker such as `-` or `1.` and emphasis such as `**`, or that follows such a mention on its line with only emphasis, spaces, a comma or "and" between them, as in `@Bob, @Carol: please check` or `- **@Bob** and @Carol`.
  A line indented four spaces or a tab, as code is, or quoted with `>`, addresses no one.
  A name inside a sentence, such as `I sent its teammate, @Bob, a message`, stays among the reply's mentions but starts no one, adds no one to the chat and counts no hand-off, and so does an addressing mention of a teammate the turn sent a message to with its token, or of the teammate whose sent message the reply answers, since those answer through `send`; a person's message, and a token's send, start every teammate they name but the sender.
  A reply the person stopped, even after its answer arrived, starts no one, and it notes each teammate it addressed, as in "Stopped before handing off to Bob."
  A hand-off to a teammate with a reply queued in the chat that hasn't started yet joins that reply.
  The teammate answers once, its prompt closes with each hand-off it joined, and its own hand-offs count from the longest chain among them.
  The queued reply always comes after the handing one, so its transcript holds the handing reply.
  A chat's replies run one at a time, apart from a wake reply the provider runs ahead, so a teammate's started prompt reply has always ended by the time another reply hands off to it.
  A teammate whose reply has ended or was cancelled, or whose queued reply is a wake reply, which follows the provider's own turn rather than a prompt, gets a new reply to the handing one, queued after the others.
  Teammates' instructions say how to address a teammate.
  A person's messages keep `@` inside quotation marks as a mention.
  The history marks in a teammate's reply or send the teammates it names outside quotation marks, whether it addresses them or not; a teammate named both inside and outside quotation marks is marked at both places, since the kit's markdown view marks a teammate's name wherever it appears.
- Every provider keeps a chat's session open after its turn, so the chat's next turn sends its prompt on it without starting the provider's program or loading the session again, which took up to 4.4 seconds a turn.
  The session closes once it has had no turn for 30 minutes (the provider timings' `idleLimit`); background work the turn left running keeps it open while the work reports at least every 4 hours (`silenceLimit`), and the 30 minutes start once the work ends.
  The next turn after a close starts the program again and resumes the session with its history, as after a restart, and quitting TeamRun closes every kept session.
  Deleting a chat, or forgetting its project, closes the sessions its turns used without waiting for the 30 minutes: the engine notes the session of each reply and of each sub-agent it starts by provider kind, and each adapter's `closeSessions` closes an idle kept session at once, and one that runs a turn or background work once that work ends.
  A background sub-agent can still run when its chat is deleted, since it holds no reply open, so the session of one that ends after the delete is closed at once.
  A failed close is logged, whether it rejects or throws, and a kind no longer registered, such as a removed agent, is skipped; the delete goes on either way.

   | Provider | What is kept | The next turn takes it when |
   |---|---|---|
   | Claude | The chat's query. | The turn resumes the same session with the same command, folder, instructions, settings, environment and servers; otherwise the kept query closes and the turn opens a new one that resumes the session. |
   | Codex | The thread's subscription on the app-server, which stays running for 30 minutes after it releases its last thread. | The turn runs on the same thread, with `turn/start` carrying the model and effort when they changed; since a resume's settings, its `cwd` among them, don't apply to a subscribed thread, a kept thread whose folder, instructions, environment, MCP servers or context window differ from the turn's, such as after an attach, a replace or a detach, is reloaded first, as for a change of tools, and a thread whose background work still runs, which isn't reloaded, gets the folder as `cwd` on `turn/start`; a change back to the default sends the default from `model/list` and `config/read`, the configured or listed default model and that model's configured or default effort; a turn after the release resumes the thread with `thread/resume`. |
   | GitHub Copilot, OpenCode, Cursor, Antigravity, Grok, fx, Muse Code, an added ACP agent | The agent's process and its session. | The turn resumes the same session with the same provider, settings, folder, approved tools, environment and session parameters, and no background work runs in it; otherwise the kept process closes, or stays with its work, and the turn starts its own process and loads the session. |
   | Pi | Pi's process in RPC mode. | The turn resumes the same session file with the same provider, mode, environment and folder; otherwise the kept process closes. |

  A kept ACP process keeps the environment and TeamRun tools token of the turn that started it, and the next turn adopts both, so its `teamrun` commands act as that turn; a kept Pi keeps its sender token the same way.
  A session has one kept ACP process: a newer one kept for it retires the older, which no turn takes and which closes once its work ends.
  Signing out of Claude closes the provider's kept queries that run no turn; one that runs a turn then stays kept after it, as every kept query does, so the chat's next turn within the idle limit runs on that query.
- Background tasks are the commands a provider runs on in the background, as a provider-neutral stream: a provider reports a task's start with its command and a way to stop it, its output and its end, and Core keeps each task, with the last 65,536 characters of its output, until the person clears it or its chat is deleted; deleting a chat stops its running tasks through their providers, best effort, and so does a task its chat reports after the delete.
  Each task is a row of `backgroundTasks`, keyed by id, with its chat, its JSON and its output in a column of its own, saved at its start and its end; a chat's rows are read without their output the first time the chat is listed or cleared, and `BackgroundTaskRead` reads one task's output, so a restart keeps the list and a chat's first list stays small.
  Nothing reruns: as TeamRun closes, once its providers have stopped, Core saves each task still running as `Stopped` with its output so far and saves nothing after that, and a row still running at the next read, after a crash, the host's stop deadline, an operating system restart, a logoff or a forced kill, reads as `Stopped` at its own start, with no output after its start, and the window shows no duration for it.
  A failed save or delete is logged and memory stays the record for this run, so a failed save never breaks a turn, and a clear that fails leaves every row, which returns after a restart.
  A task can run a sub-agent: its `subAgentId` is the sub-agent's `toolUseId`, so stopping the task stops that sub-agent.
  A task can have no stop of its own: Core keeps its stop but doesn't list it, so neither its sub-agent's card nor the Background tasks view shows a Stop for it, and only the reply's Stop ends it.
  A reply whose turn has ended keeps its Stop while any of its sub-agents still runs: that Stop cancels its open approvals and refuses later ones, stops the reply's running sub-agent tasks, and shows Stopped on each running sub-agent with no task, as no provider would end it.
  A stop the provider refuses leaves that sub-agent running and the reply's Stop in place, shows the refusal's reason as a failed Stop does, and a second Stop tries it again.
  Codex's sub-agents are tasks with no stop of their own, whose stop interrupts the sub-agent's own turn on its thread when the reply stops, then ends the commands the sub-agent left running through `thread/backgroundTerminals/clean` on that thread, whether or not the interrupt succeeded, since Codex's interrupt leaves a thread's background terminals running; the reply's Stop, and shutdown, interrupt every running sub-agent's turn at once, wait for those interrupts and the main thread's own to settle, and then clean every sub-agent's thread together, a running one's and one whose turn has already ended.
  Each Codex thread has its own terminals, so a clean ends only that sub-agent's commands, and on Windows Codex's command runner ends each command's whole tree through its job object.
  With Codex 0.165.0 on Linux, no app-server call ends the command a sub-agent runs in its sandbox: the clean ends the sandbox's outer processes, but bubblewrap runs the command in a session of its own that Codex's kill misses, and the parent's wait never learns the sub-agent ended, so a Stop of its own would show it stopped while it ran on ([openai/codex#57863](https://github.com/openai/codex/issues/57863)).
  So on Linux, when the app-server has a process id, the adapter lists the app-server's processes with their starts before the clean and takes as the sweep's roots the app-server's children whose `/proc/<pid>/environ` names one of the cleaned threads in `CODEX_THREAD_ID`.
  It reads only that variable and keeps nothing else of the file.
  Codex gives a terminal no process id (`osPid` is always null), so this variable is what ties a process to its thread.
  Codex 0.165.0 on Linux sets it in each terminal, sandboxed or not and whatever the person's `shell_environment_policy`, to the thread that started it, a sub-agent's own thread for a sub-agent's terminal (checked live); the app-server's own helper process has none, so it is never a root, and a Codex that doesn't set it sweeps nothing.
  After the clean it lists again every 100 ms until those roots are gone or the stop grace has passed, each listing bounded by the usual listing time limit.
  It then ends each process of the first listing that ran under one of those gone roots, still has the same start and no longer runs under the app-server, with what it started, as Grok's task Stop ends a tree, naming them in the log; this end-to-end sweep is unchecked live on Linux.
  A root of the main thread, another sub-agent or another chat doesn't count, even if it ends during the wait, and what it left running in the background, such as `nohup npm run dev &`, is spared.
  The reply's Stop sweeps once for all the threads it cleans, after the main thread's interrupt, with the interrupt time limit.
  A listing that fails during the wait is skipped, and only a missing or different app-server ends the wait early.
  A process id that isn't the running app-server's any more, since the app-server exited, ends nothing.
  Shutdown stops the main thread's tasks first, then the sub-agents with the interrupt time limit, and it and the release of a retired app-server wait for the stops and sweeps already running before they stop that app-server, so quitting doesn't leave a waiting sweep's command running.
  A command the sub-agent starts between the first listing and the clean isn't in that listing and would run on, but the interrupt has ended the sub-agent's turn by then, so none starts; the sweep lists again before the clean only if the live check shows one.
  A command that has already left the app-server's tree when the first listing runs, such as `setsid nohup sleep 315 &`, isn't in that listing either, so Stop doesn't end it on Linux, even though its environment names its thread.
  A sub-agent's own Stop comes back once Codex fixes it.
  Claude's are Claude Code's `local_agent` tasks, foreground or background, whose stop is the SDK's `stopTask`.
  A Grok sub-agent in the background is a task under its `subagent_id`, whose stop is Grok's `x.ai/subagent/cancel`: when Grok answers that it cancelled a running one, its `subagent_finished` ends it Stopped, and when Grok has none running by that id, TeamRun ends it Stopped itself; Copilot has no stop for one sub-agent, so its sub-agents have no task.
  What a kept Grok sub-agent starts runs under Grok's process, so it ends with Grok's tree when Grok closes after the sub-agent's Stop or its chat's deletion, at sign-out and quit, and when Grok exits by itself, also a process that ignores `SIGTERM`.
  Core keeps which teammate's reply started each task: removing a teammate from a chat stops that teammate's running tasks in that chat, and deleting a teammate stops them in every chat, each through its provider and without waiting, so each shows Stopped once its provider reports the end; a stop the provider refuses leaves its task running, and the default teammate's tasks stay as they are.
  Each provider then lets go of what followed those tasks once none runs, as when they end on their own: Claude closes the chat's query after the wake grace, Codex releases the thread, and Grok's process closes.
  `BackgroundTaskList` lists a page of a chat's tasks, since one answer is limited to 16 MiB and a chat keeps every task: every running task on the first page, then the finished commands, last ended first, at most `limit` of them, up to 500, after the one `afterTaskId` names, with how many finished commands the chat holds.
  Each listed task leaves its output out and carries at most the first 4,096 characters of its command, leading spaces left out and never half of a surrogate pair, with `commandCut` set when it was cut, so a page of 500 stays under a few megabytes; a finished sub-agent isn't listed, since only the Sub-agents view shows one, and only while it runs.
  A finished task moves only to the front when it ends, so the task a page starts after stays a stable cursor; once it is cleared the next page is empty.
  `BackgroundTaskRead` gives one task with its whole command and its output, `BackgroundTaskClearFinished` forgets its finished ones and publishes `BackgroundTasksCleared` when it forgot any, naming the chat, the time of the clear and how many it forgot rather than their ids, so the answer and the event stay within one frame however many tasks a clear removes, and so a task its provider reports again stays forgotten, and `BackgroundTaskStop` stops one through its provider, waiting up to ten seconds for the provider to report the end; a refused stop answers `ProviderError` with the provider's reason.
  A stop of a task that has already ended answers with it as it ended, and a task that ends and is cleared while its stop waits answers `NotFound`; the window takes a stop's answer only for a task it still lists.
  Claude's tasks come from Claude Code's `task_started`, `task_updated` and `task_notification` messages, for its shell tasks only: a command run in the background shows from its start, and one moved to the background from then; Claude Code sends no output while it runs, so TeamRun follows the output file the command's tool result names (`Output is being written to: …`), reading what is new every 250 ms by default (the provider timings' `taskOutputInterval`), at most the last 64 KiB of it at first and four times that once anything was sent, decoded as the end-of-file read decodes it, with UTF-16 runs found wherever they start and a run a read ends inside held back until the next read (a read that finds nothing new sends a held run of four characters or more and keeps a shorter one, a lone byte order mark or a NUL at the end, and the end of a sent run, with its mark when it is short, is read again with what follows), and from the file's start again when it is truncated or replaced, keeping what was already sent, until the task ends, is stopped, or its chat or Claude Code closes, when the rest of the file and what was held back are sent; a task whose tool result names no file, or names another file than the one `task_notification` names, shows the end of the file `task_notification` names when it ends.
  Stop is the SDK's `stopTask`.
  A chat's query takes streaming input and stays open only while a turn runs or background work holds it, as below: a turn while it is open sends its message on it, and `perTaskStopAffordance` makes an interrupt stop the turn but not its tasks; otherwise the turn starts a new query that resumes the session.
  A stopped reply's interrupt goes to Claude Code once Claude Code runs that reply, not while it runs a turn of its own ahead of it; when the reply doesn't end within the abort grace after it, the adapter ends the query, and with it every task of the chat.
  The failure Claude Code reports for an interrupted turn, such as `error_during_execution`, ends it as interrupted with no note, since the reply's status already says it stopped.
  The query is kept once no turn waits for its reply and no task runs, counting the tasks Claude Code's `background_tasks_changed` lists but its ambient ones, and closes after 30 minutes of that (`idleLimit`); after a task ended, it first waits up to the wake grace, a minute, for the turn the end may start.
  A query whose last turn wrote to Claude's one-hour prompt cache, whatever the account, as Claude Code does on a subscription within its plan's usage, and left a context of 150,000 tokens or more waits instead to compact while idle, unless the person turned off `teamrun.idleCompaction`, Compact idle Claude chats, on by default, which the engine reads for each turn and again when the compaction comes.
  54 minutes after that turn's result, inside the cache's hour, the adapter asks the turn's listener for a wake, and sends Claude Code's `/compact` in the same live query as that wake's turn, so the summary reads the chat from the cache and the next message starts from the summary rather than writing the whole chat to the cache again; it never resumes an expired session to compact it.
  The engine refuses that wake when the setting is off, the chat was rewound since (its session is reset), or a reply, a group, a question answer or a background task of the chat runs, and the query then closes; a turn before the deadline waits again from its own result, a chat kept busy by its tasks past the deadline closes after the idle limit without compacting, and a `/clear` leaves too small a context to compact.
  The other providers keep their cache longer or charge nothing to write it, so they never compact while idle: OpenAI keeps a prompt's cache up to 24 hours at the price of an in-memory one, and Gemini and Grok charge no cache write.
  A query held open only by its tasks, sub-agents or those listed tasks stays open while Claude Code sends a message or a followed task writes output at least every 4 hours (the provider timings' `silenceLimit`); after 4 hours of neither, its work is stopped as shutdown stops it and the query closes.
  A turn on the chat waits for a query that is closing so to close, then resumes the session on a new query, as after a restart, with the chat's history and settings.
  A turn with other settings than the open query's, such as another model, effort, provider, permission mode or instructions, stops the query and its tasks, as shutdown does for every open query at once, waiting for those stops up to the interrupt timeout and then for the query's interrupt up to the interrupt timeout again before it closes the query, and resumes the session on a new query; a task that ends while that query stops starts no wake turn on it.
  Each reply frame goes to the turn whose message's uuid it carries, `user_message_uuid`; a frame that names no turn waiting for a reply, or none once Claude Code has named one, belongs to a turn Claude Code started itself.
  The sender token and the turn that new tasks belong to follow the turn Claude Code runs: each turn's reply sets its own again when it begins after one Claude Code started itself.
  A message sent while Claude Code runs a turn of its own waits until that turn's result, since Claude Code would fold it into that turn; a stop while it waits ends its reply without sending it.
  A message that goes just as a task ends can still be folded into the turn Claude Code starts for that end, and then no frame names it: the adapter sets `CLAUDE_CODE_EMIT_SESSION_STATE_EVENTS`, and when Claude Code goes idle after starting a turn since the message went, with the message's reply still open, that reply ends holding only a note saying Claude Code answered it inside its own turn: completed, or interrupted when the person stopped it before then.
  That Stop interrupts nothing and leaves Claude Code's own turn, its wake reply and any later wake running, since until idle the message may still get a turn of its own after them, which the Stop interrupts as it begins; the wake reply has its own Stop.
- A provider may start a turn on its own, as Claude Code and Grok do when a background task ends: the turn shows as a new reply from the same teammate, or the same default teammate, in reply to the reply whose session woke, with that reply's requested settings and provider but the mode the chat holds when the wake starts (`WakeTurn.mode`): the teammate's `ChatMember.mode` in that chat, or for the default teammate the chat's responder mode, unless the responder is of another provider kind, when the reply's mode stays.
  Claude Code switches the query to that mode, Codex runs the wake with that mode's sandbox policy, or the plan mode's read-only one when the member plans first, and Grok, the one ACP agent whose wakes TeamRun follows, which starts the wake turn itself, gets `session/set_mode` for that mode, Default when the chat holds none, as the wake's first update arrives, sent without waiting for its answer, since the client handles an answer only after the update before it; the woken reply ends only once that answer, or the request's timeout, has come, and Grok counts as busy until then, so it isn't closed while the switch is pending.
  Nothing shows that the switch reaches a turn Grok is already running, so when the chat's mode differs from the one the session is in, which the adapter keeps for each Grok process from the last turn's mode, the Default Grok leaves an approved plan for, or the chat's mode Grok then accepted, or the last wake's switch that Grok accepted, until the process closes, the wake notes that the steps Grok took before the switch may have run in that earlier mode.
  Always approve is Grok's launch argument, which a running Grok keeps, so a wake in another mode on a Grok started that way notes that Grok still doesn't ask before its tools until its next reply; when Grok refuses the switch, even after the wake's last update, the wake notes that it runs in the mode it had, and a note the reply can't take is logged.
  The reply opens with a note naming why it started, "Started when the background task `npm test` ended.", or "Started when a background task ended." when the provider names no task, whose payload holds `source: "wake"`, the task's id and its command, or "Compacted while idle, before the prompt cache expired, so the next message starts from a summary." with `idleCompaction: true` for Claude's idle compaction; the window shows it as the reply's first line, above its Activity, in the hand-off note's muted style, with the command in the code font.
  A provider that can't resume a chat's native session and starts a new one says so in a note whose payload holds `source: "resume"`, "The native session could not be resumed, so a fresh one was started:" and the cause; the window shows it as a note about the chat, the reply's first line after any wake note, above its Activity, in the same muted style, never as a step.
  Claude's adapter names the task only when one task ended since Claude Code's last result: Claude Code answers a task that ends during a turn inside that turn, so a turn it starts later names none, nor does one that follows several ends.
  It runs as a reply does: it gets its own sender token, asks for approvals on itself, and hands off to the teammates it addresses, as a reply that starts the chain.
  While a reply of that chat runs, such as the person's own, the new reply waits as a pending reply after it; what the provider reports for it meanwhile is kept and shows once that reply ends.
  When the provider runs the turn ahead of a reply that is still waiting for its own, as Claude Code does with a message already on its query, the new reply shows and runs at once, with its approvals, and its hand-offs follow once that reply ends.
  A wake that comes while the runtime stops starts no turn: a reply it stored as the runtime began to stop ends Interrupted, and a waiting one is cancelled with its group; a wake for a teammate that has been deleted or is no longer in the chat, or for a chat that is gone, starts no reply.
  The provider then ends the turn it started: Claude interrupts Claude Code's query, follows no task that turn starts and denies its tools, and Grok cancels its prompt; Codex starts no turn of its own when a task ends.
  A task a reply reports after its teammate was removed from the chat or deleted, as Codex's listing can once the reply has ended, is stopped at once and never listed, as one reported for a deleted chat is.
  Codex's tasks are the thread's background terminals: a command becomes a task, with its output so far, as soon as Codex reports the model writing to it or polling it (`item/commandExecution/terminalInteraction`), which happens only after the command has left its first wait, or when Codex reports the thread's token usage (`thread/tokenUsage/updated`), which it does only once every tool call of the model's round has returned, so a command still running then has left its first wait, apart from the token usage Codex sends when the person stops the turn, which promotes nothing; one still running when the turn moves on to an item other than a command, or when the turn ends, becomes a task once `thread/backgroundTerminals/list` lists it; it ends when Codex completes its item; Stop is `thread/backgroundTerminals/terminate`.
  The adapter keeps its subscription to the thread until 30 minutes have passed since the end of its last turn (`idleLimit`) and none of its turns and tasks runs, and only then unsubscribes; unlike the other providers' limit, Codex's starts at the turn's end even while its tasks run.
  An app-server it retires after a Codex CLI update runs on until then, and each app-server keeps its own count per thread.
  A thread held only by its tasks or sub-agents stays subscribed while the app-server sends a notification for the thread or one of its sub-agents' threads at least every 4 hours (`silenceLimit`), and the limit waits while a later turn runs on the thread; after 4 hours of none, its tasks are stopped through `thread/backgroundTerminals/terminate` and its sub-agents as Stop stops them, and the thread is released once they end.
  The adapter keeps one app-server per provider while a turn, a held thread or a request uses it, and for 30 minutes after the last ends (the provider timings' `idleLimit`), then stops it; the next turn starts a new app-server and resumes its thread there, as after a restart.
  At shutdown, the adapter waits for a listing of the terminals still running, then stops each running task through `thread/backgroundTerminals/terminate`, before it stops the app-servers.
  Grok's tasks come from its own events: `x.ai/task_backgrounded` starts one, with its command, and the updates of the tool call it names that carry no status are its output, each holding all of it so far; `x.ai/task_completed` ends it, Stopped when Grok killed it, Completed for exit code 0 and Failed otherwise, with the rest of the output its snapshot holds.
  Grok offers its client no way to stop a task, so Stop ends the task's process tree, unchecked live: only while Grok's last `background_tasks` lists the task as running and once the tool call's `BackgroundTaskStarted` output named its `pid`.
  It lists the processes with their parents and starts, through `ps` or, on Windows, one `Get-CimInstance`, and first confirms that the pid is still the task's: its process started no later than when TeamRun learned the pid, and it runs under the Grok process TeamRun started, each parent along the way having started no later than its child, so a pid or a parent id the system has since given to a later process doesn't count; otherwise it refuses with the reason shown and ends nothing, unless TeamRun is already stopping Grok, whose stop ends the task's tree.
  A process counts as the tree's only through a parent that started no later than it, and not through a parent id the listing shows another process holding now.
  On Linux and macOS it then holds the tree still with `SIGSTOP`, listing again until no new process shows under it, at most ten listings, so a parent such as a shell running `a; b` can't start its next process meanwhile; then it sends `SIGTERM` and `SIGCONT` to each process it stopped, waits until those have ended or the stop grace has passed, holds what is left still the same way, new children included, and sends `SIGKILL` to each.
  On Windows it ends the tree in rounds, at most ten: each is one PowerShell script that opens each listed process by its id, ends it through that handle, which keeps the id from being reused meanwhile, only when it started when the listing said, parents first, waits for each to exit, all within two seconds, and lists again for the children that started since; a process the listing still shows is ended again in the next round, so one whose end failed isn't left running after one try.
  The stop reports the tree ended once its rounds are done, even when a process still shows after the tenth or a round's script fails, since a caller has nothing more to try then, and writes to the provider's log which processes still showed or which round's script failed (#344).
  Its listings and rounds on Windows run in one PowerShell that TeamRun keeps open between them and closes after 30 idle seconds, since `Get-CimInstance` answers there in tens of milliseconds where a new PowerShell takes a few hundred to start and list; a script that runs out of time ends it, and the next one starts another.
  A script gets 5 seconds there, at most half of the listing's time; one that fails or runs out of time there runs once more in a new PowerShell with the rest of that time, and the rest of that stop's scripts run in new PowerShells too, so a stalled PowerShell neither leaves the task's tree running nor holds the stop for the listing's whole time.
  The task shows Stopped when Grok's `task_completed` follows TeamRun's stop, whatever its `explicitly_killed` says; at shutdown, the adapter stops each running task, waiting for those stops at most the interrupt timeout, before it stops Grok.
  Stopping an ACP agent's process once TeamRun has sent it a prompt or opened a session with MCP servers, whether its turn closes it, it ignores a cancel or TeamRun shuts down, ends what it started too, so every agent and platform behave alike; a sign-in, a sign-in check, a model list or a probe does neither and starts no tools, so its stop lists no processes.
  Outside Windows the agent starts in a process group of its own; before its input closes, every process under it ends as Stop ends a task's tree, and once it has exited, also when it exited first, `SIGKILL` goes to every process still in its group that started since it, as `ps` tells a start up to two seconds early, which takes a child it started meanwhile or one the system gave another parent; the system gives no process the group's id while the group has a process, so a later process with the agent's id has none in it; then it waits, at most the stop grace, until those have ended, since a killed process ends only once the system next runs it.
  On Windows, once it has exited, every process listed under its id that started between its start and its exit ends, with what those started, in rounds, so a stop lists once there and the child of a later process given the agent's id stays.
  Both go through the adapter's process tree, the one Stop uses for a task, and each waits at most the kill wait, a listing that fails counting as nothing to end.
  When the agent exits by itself, the client ends what it left before it reports the exit, and a stop that follows waits for that instead of listing again.
  Only then are its running tasks shown Ended; a process that leaves the agent's group and parentage, such as a daemon, is beyond this.
  A stopped ACP turn that hasn't settled within `ProviderTimings.stopReportWait`, a minute by default, past the interrupt grace and the agent's stop, writes one line to the adapter's session log, `Resources.formatTurnStopStuck`: the step it still waits on, such as the prompt's answer, the turn's finish or the agent's release, and each sub-agent's state at the Stop, its status, whether it ran in the background and whether the turn kept it; the line only names the step and settles nothing.
  The adapter keeps Grok's process open after a turn while a task that turn started runs, and after the task ends while Grok says, through `will_wake`, that it starts a turn for that end, up to the wake grace; that turn, whose prompt id is `task-completed-` and the task's id, shows as a reply started when the task ended, a Stop on it cancels Grok's prompt, and the process closes once no task of it runs and no turn runs on it, its wake turn included.
  The open process keeps the environment of the turn that started it, so the adapter routes that turn's sender token to the wake's reply while it runs: a `teamrun` command from the wake turn, or from a kept sub-agent's shell, acts as that reply, and between wake turns the token is refused as a turn that ended.
  A turn while a task runs starts its own process, while the open one follows its tasks; once no task runs, the process is kept as every ACP agent's is between turns.
  A request on that open process while no wake turn runs, such as a permission or a question, is asked in the reply whose turn started the task while that reply keeps its approvals open, and is answered `cancelled` once it closes them; a routed call to a TeamRun tool after that turn is always answered `cancelled`, since its tools have ended.
  A request of a background sub-agent the process keeps running, whose tool call names its session as `x.ai/requestingSessionId`, is asked in the reply that started the sub-agent in the same way; any other sub-agent's request after its turn is answered `cancelled`, since the turn's end showed its row Stopped.
  Copilot, Antigravity and Muse Code report no tasks: ACP names no background command TeamRun can follow, and TeamRun doesn't offer them ACP's client terminals.
- An added ACP agent gets ACP's client terminals (`clientCapabilities.terminal`), through `AcpTerminals`: TeamRun runs each command the agent asks for with `terminal/create` itself, in the chat's folder or a `cwd` inside it, and refuses any other folder with the reason.
  On Windows, which would look for a program named without a folder in the folder it starts in before `PATH`, TeamRun finds such a command itself, as the shared MCP launcher does, only in the absolute folders the turn's `PATH` lists, with `PATHEXT`, never in the chat's folder or through the agent's own `env`, runs a batch file it finds through `COMSPEC`, and refuses a command `PATH` doesn't hold with the reason.
  The command gets the turn's environment and the agent's `env`, its output and error output go into one buffer that keeps the last `outputByteLimit` bytes, at most 1 MiB, at a character's start, and `terminal/output`, `terminal/wait_for_exit`, `terminal/kill` and `terminal/release` answer as ACP says, a kill or a release ending the command's process tree.
  Each command starts in its own process group, so once its first process has exited, a kill ends the processes it left by that group, or on Windows by their parent and start time, as an agent's leftovers are ended.
  A null `args`, `env`, `cwd` or `outputByteLimit` counts as absent, and a command that can't start exits with a null exit code, as ACP's exit codes are unsigned.
  A terminal still running when a tool call that shows it completes or fails, or when the turn ends, becomes a task: its output so far and from then on is the task's, its Stop ends the command as a kill does, and its end, once its output has closed, ends it after its last output, Stopped when TeamRun or the agent stopped it, Completed for exit code 0 and Failed otherwise.
  The agent's process stays open while one of its commands runs, so it can still read or release it, and its stop or exit ends them; a turn that starts meanwhile starts its own process, as with Grok's tasks.
  A terminal request TeamRun refuses is answered with its reason and the code `-32602`, where any other refused request still gets `-32601`.
- TeamRun wakes a chat itself, the same way for every provider, when work the chat's turn left running elsewhere ends: the replies a `teamrun_send_message` with `wake_when_done` started, one of TeamRun's own sub-agents still running when its turn ended, or a command it started with `teamrun_run_in_background`.
  The engine keeps each wake as a row of `chatWakes`, keyed by id, with the woken chat, its state and its JSON: the kind, the reply that asked, the watched chat and the ids it watches, the text and the hop.
  A send's wake waits for the target chat's whole group, its hand-offs included, and is ready once that group ends; its text names the chat, quotes the last text of each reply the send started, cut to 2,000 characters, says how a cancelled, failed or interrupted one ended, names the hand-off replies that also ended there, with their ids, and says to read a message whole with `teamrun_list_messages`.
  A send that starts no reply, because the chain stopped or no one answers, records no wake, and its result's `wakeId` is absent.
  A ready wake goes to its chat as a sending turn's message does, once the chat has no open reply, no group and no question answer waiting, so it waits while the chat is busy: a User message with the first watched reply's teammate, or its default teammate by its provider's name, from the watched chat as its sender, whose Text detail's payload holds `source: "chatWake"` and the `wakeId`, and one new reply for the turn that asked, its teammate or default teammate with that reply's requested settings and provider and the mode the chat holds when the wake starts, as a provider's own wake has.
  The payload also holds what the window shows: `wakeChatId`, the chat the work went to, and `wakeReplies`, each watched reply that still exists with its teammate's `name`, its `status` and its quoted `text` or null, and `wakeReplyId` once the wake starts a turn; the wake keeps this view from the moment it is ready, so a chat deleted while the wake waits for the asking chat to be idle still shows the replies that came back, as received from a deleted chat, and its text adds that the chat's messages can no longer be read; that reply then opens with a Note detail, `Received a message from "<chat>".`, whose payload holds `source: "wakeReceived"` with the same `wakeChatId` and `wakeReplies`.
  A wake that brought nothing back holds no replies and `wakeEnd`: `lost` when TeamRun restarted before the work ended, `chatDeleted` when that chat was deleted first.
  The window shows a chat's wake as a closed row, `Received a message from <chat> ›`, with the chat's name opening that chat and the replies' names and texts below it once opened: at the top of the reply it started, where the wake's own message is hidden, or as a row of its own when it started no turn, with the wake's notes, such as why it started none, below it; a wake that brought nothing back reads `No reply came back from <chat> ›` and says why once opened, in a region named `Why no reply came back` where one with replies is `The message received`, and a wake stored before the window's view, with no `wakeChatId`, shows as a row naming its sender's chat, never its text, read as lost or deleted by its text's opening, `ProtocolResources.wakeLostOpening` or `wakeChatDeletedOpening`, which core's texts begin with; what the teammate reads is the wake's text, which doesn't change.
  A sub-agent's wake comes from TeamRun, in the sub-agent's own chat, with its description, id, how it ended and its summary, cut to 2,000 characters, and its prompt begins `From TeamRun: `; a sub-agent that ends during its turn, or that was stopped, wakes no one, since the turn or the person who stopped it already knows.
  A command's wake comes from TeamRun too, with its description, its task id, its exit code, the signal that ended it or why it couldn't run, and its last 20 lines of output, cut to 2,000 characters; a command that ended during its turn wakes the chat once the turn ends, since the turn has no way to wait for it, and one that was stopped wakes no one.
  The payload of a sub-agent's or a command's wake, and of the `wakeReceived` note that opens its reply, `Woken when a sub-agent or background command of this chat ended.`, holds `wakeTask` instead of a watched chat: its `kind`, `subAgent` or `command`, its `name`, its `ending`, such as `ended as completed` or `exited with code 1`, and its `text`, the summary or the output's last lines as the wake quotes them, or null; the wake's row keeps this view from its creation, with no ending or text until the work ends, so one TeamRun restarted before holds `wakeEnd: lost` too.
  The window shows it as a closed row naming the work and how it ended, such as `Sub-agent "Search" ended as completed ›`, opening to that text, a command's in the code font, or to why none came, `Background command "Build" was stopped` or `Sub-agent "Plan" didn't come back` after a restart; its opened region is named for what it holds, `The sub-agent's summary`, `The command's last lines` or `Why it didn't end`, and a long name wraps within the row; one stored before the view, whose sender is its own chat, reads `A background command of this chat was stopped` or `A sub-agent of this chat didn't come back` when its text begins with core's lost opening, `ProtocolResources.wakeCommandLostOpening` or `wakeSubAgentLostOpening`, and `A sub-agent or background command of this chat ended` otherwise, never its text or IDs.
  When the reply that asked is gone, its teammate was deleted or left the chat, or its provider is off, signed out or refuses the turn, the message comes with a note saying the chat wasn't woken and why, with the provider's own reason when it gives one, and no turn starts; at the hop limit it gets the chain-stopped note instead.
  Only the provider's refusal gives that note: a read for the turn that fails, such as one of the chat or its member, fails the delivery with its own error, so nothing is stored, the wake stays ready, and the log names the read's failure.
  A ready wake survives a restart and goes once the engine has reconciled; a waiting one can't end, since the restart interrupts its replies and stops its sub-agents and commands, so it becomes a message saying TeamRun restarted before that work ended and no turn starts.
  A chat's delete deletes its own wakes; each other chat's waiting wake that watched it becomes a message saying that chat was deleted before its result came back, with no turn, and a restart does the same for one still waiting; a ready one, whose replies came back, is delivered as usual.
  A row the engine can't read, such as one of a kind a later version added, is skipped and named once in the runtime's log, and the other wakes go on; a wake that fails to settle or deliver keeps its state, and the log names its chat and the failure's kind.
  A send that fails after it stored its replies fails them, and its wake goes with them as failed.
  A wake whose reply is stored as TeamRun starts to quit starts no turn, whichever kind it is: a chat's, a sub-agent's or a command's, which `deliverWakeAsync` sends, or a turn the provider starts itself, as when a background task ends or for idle compaction, which `startWakeAsync` starts; its reply ends Interrupted, as a restart ends an open reply.
- A person's message sent while the chat's reply runs waits in Core, the same way for every provider: `WaitingMessageAdd` takes the send `MessageSend` would, without a sender token, and how it goes, and refuses one whose mentions aren't those of its text as `Conflict`, as an edit is.
  Queue waits and goes as the next turn once the chat's replies end; Stop and send stops the chat's running replies, as Stop does, and goes first; Steer goes into the running reply of the teammate it names, or of any of the chat's running replies when it names none, once that reply's turn offers a steer; until then, and when the provider refuses it, it waits as Queue.
  An adapter offers a steer through `ITurnListener.onSteerable` once its turn can take a message and withdraws it when the turn ends, and the engine steers one message of a chat at a time.
  A steered message leaves the list and is stored in the chat as the person's, its files kept with the chat's messages and its text detail naming the reply it went into (`steeredInto`); the next turn of that reply's session leaves it out of its context, since the provider already has it.
  The history shows it inside that reply, not as its own row: as the person's message, before the first of the reply's parts that started after it was stored, or at the reply's end, live and after the chat opens again (`ChatStoreService.steersOf`); a steer still waiting stays in the list, and one that fell back to Queue is its own turn.
  It is drawn as the person's own row would be, with its image previews, Copy and Rewind, and a search hit or a focus on it scrolls to the reply and flashes it there.
  Codex takes it with `turn/steer`, Pi with its `steer` command and Grok with `x.ai/interject`, each with its images; fx takes its text as a `session/prompt` marked `_meta.fx.steer`, so one with an image waits, not checked live since fx's gateway refuses every signed-in turn; Claude Code answers one only after the reply it was sent into, in a turn TeamRun doesn't show, and the other ACP providers have no way to take one, so on those it waits.
  fx answers a steer at its turn's end, so the ACP adapter waits for the answer before it closes the agent, for at most its request timeout (120 s); a steer still unanswered then is cut by the close, counts as refused and waits as a queued message, so fx may get it twice.
  Seen live, Codex 0.166.0 took a steer into the reply it was writing, and Grok 1.0.70 queued one and read it at its next safe point, which in a reply without tool calls is the reply's end; Pi 1.5.0 did the same as Grok.
  The engine keeps each waiting message as a row of `waitingMessages`, keyed by id, with its chat and its JSON: the send, how it goes, when it was added and, once its send failed, why.
  They go one at a time through `MessageSend` once the chat has no running group, open reply, unsent answer or steer still waiting for its answer, after any answers to its questions and before its ready wakes: Stop and send ones first, then the others in the order they were added; one added to an idle chat goes at once.
  `WaitingMessageList` lists them in that order, `WaitingMessageEdit` replaces one's send in its place, whose mentions must be those of its new text, as for `MessageSend`: the engine resolves none and refuses an edit whose mentions aren't as `Conflict`, keeping the message as it was; it clears the message's failure, `WaitingMessageRemove` removes one, and `WaitingMessageSendNow` makes one Stop and send; each change publishes `WaitingMessagesChanged` with the chat's whole list.
  A send that fails keeps its message with the reason, the refusal's own text or "It couldn't be sent." for any other failure, and the next one goes; the log names the chat and the failure's kind.
  When a chat's work ends, its answers, its waiting messages and the wakes watching it each go on their own: a step that fails, such as a database error reading the chat, is logged with the chat and the failure's kind, and the next step still runs, so the wakes of other chats still settle.
  A message taken off the list still sends or steers when the list can't be read again to show it; the log names the chat and the failure's kind, and the list shows again once that message is sent or steered.
  They survive a restart and go once the engine has reconciled; none goes while the runtime closes.
  A waiting message's attachments are copied into its own folder, `attachments/waiting-files/<chat>/<message>`, when it is added or edited, so they outlast the composer's prepared files and a restart; the folder goes once the message is sent or removed.
  A removal that fails is logged and the queue, the edit or the delete goes on, and at start the engine removes every waiting folder no kept message holds.
  A chat's delete deletes its waiting messages and their files, and a row the engine can't read is skipped and named once in the runtime's log.
  In the window the composer stays open while a reply runs, and the store keeps each chat's waiting list from `WaitingMessageList` when the chat opens or the window resyncs, and from `WaitingMessagesChanged` after.
  Enter adds a message the way the Chat setting `teamrun.whileReplying` names, Steer by default; Alt+Enter queues and Ctrl+Enter, Cmd+Enter on macOS, stops and sends; with Ctrl+Enter as the send key, Ctrl+Enter takes the setting's way and Ctrl+Shift+Enter stops and sends.
  A split button beside Stop sends the setting's way, and its menu offers each way with its key.
  The list above the composer shows each message's way, first line and files, a Steer one's reason to wait while the reply's provider can't steer, and a failed one's failure; its menu holds Edit, Send now and Remove.
  Edit, or Up in an empty field for the last one, puts its text in the field, where Enter saves it with its files unchanged and Escape or Cancel leaves it as it was; Add files is off and a pasted or dropped file is refused with a note while it lasts, and a save that fails keeps the edit open, with the failure in the chat's notice; once the message no longer waits, as when it went at the reply's end, the edit ends and its text stays in the field as a new message.
  While an edit lasts, Escape anywhere in the composer's card outside a menu or another field ends it without stopping the reply, once a shown tooltip has taken the first Escape as the kit's tooltips do, and its text is never saved as the chat's unsent message.
- A step that runs a command is titled in plain words, and its body shows the exact command and its output.
  The title is the provider's own where it gives one: Claude's `description`, and an ACP tool call's `title`, which is already the step's text.
  Else, for Codex, it comes from the command's only parsed action, when that is a read, a listing or a search.
  A Codex command that may write, by the check that keeps it from counting as read-only, has no title.
  Else TeamRun's `CommandTitleReader` reads the command, in the quoting of the shell that runs it: a search, a read of whole files or of a file's start or end, a folder's listing, a change of folder, or a git status, diff or log.
  A command those rules don't fit, such as one with a flag they don't know, one run by a relative path such as `bin/rg` or `tools\rg.exe`, or one behind a prefix such as `sudo`, or one that pipes, joins commands, redirects, expands a variable or runs over lines, keeps its own first line as its title.
  The provider puts the title in the step's payload as `title`, and the window shows it in place of the first line.
- A call of Terminal's `terminal_run` or `terminal_write` shows as a command step that names its terminal, read by the Providers' one `TerminalToolReader` for every provider that names the tool it called.
  Its title is `Run in <terminal>` or `Sent keys to <terminal>`, from the title in Terminal's report as the call returned, and `a terminal` until the result comes; its body shows the command, or the keys typed with control characters escaped, the output from Terminal's report, at most its last 4,000 characters, the exit code, and a last line when the command still runs in the terminal.
  Its payload holds the tool, the command, `terminalId`, `terminalTitle`, `exitCode` and a `status` of `inProgress` until the result, then `failed` for a failed call or a command Terminal killed, and `completed` otherwise.
  Claude's call, `mcp__teamrun__terminal_run` or `mcp__teamrun__terminal_write`, shows at its tool use and is sent again under the same item id with its result, read from `tool_use_result`'s `structuredContent`, in place of a separate result step.
  Codex's is an `mcpToolCall` item of the `teamrun` server, shown at `item/started` and again at `item/completed`, with its `result`'s `structuredContent`, or its `error`'s message for a failed call.
- A module tool's structured fields are for the window, not the agent, except where they hold the whole result.
  Claude Code gives the model a result's `structuredContent` in place of its text items, keeping its images, and other MCP clients may give it in place of all the content, so the fields of a tool whose content holds more, such as the Browser's page and element beside a snapshot's tree or a screenshot's image, would leave such an agent only the summary.
  So TeamRun's MCP server lists a tool's `outputSchema` and answers with its `structuredContent` only for Terminal's and Computer Use's tools, whose fields hold the whole result, and leaves every other tool's out of both, TeamRun's own included.
  A result with no content but fields, such as Computer Use's `computer_click`, answers with the fields' JSON as its one text item too, so a client that reads only the content still gets them.
  An ACP tool call's step names the TeamRun tool it calls from the agent's `IAcpAgent.mcpToolOf`, which reads Gemini CLI's and Antigravity's `_meta` (below); for an agent without one, from `rawInput.tool_name` when the agent routes MCP calls through one tool, as Grok does, and otherwise from its title, in the forms recorded: Grok's `teamrun__<tool>` and Copilot's `teamrun-<tool>`, with a tool name of letters and digits joined by single `_`.
  Such a call's step is a note, whatever its ACP kind, with `tool: mcp__teamrun__<tool>`, `input`, `servedTool` and `servedInput` as Claude's steps have, the input being the routed call's `tool_input` or else its `rawInput`; so it pairs with its call record, a Browser call is drawn as a Browser step, and no command's writes are read from it.
  Its result stays the ACP tool call's content.
  A bare tool name, as Grok first sends one, a `_meta` naming another server, and any other title can't be told from the agent's own tools, so those calls keep their ACP kind.
- A Browser call's step shows what it did to the page, from the fields the agent doesn't get.
  No MCP client passes its tool use id to a custom server: Claude Code ignores a result's `_meta`, and Codex sends `_meta._codex_apps.call_id` only to its own apps server (openai/codex#19207).
  So the step names its call instead: Claude's step of a `mcp__teamrun__` tool, and Codex's `mcpToolCall` of the `teamrun` server, add `servedTool`, the tool's name without the prefix, and `servedInput`, its arguments, which Codex's step keeps only when it keeps the arguments.
  The runtime's tool session records each Browser call it serves as a `ToolCallRecord`: the tool, the arguments, the result's fields, or `failure` with the failure's `code` and `moduleCode` for a failed call, the content's first image, and `permission`, the `id`, `risk`, `decision` and `isRemembered` of the permission request that decided the call: of the call's `requests`, the first that didn't allow it, or else the last, and none when it raised none.
  The answerer passes the request's id to its `PermissionAsk`, the `ReplyRun` notes the id of each ask it shows a card for, and the step's `permission` keeps `isAsked`, whether it showed one; a chat's mode, a remembered answer or a refusal before asking shows none.
  The answerer allows Browser's `browser.navigate` request at Low risk once without asking when it offers Allow once, as the browser design's approval cards by risk say: opening a page on a site the project already allows asks no card and shows as `low risk, allowed`.
  Every other request still asks: Browser's clicks, typing and key presses at any risk, its Medium and High requests, and every other module's Low request.
  The reply's `ReplyRun` pairs a record with the reply's first step not yet paired whose `servedTool` matches and whose `servedInput` matches as canonical JSON, keys in any order, or with any input when the step keeps none; a record whose step hasn't come waits for it, and one that comes after the reply ended is dropped.
  The record's fields merge into the step's payload and stay through the step's later reports, and its image is stored as the step's `storedPath`, as a generated image is.
  The window titles the step from them, such as `Opened docs.example.com/pricing`, `Clicked "Choose Team"`, `Typed into "Password" · hidden`, `Didn't click "Next" · the page changed before the click, so nothing was clicked` or `Couldn't open intranet.example.org · not an allowed site`, and a step the person denied as `Didn't click an element · you denied it`.
