  The step whose call a pending permission card asks for adds `waiting for your answer` after its title, in the kit's muted ToolCall note, as `Clicking "Choose Team" · waiting for your answer`, as the approved browser design draws it.
  The tool session opens each call it serves in the runtime's shared `ServedCalls` under an id of its own, passes that id to the registry as the call's `callId`, and closes it when the call ends.
  The answerer finds the `callId` of a request's question there and adds the call's `servedTool` and `servedInput` to the approval's `PermissionPayload`, so the window notes only the open step that names the same call, matched as above but never a step without its input; another open step, even of the same tool, gets no note.
  Desktop's permission question names its call from noldova-com/desktop#7; until TeamRun pins a release with it, a question names no call, the payload has no `servedTool`, and no step shows the note.
  The step then says how its call was decided, as in `Clicked "Pay" · you allowed it`:
  - `you allowed it`, `you allowed it from now on`, `you denied it` or `you denied it from now on` when the person was asked;
  - `allowed earlier` or `denied earlier` for a remembered answer;
  - `low risk, allowed` for an unasked Low allow, `allowed by this chat's mode` for an unasked Medium or High allow, and `denied without asking` for an unasked deny;
  - `nobody answered` or `stopped before an answer`;
  - `you typed it yourself` when the person took the step over.
  The step's status sets the tense: a running or waiting call reads `Clicking an element`, and one that failed or was cut off before its record came reads `Didn't click an element`.
  Just before the reply's first pending permission card, after any question card ahead of it in the group, one muted line lists the reply's Browser calls that went ahead without asking, after the teammate's name, as in `Low risk, allowed without asking: Codex read docs.example.com/pricing · took a screenshot · read the console · waited for "Plans and pricing"`: a finished read (`browser_snapshot`, `browser_screenshot`, `browser_console` or `browser_wait_for`), which raises no request, and a call whose request allowed it at Low risk with `isAsked` false; a call allowed on a card, a call that failed, and one whose `permission` is missing because it failed before asking never count.
  The line names an address only when it differs from the step before, so a read on the same page says `read the page`, `took a screenshot`, `read the console` or `waited`, and a wait for a text says `waited for "<text>"`.
  The step opens onto the page's address, which brings the chat's page forward through `browser.openPage` as the `Page` panel does, and the stored screenshot at most 15rem wide, which opens full size as any image does.
  Tab reaches a Browser step's row, its page and its screenshot, and each Browser card's answers under the low-risk line, each with the ring drawn today: the kit's accent outline one border width wide (`--nol-border-width`, 1px in the default theme), one inside the row and the page, two outside the screenshot's frame and the answers, and twice as wide in Highlight under forced colours (the design's §7 draws 2px, kept for later).
  At 640×400, a 200% zoom of the test window, each of those parts stays shown inside the window, and the low-risk line, which has no control, stays whole inside the reply above the first card; `activity-block.component.spec.ts` and `reply-approvals.component.spec.ts` check both.
  Tab goes from a step's row to its page and its screenshot, and from one card's answers to the next card's, and Shift+Tab goes back; Enter and Space open and close the row, Enter opens the screenshot full size and Escape returns to it, and the Left and Right arrow keys move along a card's answers, Enter sending the focused one.
  Under forced colours these parts and the low-risk line are drawn only in the system's colours, the line's text and shield in CanvasText, as the design's §7 draws everything but the selected tab and the agent's ring; the same two specs check the keys and the colours.
  ACP, Pi, Grok and Antigravity steps add no `servedTool`, so their Browser calls stay plain tool steps.
- A Codex MCP tool call of any other tool keeps its `arguments` in the step's payload, when their JSON is at most 4,000 characters, and its result's text, or its error's message, in the step's body, at most 6,000 characters.
- A step the person denied carries `denied: true` in its payload, whatever the provider calls the denial: Claude's tool result for a tool use whose approval the person denied, by the SDK's tool use id; an ACP tool call whose permission request the person answered with a reject option, by its `toolCallId`, sent again at once if the step already shows; a Codex command or file change whose approval request TeamRun answered with `decline`, by the request's `itemId`; and a Codex MCP tool call whose approval TeamRun answered with `decline`, by the one tool call of that server still running, since Codex's request names no item.
  A request withdrawn or cancelled, rather than denied, marks nothing, and neither does a denial Claude or an ACP agent makes on its own, or an item Codex ends as `declined` because the answer to its request failed, as when the person stops the reply while it waits.
  The reply list's preview keeps a step's `status` and `denied`, so Activity tells a denied step from a failed one before the reply's details load.
  The window shows a step that carries it, or whose result carries it, with the kit's Denied status in its header and a muted Denied at the end of its Activity row, never as Failed; a Codex item ended `declined` without it, because the answer to its approval failed, shows Cancelled, as a step whose reply ended before it did.
- A reply-panel row with a step open reads its reply's details in full with `MessageDetails` once; while the reply runs, each refresh reads with `MessageDetailsAfter` only the details after the last one it holds and the held ones a `DetailUpdated` changed since, at most 1,000, and in full past that.
  Core sends `DetailUpdated` for every step it rewrites in place, including each sub-agent a restart stops, so a row never keeps a stale step.
- A step counts as a read or a search, in the window's step counts and in which images a reply wrote, by its tool: Claude's `Read`, `Glob`, `Grep`, `WebSearch` and `WebFetch`, Codex's web search, and an ACP tool call's `read`, `search` and `fetch` kinds; a Codex MCP or dynamic tool with one of those names is another step.
- A reply's text before its last step is in-between text: while the reply works, it shows in its place as the answer does, with no bar, above the text after it, so a text keeps its left edge from its first word whether or not steps come after it, as the answer does once the reply ends.
  Once the reply completes, it moves into the reply's Activity, among the steps in its order, muted with a bar at its start, so the reply below starts at its text after the last step, and a stored reply shows the same way.
  Its project images, teammate mentions, links and Run on shell code blocks work there as in the reply's own text.
  The step counts leave it out.
  A reply that was stopped, interrupted or failed keeps all its text in place, since its text may have been cut off before the last step, and its in-between text keeps the look it had while the reply worked, at the answer's edge with no bar.
- While a reply works and its Activity is collapsed, one muted line under "Working for…" shows the icon, in the spinner's column, and the title, cut with an ellipsis, of its newest step not yet ended, as the list titles it, or of its last step when none is open.
  The line stays between steps, so it doesn't flicker; it isn't there before the first step, while the Activity is open, or once the turn ends.
  A new step's title streams in from its first letter at the pace of the reply's text, through the kit's `TextReveal` in `TitleReveal`, on this line and in the open Activity list alike; a step shown before, there when the chat or the list opened, or arriving under reduced motion or after the reply ended shows whole.
  A reader whose view starts below the line stays on the text they are reading in every painted frame as it appears and goes: the list holds the first element below the line that reaches into the view, at its place, through the kit's `hold`, so text taller than the view holds too.
- An agent's plan is one `Plan` detail per reply, whose payload is a `ReplyPlan`: its steps in order, each with its text, its status (Pending, InProgress or Completed) and the text it shows while in progress when the provider gives one, and the provider's explanation when it gives one.
  Each provider reports the whole plan each time it changes, under the one item id `teamrun-plan`, so Core replaces the reply's plan detail in place rather than adding another, and the plan stays where it first showed.
  Codex's plan comes from `turn/plan/updated`, its explanation included; an ACP agent's from a `session/update` of kind `plan`, its entries' priority unused.
  Copilot CLI's ACP session sends one empty plan and no other, so Copilot's comes from the to-do list it keeps in `session-state/<session id>/session.db` in its profile folder, the `todos` table's titles and statuses in the order added (by `created_at`, kept to the second, then as inserted; `blocked` shown as pending), read after each completed `sql` tool call of the reply's own session whose query changes that table; checked with Copilot CLI 1.0.103.
  Codex offers its `update_plan` tool only when the thread's configuration sets `tools.update_plan.enabled = true`, off by default since Codex 0.152.0 (0.146.0 added it, on by default, and older versions ignore it), so TeamRun sets it in every thread it starts, resumes or forks.
  Claude Code offers its to-do tools only when the session's environment sets `CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TODO_TOOLS`, which TeamRun sets for each Claude session; its plan comes from `TodoWrite`, which sends the whole list, or from `TaskCreate` and `TaskUpdate`, which TeamRun keeps as one list for the session by the ids their results give, applying each once its result says it succeeded, a deleted task leaving it.
  The list stays with the session when a change of model, effort, instructions, speed or permission mode opens another query on it, and `TaskList`'s result becomes the whole list, which brings a session's tasks back after TeamRun restarts.
  The adapter keeps the lists of the 50 most recently used sessions, so a chat left idle while 50 newer sessions ran loses its plan's earlier tasks until its next `TodoWrite` or `TaskList`.
  Those tools, and `TaskGet`, run without asking and never show as steps; a plan isn't activity, so a reply whose only activity is its plan isn't listed there, in Core's Activity page or in the window's.
  A status TeamRun doesn't know counts as Pending, and a step without text is left out.
- A plan-mode plan is a second `Plan` detail in the reply, a `ReplyPlan` with no steps whose `text` is the plan as Markdown, under the item id `teamrun-plan-text`, so a revised plan replaces the last one; its detail's text is the plan's title, its first line without heading marks.
  Claude's comes from the `plan` of its `ExitPlanMode` tool, which still asks the person to approve it and never shows as a step; when the person denies it, the plan text is sent again with `rejected` set, which a plan stored without it reads as `false`, and when the person allowed it and the tool ran, again with `approved` set; an `ExitPlanMode` success nobody was asked about, as in Bypass, approves nothing.
  Codex's comes from the completed `plan` item its plan mode ends with.
  Cursor's comes from its blocking `cursor/create_plan` request: its `plan` is the plan text and its `todos`, then each phase's, the checklist, and an approval asks the person to Approve plan or Keep planning; the plan text is sent again approved or refused, and Cursor is answered `accepted` or `rejected`, or `cancelled` when the ask is dismissed or the turn stops, or with a note in the reply when its plan can't be read.
  Grok's comes from the `x.ai/exit_plan_mode` request (also `_x.ai/exit_plan_mode`) its `ExitPlanMode` tool sends: its `planContent` is the plan text, asked about the same way; Grok is answered `approved`, and leaves its plan mode for Default by itself, or `cancelled`, which it takes as keep planning, as its public source, xai-org/grok-build at 2bdd1d6, defines it; a request without plan content is dismissed.
  The other ACP agents send no plan-mode plan, so in their plan modes they show their checklist alone.
  A teammate plans first once its permission mode is chosen as its provider's plan mode, such as Claude's `plan` or Codex's `plan`.
- A chat plans first while its `Chat.planFirst` is on, set with `ConversationSetPlanFirst`; `ChatMember.mode` and the chat's own settings keep their modes.
  Each reply in it whose adapter names a `planMode` starts with `RequestedSettings.planFirst`, read again when the reply starts, as its mode is, and the adapter runs that turn in its plan mode; a reply whose adapter names none runs as usual.
  Plan first holds for each member on its own: a teammate, or the chat's own provider when no teammate answers.
  While a reply plans, TeamRun's own tools follow the plan mode, which is read-only; once the reply's plan text is `approved`, they follow the turn's requested mode, and Core records that member's plan in `Chat.approvedPlanTeammates` or `Chat.approvedResponderPlan` and publishes `StateInvalidated`.
  That member's replies then start in their usual mode, while every other member's still plan first until its own plan is approved; `Chat.plansFirst(teammateId)` says which.
  A refused or revised plan keeps its member planning; turning Plan first on again makes every member plan first again, and turning it off ends it for all.
  A plan approved while Plan first is off, or in a reply that started while its member didn't plan first, changes nothing; one approved after the person turned Plan first off and on again during the reply counts, since its member plans first both when the reply started and when the plan is approved.
  A provider whose plan turn ends without asking, with its plan text left neither approved nor refused, is approved at the turn's end: while the reply is the chat's last message and its member still plans first (`Message.awaitsPlanApproval`), its plan card shows Approve plan in a row under its header and body, and `MessageApprovePlan` appends the plan text, approved, to the reply, records that member's approval, publishes `StateInvalidated`, and sends "Go ahead with the plan." as the person to the same responder, so it goes on in its usual mode; when that send is refused before any message goes in, such as for a disabled provider, the approval is undone and the plan still waits for Approve plan; writing to it instead sends another planning turn.
  An approved plan text's header says Approved where a checklist's progress would be.
  A plan-first reply that completes without a plan text from its provider takes its final answer, its last text, as its plan text, so it waits for Approve plan the same way; one that failed or was stopped doesn't.
  The window leaves out a reply's text whose text, trimmed, equals its plan text, so such an answer shows once, as the plan card.
  OpenCode's plan-first replies end this way: its `plan_exit` tool, which would hand its plan over, sits behind its own experimental plan-mode flag and is offered only to its own command line, so over ACP its plan mode answers in plain text.
  Copilot's published shapes don't show how its plan mode hands a plan over, so its plan-first replies end this way too until a live check shows otherwise.
  Claude's plan mode is `plan`: a plan-first turn opens its query in `plan`, with `allowDangerouslySkipPermissions` still set by the requested mode, and once the person allowed the reply's own `ExitPlanMode` and it ran, the adapter calls the query's `setPermissionMode` with the requested mode, so the same turn goes on in it, and the turn's result waits for it; when that fails, the turn notes that Claude Code kept its plan mode.
  A query that left its plan mode is never reused for a plan-first turn: such a turn on its session opens a new query in `plan`.
  Turning a chat's Plan first on has the engine name, to each adapter whose plan mode the chat now plans first in, the native sessions of the chat's replies it still runs, such as one whose background task runs on (`IConversationEngine.planFirstAsync`, `IProviderAdapter.plansFirst`); Claude's adapter returns each open query of those sessions that isn't in `plan` to it at once, within the request timeout, so a turn Claude Code starts on its own when the task ends runs in `plan` from its start, and logs one that doesn't switch.
  A wake reply also carries whether its member plans first as it begins (`WakeTurn.planFirst`): for such a wake on a query still out of `plan`, the chat returns it there as the wake's first message arrives, and stops the wake with a note when Claude Code refuses or doesn't answer in time; either way, the person's approval of the wake's plan leaves `plan` for the wake's mode (`WakeTurn.mode`), or, for a wake without one, for the mode the query had before.
  A query TeamRun returned to `plan` goes back to the mode it had once the chat no longer plans first: as the first message of a wake that doesn't plan first arrives, or before the person's next message that doesn't plan first on that query; when Claude Code refuses or doesn't answer in time, that reply notes that the query may still be in `plan`.
  The chat tracks its query's mode as one value, known or unknown, through every switch it makes, the push, a switch back and an approved plan's leave alike: a mode switch Claude Code doesn't answer in time may still land later, so the mode is then unknown, never `plan`, and the next push or plan-first turn sends `plan` again.
  It also keeps the mode a turn that doesn't plan first should find, from a push, even one that timed out, and from an approved plan's leave until Claude Code takes it: a wake that doesn't plan first switches to its own mode (`WakeTurn.mode`), or, without one, to that mode, and the person's next message that doesn't plan first sends its own mode, unless the chat knows the query is in it.
  Codex's plan mode is `plan`, also listed as a mode a chat can choose: its turns run in the read-only sandbox, asking on request, and `turn/start` carries Codex's experimental `collaborationMode` `plan`, with the thread's model and effort and Codex's own plan instructions.
  The collaboration mode stays with the thread, so the first turn on it that doesn't plan sends `default`: after a turn the adapter started in `plan` on that thread, whatever `thread/resume` reports, and otherwise, as after a restart, when `thread/resume` reports `plan`; an app-server that refuses the mode fails the turn with its error.
  An ACP agent plans first in its own `planMode`, which the shared adapter runs as if the chat had chosen it, with everything else the turn asked for: OpenCode's is `plan`, with edits denied at launch, Cursor's and Grok's are `plan`, and Copilot's is its Plan mode.
  When the person approves a plan-first turn's plan through the agent's own plan request, the adapter switches the session with `session/set_mode` to the mode the turn asked for, or the default, so the agent goes on in the chat's own mode, as Claude's `setPermissionMode` does, in the same turn for an agent the switch reaches.
  Cursor is switched before it is answered; its ACP documents don't say what it does after `accepted`, and that isn't checked live yet.
  Grok leaves its plan mode for Default by itself once answered `approved`, and reports it with a `current_mode_update`, as its public source, xai-org/grok-build at 2bdd1d6, does, so a `session/set_mode` sent before the answer would be undone: the turn sends the asked mode at that update instead, without waiting for its answer, since the client handles an answer only after the update before it, and sends none when the asked mode is Default; a refused switch notes in the reply that Grok goes on in Default, unless the run has closed.
  That `session/set_mode` sets only the mode Grok's next turn starts in, as its source's `acp_session.rs` says, so the rest of the approved turn runs as Default.
  So in a chat whose mode is read-only, such as Ask, the adapter sends `session/cancel` at that update instead, and once Grok answers the prompt, sends `session/set_mode` for the chat's mode and goes on in the same reply with a new prompt, "Go ahead with the plan.", which Grok runs in that mode; a refused switch fails the reply with Grok's error and sends no prompt, as does a Stop meanwhile, and a turn that ended before the cancel landed still gets the switch for its next turn.
  A steer sent between the cancel and the new prompt is held rather than interjected, so it starts no turn before the switch lands, and goes with the new prompt, after its text; with no new prompt it isn't taken, and the message goes back to the chat's queue.
  A held steer whose image can't be read isn't taken either, as an interjected one isn't, and the new prompt goes without it.
  Since Grok can start an edit before that cancel lands, a plan-first ACP turn whose chat mode is read-only, such as Ask, refuses each permission request TeamRun's rules for its own tools don't settle once its plan is approved, without asking and with a note, whatever mode the agent reports.
  Grok's `default` permission mode asks before each edit and each command outside its read-only list, as its public source's permissions guide says, so its edits come to TeamRun; a call the person's own Grok `allow` rules, saved grants or always-approve mode approve never asks, and Grok's Ask mode itself refuses no tool.
  So when Grok's `initialize` answer offers a hook that can refuse a call, under `_meta["x.ai/hooks"]`, the adapter registers a `PreToolUse` client hook on each `session/new` and `session/load` (`GrokToolGate`), which Grok asks through `x.ai/hooks/run` before each tool call, ahead of those rules, with the event's serialized name, `pre_tool_use`, in `hookEventName`.
  In a chat whose mode is read-only, such as Ask, and outside a plan-first turn's planning, `AcpTurn` refuses the call, with a note, unless Grok's earlier `tool_call` update for it marks it `read_only` in `_meta["x.ai/tool"]` or it is one of TeamRun's tools marked read-only; it also refuses a request it can't read and answers a refusal when noting it fails; every other chat's calls go on, for Grok's own rules to decide.
  Grok lets a call go on when the hook's answer doesn't come within 30 seconds or doesn't read, so the turn answers each one at once, and refuses rather than fails.
  Grok also lets it go on after an error answer, so `AcpClient` answers a hook request it can't hand on or answer with the gate's refusal, never an error, in every chat: one past its 128 open requests, one under an id already open, one with no listener, and one whose answer fails.
  A read-only chat on a Grok whose `initialize` answer offers no such hook fails with `SettingUnavailableException`, naming its mode, since Grok's `allow` rules would otherwise answer without TeamRun; other chats run there as before.
  Another teammate's transcript carries a reply's plan text where it happened, never its checklist.
- The reply's plan merges its details (`ReplyPlan.ofDetails`): the last plan text and the last checklist with steps; a plan with neither, or one that doesn't read, shows nothing.
  Once the reply ends, by finishing, failing, stopping or being interrupted, the window shows a step it left in progress as not done, with its own text, in the reply and the Plans view, for every provider; a sub-agent's plan does so once that sub-agent ends, whatever its reply's state, since a background sub-agent runs on after its reply and a stopped one ends while its reply runs.
  The stored plan keeps the provider's last status.
  The window shows it at its first plan detail's place in the reply as one block framed like the kit's code block, from its tokens: the code block's border, corners and colours, and its header's height, label text and colour.
  The header holds the plan glyph, the plan text's first line, or "Plan" when that line is a heading of its own, marked with one to six `#` or underlined with `=` or `-`, or there is no text, and the checklist's progress, such as "1 of 3", or a muted "Not approved" in its place when the plan text was refused, then the chevron; folded, the block is that header row alone.
  Opening it shows, in the block's body with the code block's padding, the plan text as Markdown, with the reply's links, file links and shell runs, then the kit's plan checklist, asks the Plans view to show that reply and shows the view through the context's `showView`, which opens it in the right dock or activates it where the person moved it, shows its dock and moves the focus into it; the reply keeps the block open while its controls are remembered.
  The checklist drops its own frame, so its steps start at the body's padding like the text above them.
- A sub-agent is one `SubAgent` detail in its reply, whose payload is a `SubAgent`: the tool use that started it, its type, description and model, its status (Running, Completed, Failed or Stopped), whether it runs in the background, and its tool uses, tokens, time and last summary when the provider gives them; the provider reports it again under the same item id each time it changes, so Core replaces it in place.
  Its own steps, plan and notes are details whose `parentSequence` is its detail's sequence, from a `TurnDetail` whose `parentItemId` names its item; a reply's text is never one of them.
  They stay out of the reply's preview, Activity count, plan and Plans view, but its file changes count in Changes; `ReplyPlan.ofDetails` given its sequence reads its own plan.
  A reply's approvals and questions stay open after its turn ends while one of its sub-agents still runs or a background task its turn started hasn't ended: a later request shows a card in that reply, naming the sub-agent, without changing the reply's status, and once that work ends, any card still open is cancelled, as when TeamRun starts again; a stopped reply closes them at once.
  While such a late card waits, the reply shows it waits, its chat is marked in Projects, and the asking sub-agent's open step waits, as while its turn runs; the window learns the chat of a reply it never saw running, as after a reload, from that reply's `MessageSummary`.
  Claude's comes from its `Agent` tool: the SDK's `task_started`, `task_progress`, `task_updated` and `task_notification` messages for its task and the tool's result update it, its own messages carry the tool use's id as `parent_tool_use_id`, its thinking stays out, and its text shows as a note.
  A spawn Claude Code refuses, such as one of an unknown type, sends no `task_started`, and its tool's error ends it Failed with that error as its summary; a stopped one's `task_notification` carries no usage, so it keeps the counts its last `task_progress` gave.
  A tool it asks to run is asked through the turn that started it, with that reply's permission, and the request's payload names it as `subAgent`; the window's approval card then says "<sub-agent> asks", and only its open step waits for permission.
  Once that reply's turn has ended, a background sub-agent's request is still asked in that reply while it keeps its approvals open; once it closes them, the request is asked through the chat's running reply instead, still naming it, and a denial still marks the result under the sub-agent's row; with no reply running, the tool is denied with a reason that says so, and a note under the row tells the person.
  A request that waits in a reply that closes its approvals before it is answered, as when Stop ends it, is asked again through the chat's running reply in the same way, and a reply that has closed them is never asked again; any other failure of the request goes back to the SDK.
  A Claude reply that ends while a sub-agent runs in the background keeps following it until it ends, so its row updates when its notification comes; when the query ends, or the runtime starts again, a sub-agent still running shows Stopped.
  A task Claude Code marks `owned_by_subagent`, such as a sub-agent's own shell command, is neither one of the chat's background tasks nor one of the ended tasks a wake is named from, so a background sub-agent's wake reply is named for the sub-agent.
  Grok's comes from its `spawn_subagent` tool call: `subagent_spawned` names the sub-agent's own session, type and model, `subagent_progress` counts its tool calls, tokens and time, and `subagent_finished` ends it with its output or error; the spawn's own end ends it when no finish came, except for one in the background, whose spawn ends as soon as it starts.
  The updates of its own session show under its row, its tool items keyed by that session, its thinking left out and its text as notes; a request whose tool call names that session as `x.ai/requestingSessionId` comes on the parent's session and is asked through the reply with `subAgent` naming it.
  A Grok sub-agent outside the background still running when its reply ends shows Stopped.
  One in the background keeps running past the turn, as Claude's do: the adapter keeps Grok's process open while it runs, as for a background task, its later updates, its own session's included, still show under its row in the reply that started it, and its end shows there with Grok's status, the chat and the Sub-agents panel naming the same one.
  It is kept once its `subagent_spawned` names it; one still unnamed when its turn ends shows Stopped with the turn's other sub-agents, and the process closes as for any turn.
  When its `subagent_finished` says, through `will_wake`, that Grok starts a turn for it, that turn, whose prompt id is `subagent-completed-` and its `subagent_id`, shows as a reply started when the sub-agent ended, with its description, as a background task's wake does; the process closes once nothing more runs on it.
  Stopping the sub-agent, or deleting its chat, cancels it through Grok; stopping the reply, during its turn or a wake's, keeps none of that turn's: each one shows Stopped at once and is cancelled through Grok, whose process stays open until Grok has answered each cancel; shutdown cancels each one, at most the interrupt timeout, before it stops Grok; signing out of Grok closes each Grok process of that provider, its background tasks and sub-agents with it; and Grok's exit, or any other stop of its process, shows a running one Stopped.
  Grok's sub-agents are on only in its Default mode: a turn or title in Plan or Ask launches Grok with `GROK_SUBAGENTS` set to `0`, since a sub-agent doesn't keep its parent's mode, and `grok agent` has no `--no-subagents` of its own.
  The chosen model and effort follow `grok agent --no-leader` as its `--model` and `--reasoning-effort`, before `stdio`, since Grok's `agent` path drops the top-level ones.
  Copilot's comes from a Copilot tool call of kind `other` whose input has an `agent_type` and a `prompt`: its description is the row's, its `agent_type` the type, and the call's end ends it, completed with its output's `content` or failed with its `message`, as for an unknown type; one whose `mode` is `background` runs on past its call's end, as Grok's background sub-agents do.
  The row's spawn is followed by its id, so its end is read also after more calls than the turn keeps.
  A Copilot sub-agent runs in the parent's session, so its tool calls are the ones whose `_meta["github.com/copilot"].agentId` names it, keyed by that id and counted as its tool uses, and an approval whose tool call names its `agentId` names it as `subAgent`.
  `initialize` asks Copilot, in its client capabilities' `_meta["github.com/copilot"].events`, for its `subagent.started` and `subagent.completed` session events, which come as `github.com/copilot/sessionEvent` notifications, heard also with ACP's `_` prefix, naming the sub-agent's `agentId` and its spawn's tool call as `data.toolCallId`; that event links the id to its spawn, also when it comes before the spawn shows, so two sub-agents started together each get their calls and approvals from the first.
  A `subagent.started` event's `data.model` is the row's model while it runs, and a `subagent.completed` event for the row's spawn gives its model, `totalToolCalls`, `totalTokens` and `durationMs`, keeping TeamRun's own tool-use count when it is larger, as a cancelled sub-agent's `totalToolCalls` of 0 is after a step; one whose `cancelled` is `true` ends the row Stopped, and otherwise the spawn's call still ends it, with its summary.
  Once an event has linked a spawn that showed, which proves the event's tool call is the spawn's ACP `toolCallId`, an id an event names is never guessed while its spawn hasn't shown; until then the guess runs as with no events, so ids that never match leave the guess as it was.
  An event replaces a guess that linked either the id or the spawn, and a later event for an id or a spawn an event linked is ignored.
  A Copilot that sends no such event leaves the guess: a new id names a sub-agent only once exactly one sub-agent could have made it, of those running when the id was first seen, the one no other id has named, also when it has ended since, since an ended sub-agent's calls can still update.
  Until then that id's calls show as the parent's steps, keyed by the id, and its approvals name "A sub-agent", so a card never names the wrong one.
  The session's text while exactly one sub-agent runs outside the background is that sub-agent's, shown as notes under its row, its thinking left out; while two or more run, the text shows as notes in the reply, and the thinking is left out.
  A call first shown as the reply's step stays there when its id is named later, since a reply's step keeps its place in the reply.
  A background sub-agent's reply streams on the parent's session with nothing that names it, so it first shows as the reply's text.
  When the parent's `read_agent` tool call, of kind `read`, names the sub-agent's `agentId` in its input's `agent_id` and its output's status is `idle` or `completed`, the sub-agent ends Completed, with the text after each `[Turn n]` line of the output as its summary.
  Each of those replies also moves out of the reply's text into a note under the row, but only when exactly one run of whole text chunks matches it, trimmed, inside one text item that streamed between the sub-agent's spawn and that `read_agent` call; the text item is sent again without it, and the window hides a text item left blank, with no gap.
  So replies that interleave, replies that are the same, a reply cut across a chunk's edge, and the parent's own copy of a reply after it read it stay the reply's text, shown twice rather than under the wrong row.
  Copilot's ACP prompt stays open after the parent's reply until each background sub-agent has ended and the parent has answered its notification, so the sub-agent runs on in the same turn, with the chat working, and its row ends as above.
  A Copilot sub-agent still running when its turn ends shows Stopped, which the window names Cancelled: cancelling the prompt kills it, and a person's Stop does that.
  One that finished but that the parent never read with `read_agent` shows the same, since that call is what ends its row as Completed.
  The prompt has no time limit, so a sub-agent that hangs keeps the chat working until a Stop.
  Codex's comes from its `collabAgentToolCall` item with the `spawnAgent` tool: the item's id is the row's, its prompt the description and its model the model, and it runs in the background, since the spawn returns at once.
  The child thread the item names in `receiverThreadIds` is followed on the same app-server: its `thread/started` gives the type, its `agentRole` or else its `agentNickname`, and its model; its items show under the row, its messages as notes as for Claude's, and count its tool uses; its `thread/tokenUsage/updated` gives the tokens; its last agent message is the summary; and each of its turns adds its `durationMs`.
  A child's notifications from before the item names it, from a thread whose `parentThreadId` is the turn's, wait until then, and are dropped when the turn ends.
  A later reply's `sendInput`, `followupTask` or `resumeAgent` call to an earlier reply's child is a row of that later reply, with the child's type and model, since the adapter keeps each thread's children across its turns.
  Codex's multi-agent v2 shape (`multi_agent_v2`, off by default at 0.164.0) sends no `spawnAgent` call and no child `thread/started`: a `subAgentActivity` item of kind `started` on the parent is the row, under its id, with the last part of its `agentPath` as the description, its model as the model and no type, and its `agentThreadId` is the child, whose turns, items and tokens come under that thread id as in v1.
  A v2 child's turn, item and token notifications from its `turn/started` before the item, through the end of its turn, wait until then, since Codex sends the item once the spawn returns, and are dropped when the reply's turn ends; a kind `interacted` for an earlier reply's child is a row of the reply that sends it, and a kind `interrupted` ends a running row as Stopped, keeping the time of the child's turn that ends after it; the kind `completed` and the child's own items for its messages to the parent show nothing, since the child's turn ends the row.
  The row ends with the child's turn (completed, interrupted as Stopped, or failed with its error), or with an ending state in an `agentsStates` the parent's calls report (completed, errored, not found, interrupted or shut down); a spawn that ends naming no child ends as Stopped when it was interrupted and as Failed otherwise; another turn on the child runs it again.
  Each child turn is a background task under the row's id, with no stop of its own, which the reply's Stop ends with `turn/interrupt` on the child's thread: during the reply's turn also a child that hasn't started its turn yet, as soon as it does, and after that turn every child whose turn still runs; the thread stays held until the last child turn ends, and the app-server's exit shows a running one Stopped.
  Codex keeps a child's result for the parent thread's next turn and starts none for it (`trigger_turn` false in its completion delivery), so when a child's turn ends completed or failed, TeamRun didn't stop it, and no turn on the parent thread will read the result, the adapter starts that turn itself: the reply's wake, named with the row's id and description, runs a turn on the parent thread, held until it ends, whose prompt says a sub-agent ended, and the wake adopts the thread's token for TeamRun's tools and, through `WakeTurn.adoptSender`, the sender token of the turn that opened the thread, which its shell keeps; Codex hands that turn the child's result, so the wake answers with it.
  Codex reads a child's result when it builds a sampling request, and in a turn it samples again only after a local tool's call or input it records for the next sampling, such as a steer; once a final answer is recorded it keeps queue-only child mail for the next turn without sampling again (its mailbox delivery phase `NextTurn`), and it reports a child's end on the parent thread with a `subAgentActivity` item of kind `completed` before it queues the mail.
  So a child that ends when no turn runs on the parent thread wakes the chat at once, and one that ends while a turn runs there, the spawning turn included, is decided when that turn ends: it wakes the chat unless an item of `Resources.codexFollowUpItemTypes` completed in that turn after the child ended, since only then did Codex sample again and read the result; Codex's own `subAgentActivity` item doesn't count.
  A turn the person stops decides no wakes, so the results it didn't read wait for the person's next message, as a stopped reply starts no reply after it.
  Turns on a resumed Codex thread take the thread's lock in order, a wake's turn too, so two turns never run on one thread; a person's turn that waits for it logs so, and one the person stops while it waits for the lock or opens its thread sends no `turn/start` and lets the thread go; a wake's turn starts only when the chat follows its reply (`WakeTurn.onFollowed`).
  A turn that starts on the thread after the child ended, counted when its `turn/start` succeeds, takes its result, since Codex hands a turn its kept results as it starts, so a wake whose turn would start after one runs no turn, and its reply says that turn took the result; of two children that end together, the first wake's turn takes both results.
  A wake the person stops before its turn starts, while it is queued or while it waits for the thread, runs none and lets the thread go.
  When the sender token of the turn that loaded the thread can't be routed to a turn or wake, because another reply still takes it, the reply says that its shell's `teamrun` command doesn't act as it, and the adapter logs it.
  A chat that takes no wake leaves the thread to be released.
  Codex's collab items themselves show no other step, and a sub-agent's own sub-agents aren't shown.
  A child's command, file-change and MCP tool approvals and its questions are asked in the parent's reply through the parent's turn, so its card is the parent's, with the row's description in the payload's `subAgent`, as for Claude's sub-agents, and the answer goes back to the child's thread; the request router sends a thread's request to its turn, else to the latest reply whose sub-agents follow the thread, else to every reply whose child it may be: each waits until a call names it, the first that names it asks, and when all of them drop it unnamed at their turn's end, it's answered `cancel` if one of those replies was stopped, and refused otherwise.
  Codex gives a child its parent turn's approval policy and sandbox when it spawns it, so a child asks only what the chat's mode asks, and a child already running keeps the policy it was spawned with.
  Muse Code shows no sub-agent rows and reports no plan usage: `muse acp` offers neither, as a live check found, only its context use and its cost when known.
  Muse Code's ACP guide lists read-only and plan modes, closing or deleting a session from the editor, audio prompts and ACP versions other than 1 as missing, and says a fresh install answers `/compact` with "Manual context compaction is not available.", which shows as the compaction's reply.
  Antigravity's comes from its `invoke_subagent` tool call, of kind `other`, whose input lists `Subagents` (from a live capture of 1.3.0): the first one's `TypeName` is the row's type and its `Role` the description, and only the first makes a row, since each capture names one and a call naming several is unseen; its `Model` is `inherit`, so the row has no model, and Antigravity reports no tool uses, tokens, duration or summary.
  The sub-agent's own steps come in the parent's session with nothing that names it, so they stay the parent's steps, as does the `start_subagent` call that follows the spawn.
  The `invoke_subagent` call ends failed, "approved but never executed", at the turn's end whether the sub-agent finished or not, so that end is never read as the sub-agent's: the row runs until its turn ends and is then inferred from the turn's end, Completed when the prompt ended with `end_turn` and Stopped otherwise; a spawn the person denied shows Stopped from the denial itself, whatever the call reports after it.
  A sub-agent that couldn't start, such as one of a type Antigravity doesn't have, shows Failed as soon as the `start_subagent` call Antigravity sends after `invoke_subagent` ends failed, with that call's output, "Tool execution failed", as its summary; its turn still ends with `end_turn`.
  Each `start_subagent` call is paired, by its call id, with the earliest running sub-agent no start call has taken yet, in the order they were spawned, so with two spawns in one turn a failed start ends its own sub-agent, and a repeated update of a failed call ends no other.
  A request still waiting when the child's turn ends or the child is stopped is withdrawn by Codex's `serverRequest/resolved`, which cancels its card; once the reply is stopped, a child's request is answered `cancel`; after the parent's turn ends, it is still asked in that reply while the reply keeps its approvals open, and once Core refuses the ask as closed, it is declined, or a question skipped, with a note under the row; any other failure of the ask goes back to Codex as the request's error.
  The window shows it as one kit tool-call row in the reply's Activity, folded by default, titled by its type and description, with the kit's status and elapsed time; opened, it shows its plan as the kit's plan checklist, its model and usage, its summary while it runs or its result once it ends, and its own steps as nested rows, and the reply's expansion remembers which are open and how many show.
- TeamRun's own sub-agents start from a teammate's tools: `teamrun_start_sub_agent` starts an `explorer` or a `helper` on a brief and answers at once with its row, `teamrun_wait_sub_agents` waits up to 50 seconds, 30 by default, for the turn's sub-agents and answers with those that ended and the ids still running, and `teamrun_stop_sub_agent` stops one.
  Each takes the sender token of the turn whose session made the call, so a sub-agent belongs to that turn's reply, and each is `Refused` once that turn has ended; a start is also refused once the reply is stopped, so no sub-agent outlives its reply's Stop.
  A missing argument, or one of the wrong type, is refused with its name.
  Each provider's own background sub-agents past its turn: Claude's, Grok's and Codex's keep running and wake the chat with their results; Copilot's hold its turn open until they end, and their results reach it in that turn; OpenCode's, Cursor's and Antigravity's end with the turn, since TeamRun keeps the process past the turn only for an ACP agent whose tasks it follows.
  A signed-in run of Copilot CLI 1.0.105 shows the hold: its prompt answered only after the background agent ended and the parent read it with `read_agent`, though the parent was told not to wait; the longest case, an agent running `sleep 97`, answered at 106 seconds.
  There `session/cancel` during the hold, or a second `session/prompt`, which cancels the first, killed the agent's command at once and left the session usable.
  TeamRun sends no such second prompt: during a turn `MessageSend` refuses a person's message with `Conflict`, the person's message waits through `WaitingMessageAdd` instead, as below, and a teammate's message is accepted and waits for the turn to end.
  OpenCode's source has background sub-agents that report to the parent through a synthetic message on its session, unconfirmed with OpenCode over ACP.
  An ACP agent names its own `backgroundSubAgentsNote`, which its teammates' instructions carry: most agents' says its background sub-agents end with its turn, so wait for each one's result before ending it; Copilot's says they keep its turn open, so no one can send it a message until they end, and a stop ends them; Grok's has none, and neither have fx's and Muse Code's, which have no sub-agents.
  A sub-agent still running when its turn ends wakes that turn's chat with a new turn once it ends, as a chat wake.
  A helper is a turn of its own in a new session of the reply's provider, in the chat's folder, with the reply's settings and team MCP servers, the reply's role followed by a note that it is a sub-agent whose last message is its answer, and no sender token or TeamRun tools, so it addresses no teammate and starts no sub-agent.
  It shows as a `Helper` row in the reply, in the background, with its model and tokens, its steps under the row, its text as notes, and its last text, cut to 20,000 characters, as its summary and the result `teamrun_wait_sub_agents` gives.
  Helpers write in the chat's folder, so only one runs at a time per folder: a helper started while another runs in the same folder, from any chat or reply, shows as running and waits for it, and a Stop ends it before its turn starts.
  The folder is counted as project activity counts it, with its links resolved and up to its Git working tree's top, so two projects whose paths reach one folder, or folders of one working tree, share the queue; a helper whose turn ahead failed still runs.
  An explorer only reads: it runs at once, beside a helper, in its provider's read-only mode, which the provider or the system enforces rather than the model's instructions: Claude's Plan, where Claude Code runs no edit or command tool and asks for anything else, and Pi's read-only mode, where TeamRun's extension blocks every Pi tool but `read`, `grep`, `find` and `ls`.
  A live Pi 1.5.0 turn on Linux in that mode read a file, and its `bash` and `write` calls were blocked with TeamRun's reason, so no file was made.
  TeamRun denies every approval an explorer asks for without asking the person, and shows a note under its row for each; it gets no team MCP servers either, since a server runs outside the provider's sandbox.
  A provider whose adapter names no read-only mode for it, today every one but Claude and Pi, runs no explorer, and starting one there is `InvalidParams` naming the provider and the adapter's own reason when it gives one; live checks on each system follow.
  Codex runs none, as the maintainer decided: its Read Only sandbox fails every write, but a command the person's own Codex rules allow runs outside the sandbox without asking, and Codex can't turn those rules off for one chat, so read-only can't be promised there, and the refusal says so.
  A provider's own sub-agents, Codex's explorer among them, still show as their own rows.
  A reply runs at most four at once: another agent than `explorer` or `helper` is `InvalidParams`, a fifth is `Conflict`, and an id the reply didn't start is `NotFound`.
  Each sub-agent is a background task under the row's id, so the row's Stop and the reply's Stop end it, and the reply stays open past its turn while one runs, as for a provider's own sub-agents; its approvals and questions are asked in the reply, naming it as `subAgent`.
  Stopping the reply or shutting TeamRun down stops its sub-agents, which show Stopped.
- A teammate runs a shell command in the background with `teamrun_run_in_background`, the same way for every provider: it takes the command, at most 8,192 characters, and a short description, starts the command in the chat's folder through the system's shell, `ComSpec` or `cmd.exe` as `/d /s /c "<command>"` on Windows, with Noldova Desktop's verbatim arguments so the command's own quotes and operators reach it as written, and `SHELL` or `/bin/sh -c` elsewhere, and answers at once with the background task.
  On Windows a command with a line break is `InvalidParams`, since `cmd.exe /c` runs only its first line, and the tool's description says to join commands with `&&` and that `cmd.exe` takes at most 8,191 characters with its own path and flags.
  The command is a background task of the turn's reply, with its output in the Background tasks view and its Stop there; it takes the sender token of the turn whose session made the call, and is `Refused` once that turn has ended.
  It is high risk and isn't among the tools TeamRun pre-approves to its providers, so the provider asks for it as for its own command tool: where the chat's mode asks, not in a mode that skips asks, such as Claude's Bypass permissions or OpenCode's Allow All, and a read-only mode refuses it; TeamRun raises no request of its own for it.
  It runs with the person's current environment, which Noldova Desktop reads as it does for the providers, and in the shell that environment names; that isn't the turn's environment, so a `teamrun send` it runs has no sender token.
  Unlike `terminal_run`, whose terminal the person sees, it runs its command unseen, so it stays out of the pre-approved tools until that is decided on its own.
  A chat runs at most 4 such commands at once, and a fifth is `Conflict`; a stopped reply stops its commands, and so does TeamRun's own stop.
  A command still running when its turn ends, or one that ended during it, wakes the turn's chat with a new turn, as a chat wake.
  A command the person stopped, from its task or with its reply, wakes no one, but from the moment the stop is accepted, even while the command is still ending, the next turn TeamRun starts for the teammate that started it in that chat, or for the chat's own provider for one it started, gets a `From TeamRun: ` note ahead of its prompt naming each such command by description and task id, so it doesn't wait for their wakes.
  The note stays until a turn told it completes, so a turn that fails or is stopped leaves it for the next one, and so does a `/compact` turn or another `/` command's; a turn the provider starts on its own, such as a wake it follows itself, has no prompt from TeamRun and isn't told.
  The note lives in memory: deleting the chat or the teammate leaving it drops it, and so does a restart.
  Nothing in it depends on the provider: the runtime's specs call it at the endpoint with a kept session token, as every adapter holds one, for each of Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot, Grok, Cursor, OpenCode and Antigravity, in each of the modes that provider's adapter offers, and check the exit code's wake, the refusal in each read-only mode, the whole process tree ending on the task's Stop, the reply's Stop and at shutdown, with no wake, and the restart's note, on every platform CI runs.
- The Plans view, `teamrun.plan`, lists the shown chat's replies with a plan from Core's Plan page oldest first, newest at the bottom, as Activity and Changes list theirs: each opens scrolled to its end and follows a new reply unless the person scrolled up, as the chat does, and loads older pages at its top; each plan shows as a message under its author with the chat's plan block, always open and without a chevron, the reply's time after the author, as the Activity and Changes rows show it, and "Not approved" after that when the plan text was refused; the message draws no footer row, the kit's hover row of Copy and the time, so a selected plan's fill ends as far below its block as it starts above its name, and gives the kit the plan's text, then its checklist a step a line, as the text its Copy writes, which the kit shows in the header on hover once the footer row is off; plans sit `--nol-space-2` apart, and the view sits first in the right dock's lower group.
  It registers with `ContentPadding.None`, so its list scrolls to the pane's own edges with the scrollbar at the end edge, and its rows carry the view padding inside the scroller: `--nol-content-padding-inline` on both sides, `--nol-view-padding-block` above the first, and `--nol-space-2` and the view padding under the last.
  Each plan's `--nol-space-2` gap sits above it rather than below, so a plan the view reveals keeps that gap under the view's top; the row takes back the kit slot's own end padding with a negative margin, since the kit's reveal sets the scroll position itself and leaves CSS scroll margins out.
  Its empty text, Loading and a failed page keep the same padding as a margin.
  Activity and Changes show the same feed and register with `ContentPadding.None` too, so their list also scrolls to the pane's own edges.
  Their rows carry `--nol-content-padding-inline` on both sides, `--nol-view-padding-block` above the first, and `--nol-space-1` and the view padding under the last.
  Their empty text, Loading and a failed page keep the view padding as a margin, as Plans' do.
  A row's Loading badge sits at the end and bottom of the row's content, inside its padding, in every feed.
  When a block in the chat asks for a reply, the view loads older pages until it has the reply and ten after it, scrolls it to the top and selects it, and scrolls it there again each time one of its rows changes size, until the person scrolls, clicks or presses a key in the view.
  A selected plan has no bar: it sits on `--nol-raised` with `--nol-radius-large` corners, its block's body on the chat's background, through the kit message's Raised selection look, so its name still ends in "selected"; its block's header, which fills the card inside its border, takes the kit's `--nol-code-header-raised`, a step lighter than the selected row in dark and the same code header colour in light, so the header and the row read apart.
  Clicking a plan in the view selects it and asks the chat to show that reply with the kit's brief flash, then scrolls the chat the least that shows its plan block.
  The plan card asks for that through the chat list's own reveal once its block has a size, so after the list has measured the reply, and a reply far outside the rendered rows still ends with its block in view; more than the list's follow distance from the chat's end, the list holds the block there while the rows settle and lets go when the person scrolls, and nearer the end it keeps following the end, which can still move the block.
  A press anywhere else in the chat, on a message, the composer or the empty area, clears the view's selection, and so does showing another chat.
- The Sub-agents view, `teamrun.subAgents`, sits after Plans in the right dock's lower group and lists every sub-agent the shown chat's replies started themselves, one level deep, from Core's SubAgents page, which pages the replies with a `SubAgent` detail of their own; a sub-agent's own sub-agents stay in its row's steps.
  Like Plans, it registers with `ContentPadding.None`: its own scrolling area fills the pane, with the scrollbar at the end edge, and carries the view padding inside it.
  The view reads every page of the chat, so the finished ones stay for the whole chat: finished ones first under "Finished", then running ones under "Running", each oldest first by when it started, so the view opens at its end and follows new sub-agents there, unless the person scrolled up from it.
  Each is a card: its status mark (the kit's spinner while it runs, a check, a red error or, for a stopped one, the kit's cancelled mark), its reply's teammate mark as the chat shows it, and its description; then its type, model, elapsed time (ticking while it runs), tools and tokens, "Background" for a background one, and "Failed" or, for a stopped one, "Cancelled", the word and mark the chat's row shows for it, so both name one end the same way; then the first two lines of a finished one's summary.
  A field the provider doesn't report is left out, never shown empty.
  A running card whose sub-agent has a running task ends its top row with Stop, the stop glyph with the label and tooltip "Stop sub-agent", which stops that task through `BackgroundTaskStop` without selecting the card.
  Stop is disabled while its stop runs, and a refused stop's reason shows under the card's details until the next stop or until the sub-agent or its task ends.
  Clicking a card selects it on `--nol-raised` with `--nol-radius-large` corners and asks the chat to show its reply with the kit's brief flash, open that reply's activity and the sub-agent's row, and scroll the chat the least that shows the row, through the chat list's own reveal once the list has measured the opened row, which holds the row there while it and the rows above it settle and stops the list following its end, unless the person scrolls first or the row is near the end, where the list follows its end; the selected card's "Open transcript" button does that again after the person scrolled away or folded the row.
  A reply far outside the chat's rendered rows takes the same path as the plan card's far reveal.
  A press anywhere in the chat outside the selected sub-agent's own row, another sub-agent's row included, clears the selection.
  Its tab's badge counts the shown chat's running sub-agents, whether or not the view was ever opened, and it has none while none runs or no chat is shown: the window keeps the count from the same pages and the sub-agents' detail events, and reads the pages again after a resync, a reconnect or a rewind.
- A provider TeamRun installs itself, such as Antigravity's ACP server, lives in `providers/<provider>/<version>` in the module's data folder.
  An install downloads the pinned build and checks its size and SHA-256, checks that no zip entry is a link or leaves its folder, unpacks it with the system's unzip (on Windows `unzip.exe` on the PATH, or else Windows' own `tar.exe` in `System32`, never another tar on the PATH) in a staging folder beside it, and moves it into place in one rename; a failed or cancelled install leaves nothing, and the provider stays Not installed.
  On an Intel Mac, before that move, TeamRun re-signs its own copy of Antigravity's server ad hoc with the system's `/usr/bin/codesign`, keeping the hardened runtime and adding only the entitlement for unsigned executable memory, without which the server's libffi never finishes starting there; codesign has a minute, and if it fails or runs out of time, nothing is installed.
  One install runs per provider: a second request joins it, and a cancel from any window stops it at whatever step it is on, ending the unzip or codesign it runs.
  An Antigravity provider can name the server it runs in its edit dialog's Binary path, its `Provider.binaryPath`: its kind's `choosesServerPath` lets it, and Core refuses a relative path or a kind without the flag; an empty field clears it.
  Only the person sets or clears it: `teamrun.providerUpdate` with a `binaryPath`, an empty one included, answers only the app's main process, through `MainProcessMethod`, and is refused from any other client, such as the command line, a module's own entry or a tool, as `Unauthorized` before Core sees it, so a program a teammate runs can't name the server TeamRun starts; such a client can still change the provider's other settings.
  Each launch looks for the chosen path alone, else TeamRun's own copy, else the first `PATH` folder holding `agy_acp_server.par` (`agy_acp_server.exe` on Windows); each needs `localharness_external` beside it.
  A chosen or `PATH` server counts only as a file and, outside Windows, one the person may run; a chosen path that is missing, a folder or not runnable fails the launch with its path, never falling back to another server.
  A kept session's key holds the chosen path, so a turn after the path changes starts the new server instead of resuming on the old one.
  TeamRun accepts any patch of the pinned major and minor, 1.3.x (`Resources.antigravityAcceptedVersionPattern`), so a server's own patch updates pass, and a 1.4 is refused until it is checked and the pin moves.
  ACP `initialize` checks every server's version, TeamRun's own, chosen or found, so one outside 1.3.x starts no chat or turn, failing with the reason.
  A provider's sign-in check starts its chosen server even when its profile is signed out, reads its version from `initialize` and stops it, so a refused version shows on the row and a signed-out one still reads as signed out; a signed-out provider without a chosen path starts nothing.
  While TeamRun's own copy isn't installed, `AntigravityInstallation` starts the server on `PATH` once to read its version, and again only when that file changes: a 1.3.x server counts as installed, so the kind isn't Not installed and Sign in shows, and the installation names it as `foundPath`; any other version leaves the kind Not installed, with that reason.
  A provider with a chosen path isn't marked Not installed, and its row's details end with the server it runs: its chosen path, or else the server found on `PATH`.
  The Binary path field is a text field, since Noldova Desktop has no file chooser yet, and only Edit has it, since a provider is added without one.
- A person adds an agent from the public ACP registry or a local ACP command through the "ACP agents…" entry that ends Add provider's "Provider type" list.
  Either opens the same search list; from Add provider, an agent added there becomes the chosen provider type once the list closes and the add and its reading of the list end, without waiting on the icons that reading fetches, and closing with none added keeps the type chosen before.
  The row's Browse opens a search list of the registry's agents for this computer, each with its icon, an SVG one drawn as a mask in the row's text colour since the registry's icons are single-colour SVGs and any other as an image, or else its first letter, name, version, description, About and Add, and under them the local commands and the form that adds one.
  The list comes from `AcpRegistrySource`, fetched at most hourly and kept in `acp-agents/registry.json` in the module's data folder, so it shows offline, and it says why when it's old.
  It leaves out the providers TeamRun runs itself; a `uvx` agent shows with its reason and no Add for now.
  An added agent or command is a row of `acpAgents`, holding the registry entry or command it was added from, and `AcpAgentsService` registers each as a provider kind at every start.
  A row that can't open is logged and the others still start.
  Adding or removing one publishes `ProviderKindsChanged`, and the window reads the kinds and their installs again.
  Those reads, and the window's own after it adds or removes one, never join a read asked for before, and an earlier read that answers late doesn't replace a later one's kinds, nor fails its caller when it fails after a later one applied.
  An agent a provider still uses, or one that's installing, can't be removed; removing one removes its files, and a command's saved secrets.
- A registry agent installs through the same `ProviderKindsService` install, cancel and Update as the other kinds, into `acp-agents/agents/<id>/<version>`, and Add starts its install.
  An install takes the registry's latest version, and when it's placed saves the entry it installed and removes the agent's other versions but the one it replaces, which a chat may still run on until the next install.
  A binary's address must be HTTPS; it is at most `releaseDownloadLimit` (1 GiB) and is checked against its `sha256` when the entry lists one.
  An entry with no `sha256` is allowed when its final address, after any redirect, is HTTPS, and its row shows Not checked.
  A zip goes through the same link and folder checks as Antigravity's and the system's unzip, a tar archive through the system's `tar`, and a raw binary is moved into place; an entry whose program isn't inside its folder installs nothing.
  An `npx` agent installs through the person's own npm, found on the PATH, as `npm install --ignore-scripts --no-audit --no-fund --no-save --loglevel=error --prefix <folder> <name>@<version>`.
  Its package must be an exact `name@version` that passes the name rule, so a tag, a flag or a git URL is refused before npm sees it, and the installed package's manifest must show that version.
  The entry's variables reach the agent's process only, never the npm step.
  A program started through a batch file on Windows, as npm's shim is, takes its arguments through `SharedMcpLauncher`'s batch escaping, the same as a shared MCP server's; `cmd.exe` can't carry a line break, so an argument with one is refused with a reason.
  On Windows an agent's command named without a folder is found the same way, only in the absolute folders `PATH` lists, never in the chat's folder the agent starts in, and refused with the reason when `PATH` doesn't hold it.
- A local ACP command keeps its name, command, arguments and, for each variable, the name and a saved secret's reference.
  The window saves each value through `context.secrets` before it sends the command, sends references only, and removes what it saved when the add fails.
  `LocalAcpCommand.toJson` writes those references, never a value; the values are read each time the kind opens, and a value that's gone or can't be read refuses the agent's start with its reason.
- `GeneralAcpAgent` runs every added agent: modes, models and effort come from the session's `mode`, `model` and `thought_level` options, images and MCP transports from `initialize`, and resume, list and close from `sessionCapabilities`.
  Sign-in comes from `initialize`'s `authMethods`: an `agent` method goes through `authenticate`, and for a `terminal` method `ProviderSignInCommand` gives the window the agent's program and the method's arguments to run in a terminal, with the program's folder first on the `PATH`.
  A method whose program or arguments aren't plain words gives no command, since a terminal types its command into whatever shell it has.
  A local command's terminal sign-in runs its command alone, without its saved values, so a local command with arguments has none and signs in with its own command.
  An agent that lists no `authMethods` needs no sign-in, so signing it in, as adding it does, starts no `authenticate` and goes straight to the check, which finds it signed in when its session opens; one that lists only a `terminal` method still signs in through that method's command.
  "Compact the context" sends an added agent `/compact` alone only when its session's latest `available_commands_update` lists `compact`, and otherwise answers with the commands it lists before anything is sent.
  ACP has no request that makes an agent compact, and an agent's own `/compact` may only ask its model for a summary, so TeamRun counts the compaction only when the agent reports it in a completed `compaction_update`, the session update ACP made stable on Oct 9, 2026, for which TeamRun advertises `clientCapabilities.session.compaction`.
  That report shows as "<agent> compacted its context."; a reply without one ends with a note that the agent answered `/compact` without reporting a compaction, so its context may not be compacted.
  Each added agent runs in the person's home folder with the one sign-in it keeps there, so its kind's `singleAccount` takes one provider of it, that provider's folder holds nothing of the agent's, and Sign out's confirmation is `Resources.formatSignOutPersonal`'s, since it signs the person's own copy out too.
  Sign-out is ACP's `logout`, where `initialize` advertises `auth.logout`.
- Claude Code is installed and updated the way its own installers do it, from its release channel at `downloads.claude.ai/claude-code-releases`: TeamRun reads the latest version and that version's `manifest.json`, downloads this computer's build into a staging folder under `providers/claude`, checks its size and the manifest's SHA-256, and runs the build's own `install` for that version with `--force`, without a shell, which puts Claude Code in `~/.local/bin` where TeamRun looks for it; when the setup leaves another version installed, as it does when that version's file is already on disk, TeamRun runs the installed Claude Code's own `install --force` for the version once, and an install that still leaves another version fails, naming the version still there.
  A channel, manifest or download that fails its checks installs nothing; a cancel stops the install until the build's own setup starts, and then lets that finish with no time limit, so a cancel never leaves an installed Claude Code half replaced; from then its kind shows no Cancel and reads Installing or Updating, setting up, and a setup that fails says why.
  The setup is a process Core starts, so quitting TeamRun, deactivating it or updating it during the setup ends the setup, which may leave the old Claude Code, the new one, none in `~/.local/bin`, or one that can't tell its version; the next start reads the version again and its kind shows what is there: the version with any update, Install when there is none, or Update to the latest for one that can't tell its version, which a new install replaces.
  A version is read only from a `--version` that exits 0 in time, so a Claude Code a quit left half-written, which exits 1 naming its own versioned file, can't tell its version rather than reading as that version; its Update runs the setup with `--force`, which replaces a version's file already on disk, as the setup without it doesn't.
  Only a native Claude Code, the one in `~/.local/bin` that its own installers make, is offered an update; one installed by npm, Homebrew, winget or an override path updates by its own means, so TeamRun never puts a second copy beside it.
  The download, its checks, the staging folder and the unpacking are shared steps (`ReleaseDownload`, `InstallStaging`, `ArchiveUnpacker`) that Antigravity's install uses too.
  GitHub Copilot's and Grok's installs run the new program for its version before they move it into place, and on Windows something such as a virus scanner can still hold its file for a moment, so a rename of it is refused.
  So each first moves the build it just ran to `ready` in its staging folder, and Grok on Windows each command copy it just wrote to `<name>.ready`, through one shared step, `InstallRename`, while the installed version stays where it is: on Windows it tries a rename refused with EPERM, EACCES or EBUSY again after 50 ms, doubling the wait up to 1 s, until its waits would pass 10 s in all; any other error, and any other system, fails at once.
  A move that still fails fails the install with the provider's own message, which names the error's code when it has one and says nothing installed was changed, with the error as its cause; only a refusal it tried again is put down to a hold, with a word to install again in a moment.
  Only then is the installed version moved aside and `ready` renamed in, with plain renames, so no version is missing while the hold is waited out.
  When `ready` can't then be renamed in, Codex, GitHub Copilot and Cursor move the version set aside back through `InstallRename` too, so on Windows its folder is missing while a hold on it is waited out, for up to 10 s.
  Grok settles its program and every command before it swaps the first, so while a hold is waited out its installed commands all stay the old version.
- Codex is installed and updated into the standalone layout its own installers make: TeamRun reads the latest release from `releases.openai.com/codex/channels/latest`, or Codex's GitHub releases when that fails, downloads this computer's `codex-package-<target>.tar.gz` into a staging folder in `<CODEX_HOME>/packages/standalone/releases`, checks it against the release asset's size and SHA-256, and unpacks it with the system's tar into `releases/<version>-<target>`, unless a complete release of that version, with its files and a `codex` that reports the version, is already there.
  It then points `current` at that release, writes `auto-update-version`, and links the command where TeamRun looks for it: `~/.local/bin/codex`, with `codex-code-mode-host` beside it on a Mac, through symbolic links replaced by a rename, and on Windows `Programs\OpenAI\Codex\bin` in the local app data folder, a directory junction to `current\bin`, which replaces only a link or an empty folder.
  TeamRun doesn't change PATH or remove earlier releases; when another Codex comes first on the PATH, the install says which one TeamRun runs.
  A cancel stops the install while it reads the release, downloads or unpacks; from just before the release moves into place the switch runs to its end, its kind shows no Cancel, and a step that then fails says why.
  A release of that version that is missing files is first moved aside into the staging folder, which fails whole while a running `codex` holds it on Windows, and then the install keeps it as it was and says why; when the new release can't then be renamed in, the old one is moved back first, through `InstallRename`, so it is kept as it was too; it is never deleted in place.
  When the old one can't move back either, the staging folder is kept, and the failure names where the old one is; the next install that succeeds removes it.
  A folder TeamRun can't write is named: the standalone folder before anything changes, or the command's folder once the new release is current.
- The Codex threads TeamRun starts stay out of the Codex app's lists.
  Codex records a thread's source once, when the app-server creates it, from the app-server's startup option, and the Codex app lists only interactive sources, `cli`, `vscode` and its own apps'; `codex app-server` records `vscode` and takes no option to change it ([openai/codex#23442](https://github.com/openai/codex/issues/23442)), and nothing in `initialize` or `thread/start`, such as the client's name or `threadSource`, changes the source.
  The standalone `codex-app-server` that Codex publishes beside each release takes `--session-source`, so each app-server start runs the first of these: `codex app-server --session-source app-server` once `codex app-server --help` names the option; the standalone `codex-app-server --session-source app-server` of the installed Codex's version; or `codex app-server`, so the threads created until the download is in place are listed.
  The download follows Noldova Desktop's Check for updates setting, as the README says: the Codex installer starts it when it reads Codex's latest version, as the setting allows and whenever the person chooses Check for updates, and after it installs or updates Codex; a start never does.
  Until it is in place, the Codex kind's installation carries a `notice`, which the Providers tab shows on its own line under Codex's heading, saying the chats show in the Codex app and when TeamRun downloads what keeps them out.
  What a start found is kept per stamp of the Codex executable, as the version check keeps its own, and read again after a read that found neither the option nor the version.
  The download reads the release `rust-v<version>` from GitHub's API, downloads this computer's `codex-app-server-package-<target>.tar.gz` into a staging folder in `providers/codex-app-server` in TeamRun's data folder, checks it against the release asset's size and SHA-256, and unpacks it with the same tar as Codex's install into `<version>-<target>`, in place of an incomplete folder of that version, then removes the other versions; one runs per version at a time, and a failed one is logged and tried again the next time TeamRun runs; the run's first download also removes the staging folders of downloads cut off before.
  An app-server whose start fails, such as an `initialize` that fails or times out, is stopped before the failure is reported, and the log says when it does not stop.
  A downloaded app-server that fails to start is also refused until TeamRun quits, the log says why, and `codex app-server` starts in its place, unless the adapter is shutting down; at shutdown the adapter stops the downloads.
  `app-server` is recorded as `mcp`, which keeps Codex's product restrictions, where a name Codex doesn't know would leave out skills limited to a product; a resume, on `codex app-server` too, keeps the source the thread was created with, so a resumed thread stays out of the list, and one TeamRun started before stays in it.
