  Checked live with Codex 0.171.0 on Linux x64, macOS x64 and Windows x64 against a local model server, each server started with the adapter's `-c features.apps=false`, which both took, as `config/read` showed: the standalone app-server's thread recorded `mcp`, the default `thread/list` left it out, and a resume and a turn on `codex app-server` kept it so, while that server's own thread recorded `vscode` and was listed.
  The threads TeamRun started before stay listed: their source is in the first line of each thread's rollout and in Codex's state database, which TeamRun doesn't rewrite, and archiving them would stop their resume until they are unarchived.
- Every tool TeamRun downloads, Antigravity, Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot and Grok, says so in one reason when its releases can't be reached or the connection drops mid-download, rather than the network's own text, and stops a download of no published size past 1 GiB.
  Only a standalone Codex, one with a `current` release, is offered an update, the latest when it can't tell its version; one installed by npm or Homebrew updates by its own means.
  It unpacks with tar on the PATH, on Windows with Windows' own `tar.exe` in `System32`, and on macOS with the system's `/usr/bin/tar`, never another tar on the PATH, such as a GNU tar first on it, which can't read the macOS zips; a Mac without `/usr/bin/tar` has no tar for it, as Windows without `System32\tar.exe` has none.
- Each kind's latest version is read off the network only when a window loads or reconnects to the runtime, when the Providers tab opens or the kinds change, and when an install or update ends, and each of these only while `ToolUpdateChecks` says the person's Check for updates setting, `desktop.updateChecks`, is `Automatic`, or `AtStart` before the first check since the runtime part started; Check for updates in the Providers tab (`ProviderKindInstallationCheck`) reads them whatever it says.
  All but the last list the installations (`ProviderKindInstallationList`), and the listing starts a version read for every kind not already reading one; an install or update reads its own kind's versions again when it ends, whether it succeeded, failed or was cancelled.
  A read that may not ask the network reads the installed versions alone and keeps the latest version an earlier check found; a check starts its own read even while one that may not ask is running.
  When a choice that allows a check arrives after the installations were listed, the service reads them all again with a check, so a stored choice that arrives during start-up still checks at start-up.
  `ToolUpdateChecks` takes the value `read` gives when the part starts, and then each change of the setting; a value that isn't a string counts as the default, `Automatic`.
  The runtime reads the default until it knows its device, so a device's stored choice that turns checks off applies only from the change that brings it, and the checks made before it count as `Automatic`'s, which is temporary until noldova-com/desktop#96 is fixed and pinned.
  The window's catalog reload after a change, such as a sent message, a chat's new title or an approved plan, doesn't list them; installation events keep the window's copy current.
- A kind TeamRun can't install on this computer says why in one reason; when the reason is a program the computer lacks, unzip for Antigravity, tar for Codex, GitHub Copilot and Cursor, and unzip, tar or npm for an ACP agent, its installation also names that program as `missingTool`, so the window can say what to install first; a kind with no build for this computer names none.
- An installed kind whose program was found outside TeamRun's own install, on `PATH`, from npm, Homebrew or winget, or at a path the person chose, names that program's path as its installation's `foundPath`; each installer counts as its own copy the one it would install or update, Codex's standalone copy, Claude Code in `~/.local/bin`, Grok in its own folder, GitHub Copilot in `~/.local/bin` or, on Windows, its own folder, and Cursor in its own folder, so such a kind offers no update and says it updates on its own.
- GitHub Copilot is installed and updated from its GitHub releases, without its own installer, which uses winget on Windows and so would accept winget's agreements for the person: TeamRun reads the latest release, downloads this computer's `copilot-<platform>-<architecture>.tar.gz`, `copilot-linuxmusl-<architecture>.tar.gz` on a musl Linux or `copilot-win32-<architecture>.zip`, into a staging folder in the install folder, checks it against the release's SHA-256, unpacks it with tar as Codex's is, and checks that its `copilot` reports the version.
  Outside Windows it then moves `copilot` into `~/.local/bin`, replacing the one there by a rename, as Copilot's own installer does.
  On Windows it moves the release into its own folder, `providers/copilot/<version>` in TeamRun's data folder, and keeps earlier versions, so a running `copilot.exe` is never replaced; TeamRun runs the newest of them when no other Copilot is found, on the PATH, beside npm's shim or in winget's links.
  Copilot's own updater may not know that folder: TeamRun's update covers it.
  Installing a version already there moves its folder aside into the staging folder first, then renames the new one in; when the folder can't be moved, as while a running `copilot.exe` holds it, or the new one can't then be renamed in, the old one is kept as it was, moved back first through `InstallRename`, and the install says why.
  When the old one can't move back either, the staging folder is kept, and the failure names where the old one is; the next install that succeeds removes it.
  As for Codex, a release that can't be reached or a download cut off gives one reason, the release asset's size bounds the download, and a folder TeamRun can't write is named before anything changes.
  A cancel stops the install while it reads the release, downloads or unpacks; from just before the release moves into place the move runs to its end, and when another Copilot comes first, the install says which one TeamRun runs.
  Only the Copilot TeamRun installs, the one in `~/.local/bin` or in its version folders on Windows, is offered an update, the latest when it can't tell its version; one installed by npm, Homebrew or winget updates by its own means.
- Grok is installed and updated to a pinned build, `Resources.grokVersion`, since Grok publishes no checksums: TeamRun keeps each build's SHA-256 and sizes, downloads this computer's gzip-compressed build from `x.ai/cli`, as Grok's installers do, into a staging folder in `~/.grok/downloads`, checks it, unpacks it with Node's zlib, no larger than its pinned size, and checks that it reports the version.
  It then moves it into place as `~/.grok/downloads/grok-<platform>-<architecture>` and puts `grok` and `agent` in `~/.grok/bin`, as Grok's installers do: relative symbolic links outside Windows and copies on Windows, each moved in by a rename, with what is in the way, such as a running `grok.exe`, renamed with `.old` first, or `.old-<id>` while an earlier `.old` is still held; the old program is set aside the same way.
  When any of those steps fails, the program and every command already replaced are moved back, so Grok is kept as it was and the install says which path failed and why; the asides are removed once all are in place.
  `bin` is made only then, so a cancel leaves no new file, and a `~/.grok` TeamRun can't write is named before anything changes.
  TeamRun doesn't change PATH or Grok's `config.toml`; when another Grok comes first on the PATH, the install says which one TeamRun runs.
  A cancel stops the install while it downloads or unpacks; from just before the program moves into place the move runs to its end.
  Grok updates itself when the person runs it, while TeamRun starts it with `--no-auto-update`, so only a Grok in `~/.grok/bin` is offered the pinned version, and only when it is newer or the installed one can't tell its version: a Grok that updated itself past the pin shows as installed at its own version with no Update, and one installed elsewhere updates by its own means.
- TeamRun doesn't install OpenCode: it runs the person's own.
  While no `opencode` is found, the kind shows as not installed, and its Install opens a terminal that runs OpenCode's own installer, `curl -fsSL https://opencode.ai/install | bash`, on Linux and macOS, which installs OpenCode 1 into `~/.opencode/bin` without administrator rights even where npm's global folder isn't writable, and `npm i -g opencode-ai@1` on Windows, the person's own install, as for a kind TeamRun doesn't install; a sign-in check, a model listing and a turn each fail with `Resources.opencodeNotFound`, which names that command, and the next use finds it without a restart.
  Updates are the person's too, and a version TeamRun doesn't support is refused with a reason, as above.
- Cursor's CLI is installed and updated to a pinned build, `Resources.cursorVersion`, 2026.10.22-27dcf5f, the build its captures were made with: TeamRun downloads this computer's `agent-cli-package.tar.gz`, or `agent-cli-package.zip` on Windows, from `downloads.cursor.com/lab/<version>/<os>/<architecture>/` into a staging folder in `providers/cursor` in the module's data folder, and checks its size and SHA-256 while it streams.
  Cursor publishes no hashes or sizes for these archives, so `Resources.cursorReleases` holds TeamRun's own first observed SHA-256 and size of each of the six builds (macOS, Linux and Windows, each x64 and arm64), confirmed by a second download of each on another computer, and the sizes of its bundled `node` and `index.js`; a new version is pinned the same way.
  The hashes are the whole check: as observed in that build, on macOS the bundled `node` is signed by the Node.js Foundation (team HX7739G8FX) and Cursor's own executables and `.node` modules by Anysphere (team DCNK4UB866), except an unused `cursor-agent-sea`; on Windows only `node.exe` is signed, by the OpenJS Foundation, and none of Cursor's files; on Linux nothing is signed; so TeamRun checks no signature.
  It checks that no zip entry is a link or leaves its folder, unpacks the archive with tar within ten minutes, checks that its `dist-package` folder holds the launcher, `cursor-agent` or on Windows `cursor-agent.cmd`, and a `node` and `index.js` of their pinned sizes, makes the launcher and `node` executable outside Windows, checks that it reports the version, run as the locator runs it, moves the folder it just ran to `ready` in the staging folder, as the other installers do, trying again on Windows while something such as a virus scanner holds it, and renames that into place as `providers/cursor/<version>`, which keeps the package's own layout.
  Installing the version already there moves its folder aside first, into the staging folder, and keeps the old one in place when either rename fails, moving it back through `InstallRename`; when the new one can't take its place and the old one can't move back either, the staging folder is kept, and the failure names where the old one is.
  A later install's sweep of staging folders leaves one that keeps such a copy, so the copy stays until an install succeeds, which then removes it with the other versions' folders.
  A cancel stops it while it downloads or unpacks, and after its version check; from just before the folder moves into place the move runs to its end.
  After an install, the other version folders in `providers/cursor`, which an earlier pin left, and the staging folders a failed install kept, are removed; one that can't be removed, such as one a running Cursor holds, stays until a later install.
  TeamRun looks for that copy last, after the PATH and the folder Cursor's own installer uses, and only at the pinned version, so a Cursor the person installed is the one it runs, and when one comes first the install says which; on Windows it runs that copy's `node.exe` with its `index.js`, as for Cursor's own install.
  Only the Cursor TeamRun installed is offered the pinned version; one installed elsewhere updates by its own means.
- Each provider TeamRun installs reports its installed version and the version an install would bring; the installations list answers at once with the versions last read and reads both again in the background, Claude Code's, Codex's and Copilot's latest from their channels within ten seconds and Grok's pinned version at once, publishing them when the read ends; the window lists the installations with its catalog and again when the Providers tab opens; TeamRun offers an update only when the one an install brings is newer, or the installed one can't tell its version, so a tool that updated itself past it is never downgraded.
  Update runs the same install and is offered in Add provider's dialog, as Update on the line under the provider type, which says "Version <n> is available"; while it runs the line atop Your providers reads Updating with its percent and Cancel, as does the dialog's line, and a failed update keeps the kind installed at its version, with the reason in its providers' account place, until an install succeeds or the versions read show it up to date.

## Instruction files and memory

A teammate's turn reads the person's and the project's instruction files and memory as the provider's own tool does, whether the provider reads them itself or TeamRun reads them and passes them in.
TeamRun reads them only for Antigravity, the person's own and the project's, without following imports, as its row says; a file or folder it can't read is skipped and the rest are read.
Each file is capped at 64K characters and all of them at 128K; the files past that are left out and named.
The caps are TeamRun's: the providers' own tools read these files whole.

| Provider | Its own tool reads | A TeamRun turn reads |
|---|---|---|
| Claude | The managed policy `CLAUDE.md` (`/Library/Application Support/ClaudeCode/` on macOS, `/etc/claude-code/` on Linux and WSL, `C:\Program Files\ClaudeCode\` on Windows); the person's `CLAUDE.md` and `rules/**/*.md` in its config folder; then, in the turn's folder and each folder above it, `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/rules/**/*.md` and `CLAUDE.local.md`, in that order; each folder's `AGENTS.md` and `.claude/AGENTS.md` only when no `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/CLAUDE.md` or `CLAUDE.local.md` exists in those folders; after each file, the files it imports with `@path` anywhere in a line, outside code spans and fenced blocks, `\ ` standing for a space, relative to the file or under the home folder for `@~/`, four deep; block-level HTML comments outside code removed; and the project's memory, `MEMORY.md` in `projects/<the repository root's path>/memory` in its config folder. | A chat turn runs with `settingSources` `user`, and `project` and `local` too when Claude Code's own `.claude.json` in that folder marks the turn's folder or a folder above it trusted (`hasTrustDialogAccepted`), as Claude Code loads a project's settings only after its trust dialog; Claude Code then reads the files itself, in its own order, with its own imports, and a turn asks for no import approval, from the provider's profile, else `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, else `~/.claude`; TeamRun appends only the role to the system prompt. A title session runs with `settingSources: []` and `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1`, and reads none of them and no memory. In a chat turn the memory loads by itself, since the SDK's auto memory stays on unless the person's own `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY` turns it off, and Claude saves to it with Write and Edit in the same folder as Claude Code, without asking. |
| Codex | `AGENTS.override.md` or `AGENTS.md` in `CODEX_HOME`, then one `AGENTS.md` for each folder from the repository root down to the turn's folder. | Codex reads them itself, since `CODEX_HOME` is the provider's profile. A chat turn's thread leaves the person's own setup to Codex: their hooks, in `hooks.json` or `config.toml` in `CODEX_HOME` or a trusted project's `.codex`, and their `notify` command run as in their own Codex, and so do their plugins while the provider keeps their own MCP servers, since a plugin can bring servers of its own. A title thread turns `features.plugins` and `features.hooks` off and sets `notify` to an empty list, since Codex runs that older command after every turn whatever `features.hooks` says. A title thread still reads the person's `AGENTS.override.md` or `AGENTS.md` in `CODEX_HOME`, as checked live on Linux x64: that's a known limit, since Codex has no setting that leaves the file out, and a title home of its own would hold a linked sign-in that a refresh can turn into a copy. Its memories, while the person has the feature on, are read from `memories` in `CODEX_HOME`, as by their own Codex, since the thread's config leaves the feature alone; Codex writes memories only from interactive threads, so a thread recorded with the `mcp` source, which keeps it out of the Codex app, as the Codex item under installs says, adds nothing to them, and one recorded `vscode` by `codex app-server` does. The person's command rules, `rules/*.rules` in `CODEX_HOME` and in a trusted project's `.codex`, still apply: an allow rule there lets a matching command run without asking and outside the sandbox. Only `codex exec --ignore-rules` turns them off, and `codex app-server` has no such option. |
| Copilot | `copilot-instructions.md` and `instructions/**/*.instructions.md` in `COPILOT_HOME`; `.github/copilot-instructions.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/CLAUDE.md` and `GEMINI.md` from the repository root down to the turn's folder, and `.github/instructions/**/*.instructions.md`, each only for the files its `applyTo` matches; a line `@path` in the first three, inside the repository. Its memory, Copilot Memory, is a setting of the person's GitHub account, kept on GitHub. | Copilot reads them itself, since `COPILOT_HOME` is the provider's profile, the person's `~/.copilot` by default. Copilot Memory doesn't reach a TeamRun chat turn: Copilot's `--acp` mode has no memory tool, even with `--enable-memory`, which gives its own prompt mode user memory, as checked live on Linux x64 with Copilot 1.0.105. That's a known limit until Copilot offers memory in `--acp` mode. `--acp` mode loads at least the person's `copilot-instructions.md`: a title session answers from it though it starts with `--no-custom-instructions`, as checked live on Linux x64 with Copilot 1.0.105. That's a known limit, since Copilot has no way to leave the file out, and a title home of its own would hold a linked sign-in that a refresh can turn into a copy. |
| Cursor | `.cursor/rules/**/*.mdc`, and `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` at the project root, with `AGENTS.md` in a subfolder for the files under it. The person's User Rules are a setting of the Cursor app, not a file. It keeps no memories. | Cursor reads the project's files itself, in the folder a chat turn's process starts in, the turn's folder. Its ACP server asks for no folder trust, as `cursor-agent -p` does without `--trust`: TeamRun's chat in the folder is the person's choice of it. On Linux x64, Cursor 2026.10.22-27dcf5f gave a chat turn the codewords of an `alwaysApply` rule and of `AGENTS.md` with no trust record in its profile, and a title session neither; the project's `.cursor/mcp.json` server didn't start, as a live check found. Every Cursor launch passes Cursor's hidden `--disable-project-configs`, so Cursor ignores the `.cursor/cli.json` files from the repository's root down to the folder it starts in, whose permission rules it would otherwise merge with no trust check: on that machine a project's `Shell(touch)` allow rule ran the command unasked without the option, and with it the command was asked through TeamRun and refused, while the rule and `AGENTS.md` still loaded. The person's User Rules aren't passed, since no file holds them. |
| Grok | The person's `Agents.md`, `Claude.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md`, `AGENT.md` and `AGENTS.md` in `GROK_HOME`, else `~/.grok`, then `rules/*.md` there; `~/.claude`'s and `~/.cursor`'s named files and `rules/*.md`, each while its compatibility setting is on; the folders `[paths] extra_rule_dirs` names; then the project's files. A rule loses its front matter, and imports aren't followed. Its memory, legacy or memory-v2, is off unless the person turns it on, and lives in its home. | Grok reads them all itself, since a chat turn runs Grok in the person's own Grok home, `GROK_HOME`, else `~/.grok`, or in an added provider's own folder, with the person's own home folder: their instruction files and rules, their compatibility settings for Claude's and Cursor's files, their memory, their permission rules, their hooks and their MCP servers apply as in their own Grok, and the turn shares their memory as two of their own Grok sessions do. TeamRun passes none of these files, writes nothing in the home, and adds only its own MCP server to the session. |
| Antigravity | The person's `AGENTS.md` and `GEMINI.md` in `GEMINI_HOME`, else `~/.gemini`, then in its `config` folder, then `config/rules/*.md`, without following imports; their skills in `config/skills` and `antigravity-cli/skills` there; then, in each folder from the repository's root down to the turn's folder, the project's `AGENTS.md` and `GEMINI.md`, then `.agent/rules/*.md` and `.agents/rules/*.md`; the project's skills in `.agents/skills`. A rule, the person's or the project's, whose front matter's `trigger` is `manual` is left out, since Antigravity applies one only when the person mentions it with @, which a TeamRun chat can't; any other rule passes whole, front matter included, so its `description` or `globs` tell the agent when it applies. Antigravity's source isn't public, so this order is not confirmed. | Antigravity runs with `GEMINI_HOME` at the provider's profile, since it keeps a session's MCP servers, values included, in its home, so TeamRun's values stay out of the person's `~/.gemini`; it reads the project's skills in `.agents/skills` itself, but not those in `.agent/skills`, which TeamRun leaves there, since a skill is loaded when a task needs it and isn't text to pass ahead of every turn. At each chat turn's launch TeamRun links the person's `config/skills` and `antigravity-cli/skills` folders into the same places in the profile, by a junction on Windows and a symbolic link elsewhere, so Antigravity reads their skills itself. TeamRun writes nothing in the person's home, skips a missing source and removes the link an earlier launch left for it, leaves alone anything else in a folder's place, and notes in the log a link that fails while the turn goes on. The links are writable: a skill Antigravity makes, edits or removes in those folders is made, edited or removed in the person's own, and no live check has shown whether 1.3.0 ever writes there. Any other launch, a title session, a model list, a sign-in or a sign-out, runs with `GEMINI_HOME` at the profile's `.teamrun-hidden-home`, which holds only a link to the profile's `antigravity-acp`, so it shares the sign-in and the sessions but none of the person's skills; something else in that link's place fails the launch. Over ACP, Antigravity's server 1.3.0 loads the skills in those folders of its `GEMINI_HOME` but no `GEMINI.md`, `AGENTS.md` or rules file, as a signed-in turn on Linux x64 showed: it listed the linked skills and answered none of the codewords in a linked `GEMINI.md`. Nor does it read the project's `GEMINI.md`, `AGENTS.md` or rules, as the same check showed. So TeamRun reads all the person's files, `GEMINI.md` included, from the home their environment names before the launch points `GEMINI_HOME` at the profile, then the project's, none above the repository's root, in that order, and passes them before the role in a new session's first prompt, and again after a compaction it reports, each under its path; the person's `settings.json`, with its hooks and MCP servers, and their credentials stay out. |
| OpenCode | The person's `AGENTS.md` in `opencode` in `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, else `~/.config`, or without it their `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`; then each `AGENTS.md` from the turn's folder up to the worktree's root, or up to the file system's root outside a repository, nearest first, or without one each `CLAUDE.md`, or without one each `CONTEXT.md`; and the files its `instructions` setting names. | OpenCode reads them itself, in the person's own setup, with their and the project's `opencode.json`, plugins and MCP servers, and TeamRun sends none. |
| Muse Code | What Muse Code itself loads: its project skills and rules load only for a folder it trusts, since TeamRun doesn't pass `--trust-workspace`. This rests on Muse Code's documents, unchecked live. | The same: TeamRun adds none of these files. |
| Pi | `AGENTS.override.md`, `AGENTS.md` or `CLAUDE.md` in the agent folder, `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` or `~/.pi/agent`, then in the turn's folder and each folder above it. | Pi reads them itself, since `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` is the provider's profile; the teammate's role comes before the turn's message. |

A Grok title session, TeamRun's one hidden prompt to Grok, runs in TeamRun's own Grok home, the `title-home` folder beside `profiles/grok/default` in its data folder, signed in through `GROK_AUTH_PATH`, which names the provider's Grok home's `auth.json`, or for the person's home their own `GROK_AUTH_PATH`; it leaves out their `GROK_CONFIG` and `GROK_CONFIG_PATH` and sets to `0` the variables of a launch outside a chat turn and those of Claude's, Cursor's and Codex's rules, skills and agents (`Resources.grokTitleOffVariables`), so it reads none of the person's instructions, memory, configuration or MCP servers.
`GROK_AUTH_PATH` isn't in Grok's documents; Grok 1.0.70 reads the sign-in from it and writes none into the title home, as checked live on Linux x64; it is unchecked on Windows and macOS.
Every other launch runs in the same home as the chat turns.
A launch outside a chat turn, such as a sign-in check, a model list or a usage read, passes `GROK_MEMORY=0`, so it neither reads nor writes the person's memory, and sets to `0` the variables for the managed MCP servers and for Claude's, Cursor's and Codex's MCP servers and hooks (`Resources.grokBackgroundOffVariables`).
Grok 1.0.70 has no variable for the MCP servers and hooks of the person's own `config.toml`, and TeamRun doesn't write that file; a live check found that a model list starts none of them, since it opens no session.
A provider saved with the folder TeamRun once made for Grok, `profiles/grok/default` in its data folder, runs in the person's home too (`GrokHome`), and that folder stays as it was.
The person's home is the one the environment read for each start names, so a `GROK_HOME` set only in their shell profile applies, and TeamRun reads Grok's images and logs in the home the latest launch named.
Grok's usual folder and `personFolders` come from the environment TeamRun started with, since a kind's descriptor is fixed for the run, so with `GROK_HOME` set only in the shell profile the window suggests `~/.grok` while turns run in the home the profile names.
An added provider keeps its own folder as its `GROK_HOME`, and its launches leave out Grok's and xAI's key and token variables, so it uses its own sign-in, while everything else is the person's.
Grok saves a session's images in `<home>\sessions\<the chat's folder, percent-encoded>\<session id>\images`, writing `.tmp` and 6 characters there and then renaming it, as Grok 1.0.70 did on Linux; on Windows, with long paths off, a plain `MoveFileExW` from such a name works only while that folder is at most 248 characters: measured, and at 250 the rename fails and a shell can still write the image.
Since a profile folder and a chat folder are both deep under the data folder, a launch on Windows whose images folder would pass 248 characters names `GROK_HOME` as a short junction to the same home, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\nol\<8 characters of the home's hash>`, kept for later launches (`GrokShortHome`).
No data moves and the chat's folder stays the same, so sessions still resume; TeamRun reads Grok's images and logs through the home itself.
The junction is used only when its path is shorter than the home's: the person's own `~\.grok` is shorter than any junction, so a chat whose own folder is too deep for Grok still fails with it, and a profile folder deep under the data folder, is the one the junction mends.
If the junction can't be made, or its name holds another home's, the launch names the home as before; a home that doesn't exist yet, or a `%LOCALAPPDATA%` that doesn't, gets no junction.
The `nol` folder is made with its parents if missing and never removed, even when empty, so two launches in different processes, such as the app and a dev build, can make it at the same time and both link.
The junction stays when the provider is removed, and it holds nothing of its own.
Grok 1.0.70 keeps a symbolic link's path on Linux, saving through `<link>/sessions/...`, and keeps a junction's on Windows: signed in on Windows x64, its image save failed with a home whose images folder was about 287 characters, and through a junction to the same home, about 150, it saved and named the image by the junction's path.
The other providers' folders, as seen on Linux, don't repeat the chat's folder with this growth: Claude Code's `projects\<the chat's folder, one character for each>\<id>.jsonl` and Cursor's `projects\<the same>\...` keep its length rather than percent-encoding it, which leaves them under 260 characters at these depths; Codex's sessions are by date and GitHub Copilot's by session id, with no chat folder in the path.
OpenCode and Antigravity weren't seen with a chat, since neither is signed in on that machine, but their documented layouts name nothing by the chat's folder: OpenCode keeps sessions and messages in `opencode.db` in its data folder and names a project's snapshots by hashes, and Antigravity names its `brain` and `conversations` entries by the conversation's id, so the chat's depth doesn't lengthen their paths.

## TeamRun's MCP server

This section is the design for TeamRun's MCP server: the server, the member tokens, the agent sessions and the read-only tools are built, the panel tools are built for the pages and chats, and the issues under [Building it](#building-it) build the rest.

### What it is

- TeamRun is the app of Noldova Desktop's `TOOLS.md`: its runtime part registers TeamRun's tools in Noldova Desktop's tool registry, opens an agent session for each teammate's turn, and serves that session's tools to the teammate's provider over MCP.
  So a tool's schema, deadline, limits, failures and permission requests are Noldova Desktop's, and MCP is only how a teammate reaches them.
- Every teammate on every provider gets the server, the default teammate included; no switch turns it off.
- A session lists TeamRun's own tools, Terminal's seven agent tools, computer use's thirteen, the Browser's eight, the Git module's eleven, `git_status`, `git_diff`, `git_log`, `git_branches`, `git_stage`, `git_unstage`, `git_create_branch`, `git_switch_branch`, `git_commit`, `git_push` and `git_pull`, and the GitHub module's three, `git_github_read`, `git_github_pr_checks` and `git_github_pr_create`, by their exact names, beside the provider's own shell tool, and refuses every other module's, one named like Terminal's or computer use's included.
  The Browser's names are the ones browser 0.0.6 declares, and Git's the ones git 0.0.6 and git-github 0.0.6 declare, listed in `Resources.browserTools` and `Resources.gitTools`.
  Computer use's names are the ones computer-use 0.0.2 declares; its runtime package exports no list of them, so `Resources.computerUseTools` lists them and a spec runs computer use's own runtime part to check that it registers exactly those.
  Computer use registers them only while its preview setting is on, and Noldova Desktop activates it only on the platforms its `module.json` declares, Windows and macOS, so with the setting off, or on Linux, a session lists none and refuses a call of one as not registered.
  Every Codex thread's config turns off Codex's own `features.computer_use` and `features.browser_use`, so the computer and browser-use tools a Codex teammate has are TeamRun's: Codex's own computer use needs the Codex desktop app, and on Windows it answered that its native pipe was unavailable.
  A provider's session kept for the chat's next turn serves that turn only while the tools it serves are the same, such as after computer use is turned on: each turn lists their names, and when they differ from those the kept session started with, the adapter reloads a kept Codex thread first and starts Claude Code's query, an ACP agent or Pi again, resuming the session, since TeamRun's server announces no change to its tools and a running client lists them only when it connects.
  A Codex thread whose background work still runs isn't reloaded, so a turn then runs at once with the tools the thread listed; a Claude Code query whose background tasks a tool change ends tells the person in a note.
  The runtime opens each turn's session with Terminal's bindings: the chat's working folder, its project's root or its own folder, as `terminal.folder`, no shared terminals as `terminal.shared`, and the chat's owner `teamrun.chat/<id>` as `terminal.owner`, so the terminals an agent opens are the chat's, in every window that shows the chat.
  It binds Git's `git.folder` to the same working folder, and the Browser's `page` to the chat's own page instance, the chat's id, `profileKey` to the chat's project's id, or `teamrun-chats` for a chat without one, and `agentName` to the teammate's name, so each chat drives its own page, made on first use.
  The Browser's `browser.openPage` takes only a UUID as the instance and a profile key of lowercase letters, digits and dashes, which chat and project ids are; a window spec runs the Browser's own check of the command's arguments on what TeamRun sends.
  The person opens the shown chat's page themself from the top bar's Browser button, `teamrun.chatPage`, the only action at the row's end since New chat left it, or with the command `teamrun.openChatPage`, "Open the chat's Browser page", which the command search finds and a key can be bound to.
  Both run `browser.openPage` for that chat, as a Browser step's page does, which opens the page or brings it forward, and a failure shows as the chat's "Couldn't open the page" notice; with no chat shown both are disabled, and the button's name and tooltip say "Open a chat to open its Browser page".
  A Browser call that fails because the chat's page is closed, `browser.viewClosed`, opens the page through the windows, as `teamrun_open_panel` with `Page` does, and is called again.
  The Browser answers the open before the page's view exists, and TeamRun's runtime can't see another module's views, so a call that still finds the page closed waits 250 ms and is called again, until the page shows, the tool's deadline passes or the call is withdrawn, and then answers the last result.
  When the page doesn't open, the call answers the first failure; that includes while the person types in a chat, since the window refuses to open a panel then and `browser.openPage` always takes the focus.
  Deleting a chat closes its terminals through Terminal's runtime service `terminal.owners`, `closeAsync("teamrun.chat/<id>")`, read with `RuntimeTerminalOwners` from `@noldova/terminal.protocol`, the runtime package's peer, whether or not a window hears the delete; a close that fails, or a Terminal that isn't loaded, is logged.
  A chat without a project that moves to a project keeps the terminals its agents opened, still in its old folder, which TeamRun releases; the next turn reaches them by the chat's owner, and opens new ones in the project's root.
  They close when the person closes them or the chat is deleted.
- The server speaks MCP's JSON-RPC, with only `initialize`, `tools/list`, `tools/call`, `ping` and `notifications/cancelled`, over streamable HTTP at the endpoint, the only way a provider reaches it; TeamRun writes the server itself, as it writes the Codex and ACP clients, and takes from `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` 1.32.1, a direct dependency of the runtime package by the maintainer's decision, only its streamable HTTP server transport and the JSON-RPC schema `McpHttpTransport` checks answers with.
  At the endpoint, `notifications/cancelled` cancels nothing: each POST has its own server, so the notice reaches one that doesn't know the call, and a call ends early only when its request's connection drops, which closes its server and aborts the call.

### Transport, lifetime and the calling teammate

- Every provider reaches the server at one loopback endpoint, since Copilot refuses a client's stdio servers: every provider moved there, and its part 3 removed the stdio bridge that Codex and the ACP providers started before, as the maintainer decided on October 25.
- The endpoint is `McpHttpEndpoint` in the runtime package: streamable HTTP, stateless and with JSON answers, at `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/mcp`, on a port the system picks when the runtime part activates, closed with every connection when it deactivates, and never bound on another address.
  When it can't listen, the runtime part logs why, with no token, and starts without it; every teammate's turn and wake opens TeamRun's tools, so until TeamRun restarts every one of them, on every provider, fails, saying that TeamRun's tool endpoint isn't serving and that the runtime's log says why; an error its server raises once it listens is logged the same way.
  Nothing turns it off: `TEAMRUN_MCP_ENDPOINT=off`, which only fell back to the bridge, went with it, and the end-to-end tests run a terminal turn with it still set, which checks that the packaged app's agent reaches its terminal at the endpoint.
  Noldova Desktop allows an app this under these conditions, which the endpoint keeps:
  - a request must name exactly `127.0.0.1:<port>` as its `Host` and carry no `Origin`, or it gets 403, so a web page can't reach it through DNS rebinding;
  - a missing, malformed, unknown, expired or revoked bearer token gets 401 with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`;
  - only POST is served, since the endpoint opens no event stream: GET and DELETE get 405;
  - each POST gets its own server over the SDK's transport, through `McpHttpTransport`, which checks each answer against the SDK's JSON-RPC schema; its tools are those of the turn the token was issued for, and nothing about a request is logged.
- A provider session's token comes from `SessionTokens` in Core: 32 random bytes that the registry keeps only as their SHA-256, in memory, each naming the turn's tool session it was issued for.
  Each turn's `TurnTools.endpoint` is that turn's `McpEndpoint`, its URL with `issue()`, `issueKept()`, `adopt(token)` and `revoke(token)`, null while the endpoint isn't serving.
  An ACP agent whose `initialize` offers HTTP MCP servers, as Copilot's, Grok's, Antigravity's, Cursor's and OpenCode's do, gets the endpoint in `session/new` and `session/load` `mcpServers` with its process's token as the `Authorization` header, never in its arguments or environment; the adapter revokes the token when that process closes, after a turn or after the background tasks it follows.
  An ACP agent whose `initialize` doesn't offer them fails every turn and wake before its session starts, since each has TeamRun's tools, saying that it takes no HTTP MCP servers and that a version of it that does is needed.
  That token is kept: it outlives its turn's tool session, and a turn the agent starts on its own when a task ends, which opens its own tool session, adopts it as `WakeTurn.useToolsAsync` hands the wake its tools, so the process's calls reach the wake.
  Between turns a kept token reaches no tools, and its calls are answered as any call is with no running turn, `Unavailable (teamrun.noRunningTurn)`, not refused.
  A module's part stops one tool session's turn from the person's own action, such as Browser's Stop or Take over, with Noldova Desktop's `context.tools.stopSession`, and the runtime part's `context.tools.onSessionStopped` listener, which it disposes as it deactivates, hands the session and reason to `ConversationEngine.stopSessionAsync`.
  For a session TeamRun opened whose turn still runs, the engine cancels the reply as the person's own Stop does, and the reply ends with the note "The turn was stopped: <reason>"; until that turn ends, its tool calls, through the endpoint or in process, are answered `Unavailable (teamrun.turnStopped)`, and so is a call already under way, which checks again just before it reaches the tool that its session is neither stopped nor ended; the stop or the turn's end also withdraws each call that reached a tool, so a browser tool that reopens the chat's page to call again doesn't.
  The agent's session stays open, so the chat's next message starts a new turn with the same agent and tools; a session TeamRun didn't open, or one whose turn has ended, changes nothing.
  `SessionTokens.adopt` moves a kept token only to an open tool session of the same membership as the one it was issued for, its owner being the member's token, which the engine keeps and never gives a provider, and never a token that isn't kept, was revoked or has expired.
  A kept token keeps every session that adopted it and reaches the latest still open, so a wake that starts while its turn still runs takes the process's calls while it runs and hands them back to the turn as it closes.
  Codex gets the endpoint in the thread's configuration, `mcp_servers.teamrun` with `url` and with `http_headers` holding a token as `Authorization`, in `thread/start` or `thread/resume` over the app-server's standard input.
  A loaded Codex thread keeps its first connection to the server, so for Codex the provider session is the thread: its token is a kept one, `SessionTokens.issueKept`, which outlives its turn's tool session.
  A later turn on the thread while the adapter still holds it, as a background task does, issues none: once its resume opens the thread, it adopts the token with `McpEndpoint.adopt`, and from then on the token reaches only that turn's tools, never another thread's or another member's; between turns it reaches nothing.
  That turn holds the thread before it reads the token, so a task that ends during the resume can't release the thread and revoke the token under it; a turn that opens no thread, or another one, lets go of that hold.
  When the resume fails and the turn starts a new thread instead, the new thread gets a kept token of its own, so the held thread's calls never reach it, and the held thread keeps its token until it is released.
  When `adopt` refuses the held token, past its lifetime or because the member was added again, the turn says that TeamRun's tools are unavailable until the chat's background tasks end, and the adapter logs it: Codex keeps the token the thread was loaded with while the tasks hold it.
  The token is revoked once the adapter unsubscribes, which unloads the thread, so the next turn's resume connects with a token of its own.
  The held thread's shell keeps the environment it was loaded with too, since Codex ignores a resume's `config` for a thread that still has a subscriber, so that later turn routes the sender token of the turn that loaded the thread to its own reply, through `TurnRequest.adoptSender`, and a `teamrun` command from its shell acts as that reply, not as a turn that ended.
  Codex 0.164.0 did so on Linux x64 with a fake model provider, ignored a resume's new header while the thread was still subscribed, and kept no token under `CODEX_HOME`.
  A token that isn't kept lapses when its turn's tool session closes, and every token 12 hours after its issue in any case; a provider that crashes without closing loses its token's reach at its turn's end, and a restart loses every token, since none is stored.
  The engine routes a session token only to the tool session it was issued for or, a kept one, the latest still open of those that adopted it, never on its own to its member's later turn, and refuses it once the member's token no longer holds, when the teammate left the chat, was added again, or the chat was deleted; so another turn's token never reaches this turn's tools.
- Claude takes TeamRun's server through `setMcpServers`, since the Claude Agent SDK TeamRun uses (0.3.263) passes `mcpServers`' stdio and HTTP entries to Claude on its command line as `--mcp-config <JSON>`, environment and headers included, where a token would be readable in the process list and kept by anything that logs command lines.
  Its query starts with `mcpServers: {}`, since that option goes on Claude's command line as `--mcp-config`, and once the query runs the adapter sends the turn's shared servers and `teamrun`, an HTTP entry with the chat's own token as its `Authorization` header, through the query's `setMcpServers`, which reaches Claude over its standard input; a refusal of the servers fails the turn and closes the chat, and each server the answer's `errors` names is logged with its error.
  The chat's token is kept: `issueKept()` issues it when the chat opens, and it is revoked when the chat closes; a later turn on the chat's open query and each wake adopt it into their own tool session, so Claude's calls reach the turn or wake that runs, and when a turn's tools refuse it, because it expired, was revoked or its teammate was added again, the open query is stopped with its background tasks, its session is resumed on a new chat with a new token, and the turn notes why.
  A wake runs on the open query, so when a wake's tools refuse the token, the wake notes that TeamRun's tools are unavailable in it, the runtime's log names the session, and the chat is marked so the next turn opens a new one; the token isn't replaced through `setMcpServers`, since no run has shown that Claude reconnects to a changed server.
  A test runs the real SDK against a fake Claude program and checks that `setMcpServers` sends the token over Claude's standard input and that Claude's arguments and environment carry none of it, so a change in the SDK's contract fails a test rather than a live turn.
- The calling teammate is named by the session token's owner, a member token of 32 random bytes that the engine keeps in memory and never gives a provider, nor writes to its logs or its database.
  A provider's session token is in its process's memory, which other processes of the same user can read, so the token keeps out other users and other machines, not programs the person runs; a tool that acts as the caller, such as `teamrun_send_message`, can't rest on the token alone.
  The member token names a member: a teammate in a chat, or the chat's default teammate.
  The engine issues it at the member's first turn and keeps it until the member leaves the chat, the chat is deleted or the runtime stops, so a kept session token, a Codex thread's or an ACP process's, still names its member in a later turn.
  Each turn opens its own Noldova Desktop agent session, a wake turn's included, with the scope `<conversation id>/<teammate id>`, or `<conversation id>/default`, and ends it when the provider's run ends, which aborts its calls in flight as `SessionEnded`; Noldova Desktop takes the stop key system-wide only while a session is open, so no session stays open between turns.
  A call whose member has no turn running fails as `Unavailable`, with the module code `teamrun.noRunningTurn`, as a `send` with an ended turn's token is refused today; a call in a turn a module stopped, until that turn ends, fails as `Unavailable` with `teamrun.turnStopped`; a call with a token the engine didn't issue, or no longer holds, fails as `Denied`, and a listing with one is refused as `Unauthorized`.
  A tool's failure reaches the agent as an MCP tool error whose text is `<code> (<module code>): <message>`, or `<code>: <message>` when it has no module code.
- A tool's arguments never name the caller: the tools that act as the caller, such as `teamrun_send_message`, take it from the session.
- A call's deadline is at most 120 seconds and a permission request's wait at most 5 minutes, so TeamRun asks each provider for a tool timeout of 7 minutes where it can set one: Claude through the SDK server's own `timeout`, which the SDK takes in milliseconds and which overrides `MCP_TOOL_TIMEOUT`, and Codex through the entry's `tool_timeout_sec`.
  An ACP agent's own timeout can't be set; where it is shorter, the agent's cancel withdraws the call.

### The tools

Each tool is a Noldova Desktop tool named `teamrun_<name>`, declared in `module.json`'s `contributes.tools`.
A tool that asks raises a Noldova Desktop permission request with the action `teamrun.<name>` in camelCase, such as `teamrun.deleteChat`; the changed item's id as its subject, a chat's and a teammate's ids joined by a dot for a membership, or a new teammate's name; and only the options Allow once and Deny, so nothing is remembered.
TeamRun pre-approves tools to its providers, through the list `RuntimePart` gives each turn, so a provider that can ask, such as Grok, Codex or Copilot, doesn't ask for them as well.
The list holds each tool marked read-only, so a teammate that only reads never stops its turn on a card.
It also holds the tools that change TeamRun, whose ask stays TeamRun's own, as the table says: a provider's ask on top would show the person a second card for the same call.
Those that don't ask change chats and projects the person sees.
`teamrun_send_message` asks, since it can reach further: a send that addresses a teammate starts that teammate's reply in the other chat, and that reply asks for its own steps under that chat's mode, or doesn't if the mode doesn't ask.
Its ask is TeamRun's own at medium risk, the same for the tool and for a `teamrun send` with a sender token, as below, so TeamRun's approval allows it without a card where the sending chat's mode is unrestricted and skips asking, and shows the card in every other mode.
It also holds Terminal's, computer use's, the Browser's and the Git modules' tools, such as `terminal_run`, `computer_click`, `browser_click`, `git_commit` and `git_github_pr_create`, which do change things outside TeamRun: a command runs in a terminal the person sees, computer use and the Git modules' changes, such as `git_commit`, `git_push` and `git_github_pr_create`, ask through the permission requests below by their risk, a page changes in the chat's own Browser page the person can see, and a provider's ask on top would be a second card for the same step.
`teamrun_check_provider` isn't marked read-only, since it can open a session in the provider, so it stays off the list and a provider may ask for it.
The list doesn't reach Antigravity, fx or an agent from the ACP registry or a local command, whose `launch` ignores it; instead, `AcpTurn` allows once, without a card, a permission request the agent's `IAcpAgent.mcpToolOf` reads as a call to TeamRun's server, `teamrun`, for a tool the turn's listing marks read-only, and asks for any other.
So a person's own ask is never widened past TeamRun's read-only tools, not even to the change tools the list holds for the other providers.
An agent built on Gemini CLI names the tool in the request's `_meta["gemini-cli"].mcp` as `server_name` and `tool_name`, checked live from Gemini CLI 0.67.0, the registry's version, in its default mode on Linux x64, where it asked before a tool marked read-only; 0.66.0 asks the same way but sends no `_meta`, so each of its requests still shows a card, as does a request naming no tool, from any other registry agent or local command.
fx 0.0.16 has no reader and still asks: it sends only the name it gave the tool, `mcp_<server>_<tool>` with characters outside `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` made `_`, cut at 64 and a name already taken given `_2`, so a server `teamrun_teamrun` with a tool `list_chats`, loaded from fx's own or a project's MCP settings, gets the same name as TeamRun's `teamrun_list_chats`, and whichever registers first keeps it while the other gets `_2`.
Antigravity's ACP server 1.3.0 names it in `_meta.mcp` as `server` and `tool`, beside `is_mcp_tool_call`, as seen in one turn on Linux x64 in its default mode, where it asked before a tool marked read-only.

| Tool | What it does | Command today | Asks |
|---|---|---|---|
| `teamrun_list_projects` | Lists each project's id, name and folder. | `list-projects` | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_open_project` | Opens a folder as a project. | `open-folder` | No. |
| `teamrun_forget_project` | Forgets a project and deletes its chats; the folder's files stay. | `forget-project` | Yes, high: it deletes chats. |
| `teamrun_list_chats` | Lists each chat's id, title, project, teammates and whether a reply runs, and then the chats without a project. | `list-chats` | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_create_chat` | Starts an empty chat in a project, or a chat without a project, in a folder of its own, when it names none. | `new-chat` | No. |
| `teamrun_rename_chat` | Renames a chat. | `rename-chat` | No. |
| `teamrun_move_chat` | Moves a chat to a project, also an empty chat without a project. A chat without a project whose folder holds files is `Refused`, since only the person copies those files, with Move to project… in the window, which asks first; a count that fails is `Refused` too. | `move-chat` | No; refused for a chat whose folder holds files. |
| `teamrun_delete_chat` | Deletes a chat and its messages. | `delete-chat` | Yes, high; for a chat without a project whose folder holds files, the ask names how many it removes, and a count that fails, such as on an unreadable subfolder, is `Refused` and deletes nothing. |
| `teamrun_add_teammate_to_chat`, `teamrun_remove_teammate_from_chat` | Adds a teammate to a chat, or removes one. | None | Removing asks, medium. |
| `teamrun_list_messages` | Lists a chat's latest messages, or those before a sequence number, naming a sent message's teammate and chat as `list-messages` does. | `list-messages` | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_send_message` | Sends a message to a chat, marked as from the caller, as a token's `send` is today; with `wake_when_done`, wakes the caller's chat with a new turn once the replies end. | `send` | Yes, medium, except in an unrestricted mode: the card names the chat, whose reply the send starts and the message. |
| `teamrun_start_sub_agent`, `teamrun_wait_sub_agents`, `teamrun_stop_sub_agent` | Starts an explorer or a helper sub-agent in the caller's reply, waits for the reply's sub-agents, or stops one. | None | No; a helper asks for its own steps in the reply. |
| `teamrun_run_in_background` | Runs a shell command in the chat's folder as a background task of the caller's reply, and wakes the caller's chat when it ends. | None | Yes, high, by the provider where the chat's mode asks, as for its own command tool; not pre-approved; refused in a read-only mode. |
| `teamrun_cancel_replies` | Stops a chat's working replies. | `cancel` | Yes, medium, unless the chat is the caller's own. |
| `teamrun_list_approvals` | Lists the id, chat, status, summary and options of each approval that waits and of every question asked. | `list-approvals` | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_list_providers` | Lists each provider's id, kind, label and sign-in status, and its plan windows where the provider reports them, as `teamrun_list_teammates` does. | `list-providers` | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_check_provider` | Checks a provider's sign-in. | `check-provider` | No, but a client may ask: it can open a session in the provider. |
| `teamrun_list_teammates` | Lists every teammate, as below. | `list-teammates` | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_create_teammate`, `teamrun_update_teammate`, `teamrun_delete_teammate` | Adds, changes or deletes a teammate: its name, role, provider, model, effort and context window. | None | Yes: high for a delete, medium for the others. |
| `teamrun_open_panel`, `teamrun_close_panel` | Opens or closes the Teammates or Providers page, a chat, a chat's Browser page, or a view: Projects, Activity, Changes, Plan, Sub-agents or Background tasks. | None | No. |
| `teamrun_show_page` | Shows an HTML file from the chat's folder as one of the chat's pages, or, with a `page_id` and the `base_version` read, as that page's next version. | None | No: low risk. |
| `teamrun_list_pages`, `teamrun_read_page` | Lists the chat's pages, or reads one's current version or one of its last 50, with its HTML. | None | No: read-only. |
| `teamrun_edit_page` | Makes a page's next version, the caller's or another teammate's, from new HTML or replacements, based on the version the caller read. | None | No: low risk. |
| `teamrun_canvas_create`, `teamrun_canvas_add_artboard`, `teamrun_canvas_revise_artboard`, `teamrun_canvas_arrange` | Makes a canvas, or its next version: adds a file as an artboard, makes an artboard's next version, or moves, changes and removes artboards and sets its pages. | None | No: low risk. |
| `teamrun_canvas_read` | Reads a canvas's current version or one of its last 50: its pages, artboards, notes, marks and guides. | None | No: read-only. |

- The read-only tools are built: each runs its command-line command inside the runtime, through the runtime part's own method handlers, and answers with the command's text, marked untrusted since it holds what people and teammates wrote; their arguments are the command's values in snake case, such as `chat_id`, and `teamrun_list_messages` lists 20 messages when no `limit` is given.
  A command's failure is `Refused` for a usage error or a method's `InvalidParams`, `NotFound`, `Conflict` or `Unauthorized`, with the module code `teamrun.<code>`, and `Unavailable` otherwise.
  Each has a low risk and a deadline of 30 seconds, 60 for `teamrun_check_provider`, and at most 120.
  Each is marked read-only for the MCP clients except `teamrun_check_provider`, marked open-world and not read-only, and resumable: for some providers, such as Copilot, the check opens a session in the person's own provider home, which may stay in its history, so a client asks the person before it runs.
  A client such as Codex asks the person before calling a tool not marked read-only unless the tool is marked neither destructive nor open-world, as Codex 0.164.0 does in `requires_mcp_tool_approval` in `codex-rs/core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs`, so each read-only tool keeps the read-only marking, the panel tools are neither destructive nor open-world, and Codex asks the person before `teamrun_check_provider`, through the approval the window shows, rather than refusing it.
- The page tools are built as Pages, behind a module boundary so that it can move to its own module repository later, as Browser and Terminal did: its runtime part is the `pages` folder in each concept folder of `src/noldova.teamrun.runtime/src` (`services`, `models`, `interfaces`, `exceptions`), its models and the names of its window methods and events are the `pages` folders of `src/noldova.teamrun.protocol/src` (`models`, `enums`), and its window part will be `pages` folders in the Window package.
  Pages takes nothing of TeamRun's internals: it uses Noldova Desktop's module database, tool, method, event, web view and log types, and TeamRun reaches it only through `Pages` (its migrations, its tools, its window methods and events, a chat's removal and the start's sweep) and gives it an `IPagesHost`, `TeamRunPagesHost`, for a call's chat, folder and author and whether a chat still exists, and an `IPagesClock` for the ping's waits.
  The Runtime package exports only `Pages`, `IPagesHost` and `IPagesClock` of it, and the Protocol package `Page`, `PageAuthor`, `PageVersion`, `PageView`, `PageRequest`, `PageListRequest`, `PageEditedListRequest`, `PageMethodName`, `PageEventName`, `PageKind`, and the canvas's `CanvasManifest`, `CanvasPage`, `CanvasArtboard`, `CanvasNote`, `CanvasMark`, `CanvasGuide`, `CanvasNoteKind`, `CanvasMarkKind` and `CanvasAxis`; the Window reaches its pages folders only through `PageDocumentComponent`, `PageCardComponent` and `PagesViewComponent`.
  The Pages boundary check, `scripts/checks/pages-boundary-check.ts`, holds this for every form of import, including `import()`, `typeof import()` and `import = require()`: a file in a `pages` folder may take only Node, the context packages, Noldova Desktop's runtime and protocol, those Protocol names, its package's base exception, `RuntimeException` or `WindowException`, in the Window package Angular, the kit and Noldova Desktop's window, and other `pages` folders, and a file outside may take or export from a `pages` folder only those entries, each by its own name, or load an entry with `import()`, as a lazily registered view does.
  Each part of Pages keeps its exceptions inside its own folders, under its package's base, the one name outside its folders that the Pages boundary check lets a `pages` file take, so a catch of the package's base catches Pages' failures too: the runtime part's `PageException` extends `RuntimeException`, and in the window part the abstract `PagesException`, with `PageSaveException` and `PagePathCopyException` under it, extends `WindowException`.
  Each takes context's `Exception` instead once Pages moves to its own repository.
  The start's sweep logs a chat whose pages it can't remove, naming the error's kind, and goes on with the next, and logs a listing of the chats that fails and stops, so TeamRun still starts.
  Each tool works within the chat of the call's scope, so another chat's page is unknown, and answers with JSON.
  A page's author is the scope's teammate, or the chat's own teammate when the scope has none, by the session's agent name, so the page names who made it and who made its current version, whichever provider each runs on.
  `teamrun_show_page` reads the file from the session's work folder, the chat's folder or its project's, and refuses one that is missing, outside that folder or reached through a link out of it, not named `.html` or `.htm`, or over 5 MB, counted in bytes as read and again as decoded, so invalid UTF-8 can't grow past it.
  A title is at most 120 characters, counted by code point in the tools' schemas and in the store.
  With a `page_id`, `teamrun_show_page` takes `base_version` and is `Stale` when the page changed since, as an edit is; its description sends a teammate to `teamrun_edit_page` for another teammate's page, and asks for pages styled for light and dark, following `prefers-color-scheme`.
  Its description, and a line of the instructions of a turn with TeamRun's tools, ask a teammate asked to show something as a page or a visual, or to make a page, a report, a dashboard, a chart or a mock-up, to write an HTML file and show it with `teamrun_show_page`, never to serve it on a local port or open it in a browser, which opens a local address only for a site the person allowed.
  `teamrun_edit_page` takes `base_version` and either `html` or `edits`, each a `find` that must appear exactly once and its `replace`, applied in order; an edit from an older version fails `Stale` and names the current version, and a find that isn't there once is `Refused`, naming the edit.
  `teamrun_read_page` answers with the `page`, the `version` read, its `editedBy` and its `html`, which a teammate wrote, so the answer marks it with `htmlTrust: "untrusted"` and the tool's description says to treat it as data.
  A canvas is a page record whose `kind` is `Canvas`; a record stored before has no `kind` and reads as `Page`, so there is no migration.
  Each canvas version keeps its `CanvasManifest` as JSON in the version's content, under the same 50-version trim, and no copy on disk: its pages, at most 40, and on them its artboards, notes, marks and guides, each artboard a version of an ordinary page of the chat, at a place and size.
  `teamrun_canvas_create` makes version 1 with the named pages, or one, `Page 1`; `teamrun_canvas_add_artboard` shows a file as a new page, as `teamrun_show_page` does, and places it on a canvas page, by default to the right of the others there, 80 apart, at 1280 by 800; `teamrun_canvas_revise_artboard` makes the artboard's page's next version from a file, new HTML or edits and points the artboard at it; and `teamrun_canvas_arrange` changes artboards' place, size, canvas page, title, lock, visibility and group, removes artboards, keeping their pages, and sets the pages, refusing one left out that still holds anything.
  Each change takes the canvas's `base_version` and is `Stale` from an older one, and refuses before it writes anything: a canvas's 201st artboard, artboards over 50 MB together, an unknown canvas page or artboard, a canvas page's name or id, a title or a group over 120 characters, or a file the page tools would refuse.
  It then writes the artboard's page version, if any, and the canvas's one version in one transaction, so neither is kept without the other.
  An older canvas version may name an artboard page version no longer kept, since a page keeps only its last 50; `teamrun_canvas_read` says so.
  The page tools, methods and server refuse a canvas's id, and the canvas tools a page's; `teamrun_show_page` with a page id, `teamrun_edit_page` and `teamrun.pageRestore` refuse a page that is an artboard of a canvas's current version, pointing to `teamrun_canvas_revise_artboard`, so an artboard's page changes only with its canvas; `teamrun_list_pages` and `teamrun.pageList` leave out canvases and the pages that are artboards of a canvas's current version, so a canvas of 200 artboards adds nothing to the Pages view; `teamrun_list_pages` lists the canvases under `canvases`, and a removed artboard's page comes back among the pages.
  An artboard's page changing sends no event, and an artboard that `teamrun_canvas_arrange` removes sends `teamrun.pageUpdated` with its page, so the page comes back in the window's pages list; the window's pages list, page cards and page documents leave canvases out until the canvas's own view and cards come.
  A canvas step is named in the reply's activity as the canvas's design writes it: "Made a canvas", "Read the canvas", "Added an artboard", "Revised an artboard" with the artboard's title and its page's version, and "Arranged the canvas" with how many artboards and whether the pages changed; a running one by the title it was given.
  Showing and editing mark their point of no return once every check has passed, just before they write, so a refusal reaches the teammate with its own code rather than as `OutcomeUncertain`, are of low risk, and are among a turn's approved tools, as listing and reading are; each has a deadline of 30 seconds, and at most 120.
- Pages serves its pages to the window from a page server of its own, since a page needs a real origin for its sandbox and Noldova Desktop gives a module none: an HTTP server on `127.0.0.1` at a port the system picks, started with the runtime part and closed with it; a start that can't listen is logged, and `pageOpen` then answers `Unavailable`, while the other page methods still answer from the database; once it listens, a failure of the server, such as an accept that fails, is logged by its kind and the server keeps serving.
  A page's address is `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/pages/<id>?k=<key>`, with `&v=<n>` for one of its kept versions, where the key is an HMAC-SHA256 of the page's id under a secret drawn at each start, so a page can't name another page's address and an address stops working at the next start.
  The server answers only `GET` with the page's own `Host`, and only the page path with its key and at most a version, with the version's HTML; anything else is refused with `403`, `405` or `404`, and a page it can't read with `500`, each with no body.
  Each page carries `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts allow-modals allow-popups-to-escape-sandbox`, which gives the page an opaque origin, so no storage, no cookies and nothing of other pages, with `default-src 'none'`, `base-uri`, `form-action`, `frame-src` and `connect-src` of `'none'`, scripts inline or from cdnjs, jsDelivr and unpkg, styles inline or from Google Fonts, fonts from Google Fonts or `data:`, and images and media from `data:` and `blob:`, as the maintainer decided on Nov 11; and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and `Cache-Control: no-store`.
  Noldova Desktop checks every navigation and request of a view against its allowed origins, so Pages gives every page view the server's origin and the five source origins: each open one once the server listens, which after a restart replaces the old port's origin, each one that opens later on its `Opened` event, and the page's own views again in `teamrun.pageOpen`, whichever reaches the runtime first; on close it takes them away from every page view still open.
  A page view may allow the Pages module's own page origin, as the maintainer decided, with Noldova Desktop's rule to follow: only the module sets it, never a tool or a page, again on each start, and it is taken away on close.
  That origin is `127.0.0.1` today and may become a cloud origin later, a change to the page server alone, since Pages takes the origin only from it.
  A view's origins that can't be listed or set are logged by the error's kind.
  The window's methods each take a `PageRequest`, a page's id and a version or null: `teamrun.pageOpen` answers a `PageView`, the page, its kept versions newest first, the version shown, its address and the path of its saved copy; `teamrun.pageRead` answers a version's HTML as a string, for Save as file; `teamrun.pageRestore` makes a kept version the page's next version, by "You", and sends `teamrun.pageUpdated`; `teamrun.pageList`, which takes a `PageListRequest`, a chat's id, answers the chat's pages, the most recently changed first; and `teamrun.pageEditedList`, which takes a `PageEditedListRequest`, a teammate's id, answers across chats the pages and canvases it made or whose latest version it wrote, the most recently changed first, at most 20, leaving out a canvas's artboards, for its Organization card.
  An unknown page or version answers `NotFound`, and a malformed request `InvalidParams`.
  The events each carry the page: `teamrun.pageShown` when a teammate shows it, new or again, `teamrun.pageUpdated` when an edit or a restore makes its next version, and `teamrun.pageStopped` when an open view of it misses its ping; an event that can't be sent is logged and the tool still answers.
  Every 5 seconds the runtime pings each open page view with `Runtime.evaluate` of `1`, the one method `webViews.json` declares, with a 3-second timeout; a view that times out sends `teamrun.pageStopped` once, until it answers again or closes, and a view that crashed is left to Noldova Desktop's own `Crashed` event.
- The window shows a page as the `teamrun.page` document, its instance the page's id and its tab named by the page with the `web_asset` glyph, from `PageDocuments` in the Window's `services/pages` folder, which registers it and opens a page each time `teamrun.pageShown` arrives; Noldova Desktop's `openDocument` places a new tab only after an open document of the same kind, so a page opens where the window opens a new document, not beside its chat.
  The document opens a web view on its own pane with the profile `pages`, asks `teamrun.pageOpen`, which gives that view its origins, and only then goes to the page's address; the view mounts in its place below the toolbar, which holds it while the page shows and hides it with the stopped page, and the More menu opens over the live view.
  The toolbar holds Reload, the title field, with the page's title and, right after it on the same row, the shown version and who made and edited it, the title cut first and both in the field's tooltip, on hover or focus, while either is cut, Open in the system browser, Save as file, Print or save as PDF, and More: copy the file's path, show it in its folder, Versions, the page's zoom, and Close.
  Every toolbar button has a tooltip; More shows the keys of Zoom in, Zoom out, Actual size and Close, and Print's tooltip is one line with its key, "Print or save as PDF · Ctrl+P", each as the platform writes it.
  Noldova Desktop opens only http, https and mailto links in the system's browser, so Open and Print open the page's address, which works while TeamRun runs, and Save as file writes the shown version's HTML, read with `teamrun.pageRead`, where the person chooses.
  A `State` that leaves the page's origin or path takes the page back to its own, so a page can't navigate itself to one of its source origins, while its own links, fragments and history stay; a `Crashed` view or a `teamrun.pageStopped` for the page shows the stopped page with Reload, as does a page or view that can't be opened or a view the host closed.
  Reload opens a new view when there is none, once for presses that overlap.
  `teamrun.pageUpdated`, and a `teamrun.pageShown` of the open page when a teammate shows it again, reload the page in place at its latest version, with the view's own reload when its address is unchanged, so the page keeps its scroll position, and a bar naming who edited it, a teammate or the chat's own agent, and the version until dismissed, and none for the version the person's own Restore makes, the one `teamrun.pageRestore` answers, which keeps the bar it would have replaced; Versions lists the kept versions, newest first, under a VERSIONS heading in the kit's uppercase menu-group look, and an older one shows read-only with the version in the tab's title and a bar offering Restore, by `teamrun.pageRestore`, and the current version.
  `webViews.json` declares the page's keys, which the document runs: reload, `Mod+R` and `F5`; zoom in, `Mod+Equal` and `Mod+Shift+Equal`; zoom out, `Mod+Minus`; actual size, `Mod+0`; and print, `Mod+P`; Close's `Mod+W` is Noldova Desktop's own close-tab key.
  `PageKeys` in the Window's `services/pages` folder, ported from the Browser's, holds the same list, so the document runs the same keys pressed on its own toolbar and bars, once for a held key and none while composing.
  A zoom press the list doesn't name, such as the number pad's + and - or a layout's own + key, goes to Noldova Desktop's zoom of the view, the same zoom the document's zoom steps.
  While the computer is offline, as the window's `navigator.onLine` says, a bar says sources from outside the page may not load, with Reload.
  The bars are full-width bands under the toolbar in small text, as the design draws them: the update and older-version bars on the selected fill, the offline bar in the warning colour on the kit's warning fill, `--nol-warning-background`, with the kit's default-size buttons: Restore and the offline Reload secondary, Dismiss and Back to the current version quiet.
- The change tools are built: each calls one of the runtime's own methods, through the runtime part's handlers, with the call's arguments as the method's payload, and answers with the method's result as JSON, or `Done.` when it has none, marked untrusted.
  Each marks its point of no return just before it calls the method, and has a deadline of 30 seconds, and at most 120.
  A method's failure is therefore `OutcomeUncertain`, as Noldova Desktop reports any failure past that point, with the module code `teamrun.<code>`, such as `teamrun.notFound`, and the tool's next tool, a listing to check what changed: `teamrun_list_projects`, `teamrun_list_chats`, `teamrun_list_teammates` or `teamrun_list_messages`.
  `teamrun_create_chat` leaves the title to TeamRun when none is given, and `teamrun_update_teammate` sends only the fields it names, so Core keeps the others, except the speed, which a new model or provider clears, and the mode and context window, which a new provider clears; with a model, effort, context window or provider it also sends the provider it read the teammate on.
  `teamrun_send_message` sends with the sender token of the turn whose session made the call, mentioning the teammates its text names, as `send` does with the token from its environment, and is `Refused` once that turn has ended.
  Once it has the token, it has the engine publish `MessageSending`, with a new send id, the chat and teammate of that turn and the chat the message goes to, before its ask; a turn no teammate answers publishes nothing.
  When the ask is denied, expires or is withdrawn, the send is stale, or the send fails, it publishes `MessageSendAbandoned` with that id; a send that stores its message publishes nothing more, since its `MessageCreated` follows.
  Every send with a sender token asks, whatever carries it.
  Core's `send` asks through the sending turn's own approval, so a `teamrun send` from a turn without the tools, such as Pi's, an ACP turn whose tools aren't listed, or a teammate's shell, shows the same card.
  The tool asks through its call's own permission request instead, since Noldova Desktop holds a tool call's deadline only while that request waits, and then sends through `ISenderSends.sendAllowedAsync`, a call inside the runtime that skips Core's ask; no method reaches it, so nothing in a payload marks a send as asked, and the person sees one card either way.
  The ask is `teamrun.sendMessage` with the chat's id as its subject, titled `Send a message to the chat "<title>"`, with Allow once and Deny, and the details Chat, Starts and Message, as `ISenderSends.describeSendAsync` gives them; in an unrestricted mode, Core sends without it.
  Starts names the teammates the send starts, as Core picks them: those the text mentions other than the caller, or else the chat's responder, a teammate that is in the chat and isn't the caller, or the chat's default teammate unless the caller is that default teammate in that chat.
  It leaves out a teammate whose provider is gone, turned off, signed out or of a kind TeamRun no longer has, for which Core starts no reply; when it starts no one, it says the message only joins the chat, or, for a send past the hand-off limit, that the chain has reached it.
  After an allow, Core works Starts out again, and a send whose Starts changed meanwhile, such as one to a chat whose responder the person changed, sends nothing: Core's `send` fails it as `Conflict`, and the tool as `Stale`.
  A send Core would refuse, such as one to a chat that doesn't exist, raises no request and fails as `send` does; a denial, an expiry or a withdrawal fails as a change tool's does, and nothing is sent.
  A send from a provider's own wake that waits behind its chat's other replies asks once that wake's reply starts, since the card belongs to that reply.
  Core's wait for the answer ends when the sending turn ends, and when the request's own signal aborts, such as when a `teamrun send` is stopped, which withdraws the card and sends nothing.
  Noldova Desktop's command line sets no timeout on a module command unless it's given `--timeout`, and TeamRun's `send` gives none, so a `teamrun send` waiting on the card ends only with the answer, the turn, or the provider's own limit on its shell command.
  Its `wake_when_done`, when `true`, asks for a chat wake, and the result's `wakeId` names it; only a turn's send may ask, so `MessageSendParams` refuses `wakeWhenDone` without a sender token, and the command line's `send` has no such option.
- A teammate can't delete the chat it answers in, forget that chat's project, remove itself from that chat, or delete itself, since its reply couldn't record the result; each is `Refused` before anything is asked.
