  Nor can it delete another chat of that project, since TeamRun deletes or moves no chat of a project while a turn runs there, and its own turn does; a chat of another project with a running turn fails as its method's `teamrun.conflict`.
- Before a change that asks, the request shows what the change is on as TeamRun has it then: a project's name, folder and number of chats; a chat's title and project; a member's name and chat; and a teammate's name, role, provider, model and effort, marked as the agent's words where the call gives them.
  A change whose item isn't found asks nothing, and its method's `teamrun.notFound` fails it.
  An answer other than Allow once fails the call as `Denied`, `Expired` or `Withdrawn`, and an item that changed or went while the person decided fails it as `Stale`; either way nothing changes.

- `teamrun_list_teammates`, like `list-teammates`, gives each teammate its id, name and role; its kind; its provider's id, label, email or user name, plan and organization, never a credential, a token or the profile folder, or none for a deleted provider; its model and effort; the chats it is in, by id and title; whether it is answering now; and its usage.
  Usage is, per chat, the latest reply's input and output tokens, cost and context use from `teamrun.teammateUsageList`, and its provider's plan windows from `teamrun.planUsageList`, joined on the provider's id and kind.
  A reply counts only while it is in the teammate's current session in that chat, so a provider change, which starts a new session, leaves the chat with no usage until the new session reports its own.
  A figure the provider doesn't report is left out, never estimated, and a teammate with no report has no usage.
  An ACP agent's reply cost is its `usage_update` cost, the session's so far, only in a new session; a reply in a loaded session has none, since each turn starts the agent again and whether its cost continues after `session/load` is unproven until a recording shows it.
- Only the person answers a provider's approval or a permission request, from the window, as the maintainer decided; no teammate answers one, its own or another's:
  - No command and no tool answers an approval: `approve` left the command line.
  - `teamrun.approvalDecide` is accepted only from the window's own process, the client `ShellClients.mainProcess`, and refused from any other client, such as the command line or a module's own entry, as `Unauthorized`, before Core sees it.
    The runtime part wraps it in `MainProcessMethod`, so a program a provider runs without asking, such as one in Codex's workspace sandbox or in a parallel call, can't answer the approval its turn waits on by calling the runtime as the command line or a module's entry.
  - The answer to a permission request is held to the same rule, through `MainProcessMethod`, once TeamRun answers them as the permissions module.
  - A teammate's permission mode, a chat's Plan first, a plan's approval, the chat's responder, the default teammate and the composer's default are the person's too, through `MainProcessMethod`: `teamrun.conversationSetMemberMode`, `teamrun.conversationSetPlanFirst`, `teamrun.messageApprovePlan`, `teamrun.conversationResponderSet`, `teamrun.defaultTeammateSet` and `teamrun.composerDefaultSave` always, and `teamrun.teammateCreate` and `teamrun.teammateUpdate` when they set `mode`.
    The change tools never set a mode, so they still create and update teammates through their permission card.
  - A send, or a waiting message added or edited, from any client but the window's process can't raise the mode its reply runs in: through `RequestedModeMethod`, Core refuses any requested mode as `Unauthorized` unless the same provider kind and mode are the chat's chosen responder's or the default teammate's, with or without a sender token.
    Modes that act without asking aren't only the unrestricted ones, such as Claude's Accept edits and Auto or Antigravity's Auto Edit, and a provider reports no order among its modes, so only a mode the person already chose passes.
    The command line sends with the default teammate's own mode, so its sends still pass.
    Such a waiting message is marked as from outside for good, and Core checks its mode again as it goes, so lowering the chat's responder or the default teammate after it was added refuses it, kept with `unchosenModeRefused` as its failure.
    An edit from the window keeps the mark, since the edit keeps the mode the other client asked for: retrying a refused message by editing it is refused again, until the person removes it or chooses that mode for the chat.
    A waiting message kept by an earlier version has no mark and goes unchecked after the update; that version accepted it under its own rules and would have sent it the same way.
  - A client names itself in its handshake, and Noldova Desktop checks the process of one that names itself `ShellClients.mainProcess`; what that proves on each platform is in its `RUNTIME.md`, "Endpoint and authentication".
    Until Noldova Desktop checks it there, Linux checks no program, so there a program a teammate runs, such as a plain `node` script, can still name itself the main process and answer; on Windows, a script the app's program runs in Node mode can too, until Noldova Desktop refuses it.
- `teamrun_open_panel` and `teamrun_close_panel` take `panel`, `Teammates`, `Providers`, `Chat` or `Page`, the chat's own Browser page, or a view, `Projects`, `Activity`, `Changes`, `Plan`, `SubAgents` or `BackgroundTasks`, and `chat_id` with `Chat` and `Page` only, and answer TeamRun's own text, such as `Opened the panel Teammates in a TeamRun window.`
  Each asks no permission and is resumable, since opening or closing a panel again changes nothing, with a low risk and a deadline of 30 seconds, and at most 120.
  A chat that doesn't exist, or a `chat_id` given or left out against its panel, is `Refused` before any window is asked, and a panel that isn't one of these is refused by the tool's schema.
- The tool asks one window: the one focused most recently among those whose middle area shows the caller's chat, taken from the call's scope, or else the window focused most recently.
  Noldova Desktop gives a window part no window id, and every window calls the runtime as the app's main process, so the runtime can't tell windows apart by themselves; so each window part's store makes its own random id when it starts and the window reports itself:
  - `teamrun.windowFocused` with its id, the chat its middle area shows and whether the window has the focus, when it activates, on each focus of its window, whenever that chat changes, and when the runtime starts again, whose new engine knows no window; the engine puts it first in its focus order only when it has the focus, so a chat changing in a window behind keeps its place, and a window it doesn't know yet joins last.
  - `teamrun.windowClosed` with its id when it deactivates; the engine drops it.
    The runtime part's `WindowClients` keeps which Noldova Desktop window and connection each report came from, and drops the window too once Noldova Desktop reports that window or its connection closed, or once another page reports from the same Noldova Desktop window, as after a reload.
- The engine publishes `teamrun.panelRequested` with a request id, the chosen window's id, the panel, the chat for `Chat`, and whether to open; every window hears it, and only the named one acts: it opens or closes the Teammates or Providers page, or the chat's document as an ordinary tab, never a preview the next chat would replace.
  For a view, which has no instances, it calls Noldova Desktop's `showView` to open it, so a view that isn't open opens in its dock, an open one activates where the person keeps it, and the dock that holds it shows; and `hideViewDock` to close it, which hides that dock with its other tabs, as the dock's own toggle does, and changes nothing when the view isn't open, isn't in a dock, or its dock is already hidden.
  It then answers `teamrun.panelAnswered` with the request id, its own id and the outcome, `Done`, `Refused` or `Failed`, also when acting threw.
- The person's typing comes first: the person is typing within 2 seconds of a key anywhere in the window while it has the focus, in a chat, a terminal tab, a search or a form, and while a chat's composer has the focus with text in it.
  Meanwhile a window neither opens nor closes anything, since Noldova Desktop's `openDocument` always makes the document the active tab, `showView` moves the focus into the view with no way to leave it, and a close can take the tab being typed in, or hide a dock with a terminal being typed in; the tool fails as `Refused`, "The person is typing in TeamRun, so the panel was left as it is; ask again when they stop."
  Once Noldova Desktop can open a document without activating it, a window opens it that way instead of refusing.
  A window that couldn't act fails the tool as `Unavailable`, "The TeamRun window couldn't change the panel."
- A window that ends without deactivating, such as when its renderer crashes, would stay first in the focus order; so the engine waits at most 5 seconds for the answer, then fails the tool as `Unavailable`, "The TeamRun window didn't answer.", and leaves that window out until it answers, even late, or reports itself again; while every window left is left out that way, a call fails with the same text.
  It doesn't ask the next window then, since a window that is only slow would open the panel as well; a window that closes while it is asked passes the request to the next, and with no window left the tool fails as `Unavailable`, "No TeamRun window is open."
- The same shown chats tell the runtime part whether any window shows a chat, for `teamrun.replyFailed`, and `teamrun.windowReload` with a window's id publishes `teamrun.windowReloadRequested`, for a Try again from another window, or fails `NotFound` for a window the engine doesn't know.
- `teamrun.windowFocused`, `teamrun.windowClosed`, `teamrun.windowReload` and `teamrun.panelAnswered` answer only the app's main process, through `MainProcessMethod`, as `teamrun.approvalDecide` does, and join `module.json`'s `contributes` with `teamrun.panelRequested` and `teamrun.windowReloadRequested`.
- What TeamRun runs outside a provider's sandbox is the person's too, so only the app's main process changes it, through `MainProcessMethod`: `teamrun.acpLocalCommandAdd`, `teamrun.sharedMcpServerCreate`, `teamrun.sharedMcpServerUpdate`, `teamrun.sharedMcpProviderSet`, `teamrun.sharedMcpTeammateSet` and `teamrun.sharedMcpSecretSet`.
  `teamrun.providerUpdate` goes through `ProviderUpdateMethod`, which refuses another client's update only when it moves the provider to another profile folder, compared as resolved paths, or changes its own MCP servers' switch, since a provider such as Codex runs the hooks and commands its profile folder names; the same folder and switch pass, so another client can still rename a provider, though a `binaryPath` in it, an empty one included, still answers only the main process, through `MainProcessMethod`.
  Any other client, such as the command line, a module's own entry or a tool, is refused as `Unauthorized` before Core sees it, so a program a teammate runs can't name a command, a server or a profile folder TeamRun later starts with the person's rights; it can still list them, and remove a shared server.
  `teamrun.providerCreate` goes through `ProviderCreateMethod`, which refuses another client's new provider only when it names a profile folder; without one, the provider gets a folder of its own in TeamRun's data folder, so the command line's `add-provider` names none.
- Activity, Changes, Plan, Sub-agents and Background tasks follow the chat shown in the middle area and show their empty state while none is shown; opening one shows nothing of the caller's chat until that chat is shown, so a teammate that wants its own chat's tasks seen opens its chat as well.

### Permission requests

- TeamRun is the app's permissions module, `desktop.product.permissionsModule: "teamrun"` in the app's `package.json`, and its answerer shows each request as a `Permission` approval on the reply of the turn whose session made the call, with the request's title as its summary, and its details, risk and module in its payload; a secret's value is never kept.
  A request from a session no running turn opened is denied at once and logged, and one that offers neither Allow once nor a labelled Allow always is denied without asking, except computer use's grants below; one that offers a labelled Allow always without Allow once, such as the Browser's ask to open a site the project hasn't allowed, gets a card with that label and Deny only.
- When the app names another permissions module, TeamRun answers no requests and logs why.
- The window shows the request with its risk and details, and when it expires: five minutes after it was asked.
  A provider's approval card says, as the kit card's `kind`, muted under its heading, the reply's provider and the request's kind in plain words, such as `Codex · command`, `Claude Code · file change` or `Copilot · tool`, from the approval's kind: command, file change or tool.
  A permission request's card for a call TeamRun serves says in that line, as the approved design draws it, the module's name, the served tool, the teammate and its chat, such as `Git · git_commit · Codex in "Gemini folder trust"`, from the payload's `servedTool`; a module TeamRun doesn't name shows by its ID.
  Another permission request's card, whose title says what it does, and a card whose reply has no provider, or one TeamRun doesn't know, leave the line out.
  A provider's card's details end with the approval's native kind, as `Kind: <native kind>`: Codex's request's own name, `commandExecution`, `fileChange` or `elicitation`, rather than its method, Claude Code's or Pi's tool name, such as `Bash`, an ACP agent's tool kind, such as `execute` or `edit`, or `other` when the tool call names none.
  A module's permission request shows only its own details, one `label: value` per line, and no details at all when it has none.
  A card shows up to 12 lines of details whole, so a commit or push card shows every line as the design draws it, and a longer request shows 12 and "Show all N lines" (`Resources.permissionDetailLines`, the kit's `detailLines`); its action, such as `git.commit` or `teamrun.deleteChat`, is an internal ID and never shows, as the design's approval cards have it.
  An ACP agent's `execute` card shows the command it asks to run beside the tool call's title, as `<title>: <command>`, from `toolCall.rawInput.command`, cut at 500 characters with an ellipsis and control characters as spaces, as Copilot sends it.
  The title stays alone only when it is exactly the command, or Grok's Execute and the command in backticks, since a title that merely holds the command can hide what runs; Antigravity's and Cursor's titles, which send no `rawInput.command`, stay as they are, as does any title with no command; every one of these is cut and has its control characters replaced the same way.
  The earlier app's approval card showed the kind and the native kind together under its heading, as `kind · nativeKind`; the line keeps that form with the provider and a plain kind in their place, and the native kind is in the details.
  A turn in an unrestricted mode allows a request by itself, but a high-risk request always waits for the person, as does one whose allowing the base remembers, below; its card shows the high risk by its risk mark alone, with no description line, as the approved browser design draws it.
- The answerer allows every request once, with two exceptions, computer use's grants that offer Allow always: the screen, `computer-use.controlScreen`, from computer use 0.0.5, and one app, `computer-use.controlApp`.
  Each card offers Allow in this chat instead of Allow once, and an allowing is answered `AllowedAlways`, which Noldova Desktop remembers for the session's scope, the chat and its teammate, so the teammate's later sessions take the screen, and its later inputs in that app run, from the grant without asking; a request in another chat asks again.
  An unrestricted mode never allows either by itself, so a remembered grant always comes from the person: a grant from the mode would outlive the mode, and for an app the base refuses `AllowedOnce`, which its request doesn't offer.
  The screen's request is of high risk, so it always waits for the person, as does system keys', `computer-use.systemKeys`, which offers Allow once.
- A module that labels its options, as the Browser does from Noldova Desktop 0.0.67, gets them on its card: its Allow always label, such as `Allow for this chat` or `Allow this site for the workspace`, between Allow once and Deny, answered `AllowedAlways`, which the base remembers as the module says; and its Take over label, `I'll type it myself`, after Deny, answered `TakenOver`, on which the module puts the person in the field and fails the call as denied.
  A label shows only when the request offers its option, and Allow always only with a label, so another module's request that offers it alone is still denied without asking.
  Both show their own label once decided, and a take-over reads as a denial on the approval.
- Computer use, a document opened from TeamRun's Settings page and the View menu, lists each computer-use grant Noldova Desktop remembers allowed always, of one app, `computer-use.controlApp`, or of the screen, `computer-use.controlScreen`, shown as "The screen": the app, its chat and teammate, and when it was allowed, oldest first and newest at the bottom, as the window sorts the runtime part's newest-first list; the page still opens at its top, as every settings page does.
  `ComputerUseGrantList` reads them through `context.permissions.listRemembered()` and `ComputerUseGrantRevoke` revokes one through `context.permissions.revoke`, so the base keeps the only copy; TeamRun stores none and logs none.
  A revoke takes only an id the list holds, so it never removes another module's answer.
  The page reads the list as it opens and again after each revoke, so a grant allowed or dropped elsewhere meanwhile shows when the page opens again; its Revoke buttons wait while a revoke runs, and a revoked row goes at once, so a second click never revokes the same grant twice.
  Deleting a chat, or forgetting its project, revokes the chat's grants with it, for every teammate, as it stops the chat's tasks, since the chat's id is never used again; a grant whose chat Core no longer has otherwise, such as one whose chat was deleted by an earlier version, is listed as "Deleted chat" until it is revoked.
  Each app shows the name computer use gives it and its icon, at the kit's icon size beside the name, through computer use's apps service, `computer-use.apps`, from computer use 0.0.12, the version `module.json` names.
  Computer use caches no description and describes one app at a time, bounding each at 5 seconds, so TeamRun's grants service keeps each identity's description it finds for `Resources.appDescriptionLifetime`, 10 minutes, while the runtime part is active.
  It keeps no answer that an app has none, which the runtime part also gives while computer use is off, so an app is named at the first listing after computer use starts.
  A listing shows the kept descriptions at once and asks only for the apps with none kept or one past that lifetime, all at the same time, so an app that changes its name or icon shows the change within 10 minutes.
  Those descriptions share one deadline, `Resources.appDescriptionsWait`, 1 second, through the signal the listing passes them, which also aborts when the request ends: at the deadline computer use's queued and running descriptions stop, and an app not yet described shows its kept description or its identity.
  The service waits out the deadline on the timer it is given, which the runtime part passes as a `NodeTimer` with `ComputerUseGrantTimings.createDefault()`, and ends that wait as the listing returns.
  Since a listing asks only for what it lacks, with more apps than one second allows the names fill in over the next listings without holding computer use's channel, which agents need on macOS; a description that fails, or answers after its deadline, is never kept.
  Two listings at once, such as a revoke's re-list while the page loads, share an app's description in flight rather than ask for it twice, and a revoke drops the revoked app's kept description and any in flight, whose answer is then not kept.
  An app with no description shows its identity's last part as before, and an app without an icon, or whose icon doesn't decode, keeps a blank slot, so every name starts at the same edge.
  The runtime part finds the service with `context.findService` for each app it describes, and takes it only when it has a `describeAsync` method, so while computer use isn't active, at another version or in the app at all, every app shows its identity.
  After an app's revoke, computer use refuses the chat's next input to the app without asking, since it asks for each input with `isRememberedOnly`; the chat asks for the app again to use it.
  The screen's grant works differently, so its revoke does too: computer use asks for the screen only as a session starts, and the session holding the screen keeps it until it ends, so after the revoke the chat's next session asks the person again.
  When the app names another permissions module, both methods fail as `UnknownMethod`, and so do they in the command-line client.
- Only the person answers one, from the window, as above, and an answer after the request's expiry or withdrawal counts for nothing, as `PERMISSIONS.md` says.
- A teammate's provider doesn't ask the person again before a call of TeamRun's change tools, Terminal's tools, computer use's tools, the Browser's tools or the Git modules' tools, since each asks for itself where it must, computer use through the permission requests below, nor before `teamrun_open_panel` and `teamrun_close_panel`, which ask nothing and aren't marked read-only: Claude's `canUseTool` allows them; Codex runs them without asking, since TeamRun's entry sets each one's `tools.<name>.approval_mode` to `approve`, which Codex 0.164.0 reads before the server's default in `tool_approval_mode` in `codex-rs/codex-mcp/src/server.rs`.
  `teamrun_send_message` is among those change tools, as `teamrun_delete_chat` is: each asks through TeamRun's own approval, so a provider the list reaches runs it without an ask of its own and TeamRun's card is the only ask.
  Antigravity, fx and the agents from the ACP registry or a local command don't take the list, as above, so one that asks before an untrusted server's tools shows its own card first, then TeamRun's.
  Grok takes no `--allow` in `grok agent`, and TeamRun never writes `[permission]` into the person's Grok configuration, so TeamRun answers Grok's ask itself.
  Grok asks about a `use_tool` call of TeamRun's server by its full `teamrun__<tool>` name, as Grok 1.0.70 did for a tool no rule allowed, and TeamRun allows a call to a tool the turn lists and the person approved without asking the person (`AcpTurn`'s `approvedTools`).
  A call by a bare name asks the person, and Grok then fails it as no MCP tool's name.
  The person's own `[permission]` rules still apply as in their own Grok: a deny rule for `MCPTool(teamrun__<tool>)` stops the call before TeamRun sees it, and an allow rule runs it without an ask.
  A project's own `.grok/config.toml` `[permission]` rules apply only in a folder the person trusted, since TeamRun keeps Grok's folder trust on (`GROK_FOLDER_TRUST=1`), which is also Grok's default; Grok 1.0.70, folder trust on, still asked before a tool that only an untrusted project's rule allowed.
  Claude's own rules apply the same way in a chat turn: an allow rule in the person's settings, or in a trusted project's `.claude/settings.json` or `.claude/settings.local.json`, runs a tool without an ask, a shared server's or one of the person's own servers' included, a deny rule stops it, and a `PreToolUse` hook that allows or denies a call settles it, each before `canUseTool` is asked; an untrusted project's rules and hooks don't load, as in the person's own Claude Code.
  Copilot starts with `--allow-tool=teamrun(<tool>)` for each approved tool whose name holds only letters, digits, `_` and `-`, so that no name widens the rule into a pattern, and Copilot 1.0.104 then runs them without asking in ACP mode and still asks before the others.
  Copilot takes a client's MCP server from `session/new` only over HTTP or SSE and refuses a stdio one, so a Copilot teammate reaches TeamRun's tools, and these rules, at the endpoint.
  Codex still asks before TeamRun's other tools as their markings say, such as `teamrun_check_provider`.
  A call of a shared server's tool asks the person as any other tool call does: signed in on Linux x64, Cursor 2026.10.22-27dcf5f asked for a shared stdio and a shared HTTP server's tool, and Copilot 1.0.105 for a shared HTTP server's, and once refused neither ran the tool.

### Questions

- A teammate's provider can stop its turn to ask the person questions, and TeamRun shows them as a question card in the reply, as it shows an approval.
- A reply shows each question and approval card where it was asked, by `createdAt`, after the last part of the reply that began before it, so an answered question stays above a plan or text that came after it.
  The cards asked after every part so far stay at the reply's end, below its edited files and written images.
- A question is an approval of the kind `ApprovalKind.Question`: its `questions` hold each question's id, header, text, options with their descriptions, and whether it takes several choices, the person's own text, or a secret.
  Its options are Answer and Skip, and `teamrun.approvalDecide` takes the answers with Answer: each question's choices and its own text.
- The turn gets every answer, and the stored approval keeps the answers to the questions that aren't secret, so a secret answer never reaches the database, an event or `list-approvals`.
  A question a turn posts, whose answer goes back as the chat's next message, keeps and sends only the answers that aren't secret, and an answer of secrets alone sends nothing.
- A sub-agent's questions name it: the provider passes the sub-agent's description in the `QuestionAsk`, as Claude Code's and Codex's sub-agents do, and the approval's payload keeps it as `subAgent`, as an approval's does.
  The question card then says "<sub-agent> asks" under its heading, and the sub-agent's row shows it waits, as for an approval.
- A Codex MCP tool call that needs approval reaches the person as a `Tool` approval.
  Codex asks with `mcpServer/elicitation/request`: a form with no fields whose `_meta.codex_approval_kind` is `mcp_tool_call`, and Codex's message is the summary, as Codex 0.164.0 builds it in `codex-rs/core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs` and types it in `codex-rs/app-server-protocol/src/protocol/v2/mcp.rs`.
  "Allow for this session" is offered only when `_meta.persist` holds `session`; the answer is `accept`, with `_meta.persist: session` for the session, or `decline`.
  Any other elicitation through Codex, such as a form with fields, a URL or a user verification, is answered `cancel` with a note in the chat, never refused unseen: an error answer reads to Codex as the person's refusal.
- Only the person answers, from the window, as above; the window grants nothing by itself, and a mode that asks for nothing never answers a question.
- Each provider asks in its own way, and TeamRun answers in the same way:

  | Provider | How it asks | Answered | Skipped, or questions TeamRun can't read | The reply stops while they wait |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Claude | `canUseTool` for the `AskUserQuestion` tool; Claude Code adds its own "Other", so every question takes the person's own text; an option's `preview` isn't shown | Allowed, with each answer under its question's text, its choices and own text joined by ", " | Denied, saying the person skipped them or that TeamRun couldn't show them | Denied, saying the person didn't answer them |
  | Codex | The `item/tool/requestUserInput` request, one choice per question, with `isOther` and `isSecret`; a question with no options takes the person's own text, as Codex's own client does | Each answer under its question's id, as its choices and its own text prefixed `user_note: `, after "None of the above" when the question has options and none was picked, as Codex's own client sends it | No answers; a question TeamRun can't read is left out with a note in the reply, and the others are asked | No answers |
  | Codex, async | gpt-6.1-sol's `request_user_input_async` tool, when Codex offers it: the tool returns at once, and the completed `agentMessage` item, with `delivery` `async`, carries `questions`, each a `title` and its `options`, as Codex 0.163.0 builds it in `codex-rs/core/src/tools/handlers/send_message_to_user_async.rs`; every question also takes the person's own text. The card doesn't hold the turn and stays open after it ends | A user message to the same teammate, queued after a running turn: `<send_user_message_question_reply>` around `{ questionItemId, question, answer }` per question, as Codex's own client sends it, while the chat shows each question quoted with its answer below | Nothing is sent, and the card closes; a question TeamRun can't read is left out, and the others are asked | The card is cancelled when the person stops its reply, not when the turn ends; a restart keeps it open, and an answer stored before a quit but not yet sent goes at the next start |
  | Copilot, Grok | ACP's `elicitation/create` in form mode, once TeamRun says it takes forms; each field is a question: a string with `enum`, `oneOf` or `anyOf` takes one choice, with each option's description, an array of them several, a boolean Yes or No, and any other string or number the person's own text | `accept`, with each field's value | `decline`, also for a number that isn't one and for a request that names no session; `cancel` for another session or a finished turn | `cancel`, also when the agent's `$/cancel_request` names the request |
  | Grok's own questions | The `x.ai/ask_user_question` request from its `ask_user_question` tool, each question named by its text: one choice, or several with `multiSelect`, with each option's description, and the person's own text; an option's `preview` isn't shown, and its Plan mode's "Chat about this" and "Skip interview" aren't offered | `accepted`, with each answered question's choices under its text, or "Other" when it has only the person's own text, and that text as the question's `notes` in `annotations`, as Grok's request types define it | `cancelled`, which Grok tells its model as the person declining; also for questions TeamRun can't read, such as two with the same text, another session or a finished turn | `cancelled`, also when the agent's `$/cancel_request` names the request |
  | Antigravity | ACP's `session/request_permission`, as Antigravity's ACP server 1.3.0 sends it: a tool call with no kind, an id starting `interaction_` and its question as the title, and one `allow_once` option per answer, named by it; one choice, and no text of the person's own, which Antigravity's answer has no place for. Any other permission request, such as one with a kind, an other id or a reject option, is a permission card as usual | `selected`, with the id of the option the chosen answer names | `cancelled`, also for a question TeamRun can't read, another session or a finished turn | `cancelled` |
  | Cursor | Cursor 2026.10.22 sends none: its ACP session has no question tool, and asked to use one it says so. TeamRun still reads the `cursor/ask_question` request, for a version that sends it, each question and option named by its id: one choice, or several with `allowMultiple`, with the request's title as the header and no text of the person's own, which Cursor's answer has no place for | `answered`, with each question's chosen option ids, as Cursor's ACP documents define it | `cancelled`, also for questions TeamRun can't read, another session or a finished turn: a skip reaches the reader as no answers, so Cursor's `skipped` isn't sent | `cancelled`, also when the agent's `$/cancel_request` names the request |
  | OpenCode | Asks in plain text: its ACP bridge forwards no questions. OpenCode 1.19.4 offers its `question` tool only to its own clients, and `opencode acp` runs as the `acp` client, whose events (`src/acp/event.ts`) cover session status, permissions and message parts but no question | Not asked | Not asked | Not asked |
  | fx | Asks in plain text: fx 0.0.16's `ask_user_question` tool works only in its own interactive shell, and over ACP tells its model to ask freeform instead. A question from one of fx's MCP servers comes as ACP's `elicitation/create`, answered as in the Copilot, Grok row | Not asked | Not asked | Not asked |

- TeamRun doesn't check an ACP field's `default`, `required` or constraints, such as a length, a pattern or a range: it asks every field, and the agent checks the values it gets.
- Codex's `functionCallOutput` items, which carry a tool's output and so a `request_user_input` answer, are kept as their type alone, so a secret answer never reaches the database through them.

- The card says "<name> asks you", where the name is the one its reply's header shows: the teammate's, or its provider's for a teammate without a name, as a permission card names its requester before the kind "Teammate".
  It gives each question its header, its text, "Pick any" when it takes several choices, its options, and an "Other…" field when it takes the person's own text, masked for a secret.
  Answer waits until every question has an answer; while only questions wait, the reply's status and its chat's mark in Projects say "Waiting for your answer", and "Waiting for permission" while a permission request waits too.
  Once answered, a question card folds to each header with its answers, a secret one shown as hidden; a skipped or withdrawn card says so in its heading, and it stays in the reply's history.
  The card forgets what was typed once the questions are no longer open.
- The options are the kit's choice rows: a question that takes one choice is a radio group named by its text, and one that takes several is a group of checkbox rows.
  While only questions wait, the reply's open step says "Waiting for your answer" too, with the kit's status for it.

### Shared MCP servers and the switches

- TeamRun keeps one list of shared MCP servers in its database, each with a name and either a command, its arguments and its environment, or an HTTP address and its headers.
  A name matches `^[a-z](?:[a-z0-9-]|_(?=[a-z0-9-])){0,63}$`, 1 to 64 characters with no `__` and no trailing `_`, so that the server in a tool's name such as Claude's `mcp__<server>__<tool>` is read exactly, and is never `teamrun`.
  Saving one, new or changed, also refuses a name that starts with `teamrun_`, since OpenCode's rule for a tool of TeamRun's, `teamrun_<tool>`, would match a tool of a server so named and run it unasked; one already saved under such a name stays readable, so its tools still reach the providers until it is renamed.
  The command, its arguments and the address hold no secret: an address with a user name, a password, or a query parameter named like a key, token, secret, password, credential or signature is refused, and a secret goes in a named variable, a header's or the bearer token's.
  A variable reaches a stdio server only through its environment, never its arguments.
  A server with a bearer token's variable names no `Authorization` header, in any case.
  An environment variable's or a header's value is never kept in TeamRun's database: the entry names a variable, and each variable takes its value from one of two places, while a server that signs in takes its bearer token from its sign-in.
  - From this machine's environment, the default: the person's current environment, whose value TeamRun reads as it starts the session, as Codex's `bearer_token_env_var` does.
  - A saved secret: the person types the value once in the server's dialog, the Window part saves it in Noldova Desktop's secret store under a new name, `teamrun.mcpSecret` and 32 lowercase hexadecimal digits, and the server keeps only that reference, in its `secrets`, by variable.
    The Window part only saves; only the runtime part reads a value, and only as it picks a turn's servers, into a `SharedMcpValues` it hands the adapter on `TurnRequest.sharedValues`, which holds the values in a closure, so no `toJson`, error, log line or debug dump reaches them.
    The runtime part removes a saved value when its variable takes another reference or the environment again, once the new reference is kept, so a turn starting meanwhile still reads a value; when an edit drops the variable; and when the server is deleted; a refused removal is logged by the variable's name only.
    A reference another variable, of this server or another, already keeps is refused, so removing one variable's value never takes another's.
    A server whose saved secret is missing, for example removed outside TeamRun, or can't be read is left out of the turn with why, "The saved secret for <variable> isn't on this machine." or "TeamRun couldn't read the saved secret for <variable>. <reason>", never as not set.
    A saved value goes to a provider only where the provider is confirmed to keep it out of its session files, the maintainer's Oct 24 rule below: Claude takes it for a stdio server, through `setMcpServers` as it takes the environment's; an ACP agent takes it wherever its confirmed `sharedServerTransports` take the server, on `session/new` and `session/load`.
    Claude's HTTP servers can't take one until a capture shows Claude keeps a literal header out of its session files, since their headers reach Claude as `${VAR}` references, and putting a value in Claude's environment would hand it to every tool Claude runs; Codex can't take one, since it reads each value from its own environment by name.
    Each shows the server unavailable with why, as `IProviderAdapter.savedSecretsUnavailable` says, and an adapter without it takes no saved secret.
  - An OAuth sign-in, for an HTTP server whose `signsIn` is set, with no bearer token's variable and no `Authorization` header.
    Sign in on the MCP servers panel starts it: the runtime part's `SharedMcpOauth` asks the server for its 401 and reads `resource_metadata` and `scope` from `WWW-Authenticate`, then the MCP SDK's client `auth()`, already a dependency of the runtime, finds the authorization server, registers TeamRun as a client, and builds the authorization address with PKCE, which the Window part opens in the browser.
    The browser comes back to a listener on 127.0.0.1 at a port the system picks, `/callback`, which takes one answer and closes, or closes after five minutes with "The sign-in wasn't finished within five minutes.", and logs a failure of its own by its kind, such as an accept that fails; an answer whose `state` isn't the one sent, or that carries an error, ends the sign-in refused.
    A request for another path, or whose target isn't an address it can read, gets 404 and the sign-in keeps waiting.
    Once an answer is taken, the five minutes no longer count and any other answer gets 404, so the sign-in ends only after its code is exchanged.
    A server has one sign-in at a time: starting another, under the same reference, which Core claims before the runtime starts it so that two starts together share it, ends the earlier one without an answer, and the panel shows "Waiting for the browser." on the server's row until the latest one ends, or until the latest start fails or ends with no address, which leaves no sign-in running.
    A start that comes once a sign-in's exchange has begun starts a new one under the same reference, which that exchange's end leaves waiting; one that comes while or once a Sign out, an edit or a delete ends the sign-in, even with none pending, reads the server again behind that removal and starts a new one under a new reference, which the removal leaves running, since a removal ends only the sign-in pending as it began; one that comes during a delete fails as the server is gone, starting nothing.
    The runtime claims the reference before the start's first request, so a Sign out or a later start while the earlier one still asks the server or registers ends it with no address, and the window opens no browser for it.
    A sign-in that ends refused never replaces a granted one; it replaces only a refused one or none.
    The code is exchanged for tokens, and the client, the tokens and when the access token expires are saved in Noldova Desktop's secret store under one reference, `teamrun.mcpSignIn` and 32 lowercase hexadecimal digits; the server keeps only that reference and the sign-in's state in its `signIn`: when the token expires, why a new sign-in is needed if one is, and when that was last set.
    The runtime publishes `teamrun.stateInvalidated` when a sign-in starts and when it ends, so the panel reads the servers again.
    As it picks a turn's servers, the runtime part reads the access token, refreshing it first when it expires within a minute, or, for a token without an expiry, when the server answers it with 401, a server that doesn't answer within five seconds counting as one that can't be reached, with the reads of one sign-in sharing one refresh, and passes the server to the adapter as a copy whose bearer token's variable is `TEAMRUN_MCP_ACCESS_TOKEN`, with the token as that variable's value in `SharedMcpValues`, so a provider takes it exactly as a saved bearer token, where `savedSecretsUnavailable` allows.
    Where it doesn't, the server is listed as unavailable to that provider with "TeamRun doesn't pass a signed-in server's token to <provider> yet, since it passes <provider> no saved secret for this server.": today Claude, which takes saved secrets for stdio servers only, and Codex and Pi, which read values only from their own environment.
    A server with no sign-in, or whose token can't be read or renewed, is left out of the turn with why, and a refused renewal sets why a new sign-in is needed on the server; the next turn tries again.
    Sign Out, an edit that turns `signsIn` off or changes the address, and a delete remove the saved sign-in; a refused removal is logged by the server's name only, and a sign-in that ends after its server was deleted or changed is removed.
    The removal ends a running sign-in without an answer, a server's first one too, and waits for an exchange or a refresh of the same sign-in already under way, which the runtime runs one at a time per reference, so neither saves tokens after it.
    An exchange that still finishes after its server's Sign out, edit or delete is never saved: its tokens are removed and the server stays signed out.
    Each request of a sign-in, a refresh or a check to the server or its authorization server is given five seconds, so a server that never answers ends it rather than holding the next turn or a removal.
    Sign Out is local only: TeamRun forgets the tokens but doesn't call the authorization server's `revocation_endpoint`, so the tokens stay valid at the server until they expire.
  - Another module's account, for an HTTP server whose `account` names it, with no bearer token's variable, no `Authorization` header and no OAuth sign-in; `GitHub`, the GitHub module's, is the only one.
    `SharedMcpServerDefinition.gitHub` is GitHub's own MCP server, `github` at `https://api.githubcopilot.com/mcp/` over HTTP, signing in with the GitHub account; added, it is off for every provider, as every shared server is, and an edit keeps its account.
    An account's token goes only to that account's own MCP origin: `SharedMcpServerDefinition` refuses, on `url`, a server whose `account` is `GitHub` at any origin but `https://api.githubcopilot.com` (`Resources.formatSharedMcpAccountAddress`), so another host or a look-alike never gets the token, and the dialog shows such a server's address read-only, with `Resources.sharedMcpAccountUrlHint`, and saves it as it was.
    The panel offers it, "Add GitHub's server" a space-2 before Add server, while the person is signed in to GitHub and no listed server is named `github` or is at GitHub's MCP origin, and adds it at once, with no dialog; the Window part reads the sign-in with `git-github.readSignIn` and follows `git-github.signInChanged`, and a GitHub module that doesn't answer counts as signed out.
    The server's dot is amber while the person isn't signed in to GitHub; its switches stay on this panel, by the maintainer's decision, for now.
    The runtime part asks `ISharedMcpAccounts` for the account's token, in `SharedMcpServersService.accountAsync` alone, as it lists the servers and as it picks a turn's servers, and passes the token as it passes an OAuth access token: a copy whose bearer token's variable is `TEAMRUN_MCP_ACCESS_TOKEN`, the token only in `SharedMcpValues`, and only where `savedSecretsUnavailable` allows, a provider that takes no saved secret listing the server unavailable without the account being read.
    An account that answers refused, such as one signed out, or whose sign-in can't be read leaves the server out of the turn with why and lists it unavailable, its dot amber, "needs a sign-in"; the token is never typed, shown, logged or saved by TeamRun.
    Noldova Desktop lets no module read another's secrets, so the GitHub module hands its token over through the service it publishes, `git-github.access` (the maintainer's decision of Nov 7, 2026): TeamRun names `git-github` as an optional dependency, and the runtime's `SharedMcpAccounts` finds the service with `findService` and its own guard, `isGitHubAccess`, and asks it, within five seconds, each time; a GitHub module that isn't on refuses with "The GitHub module isn't on, so TeamRun can't use its sign-in.", and a signed-out one with its own reason.
  Claude, whose `--mcp-config` would carry the values on its command line, takes its servers through `setMcpServers`, over its input, instead.
  An HTTP server's headers and bearer token are written as `${VAR}` references, which Claude expands from its own environment.
  A stdio server's environment gets the values themselves, read from Claude's environment as the session starts, and a variable that isn't set is left out; Claude doesn't expand a reference there.
  Every stdio server inherits Claude's whole environment, whatever its entry holds: with Claude 2.1.263 on Linux x64, a server given `env: {}`, one given a single entry and one given no `env` each got every variable in Claude's environment, which in TeamRun holds the sender token and every shared server's values, and Claude's own `CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN`, with the single entry added on top.
  So every shared stdio server, for every provider, starts through TeamRun's launcher (the maintainer's decision): the provider runs TeamRun's runtime as Node, with `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE=1`, on `launch-shared-mcp-server.js` beside `SharedMcpLaunch`, with `--variables <names> -- <command> <arguments>` as its arguments and the server's values in its environment, so no value is on a command line.
  The launcher starts the command with only the basics and the named variables from its own environment: outside Windows `HOME`, `LOGNAME`, `PATH`, `SHELL`, `USER`, `__CF_USER_TEXT_ENCODING`, `LANG`, `LC_ALL`, `TERM`, `TMPDIR` and `TZ`, Codex's `DEFAULT_ENV_VARS` in `codex-rs/rmcp-client/src/utils.rs` at `rust-v0.165.0`; on Windows Codex's `WINDOWS_CORE_ENV_VARS` in `codex-rs/protocol/src/shell_environment.rs` at that tag, matched in any case, to which libuv adds `LOGONSERVER`, as it does for every process Node starts.
  It also passes each certificate variable that is set and not empty, made absolute against its own folder and, on Windows, under Codex's spelling in place of any other case, as Codex's `utils.rs` does with `CUSTOM_CA_ENV_KEYS` from `codex-rs/network-proxy/src/certs.rs` at that tag: `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE`, `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`, `CURL_CA_BUNDLE`, `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, `GIT_SSL_CAINFO`, `CARGO_HTTP_CAINFO`, `PIP_CERT`, `BUNDLE_SSL_CA_CERT`, `npm_config_cafile` and `NPM_CONFIG_CAFILE`, so that a server behind a TLS-inspecting proxy trusts its certificate as it does under Codex.
  It never passes `TEAMRUN_SENDER_TOKEN`, in any case, even when a server names it, as Codex never passes its `NON_INHERITABLE_ENV_VARS`; and no provider takes a server that names it in its variables, headers or bearer token, which shows unavailable, "TeamRun keeps TEAMRUN_SENDER_TOKEN, TeamRun's own sender token, from the MCP servers it passes <provider>."
  It passes the server's input, output and errors through untouched, passes on `SIGTERM`, `SIGINT` and `SIGHUP`, and exits with the server's code, or 128 and the signal's number.
  On Windows the launcher finds a command named without a folder itself, only in the absolute folders `PATH` lists, with `PATHEXT`, never in the folder it runs in, which Windows would look in first, and refuses one `PATH` doesn't hold as Node refuses a missing command, `spawn <command> ENOENT`, exiting with 1 before it starts anything.
  On Windows a `.cmd` or `.bat` command, found that way, runs through `COMSPEC`, or the system folder's `cmd.exe` when it is unset or not an absolute path, with `/d /s /c` and arguments the launcher quotes and escapes itself, twice, as cross-spawn does for npm's shims: a batch file that passes `%*` on, as npm's `npx.cmd`, `npm.cmd` and every shim under `%APPDATA%\npm` do, has cmd read its arguments again, so escaped once, an argument with an odd number of quotes would leave the next one's `&` free to start a command.
  So that the server ends with the launcher however the launcher ends, the launcher first starts a watch apart from itself, fed by a pipe only the launcher holds, which ends when the launcher exits or is killed, then the server, and writes the watch a line of the server's process id in the same step as the start returns, before it waits for anything, and a second line, `exited`, once the server has exited.
  Outside Windows the server starts held: `/bin/sh` waits for a line on a fourth pipe from the launcher, which the launcher writes only after the watch's line, and then runs the command in its own place through `/usr/bin/env -u`, without the `PWD` and `SHLVL` the shell sets itself unless the server names them, since bash as `/bin/sh` sets `SHLVL` again on `exec`, so a launcher killed between the two leaves a shell that exits with 1 having started nothing.
  A command the shell doesn't find, or finds as a file it can't run, such as one without its execute bit or a folder, is answered on that pipe, and the launcher says so as Node does for a command it can't start, `spawn <command> ENOENT` or `spawn <command> EACCES`, and exits with 1, while a server's own exit 127 or 126 stays as it is.
  The server runs in its own process group, and the watch sends the group `SIGTERM`, then `SIGKILL` after 2 s; a watch whose pipe ends without the line ends nothing there.
  On Windows, ending the launcher ends the server through Node's job object but not what the server started, such as the `node` under an npm shim, so the watch ends, in one PowerShell run, the server and each process under it that started no earlier than its parent.
  The watch and its PowerShell run in the Windows system folder, `System32` under `SystemRoot` (or `WINDIR`, or `C:\Windows`), and the PowerShell is the one there, `WindowsPowerShell\v1.0\powershell.exe`, so neither takes a program of that name from the chat's folder.
  The server, and every program TeamRun starts on Windows through either process starter, starts with `NoDefaultCurrentDirectoryInExePath` set (`SharedMcpLauncher.environmentToStart`), and the programs they start inherit it, so `cmd.exe` running an npm shim and Node looking up a child's program find a command named without a folder only on `PATH`, never a file of that name in the chat's folder.
  It compares only the times Windows gives the processes, never one Node.js read, since the two clocks can disagree by more than the few milliseconds between a start and the time read around it (#443): the server counts when it started after the watch, from the watch's parent, the launcher, and the launcher didn't say it exited; otherwise, as when the shim the launcher started has ended with the launcher, or another process has taken its id, each process the server's id started after the watch counts, and before the process that now holds the id, if one does.
  A PowerShell that hasn't ended within 10 seconds, as one whose session stalls on a busy machine, is ended and the run starts once more in a new PowerShell with the rest of the end's 20 seconds, which is ended too if it hasn't ended by then (#383).
  The watch on Windows writes to the launcher's errors, which the provider logs or passes on, a line with a PowerShell's time and outcome when it takes over 2 seconds, doesn't end, can't start or exits with an error, as when its listing fails or it doesn't find the watch in it, and a line when a launcher ended before its server left nothing of the server to end; it writes nothing otherwise.
  A watch whose pipe ends there without the line ends, the same way, each process the launcher started after the watch, other than the watch and before another process took the launcher's id, with what those started, and nothing while the launcher still runs, as when the server couldn't start.
  This follows the maintainer's Oct 24 rule that a value goes over the provider's control channel only once a capture shows the provider keeps it out of its session files: with Claude 2.1.263 on Linux x64, a stdio server got its value that way, and after a turn against a fake Messages API the value was in no file under Claude's home, configuration, project or session folders, in no new temporary folder, in no command line and in no API request.
- The MCP servers panel has two lists, Added in TeamRun, the shared servers, with Add server at its heading, and From your providers' own settings under it, and the chosen shared server's details beside them, topped at the first list's top, in a column of its own, 21rem wide, that goes under the first list once the page is narrower than 48rem.
  A shared server's row is a button that chooses it, the first one until another is chosen: a dot, green when every variable it names is set and it is signed in where it signs in, amber otherwise; its name, with its command and arguments, or HTTP, under it, then which variables aren't set or how its sign-in stands, in a few words; a green-edged chip for each provider it is on for; and "+ N can" for the providers that can take it but have it off.
  The details give the server's name and command or address, its sign-in's state with Sign in or Sign out where it signs in, a switch for each provider that can take it, and "N can't take it yet" with the providers that can't, grouped on one line by each reason's few words, or by its sentence where it has none, and Show for each provider's full sentence.
  Under Teammates they say each teammate follows its provider, who gets the server, and each exception, a teammate set On or Off, with its pills and a button that removes it; Add exception offers the teammates that follow, set Off for one that gets the server and On for one that doesn't.
  Edit opens the server's dialog, and Delete asks first.
  Add Server and Edit open a dialog with the name, the type, Command (stdio) or Address (HTTP), and for a stdio server its command, its arguments and its variables' names one a line, or for an HTTP server its address, its headers as `Name: VARIABLE` one a line, Sign in with OAuth, and, while that is off, its bearer token's variable; a field the protocol's checks refuse gets a plain message of its own under it, from the Window's `Resources`, before anything is sent, and the runtime part's refusal shows under the fields.
  Under them, Values has a row for each variable the fields name, with "From this machine's environment" or "Saved secret": a saved secret shows Saved with Change and Remove, never the value; a variable without a value on this machine, from the environment or a missing secret, shows "Not set on this machine" with a password field, and typing there chooses Saved secret; Saved secret without a value shows the field, and while a variable newly chosen as Saved secret has none typed, Save stays off.
  A row with only its choice is as dense as a Settings row; a value's line sits under the choice, labelled Value, so a row with a value takes more height.
  Saved also shows when the secret was last updated, from `updatedAt` on `SharedMcpSecret` and `SharedMcpVariable`, which the runtime part stamps when it keeps a reference, and nothing for a secret kept before that field.
  The field has Show and Hide for what is being typed; a value already saved is never shown, and Change opens an empty hidden field.
  A server that signs in shows its sign-in's state under its address, "Not signed in.", "Signed in." or "Needs a new sign-in." with why, and Sign in, or Sign out once signed in, a quarter rem after it; a sign-in that can't start shows why in the panel's error line.
  Typing that moves a variable to Saved secret says so under the rows while its field has text, and the MCP servers view says which secrets were saved once the dialog closes, under the name the server was saved with, until Dismiss or a server's deletion; both go through the announcer too.
  Save saves the server first, then each new value and each cleared reference; where the secret store or the runtime part refuses, the reason shows by that row and the dialog stays open on the saved server, and the environment choice still saves; a value saved for a reference the runtime part then refuses is removed again.
- Each shared server is on or off per provider, in the MCP servers panel, and off for every provider when it is added.
- Each teammate has a setting per shared server in the MCP servers panel: "As its provider", the default, "On" or "Off".
- A shared server reaches a teammate when the teammate's setting is On, or As its provider and the server is on for the teammate's provider; the default teammate follows its provider.
- The provider's own MCP servers are listed in the MCP servers panel, a row per provider, by its kind and label, with up to three of its servers named in chips, or their count, and the provider's switch at the row's end; the state each server has without connecting, Configured, Enabled, Disabled or Needs sign-in, stays in the list the runtime gives.
  A newly added provider starts with the switch on, as a chat turn runs in the person's own setup, unless its kind gives `ownMcpServersUnavailable`; a provider stored before keeps its value, and one stored before the switch existed reads off.
  A hidden background call, such as a title session, keeps the person's servers off whatever the switch says.
  The runtime lists them through `teamrun.providerOwnMcpServerList`, without `teamrun`, and listing starts no server, runs no hook and connects nothing of the person's: Claude's are the keys of `mcpServers` in `.claude.json` in its configuration folder and of every project's under `projects`, Configured, since its `mcpServerStatus` and `claude mcp list` connect each server; Codex's come from `config/read`, Disabled where `enabled` is `false` and Enabled otherwise; Copilot's from the files its row below names, Configured; Grok's from `x.ai/mcp/list`, asked with no session, Configured, each name once, and none when its answer has no `servers` field or a null one.
  TeamRun checks Claude's and Copilot's files against 32 MB before it reads them, since Claude grows `.claude.json` with its project history; a larger one fails the list, naming the file.
  Antigravity and OpenCode offer no list, so their list is `null` and the row says "Can't list them" and, after it, the kind's short note, or "uses its own while on" where it has none; none gives Needs sign-in yet, since each would have to connect to learn it.
  The switch is the provider's `ownMcpServers`, which `teamrun.providerUpdate` sets and refuses to turn on where the provider's kind gives `ownMcpServersUnavailable`.
  A kind with an ability table gives its own-servers switch row's reason, a sentence written for people, and none when that row works or isn't checked: so does every built-in kind and an added ACP agent.
  The panel gathers the providers whose kind gives a reason on one row, by name, "Can't use their own servers in TeamRun yet", with no switch, and Why shows each one's reason.
  A held provider whose kind's "List the person's own servers" cell works also gets a row of its own above it, with its servers as an own row shows them and no switch.
  Codex gives no reason, so its switch can be turned on; it covers the servers `config/read` lists and the apps connected to the person's account, which Codex adds through its built-in `codex_apps` server and `config/read` doesn't list.
  Its kind says so through `ownMcpServersNote`: "This switch also covers the apps connected to your Codex account, such as mail and files."
  Claude's switch also covers the connectors on the person's Claude account, which load by themselves with it on and which its list doesn't name, so its note is "This switch also covers the connectors on your Claude account, such as mail and files."
  OpenCode gives no reason either, and its note says what the switch leaves while off: "While this switch is off, OpenCode still starts the MCP servers its own settings turn on, and TeamRun refuses their tools."
  Each of these kinds gives its note in a few words too, `ownMcpServersShortNote`, which the row shows after the servers' count, "3 servers and your Claude account's connectors", with the full note in the summary's tooltip; a kind with a note and no few words shows the note there, and a descriptor from before the field reads as none.
  Grok gives "Grok runs the MCP servers its own settings turn on, so TeamRun leaves them to Grok.", since a chat turn runs in the person's own Grok home, so its switch stays off while those servers run.
  An adapter turns its own servers off whatever the stored switch while its kind gives that reason, and moving a provider's profile folder turns the switch off unless the same update turns it on.
- A shared server named like one of the provider's own replaces it while both are on.
  Copilot is the exception: TeamRun turns Copilot's own servers off by name, which would turn a shared server of the same name off too, and while they're on, both load under one name and how Copilot ranks them is unknown, so Copilot's adapter leaves it out of the turn and gives those reasons.
  Each adapter's `sharedServerUnavailable` is asked with the provider and the turn's folder, whose project files may name the provider's own servers, and the list without a folder.
  Signed in on Linux x64, Cursor 2026.10.22-27dcf5f, given a shared stdio server named like one in the person's `~/.cursor/mcp.json`, started both but gave the turn only the shared one's tools.
- A change applies from the next session a provider starts: the next turn, or for Codex the thread's next start or resume.
- Where a provider can't take a server, the panel leaves out its switch and folds it, with why, under "N can't take it yet"; a teammate whose provider can't take the server doesn't get it, whatever its setting.
- Every reason a provider can't take a shared server is a `SharedMcpRefusal`, its full sentence and, where it has one, the same in a few words, which `SharedMcpUnavailability.short` carries to the window: Core's reasons, such as "not passed yet", "<variable> not set", "<variable>'s saved secret missing" and "needs a sign-in", and each adapter's, such as Copilot's "takes HTTP servers only", Antigravity's "would keep it in its files" and Codex's and Pi's "reads values only from its environment"; a value from before the field reads as none, and the panel shows the sentence instead.
  A reply's note on a server left out keeps the full sentence.
- The runtime's `SharedMcpServersService` keeps the list in the `sharedMcpServers` table, each server's definition and switches as JSON, and its methods are `teamrun.sharedMcpServerList`, `sharedMcpServerCreate`, `sharedMcpServerUpdate`, `sharedMcpServerDelete`, `sharedMcpProviderSet`, `sharedMcpTeammateSet`, `sharedMcpSecretSet`, which sets or clears one variable's reference, `sharedMcpSignInStart`, which returns the address to open, and `sharedMcpSignOut`; the list gives each server the providers that can't take it, with why and its few words, as each adapter's `sharedServerUnavailable` says, and an adapter without one can't take any; every other provider can't while a variable the server names, for its command, a header or its bearer token, isn't set in the environment Noldova Desktop reads for that list, "The variable <name> isn't set in TeamRun's environment.", the reason a turn leaves the server out with.
  The list reads only whether each variable exists, never its value, so a variable set to an empty value counts as set; it gives each variable its source and whether its value is there, `isSetAsync` for a saved secret, and a saved secret that is missing or can't be checked makes every provider unable to take the server, with why.
  A switch of a deleted provider or teammate is dropped at the server's next change.
- As a turn starts, the engine gives the provider the servers that reach the turn's teammate; a server the provider can't take, or one naming a variable that isn't set in the environment Noldova Desktop reads as the turn starts, is left out, and the reply opens with a note naming it and why.
  Claude and Codex read each variable from their own environment, which is the one Noldova Desktop read as their process started without the credential variables their adapter withholds, so a server naming one of those is one the provider can't take.
- When Claude, Codex or Grok can't use a shared server it was given, the reply notes it once a chat for each provider, from the first time it happens after TeamRun starts: 'The provider "<label>" couldn't connect to the shared MCP server <name>, so its tools aren't in this chat.', 'The shared MCP server <name> needs a sign-in in the provider "<label>", so its tools aren't in this chat.' or, for a server Grok's own settings blocked, 'The provider "<label>" blocked the shared MCP server <name>, so its tools aren't in this chat: <reason>'.
  The note names no error text of the provider's, since an error can hold a header or a token; Grok's block reason is the one exception, since it names the policy that blocked the server, which the person needs to change it, and comes from Grok's settings rather than from the server, and TeamRun drops a path's folders from it, so a home folder's user name stays out of the chat.
  Claude's adapter reports a server in the `errors` of `setMcpServers` as a connection failure at once, then reads the others' states in `mcpServerStatus`, `failed` as a connection failure and `needs-auth` as a sign-in.
  It reads them beside the chat's first turn, which doesn't wait for them, and reports each failure as it reads it; while a shared server is `pending`, it reads them again every 0.5 s, for at most 10 s or until that turn ends, and logs a server still pending at 10 s or at the turn's end rather than noting it.
  Codex's adapter reads it from `mcpServer/startupStatus/updated` for the turn's thread, `failed`, and a sign-in when its `failureReason` is `reauthenticationRequired`.
  The engine keeps which failures each chat noted, by provider id, in `ReportedSharedMcpFailures`, in memory, and drops a chat's when the chat is deleted; the adapters report through `ITurnListener.onSharedServerFailedAsync`.
- An ACP agent doesn't read a stdio entry's environment from its own, since ACP's `mcpServers` carries the values, so TeamRun reads each value from the environment Noldova Desktop reads as the session starts and sends it in `session/new` or `session/load` over the agent's input, never in its arguments, a log or TeamRun's database (the maintainer's decision).
  HTTP header values, and a bearer token as `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, go the same way, and only to an agent whose `mcpCapabilities` has `http`; a turn passing an HTTP server to another agent fails, saying so.
  An agent takes a shared server only over a transport in its `IAcpAgent.sharedServerTransports`, each listed once a run shows the agent keeps the values out of its own session files, as OpenCode's and Cursor's are, Grok's, and Copilot's for HTTP; a server over another transport is unavailable, with the agent's `IAcpAgent.sharedServerRefusal` once a test machine has shown why, as for Copilot's stdio and all of Antigravity's, and otherwise "TeamRun passes no shared MCP servers to <agent> until a test machine shows <agent> keeps their values out of its session files."
  A stdio server starts through TeamRun's launcher, as Claude's and Codex's do: its entry's `command` is TeamRun's runtime, its `args` the launcher's, and its `env` the server's values with `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`.
  Without it, OpenCode 1.19.4, on Linux x64 with no sign-in, gave a stdio server given `env: []` and one given a single entry its whole environment, with `AGENT`, `OPENCODE`, `OPENCODE_CLIENT` and `OPENCODE_PID` added and the entry on top, as Claude does.
  Through it, on the same machine with a fake sender token and another server's fake value in OpenCode's environment, a server naming its variable saw only `HOME`, `PATH` and that variable, with its value, and one naming none only `HOME` and `PATH`; after a turn the value was in no file under OpenCode's home, configuration, data or project folders, in `/tmp` or in a process's command line, and both servers and their launchers ended once OpenCode did.
  Signed in, on Linux x64 with the same probe: Grok 1.0.70 and Cursor 2026.10.22-27dcf5f kept the values out over both transports, and each launched server saw only the basics and its own variable; Copilot 1.0.105 kept an HTTP server's values out but rejects a client's stdio server, with or without `type: "stdio"`; Antigravity 1.3.0 wrote a stdio server's value, a header and a bearer token into `antigravity-acp/conversations/<session id>.db-wal` under its `GEMINI_HOME`.

### Per provider

| Provider | Its own servers off | Its own servers on | TeamRun's and shared servers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Claude | `strictMcpConfig: true`, with `settingSources` `user` in a chat turn, and `project` and `local` too in a folder Claude Code trusts, as the instruction files' table says, which bring the person's permission rules, hooks and plugins, and `settingSources: []` in a title session; a turn with neither TeamRun's tools nor a shared server also has `mcpServers: {}` and `disallowedTools: ["mcp__*"]`; otherwise `setMcpServers` sets only `teamrun` and the turn's shared servers, and `canUseTool` reads the server from a tool's `mcp__<server>__<tool>` name exactly, allows `teamrun`'s, and refuses without asking a tool of any server but those. | On, in a chat turn, `strictMcpConfig: false`: the connectors connected to the person's Claude account load by themselves, `setMcpServers` also gets the `mcpServers` of `.claude.json` in its configuration folder and, over those, the local-scope `mcpServers` under `projects`, keyed as Claude keys them by the turn's repository root, a worktree's being its main repository's, or else by the folder, which TeamRun passes itself, as it did before chat turns loaded the settings sources; the panel, which has no one folder, lists every project's by name; TeamRun expands `${VAR}` and `${VAR:-default}` in their `command`, `args`, `env`, `url` and `headers` from Claude's environment first, since `setMcpServers` leaves a stdio server's as written, a variable with no value and no default stays as written, and `TEAMRUN_SENDER_TOKEN` is neither expanded nor inherited, set empty in a stdio server's `env`, so a `teamrun send` from such a server is refused, while the rest of Claude's environment, the command folder on `PATH` among it, reaches them as it does when Claude loads them itself; a kept chat keeps the servers it started with until its query starts again; a shared server or `teamrun` wins a same name, an unreadable file is logged and the chat starts without them, Claude's reason for refusing one of them is neither logged nor shown, since those settings can hold tokens, `disallowedTools` is empty and `canUseTool` treats their tools as a shared server's, asking the person unless the mode is to bypass permissions. `disableClaudeAiConnectors` in the person's settings isn't read, so this switch alone decides, and changing it starts the chat's query again on its session. The servers Claude Code starts itself, a plugin's and an approved project `.mcp.json`'s, get Claude's environment, so a chat that serves TeamRun's tools keeps `TEAMRUN_SENDER_TOKEN` out of it, at its start and in each turn's and wake's flag settings, and none of them, nor a hook, can act as the teammate; a chat without TeamRun's tools keeps it out of Claude's environment at its start too while the person's own servers are on, so none of them gets it as Claude starts them with the query, and sets it in the first turn's flag settings, as in each later turn's, for its shell's `teamrun` command; since the flag settings reach every process Claude starts afterwards, it first waits, for at most 10 seconds, until `mcpServerStatus` shows none of the servers Claude starts itself pending and lists each one it is known to start, those of the project's `.mcp.json` in the chat's folder or one above it, less its `disabledMcpjsonServers`, and from a later turn on those the chat's last init message named, since Claude may list a server only some time after the query starts, and past that, or when their state can't be read, sets the token empty and notes the turn; a first turn can't wait for the servers of the person's plugins this way, since Claude names them only in the init message, which comes with the turn's prompt, after the token is set, so a plugin's server Claude lists late may get the first turn's token. | TeamRun's at the endpoint; shared servers through `setMcpServers`, stdio or HTTP, a stdio server through TeamRun's launcher with its variables as their values in `env`, and an HTTP server's headers as `${VAR}` references, with a bearer token as `Authorization: Bearer ${VAR}`, and `disallowedTools` empty while there are any; `canUseTool` asks the person before a shared server's tool runs, and a change of servers starts the chat's query again on its session. A server naming a variable `ClaudeEnvironment` withholds is unavailable. |
| Codex | Each server `config/read` names is turned off with `mcp_servers.<name>.enabled = false` in the thread's config, and the apps with `features.apps = false` there and `-c features.apps=false` on the app-server, with plugins off. The thread's `features` table overrides the app-server's flag, so a thread needs its own key, and the flag covers a sign-in check and a listing, which have no thread. | Those overrides and the flag are left out, and so is `features.plugins`, so a thread starts the person's servers, plugins and `codex_apps`; the adapter starts another app-server when the switch changes, and a turn in flight ends on the one it started on. Confirmed live with Codex 0.165.0 signed in with ChatGPT. | `mcp_servers.<name>` in the thread's config: TeamRun's with `url`, `http_headers` and `tool_timeout_sec`, and a turn with TeamRun's tools while the endpoint isn't serving fails before Codex starts; a shared stdio one through TeamRun's launcher, with the runtime as `command`, the launcher's `args`, `env` holding `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE` and `env_vars` naming the variables, so Codex's own filter stays as a second layer, or `url` with `env_http_headers` and `bearer_token_env_var`, which name the variables Codex reads from its own environment, in place of a server of the same name in the profile's configuration. Codex 0.164.0 on Linux x64, not signed in, started both kinds on `thread/start`, gave a stdio server only the variables `env_vars` names, and sent the HTTP server both headers. A server naming `OPENAI_API_KEY` or `CODEX_API_KEY` is unavailable. |
| Copilot | Copilot starts with `--disable-builtin-mcps` and `--disable-mcp-server=<name>` for each server named, by name only, in every source Copilot 1.0.103 loads: `mcp-config.json` under the provider's `COPILOT_HOME`, or `~/.copilot` for the default profile; for a turn, `.mcp.json` and `.github/mcp.json` in the turn's folder and each folder above it up to the repository root, or to the file system's root outside a repository; `installed-plugins/<source>/<plugin>/.mcp.json` under that home; and each plugin its `settings.json` turns on in `enabledPlugins`, whose server bears the plugin's name, as the built-in `browser-use` does. Whole-line `//` comments are allowed. A file there that exists but can't be read or parsed, or is larger than 32 MB, refuses the start, naming the file. A file there that names a server `teamrun`, or a `settings.json` that turns on a plugin `teamrun`, refuses the start too, naming the file and its source, user, project or plugin, and asking the person to rename it: turning it off by name would turn off TeamRun's own `teamrun` from the ACP session as well, and how Copilot ranks a session's server against a file's of the same name is unknown, since GitHub's documentation ranks only the files and Copilot ships as a closed binary, so TeamRun refuses rather than guess. The model listing does the same without a folder. | A chat turn leaves those arguments out, so Copilot starts the person's servers as in their own Copilot; a title session, the model listing, a sign-in and a fork keep them, whatever the switch says. Copilot 1.0.105, signed in on Linux x64, started a user server and its built-in `github-mcp-server` in a chat turn with the switch on, and neither with it off or in a title session after that turn. It loads a project's MCP files only in a folder its own trust store marks trusted, as in the person's own Copilot. | ACP's `mcpServers` on `session/new` and `session/load`; an HTTP server only when the agent's `mcpCapabilities` has `http`, which Copilot 1.0.103 gives. Its `sharedServerTransports` lists HTTP: Copilot 1.0.105, signed in on Linux x64, sent an HTTP server's header and bearer token on each request and kept both out of every file and command line. A stdio server is unavailable, saying Copilot refuses one, since Copilot 1.0.105 logs `Rejecting non-http/sse MCP server "<name>" from client` for each. A shared server named like one in those sources, for the turn's folder, is left out: outside a chat turn that keeps the person's servers, its `--disable-mcp-server=<name>` would turn the shared one off too, and in such a turn both load under one name, and how Copilot ranks them is unknown. Copilot 1.0.105, signed in, asked before a shared HTTP server's tool ran, with the tool's own name as the card's title, `probe_echo_http` for the probe's, and once refused didn't call it. |
