| Grok | Not offered: a chat turn runs in the person's own Grok home, so TeamRun turns none of the servers their Grok settings turn on off. | Always on, as the person's own Grok settings say, managed and imported Claude and Cursor servers included. When their `requirements.toml` lists `allowed_mcp_servers`, that list gates a session's servers too, so a list without `teamrun` blocks TeamRun's server; TeamRun doesn't write their file and doesn't read it before a turn, which would take a TOML reader for one key, so the block shows only in TeamRun's log, with the reason Grok's own `logs/unified.jsonl` gives, once the wait for TeamRun's tools ends, as below. A shared server the list leaves out is blocked the same way, and TeamRun reads that log for the turn's shared servers once the turn's session starts and again when the turn ends, whether its reply completed, failed or was interrupted, since Grok can log a block after the session starts, and notes each one Grok blocked since the turn started in the chat, once a chat, naming the server and Grok's reason without a path's folders, spaces in a folder's name included; a note that can't reach the chat is logged and the turn goes on; a block of TeamRun's own server or of a server the turn didn't pass makes no note. Whether a server of the person's named `teamrun` replaces TeamRun's is unchecked. | ACP's `mcpServers`, and TeamRun's `use_tool` guard (`routedMcpTool`) refusing a `teamrun__` call to a tool the turn doesn't list and asking about every other call, the person's own servers' included. Grok 1.0.70, signed in on Linux x64, kept a shared server's values out over both transports, stdio through TeamRun's launcher and HTTP, but in TeamRun's dedicated profile of the time; signed in on that machine in the person's own Grok home, it asked before a shared server's tool, ran it once allowed, and kept the values out of its home's files and its command line, so its `sharedServerTransports` are stdio and HTTP. |
| OpenCode | Their tools, and the person's custom tools and a plugin's, are refused by a rule for every tool that starts `OPENCODE_PERMISSION`, as [The runtime part](#the-runtime-part) says; in a chat turn the servers themselves, from the person's and the project's `opencode.json`, still start, since OpenCode has no setting that turns them all off, as the group's note says. A launch outside a chat turn, such as the model listing, turns each server those files name off with `mcp.<name>.enabled = false` in `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT`, and refuses to start when one of them exists but can't be read, as the group's note says. Allow All refuses them too. | On, in a chat turn, that rule asks instead, so a chat's agent asks before each of their tools and Allow All allows them; the built-in sub-agents still refuse them, since a sub-agent's ask reaches no card, and any launch outside a chat turn, such as a model list, refuses them, whatever the switch says. An agent's rule that the person's own settings give one of those tools by name decides it either way. | ACP's `mcpServers`; TeamRun's rules ask before each `<server>_*` tool but those approved, as `<server>_<tool>`. Checked live on 1.19.4: OpenCode listed TeamRun's tools before the first prompt, ran an approved one without asking, and asked for another as `teamrun_<tool>`. Its `sharedServerTransports` lists stdio, through TeamRun's launcher as above, and HTTP: OpenCode 1.19.4 on Linux x64, with no sign-in, sent an HTTP server's header and bearer token on each of its requests, and after a turn neither value was in any file under its home, configuration, data or project folders, in `/tmp` or in a process's command line. |
| Muse Code | Not offered: Muse Code's own servers run as the person set them up, and `muse acp` has no way to switch them. This rests on Muse Code's documents, unchecked live. | Not offered, as above. | Stdio and HTTP servers go in `session/new` and `session/load`; `muse acp` has no SSE servers, and a server that fails to start doesn't stop the session. |
| Pi | Not offered: Pi's own servers, from its `mcp.json`, run as the person set them up, and TeamRun can't switch them. Each of their tools is a `tool_call` like any other, as are Pi's `codemode` and `tool_search` and each call a `codemode` script makes, so Ask asks and Read only refuses; the server processes themselves still start with Pi. This rests on Pi 1.4.0's source, unchecked live. | Not offered, as above. | TeamRun's extension adds TeamRun's server and each shared server for the chat's Pi, through `pi.registerMcpServer` with `direct` exposure, from `TEAMRUN_PI_TOOLS_URL` and `TEAMRUN_PI_TOOLS_TOKEN` and from `TEAMRUN_PI_SHARED_SERVERS`, a JSON object of Pi `mcpServers` entries; every Pi TeamRun starts gets all three, empty when the turn has none, so it never takes those of a TeamRun whose turn started it. The extension adds each server in its own `try`, so a server Pi refuses, such as one with a name another extension took, never costs it its `tool_call` gate or the other servers. A stdio server runs through TeamRun's launcher, as for Claude, and an HTTP server's headers are `${NAME}` references Pi fills from its own environment, so no value travels in that JSON; a server with a saved secret, a sign-in or an account stays off for Pi, "reads values only from its environment", since a value in Pi's environment is one its commands also see; so does a server that names a `TEAMRUN_PI_` variable, such as the token of TeamRun's tools. Pi names a server's tools with each `-` as `_`, so a turn takes only the first by name of servers such as `my-api` and `my_api` and notes the other left out. A different set of shared servers starts another Pi for the chat. A server in the person's own `mcp.json` with a shared server's name wins over it, as Pi's documents say, and no shared server connects when Pi's MCP is off or another extension replaces it, which TeamRun doesn't see, so the chat doesn't show it; for TeamRun's own server it does, as the notes below say. This rests on Pi's MCP documents and source at v1.1.0, unchecked live. |
| Cursor | Cursor in TeamRun also starts the MCP servers in the person's `~/.cursor/mcp.json`, `%USERPROFILE%\.cursor\mcp.json` on Windows, outside the profile, as the README and the add hint say, each naming the file of the system it runs on. The dedicated profile holds no `mcp.json`, but Cursor reads the person's own `~/.cursor/mcp.json` from the home folder whatever `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` says, with no approval, and starts its servers: a run of `cursor-agent mcp list` in a TeamRun profile on Linux showed the person's server ready. Cursor CLI 2026.11.22-e9042b2 has no setting, flag or ACP option to skip that file, by its documents, changelog, `--help` and `acp --help`: the `disabledServers` list it reads from `CURSOR_WORKER_MCP_CONFIG_JSON` applies to `agent worker` only. A chat turn runs in the person's own setup, so TeamRun doesn't hide it there; a launch outside a chat turn points `HOME` at the profile's empty `title-home`, except on macOS, so it starts none of them; whether those servers' tools ask through TeamRun's card is not yet checked. A project's `.cursor/mcp.json` loads only once approved in the profile, so the same run skipped one; whether a project's `.cursor/cli.json` allow list lets Cursor run commands or tools without asking is not yet checked. | Unavailable: the dedicated profile names no server of its own. | ACP's `mcpServers`; the profile's allow list holds each approved tool as `Mcp(teamrun:<tool>)`, and Cursor asks before the others, since the person's allow rules that could reach `teamrun` are left out. Its `sharedServerTransports` lists stdio, through TeamRun's launcher, and HTTP: Cursor 2026.10.22-27dcf5f, signed in on Linux x64, gave a launched server only the basics and its own variable, sent an HTTP server's header and bearer token on each request, and kept every value out of its files, `/tmp` and command lines. It asked before each of their tools ran, titled `<server>-<tool>: <tool>`, such as `probe-entry-probe_echo_entry: probe_echo_entry`, with the tool call itself titled `<server>: <tool>` and its input as `providerIdentifier`, `toolName` and `args`; once refused, it didn't call the tool. Given a shared server named like one in the person's `~/.cursor/mcp.json`, it started both and gave the turn only the shared one's tools, so the shared one replaces the person's, as the rule above says. |
| fx | fx reads the person's own servers in `~/.fx/mcp.json`, whatever `FX_HOME` says, and a project's `.mcp.json`, whose servers wait for `fx mcp approve`. | Unavailable: TeamRun has no switch for fx's own servers. | ACP's `mcpServers`. None yet: no signed-in turn has run, so whether fx keeps a server's values out of its session files under `~/.fx` is unconfirmed until a signed-in capture; its `sharedServerTransports` is empty and a shared server is unavailable, saying so. |
| Antigravity | The dedicated profile holds no server TeamRun adds, as today. | Unavailable until Antigravity's ACP server is shown to read a profile's servers. | ACP's `mcpServers`, as Copilot's. None: Antigravity 1.3.0, signed in on Linux x64, keeps a session's `mcpServers`, with their values, in `antigravity-acp/conversations/<session id>.db-wal` under its `GEMINI_HOME`, so its `sharedServerTransports` is empty and a shared server is unavailable, saying so. |
| Gemini | No adapter: Gemini was removed as a provider. | — | If it returns, ACP's `mcpServers`, as Copilot's. |

- Each provider's row is from its published configuration and the code today; the issue that builds a row confirms it against the provider's program, and the panel shows a switch unavailable until it does.
- A provider can add servers of its own that its list and switch don't name, as Codex does with `codex_apps`.
  Each other provider but fx, which came after and whose row says this isn't yet checked, was checked for those from its published documents and the code, without running it:
  - Claude: with its switch off, `strictMcpConfig: true` restricts a session to the servers passed in, so the claude.ai connectors aren't loaded; the Agent SDK's shipped program says so, though the documents name only `disableClaudeAiConnectors` and `ENABLE_CLAUDEAI_MCP_SERVERS=false`.
  - Copilot: the built-in `github-mcp-server` is off with `--disable-builtin-mcps`, which TeamRun passes.
    The person adds connectors through `/mcp`; the documents don't say whether the flag covers them or the servers of bundled plugins.
  - Cursor: no built-in server or connector is documented.
    Team marketplace plugins can carry servers on Teams and Enterprise, and the documents don't say whether `agent mcp disable` reaches them.
  - Grok: its switch isn't offered, and its servers are its own settings' and those it imports from `~/.claude.json`, `.cursor/mcp.json` and `.mcp.json`.
    Account connectors are documented for grok.com only.
  - OpenCode: no built-in server.
    `websearch` reaches a hosted service with OpenCode's own provider, and `.well-known/opencode` can give an organization's defaults after sign-in, which a local `enabled: false` overrides.
  - Pi: its switch isn't offered, and its servers are those in its `mcp.json`, as its row says; that row's reading of Pi's source names none tied to an account.
  - Antigravity: a built-in Chrome DevTools server, by its changelog, with no off switch found, and none tied to an account.
- Grok offers a session's MCP tools only through its own `search_tool`, which finds them, and `use_tool`, which calls one by its `<server>__<tool>` name, so TeamRun leaves both among Grok's tools; the person's own servers' tools come the same way, as Grok's row above says.
  Grok's permission request for such a call is for `use_tool`, so when its raw input holds `use_tool`'s `tool_name`, with `tool_input` or `tool_input_file`, and `variant`, which Grok's requests add, and nothing else, the approval shows the inner tool's name and its arguments, or the file that holds them, capped at 500 characters with control characters replaced; otherwise it shows Grok's title.
  TeamRun refuses without asking, with a note, a request it takes for a `use_tool` call, by its title or by a raw input holding `tool_name` or `file` whatever else it holds, whose `tool_name` starts with `teamrun__`, unless its `tool_name` is a string, it has no `file`, and the rest of the name is one of the tools TeamRun lists for the turn; it allows such a call to an approved tool without asking, and asks the person about every other request, as for the person's own servers' tools.
  Grok waits only two seconds for a session's MCP servers before it takes the prompt, so Grok's first `search_tool` could find none of TeamRun's tools; for Grok, TeamRun sends the prompt once the endpoint has listed the turn's tools, or when the wait below ends without them, and then logs that the tools weren't listed, with the reason Grok's own `logs/unified.jsonl` gives when it blocked TeamRun's server since the turn started.
  Every ACP agent that `awaitsTeamRunTools` waits at most `teamRunToolsWait`, 5 seconds, counted from its `session/new` or `session/load` answer, and the wait ends at once when listing the tools to the turn fails (`TurnTools.failed`, which the engine settles when `listMcpToolsAsync` throws) or the agent's process ends.
  The 30 seconds it replaced came from the earlier stdio bridge, which had to start and reach the runtime before it could list; TeamRun now serves the tools on a loopback HTTP endpoint, so the wait covers only the agent's own connect and `tools/list`.
  A probe that sent each agent `initialize` and a `session/new` naming a loopback HTTP server, and timed the server's `tools/list` from the `session/new` answer, on Linux x64, 5 sessions idle and 10 with two busy threads per core loading it, found:
  - Grok: 0 to 105 ms after the answer idle, the 105 on the first, cold start; from 457 ms before to 18 ms after it loaded.
  - OpenCode: 4 to 49 ms before the answer, idle and loaded, so its wait ends at once.
  - Cursor 2026.10 and fx: they connect during `session/new` but call `tools/list` only 33 to 51 ms after `session/prompt`, never in 30 seconds without one, so a wait before the prompt can't see the tools listed; neither awaits them, and they get the prompt at once.
  Five seconds is about 48 times the slowest listing measured, so a loaded machine or a slower start still lists well within it, and the wait costs a turn nothing when the tools come.
  The general agent, Gemini CLI before 0.67, wasn't measured, as it wasn't at hand; it keeps the same five seconds.
  When the wait ends without the tools, the turn notes in the chat why, for every agent that awaits them: that it didn't list them within the wait, that listing them failed and how, or that it ended first; Gemini CLI's own reasons below take their place for it.
  The wait also ends when the turn does, so a turn that fails after its session started leaves no timer behind.
  Grok's startup lists each server's tools as part of its handshake (`run_handshake` calls `get_tool_registrations`, which calls `tools/list`), so the listing comes before any prompt.
  The guard takes a call as TeamRun's only by its full `teamrun__<tool>` name, never a bare one, since the person's own servers share Grok's session and one could list a tool of the same name.
  The guard sees only the calls Grok asks about: a call the person's own rules allow, or any call in Always-approve, runs without an ask, as in their own Grok; Grok's title, raw input and asking are unchecked live.
- Gemini CLI connects no MCP server in a folder it doesn't trust, TeamRun's and the shared servers alike, and none of its ACP answers says so; ACP has no way for a client to pass trust, which Gemini CLI takes only from its own `trustedFolders.json`, its folder-trust setting or `GEMINI_CLI_TRUST_WORKSPACE`, as its source and docs say and a turn of 0.67.0 on Linux x64 showed.
  TeamRun trusts no folder for the person, since trust also loads the folder's own Gemini CLI settings, `.env` files, hooks, commands and MCP servers.
  Instead, when TeamRun's tools are missing from a turn of an ACP agent whose `initialize` answer names it `gemini-cli` in `agentInfo.name`, a registry install or a local command alike, the turn notes in the chat, once a chat, why they aren't in it.
  `GeminiFolderTrust.isUntrusted` reads Gemini CLI's trust signals for the turn's launch, its environment, folder and arguments, as Gemini CLI 0.67.0's source does, and never writes them: `GEMINI_RESTRICTED_MODE` and `GEMINI_CLI_TRUST_WORKSPACE`, then `--skip-trust`, then the folder-trust setting merged from the system defaults, the person's `settings.json` and the system settings, then an IDE's trust, then the longest rule in `trustedFolders.json` that holds the folder.
  When they say the folder is untrusted, the note says so and how to trust it: run `gemini` in it and choose Trust folder; when they don't, or a file can't be read as Gemini CLI reads it, `GeneralAcpAgent.readMissingTools` gives a general note, that Gemini CLI didn't list TeamRun's tools and what can leave them out.
  Gemini CLI from 0.67 answers `session/new` and `session/load` only after `config.initialize()`, which in ACP mode awaits its MCP start-up, so by that answer TeamRun's endpoint has listed the tools or never will; for a session opened in the turn, TeamRun checks the tools at that answer rather than waiting (`GeneralAcpAgent.listsToolsBeforeSession`), and a later turn on that kept session notes them missing without waiting when that answer listed none of them (`AcpKeptSession.toolsUnlisted`); a kept session whose tools were listed, or an earlier version, still waits.

### What replaces the command line in teammates' instructions

- The lines on `send` and `list-chats`, with their here-documents, here-strings and `$OutputEncoding`, give way to a few lines on TeamRun's tools: that `teamrun_list_chats` lists the chats, `teamrun_send_message` sends to another chat as from the caller without waiting, and the other `teamrun_` tools change TeamRun, some after the person allows it.
  A turn whose adapter takes the tools (`IProviderAdapter.takesTeamRunTools`) names them alone, `teamrun_send_message` included; any other turn names `list-chats`, `list-teammates`, `list-messages` and `send`, and so does an ACP turn whose tools aren't listed within the wait.
  A read-only mode refuses `teamrun_send_message`, so the lines say a teammate in one answers in its reply.
- The lines on reading `From @<name> in chat ...` and the default teammate's prefix stay, and say to answer with `teamrun_send_message`.
- A turn with the tools reads that `teamrun_send_message` with `wake_when_done` wakes it with a new turn and the replies' last text once they end, so it ends its turn instead of waiting.
- A turn answering such a message reads, after the message, that the sender gets its reply by itself when its turn ends, so it only replies (`Resources.awaitedReplyNote`); when the wakes can't be read, the turn runs without that note and the log says why.
- The `command` scripts and `TEAMRUN_SENDER_TOKEN` stay for the person's command line and for a provider that can't start the server; with them, the instructions keep today's lines.

### Sessions outside TeamRun

A coding agent running outside TeamRun on the same computer, such as a Codex or Claude Code session in a terminal, can message a teammate, in its own chat for outside messages or in any existing chat, and read the replies to its own messages.
- `teamrun.outsideSessions` turns it on, off by default, and `teamrun.outsidePort`, 47391 by default, names the port, both shared across the devices of a data folder, since the runtime part has no device id to read a device setting by; `OutsideSessions` in the runtime part follows both and listens with a second `McpHttpEndpoint` on `127.0.0.1` only, with the same `Host`, `Origin` and method rules as the teammates' endpoint.
  A port that can't be opened leaves the sessions off and shows why in the Outside sessions view; Off closes the endpoint and ends every waiting read.
- One token opens it: 32 random bytes made when the sessions are first turned on, kept as the secret `teamrun.outsideToken` and never in the database or a log; the runtime keeps its SHA-256 and compares in constant time.
  Where the system's keyring can't keep the token, as on a Linux desktop with none, the sessions stay off and the view's state says so, rather than On without a token; the next line says what each other secret failure does.
  The secret call's failure reason, read with `SecretFailures.reasonOf` and never from its message, says which: `NotConnected`, the app's main process not connected yet, as just after a restart, leaves them waiting with no failure and applies again at the app's next change, even with no window open; `Undecryptable`, a kept token this machine's store can no longer decrypt, makes and keeps a new one and listens, as Noldova Desktop asks, and once that token is kept, a note under the view's state, which stays On, until the next apply tells the person to give outside agents the new token, while the old one gets the usual refusal, so no access widens and nothing waits on the person; `NoDevice` says the app has no device identity yet and to restart TeamRun; `Unprotected` and any failure without a reason keep the keyring message.
  The view shows it hidden until Show, with Copy and Reset token; a reset makes a new one, after any switch or port change already applying and before any that comes during it, so the token shown is the one accepted, ends every waiting read and forgets every message id, so the old token's ids read as unknown, the same as an id TeamRun never gave.
  The view's settings for Codex and Claude Code name the address and the variable `TEAMRUN_TOKEN`, never the token, so it stays out of every file.
- Four tools, and no others: `teamrun_list_teammates` lists each teammate's id, name, provider, model and whether it is working; `teamrun_list_chats` lists the 30 chats with the latest messages, leaving out chats with none, each with its `id`, `title`, `project` name or `null`, `teammate`, the one a message wakes when it names none, its chosen responder or else its only member, or `null`, its member `teammates` and whether it is working, and no message text; `teamrun_send_message` takes `text`, an optional `chat_id`, a `teammate_id`, needed without `chat_id`, and an optional `from`, and returns a `message_id`; `teamrun_read_replies` takes up to 50 `message_ids` and an optional `wait_seconds`, at most 30, and answers each one's state: `waiting`, `working`, `answered` with the reply, `failed` with the error, or `unknown`.
  A read gives only the reply to each of the agent's own messages, never other messages in the chat or its history.
  That reply is the teammate's own words, and the teammate knows the chat, so it can repeat what the chat holds; the maintainer accepted this as the cost of the opt-in.
  A wait ends when every reply has ended, when its time is up, when the caller goes, on Off, on a reset or when TeamRun quits, and answers what it has; it wakes on TeamRun's events rather than polling.
  Nothing is pushed to the outside agent.
- Each teammate has one chat for outside messages, without a project, titled `From outside TeamRun · <name>` and made again when it is gone.
  Making it publishes `StateInvalidated`, so it shows under Chats at once, as a chat made through a request does.
  It starts on the provider's default mode, never an unrestricted one, and never takes the teammate's own mode, so it never inherits Full Access; a mode the person raises by hand in that chat stays for that chat only.
  A teammate whose outside chat is still answering refuses the next outside message, so an outside agent reads the reply before it sends again.
- With `chat_id`, the message goes into that existing chat instead, any chat while the sessions are on, since the switch is the person's opt-in: to `teammate_id` when it is one of the chat's members, or to the chat's own teammate as `teamrun_list_chats` names it; an unknown or deleted chat, a teammate that isn't a member, or a chat with no teammate to answer is refused by name, and a chat still answering refuses it as busy, so it never queues.
  The reply runs in the teammate's mode in that chat, except that an unrestricted one, such as Full Access the person confirmed there or for the teammate, runs as the provider's default mode for that reply and every turn that follows from it, such as a wake or a sub-agent, also when the person changes the mode while it runs; the chat's mode stays as the person set it, and only the teammate's own outside chat keeps a mode the person raised by hand for outside messages.
  While any background task in that chat runs and the cap would change the mode, the post is refused as busy (`Conflict`): a provider session the tasks hold open, such as Claude's query, takes a turn in another mode only by restarting, which would end the person's tasks.
  A Codex thread held by its tasks or sub-agents ignores a resume's settings, so a turn in another mode than the held thread's sends its `approvalPolicy` and `sandboxPolicy` on `turn/start`, which Codex keeps for later turns, as its own client does when the person switches modes; a workspace-write sandbox set this way takes Codex's defaults, not the config's `sandbox_workspace_write`.
- An outside message is stored with a `MessageSender` whose `outside` is true and whose name is the `from` label, cut to 40 characters on one line with control and format characters, such as a right-to-left override or a zero-width space, and `@` taken out, or `unnamed session`; its mentions aren't read, so it starts no other teammate.
  It starts its teammate with no approval card, by design, where a teammate's message to another shows one unless the sending turn is unrestricted: the sender holds the token the person copied, as the person would; what the teammate then does still asks in its default mode, and its hops count from 0.
  The teammate reads it as from a session outside TeamRun that no one checked, to treat as data from an unknown sender, and, in the turn that answers it, told that its reply goes back to that session, so it answers or asks there rather than looking for another way to reach it; a later turn's transcript, or another member's, leaves that out, with its text unchanged between `<outside-message-<id>>` and `</outside-message-<id>>`, whose id is 12 random bytes, base64url, new each time the message is written into a transcript, after a line saying only that closing tag ends it; the sender never sees the id, so no closing form its text holds, with attributes, a format character in the name, lookalike brackets or entities, can end the message; the chat shows the quoted label, `from outside TeamRun` and an Outside chip, and links no chat.
- Message ids are 16 random bytes, base64url, mapped to the messages in the preference `outsideMessages`, which keeps the last 500.

### Building it

The design PR names these issues; each builds and tests its part, and this section's text moves into the sections above as it lands.

1. Noldova Desktop: a module's own Node entry connects to the runtime as a client, as the command line does, never as `ShellClients.mainProcess`.
2. Noldova Desktop: no client but the app's main process can connect as `ShellClients.mainProcess`.
3. Only the person answers approvals: Core accepts `teamrun.approvalDecide` and permission answers only from `ShellClients.mainProcess`.
4. Built: the server, the bridge, since removed, and member tokens for Codex and the ACP providers, Claude's in-process SDK server, the agent sessions, the MCP subset, the read-only tools with `teamrun_list_teammates`, and the new instructions.
   Left for 5: answering an ACP agent's permission request for TeamRun's own tools without asking the person, which needs a recording of how Copilot and Grok name an MCP tool in their requests; until then such a request asks the person as any other does.
   Grok's is answered so, by the tool's full `teamrun__<tool>` name, as Grok's own requests name it.
   Checked on the fakes and the real Claude Agent SDK; the live check with Codex and Grok waits on the next release.
5. TeamRun as the permissions module, whose answer to a permission request is a `MainProcessMethod`, and the tools that change chats, projects, teammates and replies.
6. Built for the pages and chats: the panel tools, `teamrun.panelRequested`, `teamrun.windowFocused`, `teamrun.windowClosed` and `teamrun.panelAnswered`.
   Left: the four views, which wait on Noldova Desktop.
7. Built: the shared MCP servers' list, the provider and teammate switches and their rules, the shared servers' table in the MCP servers panel with its add and edit dialog, passing them to Claude, with a stdio server's values over `setMcpServers`, and Codex, every stdio server through TeamRun's launcher, and to an ACP agent with their values in `session/new`, OpenCode's stdio and HTTP servers among them; Copilot, Grok, Antigravity and Cursor stay unavailable until a test machine shows each keeps the values out of its session files.
8. Built: the providers' own servers, listed by name and state, with the switch per provider, and Copilot's servers off by default.
   Left: each row above but Claude's, Codex's and OpenCode's, which are turned on, confirmed against the provider's program on a computer, which needs Copilot there; until then each of them gives its own-servers switch row's reason, as Grok and Pi do, so their switches stay off, and the panel gives the reason in each one's group and still lists the servers.
9. The loopback endpoint, part 1: the session tokens and Copilot over HTTP; part 2: every ACP agent whose `initialize` offers HTTP MCP servers, which Grok, Antigravity 1.3.0, Cursor 2026.10.22-27dcf5f and OpenCode 1.19.4 all do; part 3: HTTP for every provider, as the maintainer decided: Codex's endpoint in the thread's configuration with a token per held thread, Claude's `setMcpServers` in place of its in-process server, and the bridge removed with `teamrun.mcpToolList`, `teamrun.mcpToolCall` and `TEAMRUN_MCP_ENDPOINT=off`.
   Every teammate's turn and wake has TeamRun's tools, so it fails, naming the cause, when the endpoint couldn't listen, which stops every teammate turn of every provider until TeamRun restarts, or when an ACP agent offers no HTTP MCP servers.

## What each provider can do

Every provider answers every ability in `ProviderAbilityTable`, in `src/noldova.teamrun.providers/src/models/provider-ability-table.ts`, and its descriptor carries that row.
An answer is one of four: it works, through ACP's own methods, a provider's addition to ACP or a path outside ACP; the provider can't; TeamRun hasn't built it; or no one has seen it work or fail against the provider yet.
An answer that isn't "works" says why in the code.
An ability without an answer fails the build.
A Runtime spec takes the columns from the providers TeamRun registers, in that order, and an added ACP agent: a provider without a row fails it, and so does this table when it differs from the code.

Protocol's `ProviderAbilities.names` names each ability, and each cell's `answerLabel`, `sourceNote` and `label` say its answer, so Settings and this table read the same words.
Settings' page "What each provider can do", `teamrun.abilities`, which the Window registers with `registerSettingsPage`, shows one provider at a time, chosen from the kinds that have a table, every built-in one and each added ACP agent: each ability is a setting row in its group, Chats, Choices, Modes and approvals, In a reply, Sub-agents and commands, MCP servers or Sign-in and install, with the answer at the row's end, `Works`, `Can't`, `Not built yet` or `Not checked yet`, then where a working one comes from, or the reason as a sentence.
The page gives a reason through the cell's `reasonFor`, which leaves out a not-built reason's leading "TeamRun doesn't support this for it yet", since the answer beside it says so already, and names the provider where the reason starts with "it" or "its"; a control or command that refuses keeps the whole reason.
The page's rows are in Settings search too: the Window's `AbilitySettingsRows` gives them with `setSettingsRows` from the part's activation, one `SettingsRow` per ability with an id such as `teamrun.providers.runTurn`, its name as the title and its group and answer, such as "Chats: Works", as the description, for the provider chosen last, or the first kind with a table, and gives them again when the kinds load or the person chooses another provider.
Each row's element carries that id as `data-setting`, so opening a search result scrolls the page to the row, and the page opens again on the provider chosen last.
What TeamRun offers reads the row.
A kind's `supportsResume`, `supportsFork` and `ownMcpServersUnavailable` come from its resume, fork and own-servers switch rows.
A kind without a table has them from its constructor's last parameter, which only `ProviderKind.fromJson` gives, from the JSON it reads, and `withNamesChats`, from the kind it copies; any other kind without a table has no resume, no fork and no reason against the switch.
An ability counts as offered unless its row says it can't or isn't built: one not yet checked is built, so it stays offered.
Each row is one answer for every platform.
Two abilities are read narrowly: "A background sub-agent outlives its turn" works only where the sub-agent keeps running after the turn ends and wakes the chat with its result, so Copilot's, which holds its turn open until each one ends, can't; and "Tokens and cost of a reply" works only where TeamRun reads the reply's token counts, so a context meter and a cost alone, all TeamRun reads from Muse Code, aren't it.
An answer that differs by platform stays a flag: `singleAccount` keeps Cursor and Muse Code to one account per Mac and fx to one per computer, and the "Several accounts" cell names the platforms where it can't work, in its reason, or in its note where it works elsewhere, as Cursor's does.
